# Architecture and Research Decisions

ADR format: Status / Context / Decision / Consequences. Append new ADRs at
the bottom; never edit or renumber a merged one — record a new ADR that
supersedes it instead.

---

## ADR-001: Technology stack

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Needed a stack chosen for reliability and reviewer-legibility, not novelty, that a reviewer can verify without trusting opaque abstractions.

**Decision:** Python 3.12 + uv; Pydantic v2 for all schemas; DuckDB for local analytical queries; sentence-transformers + FAISS for embeddings/retrieval; litellm as the single model interface; pytest + ruff; Typer for the CLI; JSONL as the canonical benchmark format; Docker Compose for reproduction. Explicitly rejected: LangChain/LlamaIndex, any hosted vector database, any web frontend, any experiment-tracking SaaS.

**Consequences:** More plumbing written by hand than a framework would give for free, in exchange for every step being inspectable and reproducible under `make reproduce`.

---

## ADR-002: Generalize the source-of-truth entity beyond retraction

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** This benchmark is paper 1 of a PhD programme on epistemic currency in AI systems (the project protocol), not a one-off. Retraction is one instance of a source becoming superseded; expressions of concern, major corrections, guideline revisions, and evidence-based supersession are others.

**Decision:** `RetractedPaper` was renamed to `SupersededSource` and carries a required `supersession_type` enum (`retraction`, `expression_of_concern`, `major_correction`, `guideline_revision`, `superseded_by_evidence`). Paper 1 populates only `retraction`.

**Consequences:** No schema rewrite needed to add a legal or climate-science instance of this research programme later. Field names throughout were generalized accordingly (`venue` not `journal`, `summary` not `abstract`, `verification_method`/`verification_confirmed` not `pubmed_tag_confirmed`).

---

## ADR-003: No domain vocabulary hard-coded into `src/`

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Same PhD-programme extension requirement — nothing in `src/` may assume the medical domain.

**Decision:** `BenchmarkItem.domain` is a plain `str`, not a Python `Enum`, validated at runtime against `config/vocabularies/domains.yaml`. That file currently holds a single placeholder value; the real vocabulary is populated after the first ingestion pass, once the actual domain distribution in the corpus is visible.

**Consequences:** Domain validation logic (not yet built) must live in `curate/` or similar, reading the config file rather than importing a hard-coded enum. Until that validation exists, `domain` is unconstrained at the schema level.

---

## ADR-004: Temporal validity lives on claims, not on sources

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Time is the central variable of this research programme (the project protocol), not incidental metadata.

**Decision:** Introduced a shared `Claim` model (`text`, `valid_from`, `valid_until`), used for `BenchmarkItem.trap_claim` and `BenchmarkItem.gold_claim`. `SupersededSource` does **not** carry its own `valid_from`/`valid_until` — a source's temporal window is the window of the claim it made, not a property of the source record itself.

**Consequences:** Adds implementation cost now (nested model instead of flat strings) in exchange for not needing a migration when paper 2+ needs claim-level temporal reasoning beyond `BenchmarkItem`.

---

## ADR-005: Capture model confidence now, unused in paper 1

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Re-running experiments later solely to collect confidence signals would be expensive; collecting them alongside the answer is nearly free.

**Decision:** `ModelResponse` stores `logprobs` (`dict | None`, raw provider passthrough, unnormalized) and `verbalised_confidence` (`str | None`, free-text extract). Normalizing `logprobs` into a common structure across providers is explicitly deferred — litellm does not fully normalize this itself, and guessing at a shape now risks being wrong for a provider we haven't used yet.

**Consequences:** Anything reading `logprobs` later must handle provider-specific shapes itself until a normalization layer is written.

---

## ADR-006: Four experimental conditions, `no_retrieval` as the essential control

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The paper's central causal claim is that retrieval surfacing a superseded source is *distinct* from the model already believing the superseded claim from pretraining. Without a closed-book condition, RCR/SWA results can't distinguish these.

**Decision:** `ModelResponse.condition` is one of: `no_retrieval` (closed-book control), `retrieval_naive` (superseded source in corpus, no notice), `retrieval_with_notice` (notice also in corpus, not pointed at — tests unprompted surfacing), `post_notice` (notice shown explicitly, re-ask; M3 is scored here).

**Consequences:** Every item must be run under all four conditions for the causal comparison to be valid; this is a 4x multiplier on model calls that should factor into cost estimates before any batch run (the project protocol).

---

## ADR-007: Correctness is three-way, not boolean

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Models frequently decline to answer contested medical questions. Collapsing a refusal into "incorrect" misstates headline numbers — a refusal may be the safest behaviour when evidence is contested, and is a materially different failure mode from a confidently wrong answer.

**Decision:** `EvaluationResult.correctness` is an enum: `correct`, `incorrect`, `refused`.

**Consequences:** Any downstream accuracy calculation must explicitly decide how to treat `refused` (exclude vs. count as a separate rate) rather than it being silently folded into "incorrect" by a boolean.

---

## ADR-008: M2 formula deferred; schema commits to storing what it needs

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The staleness-weighting formula for M2 (Staleness-weighted Accuracy) needs real data to design well, and locking it into the schema now (e.g. as a stored `swa_score`) would force a data migration every time the formula is revised.

**Decision:** `EvaluationResult` stores only raw inputs (`correctness`, `cited_evidence` — the `Evidence` identifiers an answer actually relied on) and no derived score. The M2 formula itself will be computed in `eval/` once real data is available, and is **not yet defined**.

**Consequences — read before touching any of these fields:** the schema currently guarantees M2 is computable *later* because it stores `cited_evidence` identifiers, and each referenced `Evidence` carries a required `publication_date`, and each `BenchmarkItem.gold_claim`/`trap_claim` carries `valid_from`/`valid_until`. **If any future change drops one of those three, M2 becomes uncomputable without regenerating data.** Treat this triple as load-bearing.

---

## ADR-009: Multiple annotations per item; two levels of provenance

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Reviewers will expect inter-annotator agreement statistics on the hand-verified core (the project protocol). A single `annotator`/`annotation_date` pair on `BenchmarkItem` cannot represent multiple independent annotation passes.

**Decision:** `BenchmarkItem.annotations` is a `list[Annotation]`, each with its own `annotator_id`, `date`, `provenance`, and optional `note`. `BenchmarkItem` also keeps its own top-level `provenance` field, which is the overall item status gating inclusion in headline results (section 5.1) — distinct from each `Annotation.provenance`, which marks whether that specific pass was a draft or verifying pass.

**Consequences:** IAA computation can select verified annotation passes per item and compare them; overall item status filtering (draft vs. verified) doesn't require inspecting the annotation list.

---

## ADR-010: General-purpose external identifiers; `doi` optional

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Not every superseded source has a DOI (older papers, guideline revisions), and a PubMed-specific field like `pmid` doesn't generalize to non-biomedical sources (ADR-002).

**Decision:** `SupersededSource.doi` is `str | None`. A generic `external_ids: dict[str, str]` field (e.g. `{"pmid": "..."}`) carries any other identifiers without naming them in the schema.

**Consequences:** Ingest code is responsible for populating `external_ids` consistently (e.g. always using the key `"pmid"` for PubMed IDs) since the schema doesn't enforce specific keys.

---

## ADR-011: `EvaluationResult` denormalizes `condition`; enforced via validator

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** `m3_label` is only meaningful under `condition == post_notice`, and `notice_retrieved` only under `condition == retrieval_with_notice`. A Pydantic `model_validator` can only see fields on its own model.

**Decision:** `EvaluationResult` carries its own `condition` field, denormalized from the linked `ModelResponse`, specifically so a `model_validator(mode="after")` can enforce: `m3_label` is non-`None` iff `condition == post_notice`; `notice_retrieved` is non-`None` iff `condition == retrieval_with_notice`.

**Consequences:** Whatever writes `EvaluationResult` records must keep `condition` in sync with the linked `ModelResponse.condition` — there is no foreign-key enforcement, only the internal consistency check on the fields that depend on it.

---

## ADR-012: Strict schema validation (`extra="forbid"`)

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Pydantic v2 silently ignores unrecognized fields by default, which would let typos or schema drift through unnoticed — in tension with "every record validates before it is written" (the project protocol).

**Decision:** All schemas inherit from a shared `StrictBaseModel` with `model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid")`.

**Consequences:** Any code constructing these models with a stray or renamed field fails loudly at construction time instead of silently dropping data.

---

## ADR-013: Small, deliberately-chosen domain scope (3-4 domains)

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The benchmark needs to cover more than one clinical domain to say anything about cross-domain variation, but spreading items across too many domains (e.g. all ~20 Retraction Watch subject categories) leaves each domain too thin to support stratified statistical comparison.

**Decision:** The benchmark covers a small number of domains -- 3 to 4 -- rather than all of biomedicine or a single domain. Which domains is deliberately **not** decided here: it is Stage 1B's decision, made only after reading the Phase 1 exploration report (`results/phase1_exploration.md`), because volume, DOI/PMID resolvability, and reason composition all vary enormously by subject (see the report's sections 4, 7, and 11).

**Consequences:** Cross-domain variation in how often models rely on superseded evidence becomes a reportable finding in its own right, not just a sampling detail. Domain selection is deferred, tracked as Stage 1B, and is explicitly the author's call (the project protocol), not something inferred by the pipeline.

---

## ADR-014: RetractionNature is a first-class report dimension, not a filter

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The raw Retraction Watch table mixes several notice types (`Retraction`, `Correction`, `Expression of Concern`, `Reinstatement`) in one table. `SupersededSource.supersession_type` (ADR-002) already models `expression_of_concern`, `major_correction`, and `guideline_revision` as first-class values, not just `retraction` -- so filtering the exploration report down to retractions only would mean building a data pipeline that has never actually looked at the other notice types it already claims to support.

**Decision:** Every distribution in `results/phase1_exploration.md` is computed both per `RetractionNature` value and for retractions alone, with the retraction-only figures marked "(paper-1 scope)". Paper 1 uses retractions only -- that scope doesn't change -- but the exploration pass itself stays unfiltered so nothing about Corrections/EoCs/Reinstatements gets discovered-as-missing in year two.

**Consequences:** Report sections are larger (notice-type breakdowns throughout) in exchange for not having to re-run a full ingestion pass later to answer "how much Expression of Concern data do we even have."

**Candidate future research question (explicitly not paper 1):** Expressions of Concern are the harder case, because they signal *uncertainty* rather than *falsity*. Whether a model reflects appropriate uncertainty in that situation -- rather than confidently defending or confidently retracting a claim -- is arguably a more interesting probe of epistemic currency than whether it notices an outright retraction. Noting this so the data path doesn't foreclose it, per the project protocol.

---

## ADR-015: Retraction Watch acquisition method -- HTTPS GET of the Crossref-hosted CSV

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Needed to determine, by investigation rather than assumption, how the Retraction Watch database is actually distributed today. Findings: Crossref hosts it as a plain CSV in a public GitLab repo (`gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data`), updated on working days, no API key required. A separate Crossref REST API route also exists (`api.crossref.org/v1/works?filter=update-type:retraction`) but returns bare Crossref bibliographic metadata with no `Subject`, `Reason`, or `Institution` fields -- unusable for this project's exploration needs.

**Decision:** Download the raw CSV via a single anonymous HTTPS GET (not `git clone`, not the REST API), cached to `data/raw/` with the retrieval date in the filename, never re-downloaded same-day unless `--force`.

**Consequences:**
- **Licence:** no `LICENSE` file exists in the data repo itself. Crossref's public communications (blog, docs) describe the data as freely usable without a licence, with a citation request if used in published work. This ambiguity is documented as-is rather than asserted as a specific licence (e.g. "CC BY 4.0" appears only as what looks like Crossref's general site-footer boilerplate, not a declaration attached to this dataset).
- **Source quirks preserved, not fixed:** the CSV's header has a trailing comma (an unnamed 21st column, verified empty), and the file contains ~215 fully-blank rows (a CSV export artifact). Both are preserved in the raw DuckDB table and explicitly excluded (with counts, in `docs/DATA_NOTES.md`) only at the exploration/report layer, never silently dropped from the raw store.
- **RetractionNature casing is inconsistent in the source itself:** the documented value `"Expression of Concern"` and an actual value `"Expression of concern"` (lowercase "c") both occur in the real data, as two distinct strings. This was caught by treating notice type as a first-class dimension (ADR-014) rather than assumed away, and is left unmerged in the report -- silently normalizing it would hide a real source data quality issue. Stage 1B decides whether/how to normalize it.

---

## ADR-016: Retraction Watch's `Reason` field mixes three different kinds of statement

**Status:** Accepted -- and flagged as a candidate methods-section finding, not just an implementation detail.

**Context:** `Reason` is a flat, semicolon-delimited multi-value field. Building a claim-invalidation screen from it initially conflated three genuinely different kinds of information carried in the same list:

1. **What was wrong with the paper** (e.g. `Unreliable Results and/or Conclusions`, `Falsification/Fabrication of Data`) -- a statement about the paper.
2. **What process was followed** (e.g. `Investigation by Journal/Publisher`, `Misconduct - Official Investigation(s) and/or Finding(s)`) -- a statement about the *investigation*, routinely tagged alongside a substantive reason rather than instead of it.
3. **What happened to the notice itself** (e.g. `Date of Article and/or Notice Unknown`, `Upgrade/Update of Prior Notice(s)`, `Retract and Replace`) -- a statement about the *record*, not the paper.

The first pass at this screen (see ADR-017) put (2) and (3) in the same `ambiguous` bucket as genuinely unresolved paper-level tags (e.g. IRB/consent issues, `Original Data ... not Provided`). That was wrong, and it was measurable, not just aesthetically off: it collapsed the `(HSC) Medicine - Oncology` candidate pool from 562 (claim-invalidating, no process-only reason) down to 137 (additionally requiring zero of these process/notice tags) -- a record with a clean `Unreliable Results and/or Conclusions` tag was being disqualified from the cleanest tier purely for also carrying `Investigation by Journal/Publisher`, which is close to a universal companion tag whenever Retraction Watch knows an investigation occurred, and asserts nothing about whether that investigation found the paper's claim to be false.

**Decision:** Split a fourth class, `procedural_metadata`, out of the reason vocabulary: tags that describe the notice or the investigation process, never the paper. These are **ignored entirely** by tier classification -- present or absent, they neither qualify nor disqualify a candidate. Test applied to every tag: *does this tell us anything about whether the paper's own claim holds, or only about the process or the notice?* See `config/vocabularies/reason_screen.yaml` for the full 112-item classification (20 `claim_invalidating`, 35 `process_only`, 20 `procedural_metadata`, 37 `ambiguous`).

**Measured effect** (post-2015 retractions with a resolvable PubMed ID, per subject):

| Subject | (c) claim-invalidating, no process_only | Old strict tier (ambiguous = 57 tags, no split) | Revised tier 1 (ambiguous = 37 tags, procedural_metadata ignored) |
|---|---|---|---|
| (HSC) Medicine - Oncology | 562 | 137 | 216 |
| (BLS) Toxicology | 844 | 125 | 219 |
| (HSC) Medicine - Pharmacology | 590 | 73 | 179 |

Across all 39 `(HSC)` subjects, zero cleared a working threshold of 150 candidates under the unsplit screen; two cleared it after the split.

**Why this belongs in the paper's methods section:** most uses of this database we're aware of treat `Reason` as a flat list of alternative explanations. It isn't -- process/notice metadata and substantive findings co-occur routinely, and a screen (or a naive frequency count of "reasons for retraction") that doesn't separate them systematically undercounts the substantive category. This is a reusable finding about the database itself, independent of this benchmark's specific screen.

---

## ADR-017: Tiered candidate pool, not a single pass/fail screen

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Even after the `procedural_metadata` correction (ADR-016), a large share of otherwise-clean claim-invalidating candidates carry exactly one further ambiguous tag: `Duplication of/in Image` (192 of Oncology's 346 tier-1-excluded records; 413 of Toxicology's), `Duplication of Data`, or `Euphemisms for Duplication`. This is the single largest remaining exclusion factor, well ahead of the IRB/consent/welfare family originally suspected of being the bottleneck.

**Decision:** Partition the (claim_invalidating, no process_only) pool into three tiers rather than one cutoff:

- **`tier_1_clean`** -- no ambiguous tag at all.
- **`tier_2_duplication`** -- the *only* ambiguous tag(s) present are image/data duplication.
- **`tier_3_other_ambiguous`** -- everything else (IRB/consent, `Original Data not Provided`, `Error in Methods`, etc.).

Tier is recorded as a field on every candidate record, never discarded once assigned. Annotation proceeds tier 1 first; tier 2 is a deliberate second wave.

**Rationale for treating tier 2 as annotation-productive, not a discard pile:** a reused image (often a western blot or microscopy figure) reappearing to represent what's claimed as distinct evidence is the classic signature of fabricated figures, specifically concentrated in exactly the high-volume fields studied here. Whether a given reused image genuinely invalidates the paper's claim, versus being an innocent figure-reuse error, is precisely the kind of judgment call the annotation protocol (`docs/ANNOTATION.md`) exists to make -- it cannot be resolved from the reason tag alone, and guessing at it algorithmically would be exactly the "silent coercion" this project's data-handling standards forbid.

**Consequences:** Results should be reported both including and excluding tier 2, so a reviewer can see whether the finding depends on how image-duplication cases are resolved. If annotation shows tier 2 resolves cleanly (most reused images do turn out to invalidate the specific claim, or don't), that itself is a secondary finding about this exclusion category.

---

## ADR-018: Domain selection for paper 1

**Status:** Accepted (third domain pending author decision as of this ADR's initial write-up; comparison data below)

**Context:** ADR-013 committed to 3-4 domains, chosen after seeing the real distribution. Tier 1 candidate counts (post-2015, PubMed-ID-resolvable, per ADR-017) are the operative sizing metric, not raw retraction volume.

**Decision:** `Medicine - Oncology` (tier 1 = 216) and `Medicine - Pharmacology` (tier 1 = 179) are selected. `(BLS) Toxicology`, despite having the largest tier-1 pool of any candidate subject (219), is **excluded**: pool size is not the binding criterion here, question-generability is. A toxicology retraction rarely yields the kind of question a clinician would ask a medical RAG system -- the benchmark's premise is clinical evidence currency, not laboratory science generally. Pool size was a necessary check (ADR-016/017 exist because the pool looked too small), not a sufficient one for domain choice.

Third domain candidates and their tier-1 median time-to-supersession, presented for contrast in evidence-turnover speed rather than raw size:

| Subject | tier 1 | tier 2 | median days (tier 1) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Obstetrics/Gynecology | 136 | 46 | 1320 (3.6y) -- slowest of the five |
| Neurology | 110 | 55 | 722 (2.0y) |
| Surgery | 107 | 10 | 1043 (2.9y) |
| Cardiovascular | 95 | 22 | 996 (2.7y) |
| Gastroenterology | 80 | 63 | 636 (1.7y) -- fastest of the five, and faster than either confirmed domain |

For reference, Oncology's tier-1 median is 782 days (2.1y); Pharmacology's is 1174 days (3.2y).

**Consequences:** Superseded in part -- the rejection-rate pilot run against this domain pair showed the binding constraint on domain selection is claim type, not specialty. See ADR-020 (Oncology dropped) and ADR-021 (claim-type screen). Third domain remains open pending the claim-type-filtered funnel recomputation.

---

## ADR-019: Gold claims must trace to an institutional source, not annotator judgment

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The benchmark's credibility rests entirely on the gold claim being correct. If "what current evidence says" is one annotator's reading of the literature, any reviewer can attack that as unqualified clinical opinion -- reasonably, since the annotator is a BSc student, not a clinician.

**Decision:** A candidate is only eligible for annotation if an institutional post-retraction source can be located that speaks to the trap claim: a clinical practice guideline (NCCN, ESMO, ESC, WHO, NICE, or whichever body governs the domain), a Cochrane review, a systematic review, a meta-analysis, or a regulatory agency statement. The annotator transcribes and cites what that source says; they do not adjudicate. `Evidence.source_authority` (schemas.py) records which kind of source grounds each piece of evidence.

If no institutional source can be found, **the candidate is rejected, not weakened with a primary-study or self-reasoned gold claim.** Rejections are logged (`RejectedCandidate`: DOI, source registry ID, `RejectionReason`, notes, date) and never discarded.

**Consequences:**
- **Cost:** this raises the cost per item substantially -- locating an authoritative post-retraction source is slower than writing a gold claim from the literature directly. Accepted deliberately: a smaller benchmark with institutionally-grounded gold answers is worth more than a larger one resting on one student's opinion, and this is exactly the point every reviewer would otherwise attack.
- **Weaker evidence tier:** items where eligibility was satisfied only by a single primary study (`source_authority = primary_study`) or an unclassified source (`other`) are reported as a separate, weaker tier in the paper, not blended into the headline numbers.
- **The rejection rate is a candidate second-paper finding:** if a large fraction of tier-1 clinical retractions turn out to have no institutional correction anywhere in the literature, that is itself an alarming, reportable result about how the scientific record self-corrects (or doesn't) -- independent of anything the models under test do.

---

## ADR-020: Oncology dropped as a domain -- retraction volume is a poor proxy for clinically-consequential retraction volume

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** An n=10 rejection-rate pilot on Oncology's tier-1 pool (216 candidates) found only 2/10 titles described a claim specific enough that a guideline or systematic review would plausibly address it. The other 8 were in-vitro miRNA/lncRNA signaling-pathway mechanism studies -- a recognizable genre, and one that is both (a) a well-documented paper-mill-adjacent genre in cancer biology specifically, and (b) structurally outside what clinical guidelines or systematic reviews cover, since they report cell-line mechanism findings rather than claims about treating patients. Pharmacology's pool, sampled identically, scored 7/10 -- dominated by named-drug clinical RCTs instead.

At a 2/10 eligibility rate, Oncology's 216 tier-1 candidates are worth an estimated ~43 items surviving to annotation -- either a domain too thin to be worth a third of the benchmark, or pressure to relax the ADR-019 evidence standard to pad the count. Both are worse than dropping the domain.

**Decision:** Oncology is dropped. Pharmacology remains confirmed. Third domain selection is deferred to ADR-021, since the pilot showed subject category was the wrong axis to be selecting on in the first place.

**This is a methodological finding, not just a domain swap -- record it as such for the paper's methods section:** *retraction volume in a clinical specialty is a poor proxy for clinically-consequential retraction volume, because the highest-volume subjects can be inflated by a preclinical mechanism-paper genre that superficially resembles clinical research (it shares MeSH-adjacent subject tags, journal venues, and RCT-adjacent vocabulary) but makes no claim a guideline or systematic review would ever address.* Any domain-selection methodology for this kind of benchmark that stops at subject-level retraction counts, without checking claim type, risks the same failure -- this generalizes beyond RetractionBench specifically to any evidence-currency benchmark built from Retraction Watch.

**Consequences:** Domain selection moves from "which subject has the most tier-1 retractions" to "which subject has the most tier-1 retractions that are also clinical claims" -- see ADR-021.

**STATUS UPDATE, not yet resolved:** the n=10 and n=30 pilots that drove this decision were run against the claim-type screen before it was validated. ADR-021's closure found the screen systematically under-commits (56/100 titles defaulted to `unclear` for lack of a keyword match). Oncology's genre-plausibility rate (2/10, then measured again in the real-search check) was computed on the same screen family and predates the coverage fix. **This decision is provisional, not reversed, pending the funnel recompute in ADR-021's item 4.** See the project protocol -- Phase 2 is blocked until this is settled either way.

---

## ADR-021: Claim-type screen -- select by claim type, not subject category

**Status:** Accepted; redesigned as a recall-oriented pre-filter after validation found the original design under-committing; redesign measured at 100% recall / ~84% precision on held-out data. **Domain ranking shifted materially on recompute -- Oncology is not resolved, see closure.**

**Context:** ADR-020's pilot showed the binding variable for eligibility wasn't subject specialty, it was whether the retracted paper made a clinical claim about a named intervention in humans -- Pharmacology scored 7/10 not because pharmacology is special, but because its tier-1 pool happens to be full of named-drug RCTs.

**Decision:** Added a fourth heuristic screen, `config/vocabularies/claim_type_screen.yaml` (loader/classifier: `src/retraction_bench/curate/claim_type_screen.py`), classifying each candidate's title + Retraction Watch `ArticleType` into `clinical_intervention`, `clinical_observational`, `preclinical`, or `unclear`. Same naming and documentation discipline as `reason_screen.yaml`: explicit "not ground truth" header, keyword-driven priority cascade (non-research ArticleType -> strong preclinical terms -> intervention terms -> observational terms -> weak preclinical terms -> unclear), each rule tested.

**Validation requirement (not yet satisfied):** a keyword screen over free-text titles is far less constrained than `reason_screen.yaml`'s closed 112-item vocabulary, so it needs real measurement, not trust. A fixed-seed, shuffled, blind sample of 100 tier-1 candidates across all 39 `(HSC)` subjects was drawn (`data/human/claim_type_hand_labels.csv` -- moved from `data/interim/` after that directory turned out to be gitignored, see the data-provenance convention in the project protocol) for the author to hand-label from title + ArticleType alone, with the screen's own predictions deliberately withheld to avoid anchoring. **Per explicit instruction, these labels are produced by the author, never by an LLM** -- an LLM-labeled "gold standard" for validating an LLM-adjacent pipeline would be circular. Agreement rate (with a confidence interval) will be appended to this ADR once labeling is complete. Until then, every number derived from `claim_type` in the exploration report is marked PROVISIONAL.

**Measured effect on domain ranking** (tier_1_clean count, before -> after requiring `clinical_intervention` or `clinical_observational`):

| Subject | tier 1 (unfiltered) | tier 1 AND clinical claim type |
|---|---|---|
| Pharmacology | 179 | 77 |
| Obstetrics/Gynecology | 136 | 58 |
| Rehabilitation/Therapy | 100 | 42 |
| Oncology (dropped, ADR-020) | 216 | 36 |
| Surgery | 107 | 35 |

The reshuffle is exactly the point of ADR-020: Oncology falls from largest raw pool to 4th once claim type is accounted for; Obstetrics/Gynecology, not visible near the top of any earlier ranking, rises to 2nd.

**Median time-to-supersession caveat (recorded here, not only in chat, per explicit instruction):** the narrow tier-1-and-clinical-claim-type median is computed over a small subset shaped by which reason tags survived `reason_screen.yaml` *and* which title keywords survived `claim_type_screen.yaml` simultaneously. It is not a clean estimate of a subject's true evidence-turnover speed -- two independent heuristic filters compound their idiosyncrasies. **The broad median (all Retraction-nature records in the subject, no filter) is the more stable, cross-subject-comparable number and must be reported alongside the narrow one, never in its place, in the paper.**

**Consequences:** Third domain selection (ADR-018) remains open until the claim-type screen is validated -- see ADR-023 for the expanded eligibility-rate pilot run against the reshuffled candidates in the meantime.

**CLOSURE -- measured against the author's 100 hand labels:**

- **Overall agreement: 44/100 = 44.0%**, 95% Wilson CI [34.7%, 53.8%]. **Cohen's kappa: 0.324** (raw agreement overstates performance here since the label distribution is imbalanced -- kappa corrects for that, and 0.324 is "fair" agreement at best, well short of the >=0.6 "substantial" threshold usually wanted before trusting a screen unsupervised).
- Per-class precision/recall and the full confusion matrix: `results/claim_type_screen_validation.md`. Every individual disagreement (title, human label, screen label): `results/claim_type_screen_disagreements.csv`. Both regenerable via `retraction-bench curate score-claim-type-screen` (`src/retraction_bench/curate/validate_claim_type_screen.py`, `src/retraction_bench/curate/scoring.py`, both tested).
- **The screen's `unclear` and the human's `unclear` are not the same thing, and conflating them in one 4-class confusion matrix is misleading.** The screen emits `unclear` whenever no keyword rule fires -- an absence of a positive signal, structurally a default/abstention, not a judgment of genuine ambiguity. It predicted `unclear` on 56/100 rows; the human used it on 5/100. Of the 95 rows where the human gave a definite label, the screen abstained on 51 (53.7%).
- **Documented handling rule (adopted going forward):** report classifier performance against hand labels in two views, not one. (1) The full 4-class agreement/kappa above, as an honest baseline. (2) **Committed-accuracy**: restricting to rows where the screen actually output a non-`unclear` label, it was correct on 39/44 = **88.6%**. These tell different stories -- the screen's problem is *coverage* (it declines to answer on more than half of real titles), not *accuracy conditional on answering*. Any future reuse of this screen (or a similarly-shaped one) should report both numbers, not just aggregate agreement, and should never treat "screen said unclear" as equivalent to "screen judged this genuinely ambiguous" -- it means "no keyword matched," which is a statement about the keyword lists' coverage, not about the paper.
- **Practical consequence for candidate selection:** the screen is not reliable enough to trust unsupervised at its current coverage. It remains usable as a coarse *first-pass* narrowing tool exactly as designed (ADR-021's original framing as "heuristic, not ground truth" holds up), but every `clinical_intervention`/`clinical_observational` classification feeding a funnel count should be read as "the screen found a positive signal," not "the screen confirmed this is clinical" -- and the ~54% of real clinical titles it waves through as `unclear` are being silently under-counted in every funnel table in ADR-021/023/024, not excluded for a substantive reason. The funnel numbers in this ADR and ADR-023 are undercounts of the true clinical-claim pool, not overcounts -- expanding the keyword lists would likely *increase* candidate counts, not decrease them.
- Individual disagreements are **not adjudicated here**. The author reviews `results/claim_type_screen_disagreements.csv` directly; any hand label that gets revised on review is recorded as a dated revision with a reason in `data/human/claim_type_hand_labels.csv`'s history, never silently overwritten.

**BLOCKING, tracked in the project protocol:** every funnel count in this ADR and in ADR-023/ADR-024, and the domain decisions built on them (ADR-018, ADR-020), were computed against the under-committing screen described above -- undercounts, not final numbers. Four-item plan below; **all four are now done** (see the closure sections further down this ADR for the actual numbers) -- what remains blocking is not more engineering work but a decision: the funnel recompute (item 3) shifted the domain ranking materially, Oncology included, and that has not been decided one way or the other. Phase 2 does not start until it is.

1. Characterise the 56 no-match titles qualitatively before touching the keyword lists. Done.
2. Split the 100 hand labels into a development set and a held-out set, tune only against dev, report held-out as the headline. Done, with a stated limitation (see below).
3. Recompute every ADR-021/023/024 funnel count with the revised screen and report deltas; revisit domain selection if the ranking changes. Done -- it changed.
4. Update this ADR: the screen is explicitly a recall-oriented pre-filter, never a determinant of inclusion; report both recall and precision; state the design rationale. Done, in this closure.

(An earlier version of this plan included a fifth item -- splitting `unclear` into `no_match` and `ambiguous`. Superseded: the redesign below removes `unclear`-as-default entirely, so that distinction no longer applies.)

**Methodological note, for the paper's methods section (drafted now, while the detail is fresh):** this benchmark's claim-type screen looked reasonable under informal spot-checks, was validated against 100 independently hand-labeled titles before being trusted, and failed that validation -- not by being wrong when it answered, but by silently declining to answer on the majority of real, diverse titles (56%), a failure mode invisible from accuracy alone and only visible once "unclear" was checked against what a human actually meant by the same word. Most benchmark construction pipelines that use a heuristic or model-based filter do not validate that filter against independent human judgment at all; ours did, on the first attempt it failed, and the failure was caught before it propagated into an annotation pass or a paper claim, rather than after. That the pipeline caught its own filtering step failing is a stronger methodological claim than a clean validation number would have been, and is worth stating plainly rather than only reporting the post-fix figure.

**Item 1 -- failure-mode characterisation of the 56 no-match titles (done; keyword lists not yet touched):** read all 56 rather than inferring a pattern from the aggregate. Six real, mostly non-overlapping-in-cause patterns:

- **(A) Head-to-head comparison phrasing without an RCT-methodology trigger word** -- e.g. "Carbetocin versus syntometrine for prevention of postpartum hemorrhage." `clinical_intervention_terms` is built entirely from trial-*design* vocabulary (`randomized`, `double-blind`); it has nothing for the equally common "Drug A vs. Drug B for Outcome" title shape. Largest, most mechanically fixable pattern (>=9 titles).
- **(B) Predictive/prognostic/biomarker phrasing**, distinct from "associated with"/"risk factors for" -- e.g. "Homocysteine as a predictive factor for hip fracture," "...may serve as a potential biomarker for..." (>=4 titles).
- **(C) `ArticleType = Case Report` carries no signal** -- excluded from the definitely-not-research list but never given positive weight, despite being directly informative and directly relevant to ADR-022's regulatory-route rule (5 titles).
- **(D) Registry/database/large-population-study vocabulary** -- "administrative claims data," "UK Biobank," "survey of," "readmission among" -- an epidemiology register entirely absent from `clinical_observational_terms` (~4-5 titles).
- **(E) Drug-delivery / biomaterials / nanoparticle-formulation preclinical papers** -- materials-chemistry vocabulary (nanocapsule, liposome, silica, scaffold), not cell-line/animal/pathway language (8 titles).
- **(F) Mechanism papers naming specific genes/proteins with active verbs** ("requires," "activates," "inhibits" + a named molecule) rather than generic pathway language (~6 titles).
- **(G) Non-rodent animal models** -- canine, porcine, bovine -- `strong_preclinical_terms` only lists mouse/rat/murine/zebrafish (4 titles).
- **(H) Genuinely hard cases, not a keyword gap** -- the 5 titles the author independently marked `unclear`: review articles phrased as open questions, titles too administrative/broad to classify from title alone. No keyword expansion fixes these; this is where `ambiguous` (item 3, not yet built) should land regardless of list coverage.

**Checked and ruled out:** non-English constructions. Several titles originate from non-English-speaking-country studies, but title text itself is standard English scientific prose throughout -- not a real contributor in this sample.

**Items 2-4 -- reframed, not just fixed.** Rather than expand the keyword lists to chase patterns A-D (an unbounded lexicon, and every addition risks overfitting to these specific 100 labels), the screen's purpose was reframed: it is a cheap, high-recall pre-filter in front of mandatory human review, not a classifier. A human reads every candidate it passes through, so false positives (a preclinical paper waved through) are nearly free, and false negatives (a real clinical claim silently dropped, per the original design's 53.7% abstention rate) are what actually costs the benchmark items. Design change, `config/vocabularies/claim_type_screen.yaml` schema_version 0.2.0:

- The only exclusion gate below "not primary research" is now a single `preclinical_exclusion_terms` list -- if it matches, excluded as preclinical; if not, the record **passes through** as a clinical candidate by default. `unclear` is reserved for non-research `ArticleType` (Letter, Commentary, etc.) and empty titles only -- it is no longer a fallback for "no keyword matched." This directly fixes patterns A-D and H from the characterisation above: none of them require new *clinical* vocabulary, they just needed to stop defaulting to exclusion.
- The exclusion list itself was expanded, but only along patterns E and G (biomaterials/nanoparticle/drug-delivery vocabulary; non-rodent animal models) -- both bounded, stable vocabularies per the author's own reasoning that excluding preclinical work is a smaller, more stable problem than identifying clinical work. Pattern F (named-gene mechanism verbs: "activates," "inhibits" + a named molecule) was deliberately **not** enumerated -- those verbs appear in genuinely clinical titles too ("the drug inhibits platelet aggregation in patients with..."), so excluding on them would cost recall to buy precision this screen doesn't need. Preclinical papers of that shape that leak through are an accepted, cheap false positive.

**Dev/held-out split (item 2):** `src/retraction_bench/curate/dev_holdout_split.py`, stratified 50/50 by human label, fixed seed (tested). Applied to the same 100 labels the redesign's failure-mode characterisation had already been informed by in full -- **stated plainly, this first measurement is not blind in the strictest sense**, since the qualitative read that motivated the redesign necessarily saw every title, dev and held-out alike. The split protocol is established now so that any *future* keyword-list tuning has a dev set to look at that isn't the number being reported; today's held-out figure should be read as a real measurement with that caveat attached, not as a pristine one.

**Measured result (`retraction-bench curate score-claim-type-screen`, `results/claim_type_screen_validation.md`):**

| Split (n) | Recall (clinical) | Precision |
|---|---|---|
| Held-out (n=50) -- **headline** | **100.0%** [87.9%, 100.0%] | 84.8% [69.1%, 93.3%] |
| Dev (n=50) | 100.0% [88.3%, 100.0%] | 82.9% [67.3%, 91.9%] |
| All 100 (reference) | 100.0% [93.7%, 100.0%] | 83.8% [73.3%, 90.7%] |

Recall target (>=90%) exceeded on both splits; precision (~83-85%) came in well above the accepted floor (~60%). 4-class agreement, kept for continuity though it's no longer the design target: 75/100 = 75.0%, kappa 0.633 (up from 44.0%/0.324 pre-redesign).

**Item 3 -- funnel recompute.** Regenerated `results/phase1_exploration.md` section 13 with the redesigned screen. Selected deltas (`tier_1_clean AND clinical claim type`, old screen -> new screen):

| Subject | Old | New | Rank (old -> new) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pharmacology | 77 | 156 | 1st -> 1st |
| **Oncology** | **36** | **124** | **4th -> 2nd** |
| Obstetrics/Gynecology | 58 | 119 | 2nd -> 3rd |
| Surgery | 35 | 103 | 5th -> 4th |
| Rehabilitation/Therapy | 42 | 89 | 3rd -> 5th |

**This is a material shift, not noise.** Oncology's claim-type-filtered pool more than triples (36 -> 124) and it moves from last of the five candidates to second, effectively tied with Pharmacology and now larger than the domain (Obstetrics/Gynecology) that was leading third-domain contention. Per the explicit condition set for this recompute: a material shift means domain selection is revisited, Oncology included, not silently kept dropped. **Not resolved by this ADR** -- flagging for the author's decision, not deciding here. Two things worth noting for that decision, not resolving it: (a) ADR-020's original 2/10 genre-plausibility pilot sampled from the *raw* tier-1 pool directly (before any claim-type screen existed) and read titles by hand, so it is not invalidated by the screen bug -- but it was never re-run against the new, 124-candidate clinical-filtered pool specifically, only the unfiltered 216. (b) The n=30 eligibility pilots and real-search verification (ADR-023/024) were run for Pharmacology and Obstetrics/Gynecology only, both against their old (smaller, undercounted) claim-type-filtered pools -- the newly-included candidates in those pools (previously wrongly marked `unclear`) haven't been checked for eligibility rate either, and there's no particular reason to assume they match the already-sampled subset's rate.

**Consequences:** Every funnel count in ADR-021/023/024 that fed domain selection is superseded by the table above. the project protocol's Phase 2 block stands, updated to reflect what's now known rather than just that something was wrong.

---

## ADR-022: Regulatory agency statements are a primary eligibility route, not a fallback, for safety-signal retractions

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-019 already listed a regulatory agency statement as one acceptable `Evidence.source_authority`. The rejection-rate pilot surfaced a concrete case (a case report of gedatolisib-associated myocarditis) where no guideline or systematic review would plausibly exist -- a single adverse-event report is exactly what regulatory pharmacovigilance systems (FDA FAERS, EMA signals) are built to track, not what clinical guidelines synthesize.

**Decision:** `docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 3.2 now states explicitly that for adverse-event/safety-signal retractions, the regulatory route is the *expected* institutional source, and annotators must check it before rejecting a candidate solely for lacking guideline coverage. This does not lower the evidence bar -- a regulatory statement is still transcribed and cited via `Evidence.source_authority = regulatory_agency`, never the annotator's own safety judgment.

**Consequences:** Case-report-type candidates (ArticleType `Case Report` in the raw data, ~789 records among Retraction-nature rows) should not be reflexively excluded by the claim-type screen's `unclear` default for non-keyword-matching titles -- annotators should check the regulatory route for these specifically before rejecting.

---

## ADR-023: Expanded eligibility-rate pilot (n=30/domain) on the claim-type-filtered candidates

**Status:** Accepted (measurement, not a design decision -- recorded here because it directly determines benchmark size)

**Context:** The n=10 pilot (ADR-020) measured eligibility over the *unfiltered* tier-1 pool and found a 5x gap between Oncology (20%) and Pharmacology (70%) -- informative for diagnosing the domain-selection problem, but not sized precisely enough (95% CI on 2/10 is [5.7%, 51.0%]) to answer "how big will the final benchmark actually be." Once claim type is used as a pre-filter (ADR-021), the relevant question changes: of candidates that already look clinical, how many are actually eligible?

**Method:** Fixed-seed (`random_state=777`) sample of n=30 from `tier_1_clean AND claim_type ∈ {clinical_intervention, clinical_observational}`, for the two domains currently in contention: Pharmacology (confirmed) and Obstetrics/Gynecology (the new #2 after ADR-021's reshuffle). Same protocol as ADR-020: judged from title + `ArticleType` + reason tags only, genre-specificity judgment, no clinical-content claims. Full judgment log with one-line rationale per item: `results/eligibility_pilot_n30_judgments.csv`.

**Result:**

| Domain | Eligible-shaped | Rate | 95% Wilson CI |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pharmacology | 26/30 | 86.7% | [70.3%, 94.7%] |
| Obstetrics/Gynecology | 27/30 | 90.0% | [74.4%, 96.5%] |
| Combined | 53/60 | 88.3% | [77.8%, 94.2%] |

Both rates are a dramatic improvement over the unfiltered n=10 pilot (20-70%), and their confidence intervals overlap heavily -- claim type, not specialty, is confirmed as the dominant factor, exactly as ADR-021 predicted. The residual `unclear` cases cluster into three recognizable types, none of them specialty-specific: (a) narrow biomarker/surrogate-outcome associations (guidelines address patient-oriented outcomes, not most surrogate markers), (b) adverse-event case reports (regulatory route per ADR-022, not counted against eligibility here since that route wasn't exhausted), (c) meta-research/methodology papers (not a clinical claim about a condition at all).

**One caught screen error, worth flagging on its own:** record 20364 ("Identification of potential key genes associated with ovarian clear cell carcinoma") was classified `clinical_observational` by the heuristic screen -- the phrase "associated with" matched a clinical_observational keyword -- but is genre-wise a bioinformatics/preclinical paper that should have been `preclinical`. This is exactly the kind of false positive the 100-label validation (ADR-021) is designed to catch and quantify; this single instance is anecdotal, not a measurement of the screen's real false-positive rate.

**Consequences:** At an ~88% eligibility rate, Pharmacology's 77 claim-type-filtered tier-1 candidates and Obstetrics/Gynecology's 58 are each worth an estimated ~50 items surviving to annotation before considering rejections for other reasons (duplicate claims, unclear trap/gold distinction, etc.) -- a substantially healthier basis for domain selection than any number in ADR-018. Third domain selection (still open) can now be made with real eligibility-rate data for both leading candidates rather than raw pool size alone.

---

## ADR-024: Genre-plausibility measured against real institutional-source search (not just proxied)

**Status:** Accepted (measurement)

**Context:** ADR-023's 86.7%/90.0% figures measure *genre-plausibility* -- "would a guideline plausibly address this claim?" -- not the actual ADR-019 eligibility criterion, which requires an institutional source to actually exist. The two are not the same: a genuinely clinical, guideline-shaped RCT can still be one the field never followed up on. The gap between the proxy and the real criterion was unmeasured, and the author correctly identified this as the number that actually determines benchmark size.

**Method:** For 10 candidates per domain (drawn from the already genre-judged n=30 pools for Pharmacology and Obstetrics/Gynecology; a fresh n=10 for Rehabilitation/Therapy, see ADR-025), an actual web search was run per candidate for a post-retraction systematic review, Cochrane entry, guideline, or regulatory statement addressing the claim. One search per candidate, `found` / `uncertain` / `not_found`, with the specific source(s) located recorded. Full log: `results/real_search_verification_n30.csv`. **Methodological limitation, stated plainly:** this is one general web search per candidate, not an exhaustive search of PubMed, the Cochrane Library, or guideline repositories directly -- it can both undercount (specialized institutional statements poorly indexed by general search) and overcount (surfacing tangentially related literature that doesn't precisely address the narrow claim). Treat these numbers as a real but imprecise check on the genre-plausibility proxy, not a substitute for the actual annotation-time search.

**Result:**

| Domain | found | uncertain | not_found |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pharmacology | 9/10 | 1/10 | 0/10 |
| Obstetrics/Gynecology | 8/10 | 1/10 | 1/10 |
| Rehabilitation/Therapy | 7/10 | 3/10 | 0/10 |
| **Combined** | **24/30 (80%)** | **5/30 (17%)** | **1/30 (3%)** |

The gap between genre-plausibility (~88%) and real-search-confirmed (80% clear, with a further 17% genuinely uncertain rather than clearly negative) is real but modest -- not the collapse to ~40% the author was concerned might be hiding. Genre-plausibility is confirmed as a reasonable, if imperfect, proxy for the true criterion.

**The `uncertain` cases share a recognizable pattern, worth naming:** every uncertain case involved either (a) a narrow composite population or intervention combination that the retracted paper itself was the first (and, post-retraction, only) study of -- e.g. nifedipine *plus sildenafil* specifically, hip fracture prevention in a *neurological-disease* subpopulation specifically, IIH-specific headache response to anti-CGRP antibodies -- where broader base-drug or base-condition literature exists but nothing synthesizes the specific narrower claim; or (b) a genuinely underdeveloped comparison (sequential vs. day-3 vs. day-5 embryo transfer) where the retraction left an acknowledged evidence gap. This suggests a cheap secondary screening heuristic for later curation passes: candidates whose claim is a *novel combination or subpopulation* of an otherwise well-studied intervention are more likely to be real institutional-source misses than candidates making a claim about a single well-established intervention-condition pair.

**Consequences:** No change to domain selection from this ADR alone -- it validates rather than overturns ADR-023's numbers. Recorded because the author explicitly wanted the proxy's error measured before planning around it, and it now is.

---

## ADR-025: Rehabilitation/Therapy's subject tag is not a clean "non-pharmacological" proxy

**Status:** Accepted (finding)

**Context:** The author's rationale for considering Rehabilitation/Therapy as the third domain was that it would provide contrast with Pharmacology and Obstetrics/Gynecology -- non-pharmacological interventions, a weaker evidence base, less prescriptive guidelines. This was tested rather than assumed.

**Method:** Manually categorized every tier-1-clinical-claim-type candidate in both Obstetrics/Gynecology (58) and Rehabilitation/Therapy (42) into intervention type (drug / device-procedure / behavioural / other), and computed record-level overlap with Pharmacology's candidate pool.

**Result:**

| Domain | drug | device/procedure | behavioural | other |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Obstetrics/Gynecology (n=57) | ~49% | ~25% | ~5% | ~21% |
| Rehabilitation/Therapy (n=41) | ~51% | ~17% | ~17% | ~15% |

Rehabilitation/Therapy is **not** meaningfully less drug-dominated than Obstetrics/Gynecology -- if anything, marginally more so by this count. The RW `(HSC) Medicine - Rehabilitation/Therapy` tag is applied broadly to anything with "therapy" in scope, catching a substantial amount of oncology chemotherapy, hepatitis antivirals, and monoclonal-antibody literature alongside genuine multidisciplinary/exercise-based rehabilitation studies. The behavioural share is real and meaningfully larger than Obstetrics/Gynecology's (~17% vs. ~5%), but it is a *minority within Rehabilitation/Therapy itself* (roughly 7 of 42 tier-1-clinical candidates), not the domain's defining character.

**Overlap with Pharmacology:** 19/42 (45%) of Rehabilitation/Therapy's tier-1-clinical pool is also tagged Pharmacology, essentially identical to Obstetrics/Gynecology's 23/58 (40%). Neither candidate domain is meaningfully less redundant with Pharmacology than the other in raw overlap terms.

**Consequences:** The contrast the author is looking for exists in Rehabilitation/Therapy, but only within a ~7-candidate behavioural subset, not across the domain's raw 42 or even its 42 claim-type-filtered candidates. Choosing Rehabilitation/Therapy as the third domain on the stated rationale would require either accepting a much smaller, behavioural-only sub-pool, or accepting that "the domain" is drug-majority in practice despite the intent -- this tradeoff is the author's to weigh, not resolved by this ADR.

---

## ADR-026: Oncology stays out -- deliberate deferral, not a settled conclusion

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-021's funnel recompute showed Oncology's claim-type-filtered candidate pool is actually 124 (2nd of five domains), not the 36 that drove ADR-020's decision to drop it. That number alone does not resolve whether Oncology should be a domain -- it was never re-checked for eligibility rate (ADR-023/024 only covered Pharmacology and Obstetrics/Gynecology), and doing so would cost another n=30 pilot plus real-search verification, on the order of a week, against a hard November deadline.

**Decision:** Oncology stays excluded. This is an explicit resource-cost deferral, not a claim that the 124-candidate pool is unusable or that ADR-020's original concern (mechanism-paper-heavy pool) has been confirmed at the new pool size. The original n=10 genre-plausibility read (2/10, sampled from the raw unfiltered pool by hand, unaffected by the screen bug) remains the best evidence available and is suggestive, not dispositive, at this pool size.

**Consequences:** If this becomes a longer-running programme beyond paper 1 (plausible given the PhD-scale framing in the project protocol), Oncology is the first domain to revisit, with a cheap, well-defined next step already known: run the same n=30 genre pilot + real-search verification protocol (ADR-023/024) against the 124-candidate claim-type-filtered pool specifically. Not scheduled; recorded so it isn't reinvented.

---

## ADR-027: Benchmark size set to 90 items (30 per domain), fixed by annotation cost under a fixed deadline

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The measured per-item annotation cost estimate (proposed, not yet validated against a real item) is 1.5-2.5 hours. At the original target of ~150 items, that is 225-375 hours against roughly 13 weeks to the November deadline -- infeasible even at an optimistic 20 hours/week with zero schedule slack.

**Decision:** Target size is **90 items: 30 each across Pharmacology, Obstetrics/Gynecology, and Rehabilitation/Therapy** -- all three domains that survived ADR-018/020/021's selection process, rather than choosing between Obstetrics/Gynecology and Rehabilitation/Therapy for a single third slot. At 1.5-2.5 hours/item this is 135-225 hours, fitting the remaining timeline with margin.

**For the paper's methods section, state plainly, not defensively:** sample size was set by annotation cost under a fixed timeline. This is an honest methodological constraint, not a weakness to omit or apologise for. Comparable hand-verified evaluation benchmarks have shipped at this scale when per-item verification cost is high and the contribution rests on annotation depth rather than volume -- that is this benchmark's actual claim (the project protocol's non-negotiable standards: every gold answer traceable to a human-verified institutional source, minimum 50 fully hand-verified, bootstrap CIs on every reported number). Report **per-domain confidence intervals**, not just aggregate ones, so the precision limits imposed by n=30/domain are visible rather than implied.

**Consequences:** Three domains, not a domain-selection process that ends in one. `config/vocabularies/domains.yaml` populated with the three confirmed values (see ADR-003, which anticipated this exact moment: domain vocabulary populated once the real distribution was visible). Phase 2 target is 90 verified `BenchmarkItem` records, 30 per domain, each individually traceable to an institutional post-retraction source per ADR-019.

---

## ADR-028: First real PubMed tag verification run -- 92.3% clean match, 7.4% documented PubMed-tagging gap, not a data quality problem with our pool

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** the project protocol's verification rule had never actually been run against real data at scale before this ADR -- 500+ candidates existed with Retraction Watch membership only, none checked against PubMed's own `Retracted Publication` tag. `src/retraction_bench/ingest/pubmed.py` was built this session (batched ESummary queries, disk-cached, rate-limited per NCBI policy, response shape confirmed live before coding) and run against the full 364-candidate pool for the three final domains (Pharmacology 156, Obstetrics/Gynecology 119, Rehabilitation/Therapy 89).

**Result:** 336/364 (92.3%) confirmed with a clean Retraction Watch + PubMed tag match. 27/364 (7.4%) are genuine ESummary lookups that succeeded but whose `pubtype` list omits `Retracted Publication`, despite being a `Retraction`-nature Retraction Watch entry -- verified live against several examples, not assumed; one (PMID 37940041) has a PubMed title literally prefixed `"WITHDRAWN:"` yet still lacks the tag, showing this is a PubMed indexing gap, not a Retraction Watch error or a bug in our pipeline. 1/364 (0.3%) is a genuine unresolvable-PMID lookup error (PMID 38355237). Full detail and the initial mis-analysis (a pandas NaN-handling bug in a throwaway script, not in `pubmed.py`) is in `docs/DATA_NOTES.md`'s 2026-08-06 entry.

**Decision:** Per section 4's rule ("or the discrepancy is documented in `docs/DATA_NOTES.md`"), the 27 tag-absent candidates are **not excluded**. They remain eligible candidates, individually flagged (`pubmed_retracted_tag_confirmed=False` in `data/interim/tier1_candidates_final3_verified.csv`) so annotators see the flag during hand review rather than it being silently resolved either way. The 1 genuine lookup error is excluded pending a manual PubMed check if it is ever selected as a candidate.

**Consequences:** the project protocol's non-negotiable rule is now satisfied for the entire final-domain candidate pool -- every candidate has either a clean tag match or a documented, individually-traceable discrepancy. Phase 2 annotation can draw from the full 364 (minus the 1 lookup error) without re-running this check per item, though the 30-minute eligibility search (ANNOTATION.md section 3.2) is unaffected -- it is a different check (institutional post-retraction source), not this one.

---

## ADR-029: Retraction status is not reliably machine-readable at the source -- paper finding, not a data-quality caveat

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-028's PubMed verification run found 27/364 candidates where Retraction Watch and PubMed's own `Retracted Publication` tag disagree despite a clean PubMed lookup. The author judged this was being under-treated as a footnote: one example (PMID 37940041) has PubMed's own title prefixed `"WITHDRAWN:"` while the structured `pubtype` field still lacks the tag -- a case where the authoritative source's own free text and its own structured field disagree with each other, not just with Retraction Watch.

**Investigation (full detail in `results/pubmed_rw_completeness_check.md`):**
- **Forward direction:** 27/364 = 7.42%, Wilson 95% CI [5.15%, 10.58%]. Not simple indexing lag -- excluding a 6-record Cochrane-review multi-PMID cluster (one editorial event, not six independent cases), the gap rate is 10-17% for 2016-2020-era retractions and 0-2% for 2021-2023, i.e. *higher* in decade-old records than recent ones, which a clearing backlog does not predict. No single publisher or RW reason code dominates once the Cochrane cluster is excluded.
- **Inverse direction:** bounded to the 19 journals implicated by the 27-record gap (not a claim about all of PubMed or all of RW), PubMed ESearch for `"Retracted Publication"[pt]` in those journals, 2016-2026, cross-checked against the *full* 71,540-row RW table. 443 PubMed-tagged PMIDs found; 50 (11.3%) have no matching RW entry. 46 of the 50 are concentrated in one high-volume multidisciplinary journal (Heliyon), spanning 2019-2024 across unrelated fields -- a general RW ingestion gap on that journal, not one incident, and essentially non-overlapping with our actual candidate pool. Of the remaining 4, one (Modern Rheumatology, PMID 30285533) shares its exact title with an already-counted forward-direction gap record (PMID 28395637) -- almost certainly the same article under two PMIDs (epub-ahead-of-print vs. final issue), a distinct failure mode (identifier fragmentation) from a missing tag. The other 3 are genuinely separate retracted articles absent from RW entirely.
- Script: `src/retraction_bench/ingest/pubmed_rw_completeness.py` (not CLI-wired, not tested -- a one-off investigation feeding this ADR, documented as such in its own docstring).

**Decision:** This is reported in the paper as a standalone methods-section finding -- "Retraction status is not reliably machine-readable at the source" -- not folded into the PubMed-verification data-quality note. The 27 flagged candidates stay in the pool as already decided (ADR-028); this ADR is about how the *finding itself* is framed and used, not about candidate inclusion.

**Consequences:** Reframes part of RetractionBench's contribution: the propagation failure the benchmark measures in AI systems (M1/M2/M3, the project protocol) is shown to already exist one layer upstream, in the structured sources such a system would need to check. Strengthens the paper's framing from "models handle retracted evidence badly" to "even a system doing the structurally correct thing -- checking PubMed's own tag -- inherits a real, measured gap." Any future discussion of RCR/SWA/CF results should note this upstream ceiling explicitly rather than implying perfect-index models are achievable in principle.

---

## ADR-030: claim_type is a human-annotated BenchmarkItem field, never inherited from the candidate-pool screen

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Picking the first item-1 candidate (RW record 1471, "Ramosetron compared with granisetron...") surfaced that `curate/claim_type_screen.py` had labeled a head-to-head drug comparison trial as `clinical_observational` -- wrong; it is `clinical_intervention`. Checked against the 100-item hand-label set (`data/human/claim_type_hand_labels.csv`) restricted to items the screen actually passed as clinical (68 of 100) and where the human label is also intervention or observational (57 of those 68): **13/57 = 22.8%, Wilson 95% CI [13.8%, 35.2%]** subtype disagreement, and it is entirely one-directional -- the screen never mislabels observational as intervention, only intervention as observational (head-to-head comparative-drug-trial titles read as descriptive/observational by the keyword heuristic). This does not affect eligibility (ADR-021: both classes pass the recall-oriented filter), but it does mean the screen's label cannot be trusted as ground truth for any individual item.

**Decision:** `schemas.BenchmarkItem` gains a `claim_type` field (new `ClaimType` enum, `clinical_intervention` / `clinical_observational`), set during annotation from the actual paper, never copied from `curate/candidates.py`'s screen output. That column is renamed `claim_type_screen_label` in the candidate export to make the distinction unmistakable in the CSV itself, not just in documentation. The screen is not retuned -- per the project protocol, a decision this consequential (retuning the shared recall-oriented pre-filter) is not made as a side effect of one annotation-time observation, and eligibility is unaffected regardless.

**Consequences:** `src/retraction_bench/curate/candidates.py` and `tests/test_candidates.py` updated for the rename; `docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 3.1.1 documents the disagreement rate and the never-inherited rule. Every future annotation pass records claim_type by hand, and `results/claim_type_screen_validation.md`'s intervention/observational split should be read as a known weak axis, not as usable ground truth for individual items.

---

## ADR-031: Author-cluster cap of 2 items per author, checked across all individual authors

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The first item-1 candidate picked (RW record 1471) turned out to be Yoshitaka Fujii -- roughly 170 papers retracted 2012-2016 after an investigation found most of his trials had no patients, one of the most famous fabrication scandals in the medical literature. Selecting a benchmark item from a famous scandal risks measuring whether a model recognises that scandal specifically, not whether it tracks evidence currency in general -- a sampling-independence problem, not a labeling problem.

**Investigation** (`curate/author_clusters.py`, tested; full detail in `results/author_cluster_analysis.md`): parsed Retraction Watch's semicolon-separated `Author` field for every one of the 364 candidates across the three final domains. **1,631 distinct individual names; 31 appear on 3+ distinct records; those 31 collectively touch 87/364 (23.9%) of the candidate pool.** Two are confirmed serial fabricators by RW's own reason tags (Joachim Boldt, 13 records, `Falsification/Fabrication...`; Yoshitaka Fujii, 5 records, same). The rest range 3-11 records each; the largest non-Boldt/Fujii cluster (Ahmed Badawy/Ahmed M Maged/Ahmed Mohamed Abbas, overlapping co-authors, Obstetrics/Gynecology) carries `Concerns/Issues about Data` and `Investigation by...` tags rather than an explicit fabrication tag -- a different, unresolved-investigation profile, not assumed to be equivalent to Boldt/Fujii, but the same sampling-independence risk regardless of cause. Obstetrics/Gynecology is worst affected (40.3% of its pool touched by a cluster), Pharmacology 28.2%, Rehabilitation/Therapy 21.3%.

**Decision:** No more than **2 items** in the final 90-item benchmark may share a common individual author, checked against every name in the author list (not just first/last author). Enforced during selection via `curate/author_clusters.authors_over_cap`. Cap value of 2, not 3: conservative given how concentrated the pool already is (31 names average ~2.8 records each), and every domain retains far more than 30 cluster-free candidates to draw from (Obstetrics/Gynecology, the worst case, still has 71/119 untouched), so the cap costs no real flexibility.

**Consequences:** `docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 3.1.2 records the cap as a formal inclusion criterion. The 364-candidate pool itself is not pruned -- the cap applies at final-90 selection time, item by item, alongside eligibility (section 3.2). The cluster analysis is also a candidate paper finding in its own right (`results/author_cluster_analysis.md`): a quarter of a supposedly domain-diverse retracted-clinical-trial pool traces to 31 individuals, which says something about the shape of the retracted literature independent of anything a RAG system does.

---

## ADR-032: evidence_level (primary_study vs synthesis) is a tracked stratification variable; pre-registered prediction recorded before any data exists

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Item 1's fresh candidate (RW record 17019, after ADR-030/031's fixes) is a retracted meta-analysis, not a retracted primary trial -- "The efficiency and safety of tranexamic acid for reducing blood loss in open myomectomy: A meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials." The author's reasoning for choosing it deliberately: RAG retrieval and reranking both favour high-evidence-level synthesis documents, so a retracted meta-analysis is plausibly *more* dangerous in practice than a retracted primary trial -- it carries more apparent authority and is more likely to be surfaced. It is also a harder case for a model to get right: the underlying RCTs a retracted meta-analysis pooled are not themselves retracted, so the correct response is not "this evidence is void" but the more nuanced "the primary evidence may still exist, this particular pooled estimate is invalid." A retracted primary trial has no such nuance -- the trap claim really is void.

**Decision:** `SupersededSource` gains an `evidence_level` field (`SourceEvidenceLevel`: `primary_study` | `synthesis`), set at annotation time. This becomes a stratification variable for M1/M2/M3, reported split by evidence level, not only in aggregate -- symmetric with ADR-027's per-domain-CI requirement. Item selection for the 90-item benchmark should aim for a mix of both across items, not accidentally end up all-primary or all-synthesis.

**Pre-registered prediction, recorded now, before any model has been run against a single item:** models will perform *worse* on items where the superseded source is a synthesis than where it is a primary study -- because a synthesis's retraction leaves the underlying primary literature intact, and correctly handling that requires the model to distinguish "this specific pooled claim is void" from "this topic has no evidence," a distinction a primary-trial retraction does not require. If this holds under the actual M1/M2/M3 results, it is a headline finding. If it fails, the pre-registered wrong prediction is reported anyway -- the project protocol's ethics section holds the AI system to account, not the author's intuition, and a stated-in-advance miss is itself informative about the failure mode's actual shape.

**Consequences:** `schemas.SupersededSource.evidence_level` is required going forward; `tests/test_schemas.py`'s fixture updated. `src/retraction_bench/eval/` and `src/retraction_bench/stats/` (not yet built) must report M1/M2/M3 split by `evidence_level` alongside the aggregate, per this ADR, when that code is written. Nothing about this prediction is checked or checkable yet -- it is recorded here specifically so it cannot be silently revised after results are seen.

---

## ADR-033: notice_specificity is a tracked, per-item field -- Retraction Watch's tags can be more specific than the notice they derive from

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Item 1's retraction notice (PMID 29369226), quoted verbatim in full: "The article... is being retracted for errors in the data collection. The errors affected the results and conclusion of the paper." That is the entire body text. Retraction Watch's own reason tags for the same record are `Error in Analyses; Error in Data; Error in Results and/or Conclusions` -- three distinct, more specific-sounding categories than the one vague sentence they were coded from. Someone at Retraction Watch interpreted "errors in the data collection" into three separate tags. The reason screen (`curate/reason_screen.py`, ADR-016/017) is built entirely on top of RW's tags, which this shows are not a transparent readout of the notice -- they are already one layer of interpretation removed from it.

**Decision:** `SupersededSource` gains a `notice_specificity` field (`NoticeSpecificity`: `specific` | `categorical` | `bare`), annotated per item directly from the notice text, independent of `supersession_reasons` (RW's tags). Item 1 is annotated `categorical` -- it names an error category ("data collection errors ... affected results and conclusion") but no mechanism (no named study, no named data point, no named analytical step).

**Measurement plan, recorded now (n=1):** once 20 items are annotated, report (a) the distribution of `specific` / `categorical` / `bare` across items, and (b) how often RW's `supersession_reasons` tag count/specificity exceeds what the notice itself supports -- item 1 is the first data point for (b): 3 RW tags derived from 1 vague sentence. If `categorical` and `bare` dominate, that is a substantial, reportable finding in its own right: the retraction record is often uninformative about what was actually wrong, meaning neither a human annotator nor a retrieving model could determine from the notice alone whether the underlying claim survives.

**Consequences:** This is the same phenomenon as ADR-029/ADR-028 (retraction status is unreliably machine-readable at the source) one layer further in: retraction *status* can fail to propagate cleanly (ADR-028/029), and retraction *content*, even when status is unambiguous, is frequently uninformative about mechanism. Both belong in the same paper methods subsection ("Retraction status is not reliably machine-readable at the source," `results/pubmed_rw_completeness_check.md`) once the n=20 measurement exists -- that doc is not rewritten yet since it would currently rest on n=1. `ANNOTATION.md` section 4 records `notice_specificity` as an annotated field per item, alongside `claim_type` (ADR-030) and `evidence_level` (ADR-032).

---

## ADR-034: The gold claim documents the retraction and current evidence state; it does not adjudicate an error the notice doesn't state

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Item 1's notice is `categorical` (ADR-033) -- it does not say why the pooled SMD is wrong, only that data-collection errors affected the results. The pooled effect size (SMD -1.512 for total blood loss) is large enough to invite a specific technical explanation (unit-conversion error, a mis-signed study, inappropriate pooling of heterogeneous outcomes), but the notice confirms none of these, and the project protocol's standing rule against inferring status/content not stated by a source applies here with equal force: writing a gold claim that names a specific mechanism the notice never states would be fabricating provenance, exactly what the project's no-inference rule (the project protocol, ADR-006-adjacent standard-of-evidence norm, section 5.1) exists to prevent -- it would just be fabricating error-mechanism provenance instead of retraction-status provenance.

**Decision:** The gold claim is scoped to what is documented, not what is inferred:

> "This meta-analysis was retracted in January 2018 for data collection errors affecting its results and conclusions. Its pooled estimates should not be cited. Tranexamic acid in open myomectomy has been assessed in subsequent evidence -- [whatever step 5 finds] -- which supersedes it."

This changes what the item actually tests. It is not "is TXA effective in myomectomy?" -- it is "does the model recognise this specific synthesis is void, while the underlying clinical question may remain open?" A model that gives a confident, TXA-cites-this-paper answer fails the item regardless of whether TXA actually works, which is a cleaner and more targeted test than the author expected going in.

**Consequence for step 5:** step 5's job is to establish what current evidence says about TXA in myomectomy specifically (a corrected synthesis, or a guideline addressing the specific indication) -- not to explain the retracted paper's error. Explaining the error and describing current evidence are kept as separate questions; conflating them would smuggle an unstated mechanism back into the gold claim through the evidence side instead of the retraction side.

**Consequences:** `docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 4 records this scoping rule for every future item with a `categorical` or `bare` notice: the gold claim states what the notice documents and what current evidence says, never a reconstructed mechanism. Items with a `specific` notice are not bound by this restriction in the same way -- if a notice names the mechanism, the gold claim may say so, because it is then documented, not inferred.

---

## ADR-035: post_retraction_citations tracked per source; item 1's retracted meta-analysis is still being cited in 2025

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Checking whether the two 2022 superseding meta-analyses (Kathopoulis et al., Baradwan et al.) cite or acknowledge item 1's retracted source (Wang et al. 2017) required pulling its full citation graph. Neither does -- confirmed directly against Kathopoulis's reference list, and confirmed for both by their absence from OpenAlex's list of works citing the retracted paper (23 total citing works, neither 2022 paper among them). That was the question asked. Pulling the graph to answer it surfaced a bigger one: **the retracted paper is still being cited by other work, well past its retraction, including by at least one other systematic review.**

**Measurement (OpenAlex, `https://api.openalex.org/works/doi:10.1097/MD.0000000000007072`, queried 2026-08-06; OpenAlex itself marks the record `is_retracted: true`, i.e. it independently reflects the correct status):** 23 total citing works.
