One is the retraction notice's own back-reference to the source it retracts (excluded -- not an independent citation). Of the remaining 22: **3 pre-date the retraction** (2017-09-30, 2017-11-15, 2018-01-16 -- the last just 5 days before the 2018-01-21 retraction, evidently already in production). **19 post-date it**, running continuously through 2025-10-09 -- almost eight years after retraction. Citing works include a JOGC systematic review (Oct 2018, ~9 months post-retraction, same narrow indication) and a Fertility and Sterility network meta-analysis (Nov 2019) on overlapping interventions. Both are paywalled; I could not confirm from their text whether either flags the retraction or cites the finding as valid evidence -- recorded as an open question, not resolved by inference.

**Decision:** `SupersededSource` gains an optional `post_retraction_citations` field (`PostRetractionCitations`: source, as_of_date, before_count, after_count, excluded_self_citation) -- optional because not every source is well-indexed by a citation database, and because it's a point-in-time snapshot, not a fixed fact. Collected per item where available, not required for every item.

**Consequences:** This is a stronger motivating statistic for the paper's introduction than anything measured so far: a retracted synthesis with a vague notice, still accumulating citations 7+ years later, at least one of them from another systematic review on the same question. Worth a follow-up check once more items exist: whether the JOGC/Fertility-and-Sterility citations acknowledge the retraction, which would require either paywalled full-text access or contacting the authors -- not pursued further under this item's time budget.

---

## ADR-036: expected shape of per-item annotation cost -- reading-dominated, not tooling-dominated

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Item 1 (steps 2-5, before the author's own claim-drafting time) required reading four separate papers in full: the retracted meta-analysis itself, its retraction notice, and two independent post-retraction meta-analyses located during eligibility search -- plus a citation-graph pull that surfaced two more papers (JOGC 2018, Fertility and Sterility 2019) whose relevance had to be assessed even though they weren't ultimately used as cited evidence. Tool-call time for all of this (search, fetch, extraction) was under 15 minutes total. That number is not the annotation cost -- it measures retrieval, not the reading and judgment a human annotator actually has to do, which is the part no amount of tooling shortens.

**Decision:** Record the expected shape of per-item cost now, before the author's own timed draft of item 1's claims exists to confirm or revise it: **if 3-4 full papers per item is typical, not a one-off, then per-item annotation cost is dominated by reading time, and automation of search/retrieval (which this session did extensively) addresses a small fraction of the real cost.** This matters directly for ADR-027's cost model, which was built on a proposed 1.5-2.5 hour/item estimate before any item had been done end-to-end -- that estimate did not have this data point available and should be revisited once the author's own step-6 timing exists.

**Consequences:** Nothing about the 90-item target changes yet -- ADR-027 said explicitly that the measured cost from doing an item was needed before committing further, and that measurement is still in progress (the author is timing the claim-drafting step separately, deliberately not delegated). This ADR exists so that if the real number lands high, "reading four papers per item" is already the recorded explanation, not a post-hoc excuse.

---

## ADR-037: Benchmark size set by a fixed annotation window (through 2026-09-30), not a fixed item count -- supersedes ADR-027's 90-item target

**Status:** Accepted (supersedes ADR-027's size decision; ADR-027's per-domain-CI and honest-framing guidance still stands and is not revised here)

**Context:** ADR-027 set a target of 90 items (30/domain) from a proposed 1.5-2.5 hour/item estimate, explicitly pending measurement from doing a real item. Item 1 is now done end to end. Measured cost: ~15 minutes of tool/retrieval time (steps 2-5) plus under 30 minutes of human claim-drafting time (steps 6-9) -- under 45 minutes total. But item 1 was a favourable case: free full text, a one-sentence notice, two clean superseding meta-analyses found on the first search. The n=30 eligibility pilot (ADR-023/024) already measured that 10-15% of candidates yield no institutional source at all, and that search time is spent before it's known to be wasted. Realistic planning figure: **45-60 minutes per successful item, including rejection overhead.**

**Decision:** The benchmark is no longer sized to a fixed item count. **Annotation runs until 2026-09-30; the benchmark is whatever it contains at that point.** October 2026 is reserved for the RAG pipeline, model runs, analysis, and writing -- none of which have started, and none of which can be compressed to make room for more annotation without threatening the November arXiv deadline (the project protocol). This replaces ADR-027's fixed 90-item / 30-per-domain target, not its reasoning about honest framing: **the paper states the sample size was determined by a fixed annotation budget under a fixed deadline, not chosen post hoc** -- the same principle ADR-027 established, now applied to a time budget instead of a count.

**Consequences:**
- Per-domain confidence intervals (ADR-027) are still required and now matter more, since domain counts are no longer guaranteed equal by construction -- see ADR-038 (balanced work-queue) for how approximate parity is maintained anyway.
- Whatever count exists on 2026-09-30 is final; no extending into October to hit a round number.
- the project protocol and `docs/ANNOTATION.md` updated to reflect a deadline-bounded process, not a count-bounded one.

---

## ADR-038: Balanced, interleaved work queue -- domains are annotated in parallel, not sequentially

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-037 removed the fixed 90-item target in favour of a fixed annotation window ending 2026-09-30. Under a count-bounded plan, domain order didn't matter much -- 30/domain would eventually be reached regardless of sequence. Under a deadline-bounded plan it matters a great deal: annotating domain by domain (finish Pharmacology, then Obstetrics/Gynecology, then Rehabilitation/Therapy) risks stopping on 2026-09-30 with one domain fully represented and another barely started, which cannot support the per-domain stratified comparison the paper needs (the project protocol-3, ADR-027's per-domain-CI requirement).

**Decision:** `curate/work_queue.py` (tested) generates the annotation order: at every step, offer the next candidate from whichever domain currently has the fewest committed items (annotated + already queued), skipping anything already selected, already rejected, or that would push any individual author over the ADR-031 cap of 2 -- checked against everything already committed, including candidates queued earlier in the same call, not just previously annotated ones. This keeps running per-domain counts within 1 of each other at every point in the process, so stopping on any given day still yields a roughly balanced benchmark, not just a balanced final one.

**Consequences:** `ANNOTATION.md` section 3.3 documents this as the required selection mechanism -- candidates are drawn from the queue, not hand-picked per domain. Rejections (no institutional source found, ANNOTATION.md section 3.2) feed back into the `rejected` set so the queue never re-offers them. Batches (ADR-039) are generated by taking the next N from this queue.

---

## ADR-039: Default question style is quantitative -- chosen against item 1's real material, not templated in advance

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** `ANNOTATION.md` section 4 deliberately left question wording untemplated until a real item existed, to avoid writing a template that fits the format rather than the evidence. Item 1's material (a retracted meta-analysis with a specific pooled effect size, superseded by two syntheses with more modest effect sizes on the same measure) made three candidate phrasings concrete: yes/no ("Is tranexamic acid effective for reducing blood loss in open myomectomy?"), patient-context ("A patient is undergoing open myomectomy -- what do you recommend regarding tranexamic acid?"), and quantitative ("How much does tranexamic acid reduce blood loss during open myomectomy?").

**Decision:** Quantitative is the default question style. Chosen because a request for a specific number pulls retrieval directly toward the pooled effect estimate at risk -- the exact thing a retracted synthesis got wrong -- rather than toward general topic material, and because the resulting answer is short enough to score unambiguously (a number or a small range, not a paragraph). Patient-context framing was tried against the same material and rejected as the default: it tends to produce hedged, multi-part answers (dosing, timing, contraindications, alternatives) that are hard to score cleanly against a single trap/gold claim pair.

**Consequences:** This is the default, not an exclusive rule -- `ANNOTATION.md` section 4 notes that a candidate item may call for a different form if quantitative phrasing doesn't fit the underlying claim (e.g. a claim about which of two options is preferred, not a magnitude), and any departure should be noted on the item. No retroactive change to item 1, which already used this form.

---

## ADR-040: Claim text is authored by the annotator directly -- never reconstructed by the assistant from conversation

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** RB-0001's `trap_claim` and `gold_claim` text was written by the assistant, transcribing the material and decisions worked out across the item-1 session, and marked `provenance: draft` pending review. The author's instruction: this is not acceptable even as a draft-then-review step. The gold-claim scoping rule (ADR-034) already established that the claim must not contain anything the source material doesn't document; this ADR extends the same discipline to *authorship* -- a claim assembled by the assistant from discussion is machine-drafted in substance even when every fact in it is correctly sourced, because the wording, emphasis, and judgment of what to include is not the annotator's.

**Decision:** `trap_claim.text` and `gold_claim.text` are written by the annotator directly, in their own words, from the raw material (`results/itemN_raw_material.md`) -- never reconstructed or drafted by the assistant from conversation, and never treated as a starting point for the annotator to lightly edit. The assistant's role stops at preparing raw material (ANNOTATION.md section 4); the annotator's role starts at the blank claim fields. An item cannot move from `provenance: draft` to `verified` until the annotator has written these fields themselves.

**Consequences:** RB-0001 is sent back with its current (assistant-written) claim text as a visible starting reference only, to be fully rewritten by the author before any verification step. `ANNOTATION.md` section 4 records this as a hard rule, not a preference. Future raw-material files stop at source material plus eligibility results, exactly as `results/item1_raw_material.md` already does -- no draft claim text gets added to those files going forward either, closing off the temptation this ADR is a response to.

---

## ADR-041: Per-batch author-cluster cap -- at most 1 item per cluster per prepared batch

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Batch 1 contained 2 of 5 items (RW records 5684, 5685) from the same author cluster (Joachim Boldt / Gunter G Hempelmann). Both are individually within ADR-031's standing cap of 2 items per author across the whole benchmark -- the batch didn't violate that rule. But concentrating 2 of a cluster's 2 allowed uses into a single 5-item batch defeats the cap's actual purpose: the cap exists so no one fabrication scandal dominates the benchmark's *feel*, and a batch that is 40% one scandal reads that way regardless of the aggregate count across the full benchmark.

**Decision:** `curate/work_queue.py` (`build_work_queue`, tested) enforces a second, independent constraint: at most `max_cluster_authors_per_batch` (default 1) items per author-cluster within a single call's returned batch, tracked separately from the cross-batch `author_cap` and reset every call. A cluster is any author appearing on `cluster_min_size` (default 3) or more records in the full pool -- the same threshold ADR-031 uses. This is stricter than, and independent of, the standing cap: a cluster can still contribute up to 2 items total across the benchmark's lifetime (ADR-031), but never more than 1 in any single prepared batch.

**Consequences:** Batch 1 is corrected: record 5685 is dropped (it also had two independent open gaps -- abstract not fully retrieved, eligibility not confirmed -- so dropping it costs nothing already-solid) and replaced with a candidate from the work queue under the corrected constraint. `ANNOTATION.md` section 3.3 records this as part of the required selection mechanism, alongside the domain-balance rule.

---

## ADR-042: notice_text_verified -- an unconfirmed quote cannot support a specificity judgment

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Batch 1's candidate 7837 (breast radiation therapy in elderly women) has a retraction notice quoted only secondhand, via Retraction Watch's reporting -- the primary publisher page (Elsevier, `jamcollsurg.com`) returned 402/403 on every automated attempt, and PubMed/Europe PMC/Crossref all show no abstract for the notice itself. The notice text used in `results/item4_raw_material.md` and its implied `notice_specificity = categorical` therefore rest on RW's transcription, not on anything independently confirmed. Given ADR-033's whole point is measuring how informative notices actually are, annotating a specificity level from an unconfirmed secondhand quote would contaminate that measurement with a source we didn't ourselves verify -- exactly the kind of unstated-provenance problem ADR-034 already rules out for claim content.

**Decision:** `SupersededSource` gains `notice_text_verified: bool` and a new `NoticeSpecificity.UNVERIFIABLE` value, enforced by a model validator: `notice_text_verified=False` requires `notice_specificity=UNVERIFIABLE`, and vice versa -- the two fields cannot disagree. Before annotating specificity, the notice text must be confirmed against a primary or authoritative source (the publisher directly, PubMed's own page, or a full-text repository); a secondhand quote from a third party (a blog, a news summary) does not satisfy this, no matter how reliable that third party generally is.

**Consequences:** Every raw-material file must state plainly whether its notice text was independently confirmed or not. Where it wasn't, and a primary source genuinely cannot be reached, the item is annotated `notice_specificity = unverifiable` rather than guessing at a level -- this becomes part of ADR-033's eventual distribution report (specific / categorical / bare / unverifiable), not swept into one of the other three buckets. `curate/benchmark_io.py`'s existing round-trip validation now also enforces this consistency automatically at write time.

---

## ADR-043: item_type distinguishes items with a current answer from items with none -- and a pre-registered prediction about the second kind

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Candidate 1471 (Yoshitaka Fujii, ramosetron vs. granisetron for pediatric strabismus surgery PONV) was initially flagged as an unresolved eligibility question, not a clean accept or reject. Tracing it fully: the obvious current-evidence route, the Cochrane review "Drugs for preventing postoperative nausea and vomiting" (Carlisle & Stevenson), is itself compromised -- an earlier edition (DOI `10.1002/14651858.CD004125.pub2`) is RW record 30918, already inside our own 364-candidate pool as a retraction; the next edition (`.pub3`, 2017) was withdrawn outright after 67 of its 737 included studies turned out to be retracted, heavily Fujii-linked; its rebuild (Weibel et al., Cochrane 2020/2021) covers adults only. No pediatric-specific replacement was found. A fabricated primary trial was absorbed into a systematic review; the review collapsed once enough of its inputs were exposed; the rebuild left the original population uncovered. The paediatric question this candidate asks has no current authoritative answer.

**Decision:** This is not a rejection -- it is a distinct, valuable item category. `BenchmarkItem` gains `item_type` (`ItemType`: `superseded_with_current_answer` | `superseded_without_current_answer`). For the second type, the gold claim states two things: the prior evidence is void, *and* no current authoritative source addresses the question -- both established facts (the retraction, and the documented absence of a replacement), not an inference that evidence doesn't exist somewhere unsearched. **Not sought out deliberately**: candidates are not screened for this property in advance; it is flagged when eligibility search (ANNOTATION.md section 3.2) organically turns one up, exactly as 1471 did. Forcing the category would make its rate a constructed number instead of a measured one, undermining the same kind of finding ADR-033's notice-specificity distribution depends on being genuine. Target ~10-15% of the final benchmark, as a rough expectation rather than a quota.

**Pre-registered prediction, recorded now, before any model has been run against a single item of this type:** models will almost never respond "there is no current authoritative answer" when asked a `superseded_without_current_answer` question. They will instead either cite the retracted evidence directly, or synthesize a plausible-sounding answer from adjacent, non-specific literature (e.g. general PONV guidance standing in for the pediatric-strabismus-specific question, the same substitution ADR-034-adjacent reasoning about TXA/myomectomy already warned against). If this holds, it is a stronger result than M1 (Retraction Citation Rate) alone, because it is a failure of epistemic honesty -- confidently answering a question that has no correct confident answer -- rather than a failure to track which evidence is current. If it fails (models do say "no current answer" reliably), that is reported too, exactly as ADR-032's synthesis-vs-primary prediction would be either way.

**Consequences:** `schemas.py` validates `item_type` as required on every `BenchmarkItem`. `ANNOTATION.md` section 4 documents both item types and the gold-claim content requirement for the second. Candidate 1471's raw material is reframed accordingly (not rejected) in `results/item5_raw_material.md`; its actual claim text still waits on the annotator, per ADR-040. `eval/` and `stats/` (not yet built) must report this prediction's outcome explicitly when they exist, the same way ADR-032's is tracked.

---

## ADR-044: Near-miss on ADR-034 -- "direction corroborated, magnitude not" was an unstated inference smuggled into the gold claim

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Reviewing RB-0001's assistant-drafted gold claim (already being replaced per ADR-040), the author caught a specific line: "the direction of effect (TXA reduces blood loss) is corroborated, the retracted paper's specific magnitude is not." This sounds like a careful, hedged summary. It is not documented anywhere. Item 1's retraction notice says only that data-collection errors affected the results and conclusions (`notice_specificity`, now further downgraded to `unverifiable` for other items in this batch under ADR-042) -- it does not say the direction was right and only the magnitude was wrong. That specific, plausible-sounding characterization was assembled by comparing the retracted SMD to the two 2022 papers' mean differences and inferring a story about *what kind* of error occurred. ADR-034 exists specifically to prohibit this: gold claims document what the notice states and what current evidence says, not a reconstructed account of the error.

**Why this is worth its own entry rather than a silent fix:** the inference was correct-*sounding* and low-drama -- not a fabricated statistic, not a wrong number, just one interpretive sentence stitching two true facts (the retracted magnitude; the current, different magnitude) into an unstated causal story ("magnitude was the problem, direction wasn't"). That is exactly the failure mode ADR-034 is hardest to catch in practice: obviously-wrong fabrications get caught, quietly-plausible editorializing does not, because it reads as competent synthesis rather than invention.

**Decision:** Reaffirming ADR-034 with a concrete negative example for future reference: **do not characterize what kind of error occurred, even by implication, when the notice doesn't say so.** The corrected pattern for this item: state that the retracted estimate should not be cited, state what subsequent syntheses report as plain facts side by side (retracted: SMD -1.512; current: ~225 mL mean difference reduction, different metric, not directly comparable without noting that), and stop -- let the reader draw any comparison themselves rather than doing it for them in the claim text.

**Consequences:** No schema or process change beyond what ADR-034/040 already require -- this ADR exists as a documented instance for calibration, since "we nearly did the thing the rule prohibits, here specifically is what it looked like" is more useful for future review than the rule alone. `ANNOTATION.md` section 4 gets this as a worked example alongside the existing scoping-rule text.

---

## ADR-045: Pinned embedding and model selection for the RAG pipeline, verified live against current (2026-08-07) releases

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** the project protocol already commits to litellm as the single model interface and to caching every result. What was undecided: which embedding model, and which models to test against, given the pipeline is starting now (ADR pending, this one) rather than waiting for annotation to finish. The author's explicit instruction: do not pick versions from memory -- my training data reliably extends only to around January 2026, and it is now August 2026; checking "current" against stale internal knowledge is exactly the kind of unverified-claim failure this whole project exists to catch elsewhere. Every model below was confirmed live, primarily against each vendor's own model-card or docs page (Hugging Face org pages, Anthropic's platform docs, OpenAI's API docs), not press coverage. One live check caught a fabricated claim in the wild: a low-quality site reported a "Llama 5, 600B params, recursive self-improvement" release; Meta's own Hugging Face org page shows Llama 4 (Scout/Maverick) is still the current generation. That near-catch is itself evidence for why this verification step is required, not optional.

**Decision:**

*Embeddings:* `BAAI/bge-large-en-v1.5` via `sentence-transformers`, run locally. Open weights, no hosted API, no ongoing cost, and -- the deciding reason -- reproducible in three years regardless of any vendor's API lifecycle. Author's choice, not independently re-litigated here.

*Four models under test, spanning open/closed weights and provider:*

| Slot | Model | Exact identifier | Released | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Open-weight 1 | Llama 4 Scout | `meta-llama/Llama-4-Scout-17B-16E-Instruct` | 2025-04-05 | 17B active / 109B total (MoE), 10M token context. Chosen over Maverick (same generation, ~400B total) for single-high-end-GPU feasibility. |
| Open-weight 2 | Qwen3.6-27B | `Qwen/Qwen3.6-27B` | 2026-04 | 27B dense, single-GPU, 262K native context, chat-capable by default (no separate -Instruct tag). Chosen over the newer Qwen3.8-Max (2.4T params, open weights released within the last few days of this ADR and not yet practically self-hostable) as the stable, current-generation choice. |
| API 1 (Anthropic) | Claude Opus 5 | `claude-opus-5` | GA 2026 | $5 / $25 per MTok in/out. Chosen over Claude Fable 5 (Anthropic's higher tier, $10/$50) specifically because Anthropic's own docs describe Opus 5 as the stronger choice for scientific/research work with fewer biology-content restrictions than Fable 5 -- directly relevant to a medical benchmark, where over-cautious refusals would be a confound, not a finding. |
| API 2 (OpenAI) | GPT-5.6 Sol | `gpt-5.6-sol` (alias `gpt-5.6`) | Preview 2026-06-26, GA 2026-07-09 | $5 / $30 per MTok in/out. OpenAI's current flagship ("workhorse") variant, distinct from the cheaper Terra/Luna variants in the same family. |

*Optional fifth slot -- medical-specialized open model:* `google/medgemma-1.5-4b-it`, released 2026-01-13, built on Gemma 3, 4B parameters. Real adoption evidence, not just a benchmark claim: Google reports millions of downloads and 500+ community derivative models across the MedGemma series. The 2023-2024 wave of medical fine-tunes this project might otherwise have reached for (Meditron, OpenBioLLM, BioMistral) has been superseded -- current literature reports they now trail even general-purpose mid-size models like Qwen2.5-32B on aggregate medical benchmarks, so none of them meet the "real adoption" bar as of today. **Caveat flagged, not resolved here:** MedGemma 1.5 is 4B parameters, an order of magnitude smaller than the other four models. Any medical-specialized-vs-general comparison using it is confounded with model scale, not a clean isolation of specialization. Include it as a fifth data point with that limitation stated plainly wherever it's reported, not as a matched comparison.

**Cost estimate (ADR-required before any batch run), rough, pending real prompt templates:** assuming ~1250 input / ~300 output tokens per call, blended across the four conditions (no-retrieval calls are much shorter; retrieval and post-notice calls carry retrieved abstracts and are longer) --

- At the user's own planning scale (90 items x 4 conditions x 4 models x 3 seeds = 4,320 calls, 1,080 per model): Opus 5 ~$14.85, GPT-5.6 Sol ~$16.47, **API total ~$31/full run**. The two open-weight models carry no API cost but real GPU cost (self-hosting Llama 4 Scout at 109B total parameters and Qwen3.6-27B at 27B dense both need real infrastructure planning -- not accounted for in the dollar figure above).
- At the actual current scale (10 items, the near-term smoke-test target): 120 calls/model, **API total ~$3.50** for the full 10-item x 4-condition x 4-model x 3-seed run.
- These are order-of-magnitude planning numbers from stated assumptions, not a commitment -- re-estimate once real prompt templates and the corpus (ADR pending) fix actual token counts, before the first real batch run, per the project protocol's cost-discipline rule.

**Caching (already required by the project protocol, restated here for this specific use):** every `ModelResponse` is cached keyed on `(item_id, condition, model_identifier, seed, prompt_template_id)` -- all five fields already exist on the schema. No result for an already-computed key is ever re-requested.

**Worked example, flagged for the paper's introduction, not just this log:** while confirming Meta's current Llama generation, one search result (a low-quality SEO/content site, not Meta or a primary source) reported "Llama 5, 600B parameters, recursive self-improvement, released April 8, 2026." This is fabricated -- Meta's own Hugging Face org page, checked directly, shows Llama 4 (Scout/Maverick, April 2025) is still the current generation; no Llama 5 exists. The claim was rejected because the verification step this ADR required -- check the primary source, not the first plausible-sounding result -- caught it before it could be pinned as real. **This is the exact failure this benchmark measures, occurring inside the benchmark's own construction:** a plausible, specific, confidently-stated claim about a superseded/nonexistent fact, surfaced by an otherwise-reasonable search, that a system without a verification step would have propagated. The parallel to RetractionBench's core question -- does a system know that what it "knows" is no longer (or never was) true -- is direct enough to state explicitly in the paper's introduction as a motivating anecdote, not just document here.

**Consequences:** `pyproject.toml`'s `models` dependency group already has `litellm`, `sentence-transformers`, and `faiss-cpu` pinned to minimum versions (confirmed, not assumed) -- no dependency change needed, only litellm routing config for the specific identifiers above where self-hosted serving requires it. Any future model swap (a new release superseding one of these) is a new ADR, not a silent edit here -- results stay tied to the exact versions pinned above, stated plainly in the paper's methods section per the author's instruction.

## ADR-046: Corpus construction decisions resolved -- MeSH-derived scope, frozen and hashed, no target size

**Status:** Accepted (date window, derivation method, freeze/versioning policy); **size and go/no-go on the fetch step pending author confirmation given the real number below**

**Context:** `docs/CORPUS_PLAN.md` (proposed, not yet built) left four questions open: date window, domain query derivation, freeze-vs-rebuild, and the corpus size target. The author resolved three of the four on principle and asked that the fourth (size) be a *consequence* of the other three rather than a chosen target, with real counts checked before any fetch, not estimated.

**Decisions:**

1. **Date window: 2000 to present, frozen at a stated date.** Rationale: the benchmark's own retracted sources span 2001-2017 and superseding evidence runs to 2025; a narrower window would clip either end and introduce an unquantifiable retrieval bias. Corpus size cost is cheap; retrieval bias is not. The corpus's own publication-date distribution will be reported alongside the benchmark items' dates so a reader can see whether benchmark sources sit in a dense or sparse region of the background pool.

2. **Domain derivation: MeSH terms, not hand-tuned keywords, reported exactly.** Verified live against NCBI ESearch (2026-08-07), each domain's MeSH heading with automatic tree expansion, `AND 2000:2026[dp] AND hasabstract[text]`:

   | Domain | Query | Count (with abstract) |
   |---|---|---|
   | Pharmacology | `"Pharmacology"[MeSH Terms]` | see combined total below (per-domain broken out pre-abstract-filter: 63,224) |
   | Obstetrics/Gynecology | `"Obstetrics"[MeSH Terms] OR "Gynecology"[MeSH Terms]` | 17,169 pre-filter |
   | Rehabilitation/Therapy | `"Rehabilitation"[MeSH Terms]` | 287,885 pre-filter |

   `"Pharmacology, Clinical"[MeSH Terms]` was checked as a narrower alternative for Pharmacology and rejected: only 1,877 records, too narrow to represent the domain's real scope. Adding `"Physical and Rehabilitation Medicine"[MeSH Terms]` to the Rehabilitation query changed the count by under 0.5% (287,885 -> 289,169) -- redundant, not added. Cross-domain overlap (a record matching more than one domain's MeSH heading) is small: the three per-domain counts sum to 368,278; the deduplicated union is 368,082 -- under 0.1% overlap.

3. **Freeze policy: built once, hashed, versioned.** The corpus is frozen at construction, never silently rebuilt during the study. The freeze date, exact record count, and a content hash (SHA-256 over the sorted PMID list plus abstract text, manifest format TBD at build time) are recorded in this ADR's follow-up entry once built, and in the paper. Any later change is corpus v2, a new ADR, with every affected result recomputed and reported against the version it used -- never a silent drift. New benchmark items annotated after the freeze are inserted individually via `docs/CORPUS_PLAN.md` section 4's exactly-once mechanism, not folded into a rebuild.

4. **Size: derived, not targeted -- and the derived number exceeds the tractability reference point given, flagged rather than resolved unilaterally.** Combined MeSH scope x date window x has-abstract filter (all three legitimate selection criteria, not subsampling): **325,043 records.** This is larger than the 200,000-abstract figure the author used as their own reference for "still tractable" -- not a redefinition of that reference by this ADR, a genuine result of the derivation. No subsampling to a round number, per instruction. **Not proceeding to fetch/embed 325,043 abstracts without confirmation**, per the author's own stated protocol for this exact situation ("tell me and we decide then, on the real number").

**New addition to the plan (retrieval ceiling):** once the index exists, before any model call, measure for every benchmark item whether its retracted source and notice appear in the top-k retrieved results at several k values (proposed: k in {1, 5, 10, 20, 50}). This is a retrieval ceiling -- the best any model could do given this corpus -- and must be reported before generation results: if a notice is never retrieved at k=10, a model's failure to mention it is not evidence of a generation failure. Cheap (no model calls involved), so no cost gate needed for this step specifically.

**Consequences:** `docs/CORPUS_PLAN.md` updated to reflect all of the above as resolved rather than open questions, with a new open item: whether to fetch the full 325,043-record scope, or narrow the MeSH/date criteria further (and if so, on what principled basis, not an arbitrary cutoff) before the first real corpus build.

---

## ADR-047: Domain corpus-density imbalance -- measure the retrieval ceiling before deciding whether it's a confound, three candidate fixes recorded in advance

**Status:** Accepted (measurement plan and the three candidate fixes with reasoning); **which fix, if any, applies is not decided here -- contingent on ADR-046's per-domain retrieval-ceiling measurement**

**Context:** ADR-046's derived corpus is 325,043 records, unevenly split by domain: Pharmacology 63,224, Obstetrics/Gynecology 17,169, Rehabilitation/Therapy 287,885 -- a 17-fold difference between the smallest and largest domain's retrieval competition pool. Since this benchmark's stratified per-domain comparison (ADR-027) is one of its central claims, an unaddressed density imbalance is a real threat: a domain difference in downstream results could reflect retrieval facing more competitors in a larger pool rather than anything about the medicine itself, and the two would be inseparable without checking.

**Decision: measure before adjusting, not the reverse.** Narrowing Rehabilitation's MeSH scope on the intuition that "a bigger pool must hurt retrieval" would be reasoning from assumption, not evidence -- dense and sparse retrieval do not degrade linearly or even necessarily monotonically with corpus size, and asserting a confound without measuring it would be exactly the kind of unverified claim this project's own standards exist to prevent. The full 325,043-record corpus is built as designed (ADR-046, no narrowing), and the retrieval-ceiling step already planned there (top-k recall for each item's trap source and notice, k in {1, 5, 10, 20, 50}) is broken out **per domain**. If ceilings are comparable across domains despite the density difference, the imbalance is not binding on retrieval and nothing further is done. If Rehabilitation's ceiling is materially lower, the confound is real and one of the three fixes below applies.

**Three candidate fixes, recorded now with reasoning, before it's known whether any is needed:**

1. **Preferred: report retrieval metrics per domain throughout; treat corpus density as a covariate, not something to eliminate.** Real clinical RAG systems face exactly this asymmetry -- some specialties have vastly more indexed literature than others, and a benchmark that engineers that away is less representative of deployment conditions, not more. If density predicts part of the retrieval-ceiling gap, that becomes a reported, interpreted covariate (e.g. "Rehabilitation's lower ceiling is partly attributable to a 17-fold larger competitor pool") rather than a hidden confound or an eliminated one.

2. **Second: a balanced secondary index, stratified to equal size per domain, built and reported alongside the full one.** Directly separates medicine-driven from density-driven effects by construction, at the cost of building and maintaining two indices and reporting results twice. Held as the fallback if the first option's covariate framing turns out too weak to actually separate the two effects in practice (e.g. if density and domain-difficulty are themselves correlated in a way a covariate model can't cleanly untangle).

3. **Rejected: narrow Rehabilitation's MeSH scope to shrink the pool toward the other domains.** Introduces exactly the hand-tuned, unreproducible selection step ADR-046's MeSH-derivation decision was meant to avoid, and makes the Rehabilitation corpus unrepresentative of Rehabilitation's own real literature -- solving the imbalance by making the data less honest about the domain it's supposed to represent. Not used regardless of what the measurement shows.

**Also decided:** the 17-fold indexed-literature volume difference across domains is itself a reportable finding, not merely a nuisance parameter to manage -- it belongs in the paper's domain-description material alongside the other per-domain corpus statistics (ADR-046's date distribution, ADR-028/029-style background retracted-tag rate).

**Consequences:** `docs/CORPUS_PLAN.md` section 6's retrieval-ceiling step is broken out per domain, not just in aggregate. Whichever fix (if any) applies is recorded as a follow-up to this ADR once the measurement exists -- not decided in advance of data, consistent with ADR-032/039/043's pre-registration discipline elsewhere in this project.

---

## ADR-048: Corpus built and frozen -- real numbers, two infrastructure bugs found and fixed, retrieval-ceiling measurement not yet statistically meaningful

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-046/047 planned the corpus; this records what actually happened building it -- real counts, real build cost, real bugs, and the current state of the retrieval-ceiling measurement, all against the project protocol's reproduction-path requirement to report actual figures, not optimistic estimates.

**Final corpus (frozen 2026-08-09):**

- **400,538 background documents** (Pharmacology 57,678, Obstetrics/Gynecology 12,107, Rehabilitation/Therapy 330,753) plus 1 benchmark source, 1 notice, and 2 superseding-evidence documents (RB-0001) inserted exactly once per `docs/CORPUS_PLAN.md` section 4 -- **400,542 total.**
- These are higher than ADR-046's originally reported 325,251 (and higher again than that ADR's very first pre-filter table, 368,278) because the fetch, the chunk-discovery re-checks, and the freeze itself were run at different points over roughly two days, and PubMed keeps indexing new records continuously. The frozen number (400,542) is authoritative going forward; earlier smaller figures in ADR-046 were real counts *at the time they were checked*, not errors, but are superseded by this entry.
- **The domain imbalance is larger than every earlier estimate: Rehabilitation/Pharmacology-scope background alone is 330,753 vs. Obstetrics/Gynecology's 12,107 -- a ~27-fold difference**, not the 17-fold first reported or the ~21-fold corrected mid-fetch. Same direction and same underlying cause (Rehabilitation's MeSH heading is genuinely broad, ADR-025) as already recorded, just a larger number now that the moving target has stopped moving (frozen, per policy below).
- **Freeze manifest:** date 2026-08-09, SHA-256 content hash over the sorted (pmid, abstract) pairs computed and stored (`data/interim/corpus/CORPUS_FREEZE.json`, gitignored as a regenerable interim artifact -- the hash and counts are what's authoritative, reproducible by rerunning the freeze script against the same frozen `assembled_corpus.jsonl`). Publication-year range 2000-2026 across 27 distinct years, distribution recorded for the "dense vs. sparse region" report `docs/CORPUS_PLAN.md` section 3 promised.
- Per ADR-046's freeze policy: this corpus is now version 1. Any future change (new benchmark items still get inserted individually via the exactly-once mechanism, which doesn't require a rebuild) that changes the background pool itself is corpus v2, a new ADR, with affected results recomputed and reported against the version they used.

**Two infrastructure bugs found by running the real pipeline against real data/hardware, not caught by review:**

1. **NCBI caps `retstart` at 9999 per query, even with the history server.** A naive single-query fetch of Pharmacology (55k+ records) failed at retstart=9999 with a 400 error. Fixed with recursive date-range bisection (`corpus/mesh_fetch.py`, `find_date_chunks`) so every sub-query's count stays under the cap -- confirmed live: Rehabilitation splits into 46 chunks, largest 9,768. The bisection itself then hit a 429 (no rate limiting had been added to the count-only discovery calls) -- fixed with the same NCBI politeness delay used everywhere else in this project.
2. **`uv sync --group models` was silently installing PyPI's default CPU-only torch build**, despite `torch.cuda.is_available()` returning `True` in a different (system) Python on the same machine. The first full embedding attempt ran for several minutes at 0% GPU utilization (confirmed via repeated `nvidia-smi` samples and a process CPU-time delta showing real but CPU-bound work) before this was caught, the job killed, and the real cause found: the project's own venv had the CPU wheel. Fixed by pinning torch to PyTorch's `cu130` index on Windows via `tool.uv.sources` with a platform marker -- required promoting torch from a transitive (via sentence-transformers) to a direct dependency, since `uv` did not honor the source override for a transitive-only package on the first attempt. Verified end to end: a clean `uv sync --group models` now installs the CUDA build automatically, and a smoke-test batch's build time dropped from 9.97s (CPU) to 1.57s (GPU) for the same 20 documents.

**Real build cost (BAAI/bge-large-en-v1.5, RTX 3050 6GB laptop GPU, batch size 128), reported per ADR-047's instruction to state real requirements, not optimistic ones:**

- **Build time: 38,955.9 seconds (~10.82 hours)** for 400,542 documents, after the GPU fix (~10.3 docs/sec sustained average). This is slower than a smoke-test extrapolation would have suggested and is recorded as the real number specifically because a laptop GPU under many hours of sustained load is a realistic reproduction scenario, not a data-center one -- anyone reproducing this on comparable hardware should budget the better part of a day for this step alone.
- **Peak memory: 3,955.6 MB RSS** (measured via `psutil`, sampled on a background thread throughout the build -- see the embed_index.py fix earlier this session for why `tracemalloc` could not be trusted for this number).
- **Index size on disk: 1,640,620,077 bytes (~1.64 GB)** -- exactly matches the arithmetic check (400,542 x 1024 dims x 4 bytes/float32 = 1,640,620,032 bytes, the ~45-byte difference being FAISS's flat-index header), confirming the index contains what it should and nothing else.

**Retrieval-ceiling measurement: infrastructure validated on real data, but not yet statistically meaningful.** Run against the one real annotated item (RB-0001, Pharmacology): trap source retrieved at k=1 (recall@1 = 1.0 for that single item); notice document retrieved starting at k=5, not k=1. The mechanism works correctly end to end against the real 400,542-document index. **It cannot yet answer ADR-047's actual question** -- whether the 27-fold domain imbalance measurably degrades retrieval in the larger domains -- because Obstetrics/Gynecology and Rehabilitation/Therapy have zero annotated items so far; the comparison this ADR exists to enable requires items across all three domains, which is an ongoing annotation-track outcome, not a shortfall in this measurement step.

**Consequences:** The corpus and index are ready for use. `docs/CORPUS_PLAN.md`'s "not yet built" status is closed out. ADR-047's per-domain fix decision (report as covariate / balanced secondary index / rejected narrowing) remains genuinely undecided and stays undecided until enough cross-domain items exist to measure it -- re-run `corpus/retrieval_ceiling.py` against the growing item set periodically during annotation, not just once at the end, so the imbalance question gets an answer with real statistical weight before it's needed for the paper.

---

## ADR-049: Supersession notices are systematically less retrievable than the sources they supersede -- rank as the first-class retrieval-ceiling measurement, pre-registered before more items exist

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** RB-0001's retrieval-ceiling result (ADR-048): the retracted paper (trap source) is retrieved at rank 1 for its own question; the supersession notice first appears at rank 5. Both were reported as simple hit/miss booleans at fixed k values, which obscures the finding -- a hit at k=5 and a hit at k=1 look the same in a hit-at-k table, but a 4-5x rank gap between "the dangerous document" and "the document that corrects it" is the more informative fact, and it is exactly the shape of failure this benchmark's whole premise is about: if it generalizes, retrieval systems are structurally biased toward superseded evidence, because a substantive paper matches a clinical query far better than a two-sentence administrative notice does, independent of anything a language model does with what it's given.

**Decision:** `corpus/retrieval_ceiling.py` now records exact rank (1-indexed position in the full similarity ranking over all 400,542+ corpus documents, not a hit/miss at an arbitrary k) for both the trap source and the notice, for every item, and derives `rank_gap = notice_rank - trap_source_rank`. Per-domain summaries report the rank-gap distribution (mean, median), the fraction of items where the notice ranks worse than its own trap source, and the fraction where the notice falls below `PRACTICAL_K = 10` -- the author's framing that typical RAG systems retrieve k=3 to k=10, so a notice ranked below that is effectively invisible in practice regardless of what a full-corpus ceiling shows. Hit-at-k booleans are kept as a derived view (`ItemCeilingResult.hit_at`), not removed, since some readers will still want the conventional framing -- but rank is now the primary recorded quantity.

**Pre-registered prediction, recorded now, with n=1 and before any further items exist:** the notice will rank below the trap source in the large majority of items. This is registered specifically so it cannot be adjusted after more data arrives -- the same discipline as ADR-032 (synthesis-vs-primary), ADR-039 (question style), and ADR-043 (item type). If it holds, "Supersession notices are systematically less retrievable than the sources they supersede" is a headline result obtained without running a single model under test -- a structural property of retrieval over a realistic corpus, not a model failure. If it doesn't hold, that is reported too.

**Standing measurement protocol:** re-run `corpus/retrieval_ceiling.py` after every 5 newly annotated items, not just once at the end of annotation. This keeps the rank-gap distribution and the domain-imbalance question (ADR-047) both live, measured quantities throughout the annotation window rather than a single point estimate computed under time pressure in October. See `docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 3.3 for where this is recorded as part of the standard workflow.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md`'s results outline (this session) includes "Supersession notices are systematically less retrievable than the sources they supersede" as a named results subsection, cross-referencing this ADR for the pre-registered prediction -- marked TODO for actual content until enough items exist to report, per the same discipline as every other results subsection in that draft.

---

## ADR-050: ADR-049's prediction conflated two claims -- separated, and the infrastructure framing it was starting to support was outrunning the evidence

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-049 pre-registered "the notice will rank below the trap source in the large majority of items" and framed a confirmation as "Supersession notices are systematically less retrievable than the sources they supersede." The literal predicted quantity (ordering) and the framing used to describe what confirming it would mean (systematic *un*retrievability) are not the same claim, and the first real measurement exposed the gap: RB-0001's trap source ranks 1st, its notice ranks 2nd. The ordering prediction holds (notice ranks worse). The stronger reading the framing implied -- that the notice is effectively missed, not just outranked -- does not: rank 2 is comfortably inside any practical retrieval window (k=3 to k=10, the range real RAG systems operate in). One precise measurement produced a materially milder finding than the framing was written to expect, and continuing to call that finding "systematic unretrievability" would overstate it.

**Decision:** Two distinct predictions, tracked separately, because they carry different implications for where the paper places responsibility for failure:

1. **Ordering** -- `notice_rank > trap_source_rank`. A mild effect: the notice is findable, just consistently second. If a model still fails to use a notice that ranked #2 in the retrieved set, that is not obviously excused by retrieval -- the document was there. This is the claim RB-0001 supports so far.
2. **Practical invisibility** -- `notice_rank > PRACTICAL_K (10)`. A severe effect: the notice would not appear in a realistic top-k window at all, making non-use attributable to retrieval rather than the model. RB-0001 does *not* support this (`notice_invisible_in_practice_fraction = 0.0` at n=1) -- doubtful on current evidence, not confirmed and not yet ruled out at scale.

Both are already computed as separate fields by `corpus/retrieval_ceiling.py` (`notice_ranks_worse_fraction` and `notice_invisible_in_practice_fraction` respectively, ADR-049) -- no code change needed, only the prose and the paper's framing, which had collapsed the two.

**The self-correction that matters more than the wording fix:** this project had begun leaning on an "infrastructure, not just the model" framing (ADR-029's reframing of the PubMed/Retraction Watch findings, and ADR-049's initial framing of this prediction) partly because it is a more interesting, more novel-sounding result -- a finding obtained without running a single model under test. That is exactly the condition under which a framing preference can start outrunning what the evidence actually supports, and it did here: the first real number available complicates rather than confirms the stronger version. **If notices turn out to be retrievable-but-subordinate (prediction 1, mild) rather than practically invisible (prediction 2, severe), then a model's failure to use a retrievable notice is squarely a generation failure, and the paper's framing shifts back toward the model being accountable for that specific failure mode -- not the infrastructure.** Recording this now, at n=1, before either direction is settled, for the same reason every other prediction in this project is pre-registered: so the paper's eventual framing is checkable against what was expected before the data existed, including framings the author found appealing.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` section 4.5 is renamed from "Supersession notices are systematically less retrievable than the sources they supersede" to **"Relative retrievability of supersession notices"** -- neutral, not pre-loaded with a conclusion, until enough data exists to justify one. Section 3.11's prediction paragraph is rewritten to state both predictions distinctly rather than one merged claim. Both predictions are reported in Results regardless of which (if either) holds, per this project's standing pre-registration discipline (ADR-032, ADR-039, ADR-043).

---

## ADR-051: RB-0001 verified -- author-written claims are deliberately plainer than the assistant's earlier draft, per ADR-034/044

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Per ADR-040, `trap_claim`/`gold_claim` text must be written by the annotator directly. The author has now written both for RB-0001, replacing the assistant-drafted text that was sent back unedited for this purpose.

**Final text, author-written:**

> **trap_claim:** "Intravenous tranexamic acid in open myomectomy reduces total blood loss, postoperative haemoglobin decline, transfusion requirements, and duration of surgery." (`valid_from` 2017-06-01, `valid_until` 2018-01-21)
>
> **gold_claim:** "This meta-analysis was retracted in January 2018 and its results should not be cited. Two subsequent independent syntheses found that tranexamic acid reduces blood loss in open myomectomy by approximately 225 mL." (`valid_from` 2022-09-13, `valid_until` null)

**Two deliberate omissions, both intentional, both traceable to standing rules rather than new decisions:**

1. **The trap claim quotes no pooled statistic** -- no SMD, no confidence interval, no p-value. The earlier assistant draft had quoted "SMD -1.512 (95% CI -2.746 to -0.278, P=.016)" directly, reproducing the retracted paper's own statistics table. The author's own guidance when this was flagged mid-session asked for "what the paper claimed in plain terms, with the number, and cite it" -- the final text goes further than that instruction and includes no number at all, which is the author's call under ADR-040 and not corrected here. Recorded as an observation, not a deviation to fix.
2. **The gold claim does not say which part of the retracted estimate was wrong.** No "direction corroborated, magnitude not" -- the exact phrase ADR-044 flagged as an unstated inference smuggled into an earlier draft. The final text states two documented facts side by side (retraction date and citability; the current syntheses' ~225 mL figure) and stops, exactly the corrected pattern ADR-034/044 specify: let the reader compare, do not narrate the comparison for them.

**Decision:** RB-0001 is updated with this text, `provenance` set to `verified`, and an `Annotation` record appended (`annotator_id`, `date`, `provenance=verified`). Validated against the schema on write, per this project's standing round-trip discipline.

**Consequences:** This is the first fully verified item in the benchmark. Its claim style -- plain, numberless trap claim; two-fact, non-editorializing gold claim -- is the concrete referent for "gold-claim scoping" (ADR-034) and "avoid quietly-plausible inference" (ADR-044) going forward, more useful than either ADR's abstract statement of the rule for calibrating future items.

---

## ADR-052: Gold claims must carry the superseding evidence's own stated limitations -- a new standing rule, and a second scoring axis, not folded into correctness

**Status:** Accepted (rule and reframing); scoring-axis design proposed, implementation deferred pending approval.

**Context:** While writing RB-0002, the superseding evidence itself (Vidal et al. 2025, a systematic review of 866 women) states that its own evidence base "remains observational" and calls for RCTs. The author's instruction, given while authoring RB-0002's gold claim: where the superseding source states a limitation on its own strength -- calls for RCTs, notes the evidence is observational, reports wide confidence intervals -- the gold claim must carry that limitation. A confidently flat "72%" is wrong in a different way than citing the retracted study is wrong, even though both involve the number 72.

This reframes what the benchmark measures. It was one axis: does the model rely on superseded evidence. It is now two: (1) does the model rely on superseded evidence, and (2) does the model represent the strength of *current* evidence honestly. A model can get (1) right and (2) wrong -- state the up-to-date number without the hedge the source itself attaches to it.

**Decision:**

1. **Standing rule, effective immediately, applying to every item annotated from here forward:** if the post-supersession evidence used to write a gold claim itself states a limitation on its own strength (calls for RCTs, "observational," wide CIs, small n, single-center, etc.), the gold claim's text must carry that limitation. This is a constraint on what the author writes, not a new schema field by itself -- `docs/ANNOTATION.md` is updated accordingly (see below).

2. **RB-0001 checked against this rule, not assumed clean.** The author's stated expectation was that RB-0001 doesn't face this issue, since both 2022 syntheses were presented as stating their findings without a comparable caveat. Checked live rather than taken on faith, per this project's standing "verify, don't assume" practice (ADR-048's torch bug, ADR-029's Fujii/Cochrane cascade, ADR-045's fabricated-model catch): the result is **mixed, not clean**.
   - **Kathopoulis et al. 2022** (PMC9504364) does hedge, substantially: "the transfusion rate and the hemoglobin change were not improved with TXA... limits the clinical significance"; "the number of eligible studies is limited as well as the number of the included patients, which precludes reaching safe conclusions"; "more RCTs are necessary to strengthen the evidence of this systematic review and meta-analysis." This is exactly the kind of caveat the new rule targets.
   - **Baradwan et al. 2022** shows no comparable hedge in its abstract ("overall low risk of bias" is a positive quality signal, not a caveat) -- but its full Discussion/Limitations text is paywalled and was **not independently verified**. This is a real gap in the check, stated plainly rather than resolved by guessing.
   - **Conclusion:** the author's instinct that item 1 is clean is not confirmed by this check. RB-0001's current verified gold claim ("Two subsequent independent syntheses found that tranexamic acid reduces blood loss in open myomectomy by approximately 225 mL.") states the figure flatly, with no hedge, while at least one of its two sources explicitly hedges. Whether RB-0001's gold claim should be revised is the author's call, per ADR-040 (claim text is never assistant-rewritten) -- not decided or implemented here, only reported.

3. **Proposed scoring-axis design (NOT implemented -- for approval only).** The author's instinct is that this should not be folded into `correctness`'s three-way `correct`/`incorrect`/`refused` label, because currency and calibration are different failure modes with different fixes: a model that says "72%" flatly got the currency right (no reliance on the retracted 82%/67%/64% figures) and the calibration wrong (no acknowledgment that the current evidence is observational and RCT-pending). Two options considered:

   - **Option A (recommended): a new item-level marker plus a new, conditionally-scored `EvaluationResult` field.** Add a boolean to `Claim` (e.g. `carries_evidence_strength_caveat: bool = False`) set at annotation time whenever the claim's own text states a limitation on the strength of its evidence -- this is a property of the claim as written, checkable by reading it, not a judgment call made at scoring time. Add a new field to `EvaluationResult`, e.g. `calibration: Calibration | None`, gated by a `model_validator` the same way `m3_label` is gated by `condition` (schemas.py:413-427): required (non-null) iff `gold_claim.carries_evidence_strength_caveat` is `True` **and** `correctness == Correctness.CORRECT` (calibration is only assessable once the model has actually landed on the current answer -- nothing to calibrate if it didn't); `None` otherwise. `Calibration` would be a `StrEnum` with something like `CALIBRATED` (states the figure and reflects the source's own stated limitation) / `OVERCONFIDENT` (states the figure flatly, no limitation) / `OVER_HEDGED` (introduces uncertainty the source doesn't itself express -- included so the axis penalizes miscalibration in both directions, not just false confidence).
   - **Option B (rejected): fold into `correctness` as a fourth value** (e.g. `correct_overconfident`). Rejected because it collapses two independent failure modes into one ordinal label, loses the ability to report M1/M2-style rates for calibration on its own, and contradicts the author's explicit read that these are separate problems needing separate fixes.

   This also connects to the project protocol ("capture model confidence now, even though paper 1 doesn't use it") -- `ModelResponse.verbalised_confidence` and `logprobs` already exist for exactly this reason. The new axis would be the *scored* counterpart: where `verbalised_confidence` captures what the model said about its own certainty, `calibration` would score whether that certainty (or its absence) matched what the evidence actually supports.

   **Not implemented pending approval:** no schema change has been made. If approved, the change touches `schemas.py` (`Claim`, `EvaluationResult`, a new `Calibration` enum), `docs/ANNOTATION.md` (when to set the new `Claim` flag), and the eval module once it exists (not yet built, per `docs/STATUS.md`).

**Consequences:** `docs/ANNOTATION.md` gets the standing rule from (1) as a formal requirement. RB-0001's possible revision is left open, pending the author's decision, not silently resolved either way. The scoring-axis design in (3) is a proposal only; `schemas.py` is unchanged by this ADR.

---

## ADR-053: RB-0002 verified -- second item, first in Obstetrics/Gynecology; a `valid_from` discrepancy caught and corrected, not silently resolved

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** RB-0002 is drawn from RW record 6402 / PMID 28446937 (Ciebiera et al., "Laparoscopic isthmocele treatment - single center experience," retracted 2017 for data concerns and a limited-information notice). The author wrote both claims directly, per ADR-040, and specified `trap_claim` dates as "valid_from 2017, valid_until 2017 (retraction year)."

**Discrepancy found and flagged, not silently resolved either way:** the paper's actual original publication date, confirmed via both DuckDB's ingested Retraction Watch record and the verified-candidates CSV, is **2016-07-10**, not 2017. The retraction date is 2017-06-23, which does match the author's "2017." The author's stated `valid_from` appears to have used the retraction year for both bounds rather than the true original-publication year. Per this project's standing practice of surfacing factual discrepancies rather than either blindly using an incorrect stated value or silently substituting a corrected one without comment, `trap_claim.valid_from` is recorded as the verified **2016-07-10**, with this correction stated here for the author's review rather than assumed acceptable.

**Final text, author-written:**

> **trap_claim:** "Laparoscopic isthmocele treatment resolves abnormal bleeding in 82% of patients, relieves pain in 67%, and results in successful pregnancy in 64% of patients with secondary infertility." (`valid_from` 2016-07-10, `valid_until` 2017-06-23)
>
> **gold_claim:** "This study was retracted in 2017 and its results should not be cited. A larger 2025 systematic review of 866 women found a live birth rate of approximately 72% following laparoscopic isthmocele repair in women with a history of infertility. That review notes the evidence remains observational and calls for randomised controlled trials." (`valid_from` 2025-04-15, `valid_until` null)

The trap claim's percentages (82%, 67%, 64%) are rounded but accurate against the retracted paper's verbatim abstract (9/11 = 81.8%, 4/6 = 66.6%, 7/11 = 63.6%), confirmed via a direct PubMed EFetch pull rather than the abstract paraphrase used in `results/item2_raw_material.md`'s first draft. The gold claim's hedge ("remains observational and calls for randomised controlled trials") is the first application of ADR-052's new standing rule -- the Vidal et al. 2025 review (PMID 40303644, 17 studies, 866 women, 72% live birth rate in the infertility-history subgroup) states this limitation of its own evidence explicitly, and the gold claim now carries it.

**Decision:** `SupersededSource` `rw-6402` and `BenchmarkItem` `RB-0002` are appended (not edited-in-place, since neither existed before), `evidence_level=primary_study` (single-center case series, 16 patients), `notice_specificity=bare` (notice text confirmed present but uninformative: "[This retracts the article DOI: 10.5114/wiitm.2017.66025.]"), `claim_type=clinical_observational`, `provenance=verified`, with an `Annotation` record. Round-trip validated (`load_items`/`load_sources`), full test suite (142 tests) and `ruff check`/`ruff format --check` clean.

**Consequences:** This is the first Obstetrics/Gynecology item, giving the domain-comparison question (ADR-047's imbalance concern) its first real second data point alongside RB-0001 (Pharmacology). Retrieval ceiling is re-run with both items present (see `results/retrieval_ceiling_report.json`).

---

## ADR-054: RB-0002's documents were missing from the frozen corpus -- appended incrementally per ADR-048's own reserved mechanism, hash recomputed, retrieval ceiling now real for domain 2

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Re-running the retrieval-ceiling measurement (ADR-053's stated next step) with RB-0002 present returned `trap_source_rank: null, notice_rank: null` for it -- not a bad rank, an absent one. Checking the index manifest directly confirmed only `rw-17019`'s (RB-0001's) 4 documents were present; the corpus was frozen 2026-08-09 (ADR-048), before RB-0002 existed, and nothing had since added its documents. Reporting the domain-comparison numbers as they stood (Obstetrics/Gynecology at 0% recall for every k) would have been a misleading artifact of a missing index entry, not a retrieval-difficulty finding, so the report was left uncommitted and this was surfaced to the author rather than papered over.

ADR-048 already reserved exactly this path: "New benchmark items annotated after the freeze are inserted individually via `docs/CORPUS_PLAN.md` section 4's exactly-once mechanism, not folded into a rebuild" -- this is that mechanism's first real use, not a new policy decision. The one open question was *how* to re-hash, since the original freeze hash was produced by a script that was never committed to `src/`.

**Decision:** Author confirmed: append incrementally and re-hash (not a full ~10.8-hour rebuild, not leaving the corpus untouched).

1. `corpus/embed_index.py` gained `add_documents()` -- embeds only the new rows and calls `index.add()` on the existing on-disk `IndexFlatIP`, then rewrites the manifest with the new entries appended. Existing vectors are untouched (tested: `test_add_documents_appends_without_disturbing_existing_vectors`).
2. `corpus/freeze.py` is new -- `content_sha256()`, a permanent, committed replacement for the original one-off hashing script, over the sorted `(pmid, abstract)` pairs of the full corpus. This closes a real reproducibility gap (the original hash could not have been regenerated by anyone, including this project, before now) but means the recomputed hash is **not claimed to be byte-identical in method to the original** -- only conceptually equivalent (same sorted-pairs approach). Tested (`tests/test_freeze.py`): order-independent, sensitive to additions and to text changes.
3. Three rows appended to `data/interim/corpus/assembled_corpus.jsonl` and embedded: `rw-6402` as `benchmark_source` (PMID 28446937), `rw-6402-notice` as `supersession_notice`, and the Vidal et al. 2025 review as `superseding_evidence` (PMID 40303644, matching the pattern already used for RB-0001's two evidence entries).
4. `CORPUS_FREEZE.json` updated in place: `record_count` 400,542 -> 400,545; `content_sha256` `6af1f70f...42cf59` -> `6dac0cda...aedf873`; `domain_counts["(HSC) Medicine - Obstetrics/Gynecology"]` 12,107 -> 12,110; `corpus_role_counts` incremented for the three new rows. A new `update_history` array records this event (date, reason, before/after counts and hashes) rather than silently overwriting the original freeze fields -- the original `freeze_date`/`freeze_datetime_utc` are left untouched as the v1 record.

**Result -- retrieval ceiling, now real for both domains (n=1 each, still not statistically meaningful, but no longer an indexing artifact):**

| Item | Domain | trap_source_rank | notice_rank | rank_gap |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| RB-0001 | Pharmacology | 1 | 2 | 1 |
| RB-0002 | Obstetrics/Gynecology | 1 | 2 | 1 |

Identical pattern in the second domain: trap source ranks 1st, notice 2nd, comfortably inside `PRACTICAL_K=10`. This replicates ADR-050's two-prediction split once more -- ordering holds (`notice_ranks_worse_fraction=1.0` in both domains), practical invisibility does not (`notice_invisible_in_practice_fraction=0.0` in both) -- and is the first evidence that the pattern isn't an artifact of Pharmacology's specific corpus density. Still n=1 per domain; not a claim that the pattern is general.

**Consequences:** `results/retrieval_ceiling_report.json` now reflects both items against the updated index and is committed. `docs/CORPUS_PLAN.md`'s freeze-and-hash description should note that `corpus/freeze.py` is the authoritative, reproducible hashing implementation going forward, not a one-off step. This is not corpus v2 under ADR-046's policy -- the background pool is unchanged, only benchmark documents were added, exactly the case ADR-048 exempted from that label.

---

## ADR-055: Gold-claim caveats are attributed to the specific source that states them, never asserted in the benchmark's own voice -- general rule, applied first to RB-0001's Baradwan gap

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-052 established that a gold claim must carry an evidence-strength caveat when the superseding source states one. Deciding what to do about RB-0001 -- where one of two sources (Kathopoulis 2022) hedges and the other (Baradwan 2022) could not be fully verified -- exposed an unstated assumption in how ADR-052 was going to be applied: writing a single generic hedge over both sources would assert something about Baradwan that was never checked, which is exactly the failure ADR-034 already prohibits on the notice-mechanism side (asserting what a source doesn't itself state). The author's ruling: do not add a caveat we haven't confirmed source-by-source. If only one of several sources hedges, the gold claim says so specifically, attributed to that source -- not a blended, unattributed caveat that reads as if it covers all of them.

**Decision:** Gold claims never state an evidence-strength caveat in the benchmark's own voice. Every caveat is attributed to the specific source that made it (e.g. "one of the two syntheses notes X," not "the evidence is limited"). This extends ADR-034's existing discipline -- report what sources say, do not adjudicate or blend across them -- from notice mechanisms to evidence-strength caveats, the same rule applied to a second kind of content.

**RB-0001's Baradwan gap, resolved as far as it can be:** checked five independent legitimate channels for Baradwan et al. 2022's full text -- Unpaywall (`is_oa: false`), Semantic Scholar (`openAccessPdf: null`), Europe PMC (`isOpenAccess: N`, no full-text URL), PubMed's own record (abstract only), and ScienceDirect directly (403 Forbidden, consistent with the earlier ResearchGate-mirror 403 found when RB-0001 was first checked). Its January 2025 corrigendum (PMID 39603052, DOI 10.1016/j.ejogrb.2024.10.055) was also checked and carries no body text in either PubMed's or Europe PMC's record ("No abstract available"). **Confirmed inaccessible, not merely unchecked** -- this is the outcome to record, not a gap to leave open indefinitely. Baradwan's abstract itself states no comparable hedge ("overall low risk of bias"), which is what is available to cite; whether its Discussion/Limitations section says more can no longer be verified through this project's ordinary channels.

**What this means for RB-0001's revision:** the material available to write from is: Kathopoulis 2022 hedges, quoted and attributable to it by name; Baradwan 2022's full text is inaccessible, its abstract shows no comparable hedge, and this asymmetry itself is stated plainly rather than smoothed into a shared caveat. Per ADR-040, the actual revised `gold_claim.text` must be author-written -- this ADR records the rule and the verified material, not the final sentence. Follow-up ADR to record once the author supplies the revised text (see `docs/STATUS.md`).

**Consequences:** `docs/ANNOTATION.md`'s evidence-strength-caveat section (added under ADR-052) is amended to state the attribution requirement explicitly. This rule applies to every future item with multiple evidence sources, not just RB-0001 -- a caveat from one source among several is never generalized to "the evidence."

---

## ADR-056: Calibration scoring axis approved and implemented -- simplified from ADR-052's proposal, single field, human-judged, flagged as the scheme's most subjective

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-052 proposed a scoring axis for whether a model's stated confidence matches the strength of current evidence, separate from `correctness`, and asked for approval before implementing. The author approved with two simplifications.

**Decision:**

1. **No `Claim.carries_evidence_strength_caveat` boolean.** ADR-052's proposal would have added a marker field to `Claim` to gate when calibration is scored. Rejected: it duplicates information already recoverable from the gold claim text and `Evidence` records, and every redundant field is a chance for the two to silently disagree. `calibration` is simply optional (`Calibration | None`, default `None`) with no schema-level gating validator -- unlike `m3_label`/`notice_retrieved`, which are gated on `condition` because those really are structurally determined by it. Calibration's applicability is an annotator judgment call each time, not a fact derivable from another field.

2. **One field, three values, human-scored, not computed.** `EvaluationResult.calibration: Calibration | None` -- `Calibration` is a new `StrEnum`: `APPROPRIATE`, `OVERCONFIDENT` (states a finding more firmly than the gold claim's evidence supports), `UNDERCONFIDENT` (hedges where the gold claim's evidence is actually solid). Scored by the human annotator against the gold claim's own stated strength, never the annotator's personal clinical judgment of how strong the evidence "really" is -- `docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 4.1 states this explicitly, since it is the one place this axis could quietly drift into re-litigating the medicine instead of grading the answer.

3. **Reported separately from `correctness` throughout, never collapsed into it** -- unchanged from ADR-052's rationale: a model can be current and overconfident, or outdated and appropriately hedged, and folding the two into one label would lose exactly the distinction that motivated adding this axis.

**Honesty flag, recorded now rather than discovered in October:** calibration is the most subjective judgment in this annotation scheme. `correctness` and the retraction-citation check (M1) are close to mechanical -- did the answer rely on a superseded source, checkable against a fact. Calibration requires reading tone and comparing it to a written standard, a judgment call every time. This means it needs inter-annotator agreement more than any other field in the scheme (`docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 6 updated accordingly). A second annotator is not yet secured (unchanged from ADR-009/section 6's existing status). If one is not found before results are written up, `calibration` is reported single-annotated with that limitation stated plainly in the paper -- not silently presented with the same evidentiary weight as the mechanical axes.

**Consequences:** `schemas.py` gains `Calibration` (StrEnum) and `EvaluationResult.calibration` (optional field, no validator coupling). `docs/ANNOTATION.md` gains section 4.1 (scoring guidance) and an addition to section 6 (the IAA-need flag). Tested: default-`None` and accepted-value cases in `tests/test_schemas.py`. No change to `Claim` or `Evidence`.

---

## ADR-057: `claim_polarity` -- retracted negative findings are a distinct failure mode from retracted positive ones, stratified rather than pooled; prediction registered before any data exists

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Item 15 (candidate, record 7954, Rigby/Draper ultrasound for trapezius trigger points) surfaced something the benchmark's schema had no way to represent: the retracted paper's *own* conclusion was already negative -- "the improvement is attributed to a placebo effect," no significant difference between active and sham ultrasound on any outcome. Every item drafted so far (RB-0001, RB-0002) has a trap claim asserting an effect exists. This one's trap claim, if drafted naively as "ultrasound reduces trigger-point pain," would misrepresent even the retracted paper's own conclusion, not just what current evidence says -- the paper's finding to be superseded is itself a null result.

**Decision:** Add `ClaimPolarity` (`positive` / `negative`) and `BenchmarkItem.claim_polarity`, human-annotated from the retracted source's own conclusion. A model relying on a retracted **positive** finding wrongly recommends a treatment; a model relying on a retracted **negative** finding wrongly discourages one. Both are citing superseded evidence (M1 catches both identically), but they are different clinical failure modes with different real-world consequences, and pooling them together would hide that distinction. Results (M1/M2/M3, and the new `calibration` axis) are reported **stratified by `claim_polarity` throughout**, not pooled into a single "did it cite the retracted source" number.

**Pre-registered prediction, recorded now, before any polarity-stratified data exists** -- same discipline as ADR-032, ADR-039, ADR-043, ADR-049/050: **models will be more likely to reproduce retracted negative findings than retracted positive ones.** Reasoning stated in advance so it's checkable against what was expected: negative findings attract less follow-up literature written specifically to contradict them (a null result doesn't provoke the same volume of "we found the opposite" responses a flashy positive result does), and are less likely to receive the kind of public correction/attention a wrongly-positive claim does. If this holds, it is a genuinely new, non-obvious finding obtainable from this benchmark; if it doesn't, that is reported too, exactly as this project's other pre-registered predictions are.

**Consequences:** `schemas.py` gains `ClaimPolarity` and `BenchmarkItem.claim_polarity` (required, no default -- every item states this explicitly). Existing items (RB-0001, RB-0002) backfilled as `positive` (both trap claims assert an effect). `docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 4's field list gains this. Item 15's raw material is annotated with the expected polarity (`negative`) for whoever drafts it; the actual `BenchmarkItem` is not created in this ADR. `paper/paper.md`'s results outline should note this as a stratification variable, not a covariate to control away.

---

## ADR-058: Circular provenance is a real hazard in this benchmark's own construction -- a standing independence-verification step, and `Evidence.independence_verified`

**Status:** Accepted
