**Context:** While deciding item 16's (candidate, record 18340, pregnancy oral health) eligibility, a search summary attributed specific OHIP-14 figures to "a large study from Kashmir" in a way that could plausibly have been describing the *retracted paper's own data* rather than an independent source -- not resolved either way in that pass. This exposed a hazard specific to how this benchmark is built: a superseding source found via literature search can itself have cited, or derived its reported figure from, the very paper being retracted, without that being obvious from an abstract or a search summary. If that happens unnoticed, the retracted claim ends up inside the benchmark's own gold answer -- the exact failure this benchmark exists to measure, occurring inside the annotation process that is supposed to be the ground truth.

**Decision:** A standing verification step, applying to every `Evidence` entry from here forward: before writing a gold claim from a source, confirm that source's reference list or included-studies table does **not** include the retracted paper. `Evidence.independence_verified: bool` records the outcome -- `true` only after this check is actually performed and comes back clean; `false` if the check either finds the retracted paper present (that evidence should not be used) or if the source's own text could not be reached to check at all. No default: every entry must state this explicitly, the same discipline as `notice_text_verified` on `SupersededSource` (ADR-042).

**Checked now, retroactively, for the three existing `Evidence` entries (RB-0001, RB-0002):**

- **Kathopoulis et al. 2022** (RB-0001) -- **verified independent.** Its 4 included RCTs (Abdul 2019, Opoku-Anane 2020, Shady 2018, Caglar 2008) are all primary trials; the retracted meta-analysis (PMID 28591045) is neither among them nor in its reference list.
- **Baradwan et al. 2022** (RB-0001) -- **not verified, and cannot be**, per ADR-055: full text confirmed inaccessible across 5 legitimate channels. `independence_verified=false` records this honestly as unchecked, not as a finding that it fails.
- **Vidal et al. 2025** (RB-0002) -- **verified independent.** Its 17 included studies (Li 2014 through Vissers 2023) do not include the retracted paper (Ciebiera et al., PMID 28446937), which also does not appear in its reference list.

**Consequences:** `schemas.py`'s `Evidence` gains `independence_verified: bool` (required). `docs/ANNOTATION.md` gains the standing verification step. `data/benchmark/items.jsonl` backfilled for all three existing evidence entries with the checks above. `paper/paper.md`'s Methods section should name this as a specific, identified hazard of this benchmark's construction method and the control applied -- a real methodological point for reviewers, not just an internal bookkeeping field.

---

## ADR-059: RB-0001's gold claim revised -- Kathopoulis's hedge attributed by name, Baradwan's inaccessibility recorded on the item, not smoothed over

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-052 found RB-0001's original gold claim didn't reflect that one of its two sources hedges; ADR-055 established that any such caveat must be attributed to its specific source, never asserted generically, and confirmed Baradwan's full text is genuinely inaccessible. Per ADR-040, the actual revision had to be author-written -- this records the final text now supplied.

**Final text, author-written:**

> **gold_claim:** "This meta-analysis was retracted in January 2018 and its results should not be cited. Two subsequent independent syntheses found that tranexamic acid reduces blood loss in open myomectomy by approximately 225 mL. Kathopoulis et al. 2022 describe their finding as indicating that intravenous tranexamic acid may reduce intraoperative blood loss, rather than stating it as established."

The caveat is attributed to Kathopoulis specifically ("Kathopoulis et al. 2022 describe..."), not asserted as a property of "the evidence" generally -- Baradwan's effect-size figure is folded into the shared "~225 mL" statement without a caveat attached to it, since Baradwan's own hedging status (if any) remains unverified, not because it was checked and found clean. This is the intended reading under ADR-055: attribute what you checked, don't extend it to what you didn't.

**Decision:** `RB-0001.gold_claim.text` updated in place; a new `Annotation` entry recorded (`provenance=verified`, noting the revision and its basis). `Evidence.independence_verified` backfilled per ADR-058 (Kathopoulis `true`, Baradwan `false` with the inaccessibility reason in its `note`). Round-trip validated; `provenance` remains `verified` (this is a correction within an already-verified item, not a demotion).

**Consequences:** RB-0001 is now the concrete referent for the attribution rule (ADR-055) the same way it already is for gold-claim scoping (ADR-034/044) and plain claim style (ADR-051) -- a real worked example future items can be checked against, not just an abstract statement of the rule.

---

## ADR-060: RB-0003 verified -- a `superseded_without_current_answer` item where adjacent authoritative guidance exists but doesn't answer the specific question asked; a sub-shape distinction proposed, not implemented; and a noted resonance with the benchmark's own subject matter

**Status:** Accepted (RB-0003's record); sub-shape design proposed, implementation deferred pending approval.

**Context:** RB-0003 (record 7837, Chu et al. 2016, retracted 2017) measured how much adjuvant radiation therapy use changed among women 70+ with stage I ER-positive breast cancer after the CALGB 9343 trial -- a practice-pattern/uptake claim, not a treatment-efficacy claim. No published analysis has re-measured this specific figure since the retraction. NCCN guidelines do exist and are authoritative (Category 1 recommendation, based on CALGB 9343 and PRIME II) -- but NCCN states what clinicians *should* do, not how much they actually changed what they *did*. The author's judgment: these are different questions, so the item is `superseded_without_current_answer`, per ADR-043.

**The judgment call worth recording, in the author's own reasoning:** this is a distinct sub-shape from the Fujii-type case (record 1471, item 5) where nothing authoritative exists on any adjacent question either -- there, a model retrieving broadly finds nothing solid to lean on. Here, a model retrieving broadly is likely to *find NCCN* -- real, authoritative, directly on the underlying clinical topic -- and could easily answer confidently and correctly-sounding using it, without the mismatch ever surfacing: NCCN doesn't actually answer the practice-pattern question posed. That is a subtler, and per the author's read, probably more common failure than citing a retracted number outright: not absence of evidence, but presence of adjacent-but-non-answering evidence that looks like it should settle the question.

**Proposed design (NOT implemented -- for approval only):** a new field, gated on `item_type == SUPERSEDED_WITHOUT_CURRENT_ANSWER`, the same conditional-field pattern already used for `m3_label`/`notice_retrieved` on `EvaluationResult` (schemas.py:411-425). A `NoAnswerReason` `StrEnum` with two values:

- `NO_ADJACENT_EVIDENCE` -- the Fujii-type case: no authoritative source addresses the underlying clinical question at all, not just the specific figure.
- `ADJACENT_EVIDENCE_SCOPE_MISMATCH` -- the RB-0003 case: authoritative evidence exists and addresses the underlying clinical decision, but not the specific question asked (different question, different metric, different population slice, etc.).

This makes the sub-shape a measured, reportable distribution the same way `RejectionReason` already makes rejection reasons measured (ADR-036/037) rather than an undifferentiated bucket -- and it matters for interpretation: `NO_ADJACENT_EVIDENCE` items test whether a model recognizes the absence of an answer; `ADJACENT_EVIDENCE_SCOPE_MISMATCH` items test something closer to relevance discrimination, whether a model notices that what it found doesn't actually answer what was asked. Two different capabilities, currently invisible inside one `item_type` value. **Not implemented pending approval** -- if approved, touches `schemas.py` (new enum, new gated field on `BenchmarkItem`), `docs/ANNOTATION.md` (when to set each value), and RB-0003 itself (would be backfilled as `ADJACENT_EVIDENCE_SCOPE_MISMATCH`).

**A noted resonance, for the paper's discussion, not acted on further here:** RB-0003's own trap claim is itself a measurement of evidence uptake -- how slowly clinical practice changed after a practice-changing trial (2.95% over roughly a decade). That is the same phenomenon this benchmark studies in models (does a system track that a source of record has been superseded), observed instead in humans. Worth a sentence in `paper/paper.md`'s discussion section once it's drafted -- the benchmark's subject and one of its own items' subject are the same question asked of two different kinds of system, which is a nice, honest thing to say plainly rather than something to force into a bigger claim than it supports.

**Decision:** `SupersededSource` `rw-7837` and `BenchmarkItem` `RB-0003` appended, `evidence_level=primary_study` (NCDB registry analysis, not a synthesis), `notice_specificity=unverifiable` (four independent routes tried against the retraction notice's primary text at raw-material stage; only a Retraction Watch secondhand quote available), `claim_type=clinical_observational`, `item_type=superseded_without_current_answer`, `claim_polarity=positive`, `provenance=verified`. The trap claim's `valid_from`/`valid_until` (2016-04, 2017-05) use the paper's and notice's print-issue months, confirmed via direct PubMed lookup to match exactly (epub-ahead-of-print dates, 2016-01-27 and 2017-04-24, are the `SupersededSource`'s own `original_date`/`supersession_date` instead) -- checked, not assumed, and no discrepancy this time. The NCCN evidence entry (`Evidence`, JNCCN Guidelines Insights, PMID 28404755) has `independence_verified=false`: its full text is paywalled and its reference list could not be checked against `rw-7837`, per ADR-058's standing rule -- not assumed clean just because NCCN guidelines structurally cite outcomes trials rather than utilization studies. Round-trip validated; 152 tests, ruff clean.

**Consequences:** This is the benchmark's third item and the first `superseded_without_current_answer` example, giving ADR-043's "target ~10-15% of the benchmark" prediction its first real data point. `docs/STATUS.md` and the candidate count should be updated to reflect 3 annotated items and 12 remaining prepared candidates.

---

## ADR-061: RB-0004 verified -- a cascading-retraction item with no current pediatric answer; questions must not presuppose the retracted finding's direction, and ADR-039's own worked example violates the rule it now sits next to

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** RB-0004 (record 1471, Fujii Y et al., ramosetron vs. granisetron for pediatric strabismus-surgery vomiting, retracted 2016 as part of the ~180-paper Fujii fabrication wave) is the fourth item and second `superseded_without_current_answer` example. The current-evidence chain here is unusually deep: the fabricated trial was pooled into a Cochrane review (`CD004125.pub2`, itself in this project's 364-candidate pool as a separate record); that review was withdrawn once 67 of 737 included studies proved retracted; its rebuild (Weibel et al.) covers adults only; the best pediatric-specific source now available (Pankiv et al., BMJ Open 2024, PMID 38388499, an umbrella review) discusses the relevant drug class but provides no pooled ramosetron-vs-granisetron comparison and flags 9 of its own included reviews as AMSTAR-2 "critically low confidence." Three simultaneous difficulties for a model to represent honestly at once: the trap claim is void, no replacement exists for this population, and what little evidence does exist is itself quality-flagged -- carried into the gold claim with the caveat attributed specifically to the BMJ Open review, per ADR-055.

**Checked, not assumed, per ADR-058:** the BMJ Open review's full text was read directly and confirmed to neither cite nor mention Fujii Y or the retracted paper (PMID 11371485) anywhere -- `independence_verified=true` on this basis, an actual check rather than the structural-reasoning shortcut ADR-060 explicitly declined to rely on for RB-0003's NCCN citation.

**New rule: questions must not presuppose the direction of the retracted finding.** Caught while drafting this item: a first-draft question, "how much more effective is ramosetron [than granisetron]," already asserts the trap claim's own direction before any evidence is retrieved -- the model is being prompted toward the retracted conclusion by the question itself, not asked to determine the relationship independently. The revised question, "how does ramosetron compare with granisetron," asks for a comparison without asserting which way it points. `docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 4 gains this as a formal rule (new paragraph, "Question neutrality").

**This rule sits in direct tension with ADR-039's own worked example, and that tension is recorded rather than smoothed over:** ADR-039 established quantitative question form and gave "How much does X reduce/increase Y?" as the pattern to follow -- which is itself a presupposing form, asserting a direction (reduce/increase) before any evidence is cited. That example predates this rule (drafted for item 1, before this failure mode was noticed) and should not be copied verbatim into new items; `docs/ANNOTATION.md` now says so explicitly next to the original example rather than quietly editing ADR-039's own historical text.

**Audit of RB-0001 through RB-0003 against this rule, performed now, not deferred -- reported, not fixed:**

- **RB-0001**: "How much does tranexamic acid reduce blood loss during open myomectomy?" -- **presupposes the direction.** "Reduce" asserts the trap claim's own conclusion (TXA reduces blood loss) before any evidence is cited. Same flaw as this item's first draft, uncaught until now.
- **RB-0002**: "How effective is laparoscopic isthmocele repair for restoring fertility?" -- **milder, related version.** Does not embed a specific numeric direction, but "how effective" presupposes the treatment has some positive degree of effectiveness rather than neutrally asking about the relationship (e.g., "what is the relationship between laparoscopic isthmocele repair and fertility outcomes?"). Less severe than RB-0001/RB-0003's pattern, still a presupposition.
- **RB-0003**: "How much did adjuvant radiation therapy use decline among women over 70 with stage I ER-positive breast cancer after the CALGB 9343 trial?" -- **presupposes the direction**, same pattern as RB-0001: "decline" asserts the trap claim's own conclusion (usage declined) before any evidence is cited.

**Not fixed here, per instruction.** Whether and how to revise these three questions -- and whether a revision to an already-`verified` item's `question` field needs the same author-written discipline as `trap_claim`/`gold_claim` under ADR-040 -- is the author's call, to be made and recorded separately.

**Consequences:** `docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 4 gains the question-neutrality rule, flagging ADR-039's own example as superseded in practice (not in text -- ADR-039 itself is not edited, per this log's append-only policy). RB-0001-0003's `question` fields are left as-is pending the author's decision. RB-0004 itself already conforms (drafted after the flaw was caught). This is the benchmark's fourth item; `docs/STATUS.md` should be updated to reflect 4 annotated items and 11 remaining prepared candidates.

---

## ADR-062: The operative test for question neutrality -- direction/magnitude vs. topic; RB-0001 and RB-0003 revised, RB-0002 left as-is

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-061 stated the question-neutrality rule as "must not presuppose the direction of the retracted finding," and its own audit flagged RB-0002's "how effective is..." phrasing as a "milder, related version" of the same flaw, without a way to say precisely why it's milder or whether it actually needs fixing. "Presupposes something" turned out to be too broad a test to apply consistently -- nearly any well-formed question presupposes its own topic.

**Decision -- the operative test:** does the question presuppose a *specific direction or magnitude* of the finding, or only the *topic* the finding is about? Presupposing direction/magnitude is leading -- "how much does X reduce Y" already agrees with a trap claim that asserts a reduction, before any evidence is retrieved. Presupposing only topic is not leading and is how questions are normally asked -- "how effective is X" presupposes the question concerns X's effectiveness, not which way the answer points, and a clinician asking in practice does not know in advance whether a treatment helps, only that they want to know whether it does.

Applying this test to the three earlier items:

- **RB-0001**, "How much does tranexamic acid reduce blood loss...": presupposes direction (reduce). **Revised** to "What effect does tranexamic acid have on blood loss during open myomectomy?"
- **RB-0003**, "How much did adjuvant radiation therapy use decline...": presupposes direction (decline). **Revised** to "How did adjuvant radiation therapy use change among women over 70 with stage I ER-positive breast cancer after the CALGB 9343 trial?"
- **RB-0002**, "How effective is laparoscopic isthmocele repair...": presupposes only topic (effectiveness), not a direction or magnitude. **Left as-is** -- this is the test that distinguishes it from the other two, not a judgment call made case by case.

Both revisions are author-written (ADR-040 applies to `question` the same way it applies to `trap_claim`/`gold_claim`) and recorded as dated `Annotation` entries on the affected items, not silent overwrites -- the prior wording remains visible in each item's annotation history, not erased.

**A revised question invalidates the item's stored retrieval-ceiling rank, and this generalizes beyond this specific revision.** `corpus/retrieval_ceiling.py` embeds the question text as the query; changing it changes what gets retrieved and re-ranked. RB-0001's and RB-0003's `results/retrieval_ceiling_report.json` entries (both measured under their old question text) are now stale and must be re-measured before being trusted again. `docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 4 now states this as a standing rule -- re-run the ceiling for any item whose question is revised, not just for newly annotated items -- so a future session doesn't revise a question and leave a stale, silently-wrong rank behind.

**Consequences:** `docs/ANNOTATION.md`'s question-neutrality paragraph is rewritten around this operative test, replacing the broader "presupposes something" framing. RB-0001 and RB-0003 updated in place with dated annotations; RB-0002 unchanged. Retrieval ceiling re-run for all four items as part of this same session's work (see the corpus-update entry following this ADR).

---

## ADR-063: `NoAnswerReason` implemented as proposed in ADR-060 -- no_adjacent_evidence vs. adjacent_evidence_exists, gated on item_type

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-060 proposed, without implementing, a field distinguishing two sub-shapes of `superseded_without_current_answer`: the Fujii-type case where nothing authoritative addresses the topic at all, versus RB-0003's case where authoritative evidence exists but doesn't answer the specific question asked. The author approved the design as proposed and asked for it to be built now rather than deferred, since the distinction tests two different model capabilities and collapsing them wastes both items.

**Decision:** `NoAnswerReason` (`StrEnum`): `NO_ADJACENT_EVIDENCE` / `ADJACENT_EVIDENCE_EXISTS`. `BenchmarkItem.no_answer_reason: NoAnswerReason | None`, gated by a `model_validator` the same way `m3_label`/`notice_retrieved` are gated on `EvaluationResult.condition` (schemas.py): required iff `item_type == SUPERSEDED_WITHOUT_CURRENT_ANSWER`, must be `None` otherwise. Two values to start, per instruction -- add more only when an item genuinely doesn't fit either, not speculatively ahead of need.

Backfilled on the two existing `superseded_without_current_answer` items:

- **RB-0003** (`ADJACENT_EVIDENCE_EXISTS`) -- NCCN guidelines authoritatively address the underlying clinical decision (whether to give radiation), just not the retracted paper's specific practice-pattern question (how much clinician behavior changed).
- **RB-0004** (`NO_ADJACENT_EVIDENCE`) -- the Fujii-type case: the entire evidence chain collapsed (fabricated trial -> withdrawn Cochrane review -> adults-only rebuild), leaving nothing authoritative on this pediatric comparison at all.

Both backfills recorded as dated `Annotation` entries, tested (`test_benchmark_item_requires_no_answer_reason_under_without_current_answer`, `test_benchmark_item_rejects_no_answer_reason_outside_without_current_answer`, `test_benchmark_item_accepts_no_answer_reason_under_without_current_answer`).

**Consequences:** `schemas.py` gains `NoAnswerReason` and the gated field; `docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 4's field list gains it. 155 tests, ruff clean. Once enough `superseded_without_current_answer` items exist, the split becomes a reportable distribution the same way `RejectionReason` already is -- not yet meaningful at n=2.

---

## ADR-064: Corpus updated for RB-0003/RB-0004; retrieval ceiling re-run across all four items -- consistent pattern across two domains, but the domain-imbalance question (ADR-047) is still untested

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** RB-0003's and RB-0004's documents were not yet in the frozen corpus (only RB-0002's were, from ADR-054); RB-0001's and RB-0003's stored ranks were additionally stale after ADR-062's question revisions. Both needed resolving before the retrieval ceiling could be trusted.

**Decision:** `add_documents()`/`content_sha256()` (ADR-054's mechanism) used again: 6 rows appended (benchmark source + notice + one evidence entry each for RB-0003 and RB-0004). Corpus: 400,545 -> 400,551 documents; hash `6dac0cda...` -> `3f7561a9...`; `corpus_role_counts` now `benchmark_source: 4, supersession_notice: 4, superseding_evidence: 5` (RB-0001 has two evidence entries, the other three items have one each). Recorded in `CORPUS_FREEZE.json`'s `update_history`, not a silent overwrite. Retrieval ceiling then re-run for all four items against the updated index and the current (post-ADR-062) question text.

**Result:**

| Item | Domain | trap_source_rank | notice_rank | rank_gap |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| RB-0001 | Pharmacology | 1 | 2 | 1 |
| RB-0002 | Obstetrics/Gynecology | 1 | 2 | 1 |
| RB-0003 | Obstetrics/Gynecology | 1 | 2 | 1 |
| RB-0004 | Pharmacology | 1 | 3 | 2 |

Every item's trap source ranks 1st. Every notice ranks worse but stays comfortably inside `PRACTICAL_K=10` (`notice_invisible_in_practice_fraction=0.0` in both domains). RB-0004's notice ranks 3rd rather than 2nd -- the first item where the gap is 2, not 1 -- plausibly because its notice text is the longest and most specific of the four (the full Fujii fabrication-and-institutional-investigation notice, quoted in ADR-061's context, versus the terser notices on the other three), giving it more surface area to compete with unrelated background documents on, though this is one data point and not a claim about why. Pharmacology (n=2): mean rank gap 1.5. Obstetrics/Gynecology (n=2): mean rank gap 1.0. Both domains show the identical qualitative pattern (ordering holds, practical invisibility does not) first seen at n=1 in ADR-050.

**What this result does NOT show, stated plainly rather than left to be assumed:** the author's framing for this measurement described it as covering "all three domains," since ADR-047's domain-imbalance question needed genuine cross-domain data. Checked against the actual persisted data before reporting: **all four items are in Pharmacology or Obstetrics/Gynecology. Zero items are in Rehabilitation/Therapy** -- the domain ADR-047 was specifically worried about, being ~27-fold larger than Obstetrics/Gynecology in background-document count. This table is a real, useful replication across two domains of comparable size, not yet a test of whether Rehabilitation's much larger competitor pool degrades retrieval. That question remains open until a Rehabilitation item exists.

**Consequences:** `results/retrieval_ceiling_report.json` committed with all four items. `docs/STATUS.md`'s domain-coverage claims should be corrected to reflect 2 of 3 domains represented, not 3. The Rehabilitation-imbalance question stays flagged as unresolved in `docs/STATUS.md`'s next-action list, not implicitly closed by this measurement.

---

## ADR-065: Item 6 rejected (abstract irretrievably overwritten); measured how common that failure mode is across the full candidate pool (6.3%, 95% CI [3.9%, 8.8%]); completes a three-way "content unreachable" pattern for the paper

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Item 6 (record 5685, Boldt J et al., "Six different hemofiltration devices for blood conservation in cardiac surgery," Ann Thorac Surg 1991) was flagged at raw-material stage: its original abstract is not merely hard to find, it has been overwritten in PubMed's own structured record -- the `<Abstract>` field contains only the retraction statement, confirmed directly against PubMed's own EFetch XML. No trap claim can be written from this without fabricating the content it would need to represent.

**Decision -- item 6 rejected.** Logged as `RejectedCandidate` (`rw-5685`, `RejectionReason.OTHER`), notes distinguishing this explicitly from `notice_specificity=unverifiable`/`bare` (which concern the notice) and `no_institutional_source` (which concerns current evidence) -- here it is the retracted paper's own original content that is gone from the record meant to preserve it, a third, distinct failure mode.

**Measured across all 364 candidates, per the author's request, with a validated classifier and a bootstrap CI -- not estimated from item 6 alone:**

| | n | proportion | 95% CI |
|---|---|---|---|
| `replaced_by_retraction_text` (by row) | 23/364 | 6.32% | [3.85%, 8.79%] |
| `replaced_by_retraction_text` (by unique PMID) | 17/296 | 5.74% | -- |
| `no_abstract` | 26/364 | 7.14% | -- |
| `original_retained` | 315/364 | 86.54% | -- |

Classifier (opening-phrase match, or short text + "retract" root + no structured-abstract markers) validated against 5 known cases (the four already-verified RB-0001-0004 sources, all `original_retained`; record 5685, `replaced_by_retraction_text`) before running at scale -- caught and fixed one miss during validation (the word-count threshold was initially too low for record 5685's own, longer-than-typical retraction statement). Spot-checked 8 of 23 `replaced` results and the 10 shortest `original_retained` results by hand afterward; all correctly classified. Full methodology and per-record classification: `results/abstract_overwrite_measurement.md`/`.json`.

**A finding along the way, not acted on further here:** checking PMIDs before fetching found only **296 unique PMIDs across the 364 candidate rows** -- 68 rows duplicate another row's PMID. This traces to Retraction Watch's own source data, not this project's pipeline, and has not been surfaced before now. Both row-level and unique-PMID proportions are reported above and agree closely, so it does not change this measurement's conclusion, but candidate-pool-wide counts elsewhere in this project (author-cluster analysis, domain tallies) may need to dedupe on PMID rather than `record_id` -- flagged as a follow-up, not investigated further in this session.

**This completes a three-way pattern of "the record fails to deliver the retracted content" worth stating in the paper's methods/discussion, not folded into a single "data quality" footnote:**

1. **Notice not machine-readable** (`notice_specificity = unverifiable`) -- the source paper's own content is intact; what's missing is a verifiable explanation for why it shouldn't be trusted.
2. **Content uninformative** (`notice_specificity = bare`) -- the notice is verifiable but says nothing substantive about the mechanism.
3. **Claim erased** (this ADR) -- the retracted paper's own abstract is gone, overwritten by retraction boilerplate in the very record meant to preserve it. ~1 in 16 candidates in this pool. A RAG system retrieving this record would not retrieve the trap claim at all -- a different, arguably safer outcome than retrieving the original claim, but one that makes the record useless for this benchmark's construction method (hence item 6's rejection).

**Consequences:** `data/benchmark/rejected_candidates.jsonl` gains `rw-5685`. `results/abstract_overwrite_measurement.{md,json}` committed; the raw per-PMID fetch cache lives in `data/interim/abstract_overwrite/` (gitignored, regenerable). `docs/STATUS.md`'s candidate count drops by one (item 6 no longer offerable). `paper/paper.md`'s methods section should name this three-way pattern explicitly once drafted.

---

## ADR-066: The 364-candidate pool is actually 296 unique candidates -- what the 68 duplicate rows are, and every figure computed against 364 recomputed

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-065's abstract-overwrite measurement found only 296 unique PMIDs among the 364 candidate rows and flagged it as a follow-up rather than resolving it. The author's instruction this session: do not leave it flagged -- every rate reported against 364 is wrong, recompute all of them, and determine what the duplication actually is, since "same article under two PMIDs" and "distinct Retraction Watch records pointing at one paper" have different implications.

**What the duplicates actually are, checked directly rather than assumed either way:** neither of the two hypotheses. **All 64 duplicate-PMID groups involve exactly one `record_id` appearing more than once in `tier1_candidates_final3_verified.csv` -- never two distinct record_ids sharing a PMID.** Every duplicated row is byte-identical to its sibling except for the `domain` column: 32 records are tagged both Obstetrics/Gynecology and Pharmacology; 26 both Pharmacology and Rehabilitation/Therapy; 2 both Obstetrics/Gynecology and Rehabilitation/Therapy; 4 all three. This is neither a Retraction Watch data-quality issue nor a citation/PMID-assignment error -- **it is this project's own candidate-pool construction** (`curate/candidates.py`'s per-domain MeSH export, ADR-021): a single retracted paper whose MeSH headings satisfy more than one domain's query gets exported once per matching domain, and the three per-domain CSVs were concatenated into `tier1_candidates_final3_verified.csv` without deduplicating on `record_id` across that concatenation.

This is itself a finding, not just a bug report: **cross-domain MeSH overlap in this retracted-clinical-trial pool is 64/296 = 21.6% of unique candidates** -- two orders of magnitude higher than the <0.1% overlap ADR-046 measured for the full ~400k-document background corpus using the same three MeSH queries. Retracted clinical trials cluster on topics (platelet function in critically ill patients, ovulation induction, cesarean-scar/pregnancy-adjacent pharmacology) that genuinely span this project's domain boundaries far more than the general literature does -- plausibly because the domains are procedural/specialty groupings (who treats the patient) rather than mechanism-of-action groupings, and a single trial's *subject* can legitimately belong to more than one specialty's literature.

**Corrected N is 296, not 364. Every figure below is recomputed against it; the original ADR entries are not edited (append-only), superseded here instead.**

| Figure | ADR | Old (n=364) | New (n=296) |
|---|---|---|---|
| PubMed tag clean match | ADR-028 | 336 (92.3%) | 271 (91.6%) |
| PubMed tag gap | ADR-028/030 | 27 (7.4%) | 24 (8.11%) |
| PubMed lookup error | ADR-028 | 1 (0.3%) | 1 (0.34%) |
| Gap rate, Wilson 95% CI | ADR-030 | 7.42%, [5.15%, 10.58%] | 8.11%, [5.51%, 11.78%] |
| Author-cluster share (overall) | ADR-031 | 87 (23.9%) | 87 (29.4%) |
| Abstract replaced by retraction text | ADR-065 | 23 (6.32%), 95% CI [3.85%, 8.79%] | 17 (5.74%), 95% CI [3.04%, 8.45%] |

**Why the numerators mostly held steady and only the denominator moved:** `curate/author_clusters.author_record_map` already keys by `record_id` in a `set`, so a name's record count was never double-counted even against the raw 364-row pool -- the 87-record author-cluster figure was already correct as a count; only the percentage (divided by the wrong 364) was wrong. The PubMed-tag and abstract-overwrite checks, by contrast, were run per-row rather than per-unique-record, so a handful of the 68 duplicated rows fell on the "gap" or "replaced" side and were counted twice -- their numerators shift slightly (27->24 gap, 23->17 replaced) in addition to the denominator.

**Author-cluster per-domain breakdown (ADR-031) is affected differently, and not fully resolved here.** Splitting 87 touched-records by domain requires assigning each of the 64 cross-listed records to exactly one domain, and there is no principled single answer -- a record legitimately matched more than one domain's MeSH criteria. Using "first-listed domain in the original per-domain export order" as one reasonable, documented convention (not a claimed-correct partition):

| Domain | Old share (n=364 basis) | New share (this convention) |
|---|---|---|
| Obstetrics/Gynecology | 48/119 = 40.3% | 48/119 = 40.3% (unchanged -- none of its cluster-touched records lost their Ob/Gyn tag under this convention) |
| Pharmacology | 44/156 = 28.2% | 24/120 = 20.0% |
| Rehabilitation/Therapy | 19/89 = 21.3% | 15/57 = 26.3% |

Flagged, not resolved: a different convention (e.g., "domain the record was actually annotated under, where known") would shift these further for the handful of cross-listed records already used as benchmark items (`rw-17019`/RB-0001 is Ob/Gyn+Pharmacology cross-listed, annotated as Pharmacology; `rw-7837`/RB-0003 is Ob/Gyn+Rehabilitation cross-listed, annotated as Ob/Gyn). The overall 29.4% figure is solid; the per-domain split is convention-dependent and should be presented as such if it reaches the paper.

**Not recomputed, flagged as a related but separate consideration:** ADR-021's five-domain funnel table (Pharmacology 156, Oncology 124, Obstetrics/Gynecology 119, Surgery 103, Rehabilitation/Therapy 89 -- the same per-domain MeSH+claim-type counts that became this pool) almost certainly has the same cross-domain-overlap property, since it was produced by the same unheduped per-domain export. It already fed a domain-selection decision that has since been acted on (ADR-026, three domains chosen) and reproducing the much larger upstream pool to recompute it is out of scope for this correction -- noted here so a future session doesn't treat that table as a partition either.

**Consequences:** `results/author_cluster_analysis.md` and `results/abstract_overwrite_measurement.md` updated in place with corrected headline figures and a note pointing to this ADR, rather than left stale while only this entry has the truth. `docs/STATUS.md`'s candidate-pool description corrected to 296 unique (364 rows, 68 domain-duplicated). Any future candidate-pool statistic should compute against `df.drop_duplicates(subset="record_id")`, not the raw CSV -- worth adding as a one-line convention note wherever the pool is loaded, though not enforced in code by this ADR.

---

## ADR-067: Rehabilitation-only pre-screen -- 1 of 30 candidates survived a three-check screen (abstract intact, notice reachable, eligibility source readable), the eligibility check is the dominant filter

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The author lost time on three earlier candidates that turned out unusable only after opening the raw-material file, and asked for a harder pre-screen specifically for the next (Rehabilitation-only) batch: confirm the abstract still exists, the retraction notice is reachable, and the eligibility source is genuinely readable, before handing anything over -- and to report the survival fraction, since a low rate would itself be informative about whether Rehabilitation's corpus-size disadvantage (ADR-047) is the only asymmetry between domains.

**Method:** Three checks, cheapest first, over candidates drawn from a Rehabilitation-only balanced work-queue pull (73 eligible after excluding already-used/rejected record_ids): (1) abstract-overwrite classifier (already validated, ADR-065/066) applied to each candidate's own PubMed record; (2) retraction-notice PMID existence, checked via direct EFetch, distinct from a notice existing-but-bare; (3) for survivors of 1-2, a real search for current institutional evidence and a direct read of the best candidate source, not a search-summary citation.

**Result: 1 of 30 candidates drawn fully passed all three checks.** Stage yields: abstract intact 22/30 (73%); of those, notice reachable 19/22 (86%); of 11 stage-2 survivors actually checked for eligibility (9 more remain unchecked, budget-limited), 1 passed (9%). **The eligibility-source check is the dominant filter** -- most candidates that had an intact abstract and reachable notice still failed because no current, freely-readable institutional source could be found within a reasonable per-candidate budget: some questions have no systematic review or guideline yet at all (single-center retrospective studies only), some have one that's paywalled with no open-access route found (Wiley, Taylor & Francis), and at least one nominally-free source (NICE guidance) was blocked by anti-bot protection rather than confirmed genuinely unreadable. Full per-candidate accounting: `results/rehabilitation_prescreen_2026-08-10.md`.

**A related, not-fully-disentangled finding:** many Rehabilitation-tagged candidates are not substantively about physical rehabilitation at all -- cancer immunotherapy xenograft studies, dental bone-graft rabbit models, thyroid cancer genetics, hepatitis B antiviral cohorts all appeared in this pull, consistent with PubMed's "Rehabilitation" MeSH heading being administratively broad (ADR-025). Whether the low eligibility-survival rate reflects (a) this domain-purity issue -- off-topic candidates whose real literature lives in journals this pass didn't find open-access routes into -- or (b) genuine physical-rehabilitation questions having thinner or more often paywalled current evidence than pharmacology/Ob·Gyn topics have had so far in this project, is not distinguished by this one pass. Flagged as an open question needing a larger sample, not resolved here.

**One candidate delivered, with two disclosed structural wrinkles, not silently smoothed:** record 20361 (Choi et al., entecavir vs. tenofovir HCC risk in chronic hepatitis B, JAMA Oncology, retracted-and-replaced 2019). (1) A PubMed-tag verification gap -- confirmed directly, no `Retracted Publication` tag despite a real, corroborated JAMA retraction -- handled via the project protocol's documented-discrepancy pathway, consistent with the ~8.1% gap rate already measured pool-wide (ADR-028/030/066), not a new problem. (2) This is a "Retract and Replace" case with a same-author corrected replacement paper (PMID 32782369) available -- a structural shape this benchmark has not had before, requiring a deliberate future annotator decision about whether the gold claim should rest on that replacement, an independent synthesis, or both. Raw material: `results/item17_raw_material.md`.

**Consequences:** Only 1 of the requested 5-candidate batch is ready; delivering 4 more at the same rigor would need either a substantially larger screening budget or a relaxed criterion (author's call, not decided here). `docs/STATUS.md` updated to reflect 1 new Rehabilitation candidate ready (item 17) and this finding. If the low survival rate holds up under more sampling, it belongs in the paper's methods alongside ADR-047's corpus-density concern as a second, distinct asymmetry between domains.

---

## ADR-068: The 30 screened Rehabilitation candidates hand-classified -- 90% incidentally tagged, only 1 genuinely about rehabilitation; the domain-tagging problem is confirmed, the "is rehabilitation research underserved" question is not yet testable

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-067 found a low (~9%) eligibility-pass rate among Rehabilitation-tagged candidates and flagged, without resolving, two very different explanations: incidental MeSH tagging (ADR-025's known concern) versus a substantive claim that non-pharmacological rehabilitation research attracts less institutional synthesis than pharmacology does. The author asked for the 30 already-screened candidates to be hand-classified by whether their core subject is genuinely rehabilitation (physiotherapy, exercise, functional recovery, the author's own test) or incidental, classified before looking back at which passed or failed, so the classification wouldn't be shaped by the expected answer.

**Result: 1 of 30 (3.3%) genuine, 2 of 30 (6.7%) borderline, 27 of 30 (90%) incidental.** The genuine case: record 21065, test-retest reliability of dynamic balance measures in lower-limb amputees. The two borderline cases (16669, peripheral neuromodulation for sacroiliac pain; 24292, neural therapy for post-surgical discomfort) are device/injection-based pain interventions arguably within physiatry's scope but not physiotherapy or exercise specifically. The 27 incidental candidates span oncology, pharmacology/cardiology, obstetrics/gynecology, dentistry/material science, hepatology (including record 20361, ADR-067's one eligibility pass), endocrine surgery, hand surgery, vascular intervention, urology, regenerative medicine, and nephrology psychometrics -- none with any rehabilitation-medicine framing. Full table: `results/rehabilitation_prescreen_2026-08-10.md` addendum.

**Eligibility-rate split by category:** genuine (n=1): reached the eligibility check, failed it (population mismatch -- the closest current source addressed stroke, not amputation -- plus a structural mismatch, the retracted paper being a reliability study rather than a treatment-efficacy trial). Borderline (n=2): neither reached the eligibility check at all, both failing earlier at notice-reachability. Incidental (n=27): 10 reached the eligibility check, 1 passed (10%, consistent with the ~9% pooled figure, since incidental candidates are nearly the whole sample).

**This does not confirm either of the author's two hypotheses, and explains why: there is only one genuine data point.** The single genuine candidate's eligibility failure is consistent with "rehabilitation research is underserved" but is not evidence of it at n=1 -- it could equally be this specific paper's population/structural mismatch, unrelated to the field generally. **What the classification does establish cleanly, with real statistical weight (27/30), is that the domain-tagging problem ADR-025 warned about is real and large:** the large majority of what this project's MeSH-based Rehabilitation query returns is not about rehabilitation. The pooled ~9% eligibility rate (ADR-067) is therefore mostly a measurement of synthesis coverage for a grab-bag of oncology/pharmacology/dentistry topics that happen to carry a Rehabilitation tag, not a measurement of rehabilitation research specifically.

**A consequence worth stating plainly, not just implying:** at this draw rate (1 genuine candidate per ~30 drawn), properly testing whether genuine rehabilitation research is underserved would require drawing on the order of 300 candidates to reach even 10 genuine data points -- far beyond the 73 currently eligible in this domain's remaining queue (89 total Rehabilitation-tagged rows, minus already-used/rejected). The MeSH-tag-based domain construction cannot supply that sample size for this specific sub-question, whatever is decided about the domain's status in the benchmark.

**Not decided here, per instruction:** whether to keep Rehabilitation with a small, explicitly-asymmetric item count and report why, or replace it with Surgery (ADR-021's fourth-ranked domain, genuinely non-pharmacological, the contrast originally wanted). Both paths are affected by this finding differently than either was before it: keeping Rehabilitation now means keeping a domain whose own candidate pool is 90% not about its own subject, a fact that would itself need stating in the paper regardless of which path is chosen; replacing it with Surgery would need this same classification exercise run against Surgery's candidate pool before assuming it fares better.

**Consequences:** `docs/STATUS.md` updated with this finding and the two-path decision it feeds, left open. No item is annotated or rejected as a result of this ADR by itself -- it is a measurement, not a selection action.

---

## ADR-069: `replacement_exists` added -- a "Retract and Replace" is a distinct failure shape from a void retraction; item 17 taken despite its evidence source failing independence on a deeper check

**Status:** Accepted (field); item 17 raw material updated, not yet annotated

**Context:** Item 17 (record 20361) is a JAMA Oncology "Notice of Retraction and Replacement" case, not a plain retraction -- the original manuscript was withdrawn *and* replaced by a same-author corrected version. The author's instruction: take this item specifically because that structural shape is new to the benchmark and worth one example more than a fifth item of a familiar kind, and add a field naming what it tests -- a model citing the original when a citable corrected replacement exists fails differently than one citing a retraction with no replacement at all; the first case has a specific right answer (cite the replacement, not the original), the second doesn't (the evidence is simply void).

**Decision:** `BenchmarkItem.replacement_exists: bool`, required (no default, same discipline as `claim_polarity`/`independence_verified` -- every item states this explicitly rather than defaulting silently). Backfilled `false` on RB-0001 through RB-0004 (none are retract-and-replace cases). Tested (required-field validation).

**Completing item 17's raw material surfaced a second, more consequential finding: the source that passed the Rehabilitation pre-screen's readability check fails ADR-058's independence check.** The pre-screen (ADR-067) confirmed Oh et al. 2022 (Cancers, PMC9179302) was on-topic and freely readable -- sufficient for that pass's three checks. Completing the file for real annotation required the deeper, not-yet-run independence check, and it fails: **the review's own Table 1 cites "Choi 2019 Korea" -- the retracted paper itself (PMID 30267080) -- with its data pooled directly into the meta-analysis.** Using this review as-is would put the retracted paper's own data back inside the benchmark's gold answer, exactly the hazard ADR-058 exists to catch. Per that ADR's rule, this evidence should not be used, not merely flagged -- it is excluded from this item's evidence entry, not carried forward with a caveat.

**Also identified, not yet fully verified:** the likely same-author replacement paper (Choi et al., *Scientific Reports* 2020, PMID 32782369 -- exact author-list match, ~16 months after the retraction) is much smaller (404 vs. ~24,000 patients) and its own conclusion is hedged (loses significance after propensity adjustment, calls for more data) -- an ADR-052/055-type caveat if used. A second candidate current-evidence source, AASLD's 2018 Hepatitis B guidance, was found but not yet confirmed as genuinely post-supersession (its exact date relative to the original paper's September 2018 online publication is unconfirmed) or independence-checked itself.

**This item's raw material is genuinely more complex than any prior item's, and is left honestly unresolved rather than forced to a clean state.** `results/item17_raw_material.md` states plainly: the disqualified source, the likely-but-unconfirmed replacement, the not-yet-verified guideline candidate, and what remains to be checked before a gold claim can responsibly be drafted. Per the author's instruction, the item is kept (not swapped for an easier one) precisely because working through this complexity is itself informative about a failure shape this benchmark hasn't tested before.

**Consequences:** `schemas.py` gains `replacement_exists`; `data/benchmark/items.jsonl` backfilled for RB-0001-0004. Item 17 is not yet a `BenchmarkItem` -- the author annotates it next, per their own statement, with the evidence-source question still open. `docs/STATUS.md` updated to reflect both the new field and item 17's actual state (complex, not simply "ready").

---

## ADR-070: Author-cluster cap amended to count author *groups*, not just individual names -- item 11 rejected for author-group concentration, a fourth (and now second) rejection reason

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Items 11 (record 5667, adjunctive ECT) and 14 (record 5690, ECT alone) were flagged before recording: both retracted in 2016, same core research team. Checked directly: they share 8 of 11 authors (Wei Zheng, Yu-Tao Xiang, Ying-Qiang Xiang, Xiao-Lan Cao, Tong Guo, Zheng-Rong Liu, Helen F K Chiu, Gabor S Ungvari) -- 72.7% of the smaller author list. Neither author had appeared in any previously recorded item, so using both would sit exactly at ADR-031's original per-author cap of 2, not exceed it.

**Decision:** The author asked to keep item 14 (contradictory direction -- current evidence reverses the trap claim, the rarer and harder shape, against item 11's concordant/narrowing direction) and to amend ADR-031: **the cap counts author groups, not individual names.** Operationalized as: two candidate records are one unit if the smaller record's author list overlaps the other's by more than half (`|A ∩ B| / min(|A|, |B|) > 0.5`). Two papers meeting this threshold count as a single use against the cap, regardless of what the per-individual-author count would otherwise allow -- eight shared authors makes two papers effectively one research unit, and the per-author cap's letter (2 uses each) doesn't capture that when it's satisfied by using both papers in full rather than by two unrelated papers each touching an author once.

**Item 11 rejected for author-group concentration** -- a reason distinct from eligibility failure (`rw-18340`) and from topical redundancy (`rw-6531`, ADR-074): the candidate itself was eligible and well-formed, but recording it alongside item 14 would concentrate two of nine items in one research team regardless of what the per-author rule technically permits.

**Re-checked every one of the 9 items that exist after this session's recording (RB-0001 through RB-0004, plus the 5 new items this ADR's sibling entries record) pairwise against the amended rule: zero breaches.** No other pair shares any author at all, let alone a majority. The amendment changes nothing else already recorded; it only closes the gap the ECT pair exposed.

**Consequences:** `docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 3.1.2 gains the author-group operationalization. `data/benchmark/rejected_candidates.jsonl` gains `rw-5667` (reason: author-group concentration). `results/author_cluster_analysis.md`'s existing cluster table is unaffected (it already used per-record overlap for the well-known Boldt/Hempelmann and Badawy/Maged/Abbas clusters; this formalizes the same intuition as an enforced rule rather than an observation). Rejection-reason taxonomy now has four members, tracked distinctly in the paper: no institutional source, redundancy, author-group concentration, and (from earlier sessions) claim-type-screen false positive / not a clinical claim.

---

## ADR-071: Item 8 has no separate notice document -- 5 of 296 candidates share this property; the `retrieval_with_notice` condition cannot be run for them

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Item 8 (record 12945)'s Retraction Watch entry has `retraction_pubmed_id` and `retraction_doi` identical to the *original* paper's PMID/DOI -- confirmed directly against PubMed (`PublicationTypeList` is `Journal Article` only, no `Retracted Publication` tag, and the title carries no "Retracted:" prefix in PubMed's own record, though Crossref's title for the same DOI does read "Retracted: [original title]"). There is no PubMed record distinct from the original that a `retrieval_with_notice` condition could retrieve -- the notice, such as it is, exists only as a retitling visible to Crossref, not as a separate indexed document.

**Measured across the full 296-candidate pool, per the author's request:** `retraction_pubmed_id == original_paper_pubmed_id` for **5 of 296 candidates (1.7%)**: record 12945 (item 8), plus records 71762, 16851, 8610, 8140 (not yet annotated). Two of the other four (8610, 8140) were independently encountered during the Rehabilitation pre-screen (ADR-067) and failed the abstract-intact check there -- 8610's PubMed record shows only retraction boilerplate where an abstract should be, 8140's shows no abstract at all. This is consistent, not coincidental: when a single PubMed record must carry both the original content and the retraction status, the retraction appears to sometimes overwrite the abstract field entirely (matching ADR-065's "claim erased" pattern) rather than merely omitting a separate notice.

**Decision:** No schema change -- this is measurable directly from `SupersededSource.notice_text_verified=False` combined with `supporting_sources` resolving to a `benchmark_source` corpus document with no corresponding `supersession_notice` document, so no new field is needed to detect it. Recorded here as a finding: 5/296 items in this pool cannot support the `retrieval_with_notice` experimental condition at all, a distinct constraint from `notice_specificity` (which describes an existing notice's informativeness) -- this is the absence of a retrievable notice document as a distinct object, a fourth variant alongside ADR-065's three-way "content unreachable" pattern (notice unreadable / notice uninformative / claim erased).

**Consequences:** `docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 4 should note this as a checkable property during raw-material preparation (check whether `retraction_pubmed_id`/`retraction_doi` differ from the original before assuming a separate notice document exists). `paper/paper.md`'s methods section, wherever `retrieval_with_notice` is described, should state that this condition is not defined for this subset of items and report the 1.7% rate as part of the retrieval-condition coverage discussion.

---

## ADR-072: `notice_specificity` categorical-vs-specific boundary rule -- naming the *kind* of problem is categorical, naming *which* data/analysis/study is specific

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Item 9 (record 2404)'s retraction notice names the mechanism category precisely ("the source data in some cases are inconsistent and requires further evaluation to determine the true magnitude of effect") but never identifies which specific data points, analyses, or sub-studies were inconsistent. This sat on the boundary between `categorical` and `specific` (ADR-033) without a clean rule to resolve it.

**Decision:** The operative test: **a notice is `specific` only if it names which data, analysis, or study was the problem; a notice that names what kind of problem occurred (data inconsistency, image manipulation, analytical error) without saying which specific data/analysis/study is `categorical`**, even if the category is named precisely and even if it includes qualifying detail (here, that the concern is about *magnitude* of effect specifically, not direction). Item 9's notice is `categorical` under this rule. Contrast with item 15 (record 7954)'s notice, `specific` under the same rule: it names the exact mechanism (a named device's disclosed failure, reported by the manufacturer to the author) not just the category (error in data).

**Consequences:** `docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 4's `notice_specificity` guidance gains this operative test, the same pattern as ADR-062's direction/magnitude-vs-topic test for question neutrality -- a concrete rule rather than a case-by-case judgment call. Once enough items exist, `categorical` vs `specific` classification can be checked for inter-rater consistency against this rule specifically.

---

## ADR-073: ADR-019's institutional-source standard amended for epidemiological claims -- a national statistics body or survey programme is primary data, not synthesis, and is the authoritative source for this claim type

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-019 required a candidate's gold claim to trace to an institutional source: a guideline, Cochrane review, systematic review, meta-analysis, or regulatory statement -- written with clinical-intervention claims in mind. Item 10 (record 16626, rural-vs-urban post-neonatal mortality in Nigeria) nearly failed eligibility under this standard: no guideline body issues a "guideline" on a national mortality rate, and no systematic review synthesizes a single country's household survey data the way a review synthesizes RCTs. The standard didn't fit the claim type, not because no authoritative source existed, but because ADR-019 didn't recognize the source class that actually governs epidemiological claims.

**Decision:** For epidemiological claims (prevalence, incidence, mortality rates, and similar population-level descriptive statistics), **the authoritative source is a national statistics body or a survey programme** (e.g. the DHS -- Demographic and Health Surveys -- program, or an equivalent national statistical office) -- **primary data, not a synthesis**, and eligible under ADR-019 on that basis despite not being a guideline/Cochrane review/systematic review/meta-analysis/regulatory statement. `schemas.SourceAuthority` gains `NATIONAL_STATISTICS_BODY` to record this distinctly from `PRIMARY_STUDY` (a single research study) and `REGULATORY_AGENCY` (a body regulating a product/practice, not reporting population statistics).

**Consequences:** `docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 3.2 gains this as a named eligibility route, the same way regulatory-agency statements were already named as the expected route for adverse-event/safety-signal retractions -- future epidemiological candidates should not be wrongly rejected or forced into the weaker "primary study" evidence tier for lacking a guideline/synthesis that structurally cannot exist for this claim type. `schemas.py`'s `SourceAuthority` enum updated (additive, no existing records affected). Item 10 uses this route: its post-supersession evidence is the Nigeria DHS 2023-24 report, `source_authority=national_statistics_body`.

---

## ADR-074: Five items recorded (RB-0005 through RB-0009); two further rejections logged with distinguishable reasons

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The author annotated seven candidates this session, rejected three, and asked to record the surviving five, following ADR-070's resolution of the items 11/14 conflict.

**Recorded, all author-written per ADR-040, all `provenance=verified`:**

- **RB-0005** (record 12945, Obstetrics/Gynecology): HBV preconception prevalence in rural China. `item_type=superseded_with_current_answer`, `claim_polarity=positive`. No separate notice document (ADR-071). Evidence: a 2023 Gut systematic review (3,740 studies, 231M people) -- population-match caveat stated directly in the gold claim (general female population and pregnant-women subset, not the specific preconception population), per ADR-034's scoping discipline. `independence_verified=false`: the review's 3,740-study supplementary list could not be searched in this pass.
- **RB-0006** (record 2404, Pharmacology): vedolizumab and biliary inflammation in PSC-IBD, from a retracted conference abstract. `item_type=superseded_with_current_answer`, direction contradictory -- 2024 systematic review finds vedolizumab associated with *worsening* liver enzymes, not improvement. `notice_specificity=categorical` under ADR-072's new boundary rule. `independence_verified=true`: the review's three named vedolizumab cohort studies (Lynch 2019, Caron 2019, Christensen 2018) checked directly, none is the retracted abstract.
- **RB-0007** (record 16626, Obstetrics/Gynecology): rural-vs-urban post-neonatal mortality in Nigeria. `item_type=superseded_without_current_answer`, `no_answer_reason=adjacent_evidence_exists` (the Nigeria DHS 2023-24 report exists and is authoritative but reports mortality by geographic zone, not the retracted paper's rural-vs-urban framing). First use of ADR-073's `national_statistics_body` source authority. `independence_verified=true` trivially: the DHS program is the retracted paper's own data source, not the reverse.
- **RB-0008** (record 5690, Pharmacology): ECT alone vs. antipsychotic monotherapy for schizophrenia. `item_type=superseded_with_current_answer`, direction contradictory -- the 2005 Cochrane review (Tharyan & Adams) finds medication favoured over ECT alone, the opposite of the retracted meta-analysis's claim. Kept over record 5667 per ADR-070. `independence_verified=true` by temporal precedence: the Cochrane review predates the retracted paper by over 11 years.
- **RB-0009** (record 7954, Rehabilitation/Therapy): low-intensity ultrasound for chronic trapezius trigger points. **First negative-polarity item, first Rehabilitation item.** `item_type=superseded_with_current_answer`, `claim_polarity=negative` (the retracted trial's own conclusion was that ultrasound doesn't outperform placebo). Gold claim's conclusion/caveat language checked against the primary PubMed structured abstract before recording, per the author's blocking instruction: core content (pain-reduction finding, parameter heterogeneity, call for more high-quality large-sample trials) confirmed verbatim; the phrase "necessitates cautious interpretation" is the annotator's own gloss on the stated heterogeneity, not a direct quote -- the paywalled full-text Discussion was not checked for whether it appears there too. `independence_verified=false`: the review's 16-study list was not checked against the retracted trial.

**Two further rejections, each logged with a reason distinguishable from the other three already in the taxonomy (no institutional source, author-group concentration):**

- **`rw-6531` (item 12, record 6531) -- redundancy.** Near-duplicate of item 7 (record 6414, kept): same author cluster (Iwamoto/Matsumoto/Takeda), same drug class (bisphosphonate fracture prevention), same population shape (elderly patients with a neurological comorbidity), same no-current-answer structure. The candidate was eligible; recording it would add repetition, not coverage. First redundancy-based rejection in this project.
- **`rw-18340` (item 16, record 18340) -- no institutional source, compounded by a circular-provenance risk.** No systematic review/guideline found for pregnancy's effect on periodontal health. More importantly: the only Kashmir-valley OHIP-14 figures located during eligibility search may themselves be citing the retracted paper's own data, unresolved when first flagged (`results/item16_raw_material.md`). Rejected rather than risk importing the retracted claim into a gold claim through an uncorrected downstream citation -- a real, encountered instance of the exact failure this benchmark measures, occurring inside its own annotation process, not a hypothetical. Belongs in the paper's methods as such.

**Rejection-reason taxonomy is now four-wide and each member is separately countable:** no institutional source (`rw-18340`, and earlier candidates), author-group concentration (`rw-5667`), redundancy (`rw-6531`), and not-a-clinical-claim/claim-type-screen false positive (`rw-18336`, `rw-5833`, earlier sessions). Each says something different about the pool and should be reported as a separate rate in the paper, not folded into one "rejected" bucket.

**Consequences:** `data/benchmark/items.jsonl` gains RB-0005 through RB-0009 (9 items total). `data/benchmark/rejected_candidates.jsonl` gains `rw-6531` and `rw-18340` (7 rejections total across the project). 156 tests, ruff clean. Corpus update and retrieval-ceiling re-run recorded separately (see the following ADR).

---

## ADR-075: Domain selection settled at two -- Pharmacology and Obstetrics/Gynecology. Rehabilitation/Therapy does not become a third domain; the attempt is reported as a methods finding instead. Supersedes the domain-selection intent of ADR-018/025/047.

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-018 left the third domain open pending further data; ADR-025 tested and flagged, without yet quantifying at annotation scale, that Rehabilitation/Therapy's subject tag is not a clean non-pharmacological proxy (behavioural candidates were ~17% of its tier-1-clinical pool, itself a minority); ADR-047 registered the domain's ~27-fold larger background corpus as an open question for the retrieval-ceiling measurement to resolve. ADR-067/068 this session went further than any prior check: hand-classified 30 actually-screened Rehabilitation candidates and found 90% incidentally tagged, only 1 genuinely about rehabilitation (physiotherapy/exercise/functional recovery) in the entire draw -- a starker version of exactly what ADR-025 predicted, now measured at the point where it actually matters (candidates an annotator would otherwise spend real time on), not projected from an upstream category count.

**Decision:** **Two domains, not three: Pharmacology and Obstetrics/Gynecology.** Rehabilitation/Therapy is not swapped for Surgery (ADR-021's fourth-ranked, genuinely non-pharmacological alternative) despite Surgery being closer to the original contrast the author wanted -- explicitly not pursued: it is 2026-08-10, seven weeks remain against the November deadline, and the benchmark already has 9 items. Adding a domain now would mean re-running pre-screening, eligibility piloting, and balancing from zero for a domain with no annotated items yet, against a hard deadline that does not reward that trade. A smaller two-domain benchmark that ships beats a three-domain one that doesn't, per the project protocol's standing priority.

**The Rehabilitation attempt is reported in the paper as a methods finding, not discarded as wasted effort:** the 27-fold corpus-density imbalance (ADR-047), the ~9% technical eligibility-pass rate (ADR-067), and the 90% incidental-tagging rate (ADR-068) together are a substantive, citable result about selecting clinical domains from Retraction Watch subject tags via MeSH-derived queries -- a methodological hazard worth naming for anyone building a similar benchmark from the same kind of registry data, independent of anything the models under test do. This is arguably a more useful contribution to the paper than a third, thin domain would have been.

**ADR-047's corpus-imbalance question is closed, but not by the route it expected.** It cannot be answered, because the domain it concerned does not yield items to test it with -- a null result with a specific, documented cause (domain-tagging failure), not an inconclusive one. This should be stated exactly that way in the paper: the question was well-posed, the measurement approach (retrieval ceiling per domain) was sound and already validated across Pharmacology and Obstetrics/Gynecology, but the third domain needed to answer it does not exist in this pool in a form that supports annotation.

**RB-0009 (item 15, record 7954) is kept as a Rehabilitation-tagged item and reported as such, but is not treated as an analysis stratum.** It remains the benchmark's one Rehabilitation-domain example and its first negative-polarity item -- both worth keeping and reporting individually -- but with n=1, no domain-level Rehabilitation statistic (recall, retrieval ceiling, or otherwise) is computed or claimed. The retrieval-ceiling report and any domain-stratified results table carry Pharmacology and Obstetrics/Gynecology as the two strata; RB-0009 appears in the per-item table, not a per-domain row of its own.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md`'s domain-selection section needs rewriting to reflect two settled domains plus the Rehabilitation methods finding, replacing whatever draft language assumed three. `docs/STATUS.md` updated. No further Rehabilitation candidates are pre-screened or annotated as a domain-building exercise -- ADR-067/068's STOP instruction stays in force, now permanently rather than pending a decision. `results/rehabilitation_prescreen_2026-08-10.md` and the classification addendum are the primary source material for the paper's methods section on this point.

---

## ADR-076: Item 17's near-miss -- a gold claim was nearly drafted on a source that had absorbed the retracted paper's own data. Independence checking moves earlier, into pre-screening. Full evidential independence may be structurally unverifiable for retract-and-replace items, and the benchmark excludes rather than accepts that uncertainty.

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Item 17 (record 20361) passed the Rehabilitation pre-screen's three checks (ADR-067) on the strength of Oh et al. 2022, a systematic review that was open access, on-topic, and carried a useful evidence-strength caveat. The author's own draft gold claim used it. Completing the item's raw material for real, per this session's earlier work, ran the independence check ADR-058 requires and found the review's own included-studies table cites the retracted paper directly, with its data pooled into the meta-analysis. **Had the item been drafted and recorded on the pre-screen's basis alone, the benchmark's gold answer for this item would have rested, transitively, on the very study it declares void.** The author calls this the most instructive near-miss in the project so far, and asked for it recorded as such, not folded quietly into the item's own raw-material notes.

**Why it happened: the independence check runs at drafting time, not pre-screening time.** The three-check pre-screen (abstract intact, notice reachable, source readable and on-topic) was designed to be cheap and fast, explicitly deferring the expensive step (a real search for current evidence) to the end. Independence verification (ADR-058) is more expensive still -- it requires reading a candidate source's own reference list or included-studies table, not just confirming the source itself is reachable -- and was never inserted into the pre-screen's stage sequence at all. A source can be perfectly readable, perfectly on-topic, and still fail independence; readability and topical fit say nothing about whether the source's own analysis has quietly absorbed the thing it's meant to supersede.

**Decision -- two consequences, both binding on how pre-screening works from here:**

1. **Independence checking moves into pre-screening, as a fourth stage, not left for drafting time.** `docs/ANNOTATION.md`'s pre-screen protocol (ADR-067) gains this stage explicitly, run on the eligibility-source candidate before it is presented as a pass. **This has a real, acknowledged cost: it will slow pre-screening further and reduce yield below the already-low rate ADR-067/068 measured** (that measurement was three-stage; a four-stage version screening the same candidates would find some additional failures at the new stage, on top of the ones already found). Accepted anyway -- the alternative is exactly the near-miss that happened here, at annotation-time cost instead of pre-screen-time cost, and annotation time is scarcer.
2. **This is structural for retract-and-replace items specifically, and is recorded as a benchmark limitation, not just a per-item caveat.** When a retraction is corrected rather than withdrawn outright, later independent syntheses will legitimately -- correctly -- include the corrected replacement in their own pooled analyses. Tracing whether a given later source used the *original* void version or the *replacement* version, from outside the source's own methods section, may be impossible in principle, not merely inconvenient in practice: a citation alone does not disambiguate which version was pulled, and not every source's methods are detailed enough to say. **The benchmark's response to this uncertainty is to exclude, not accept with a caveat** -- an evidence source with unresolvable version-provenance is treated the same as one that fails independence outright, per ADR-058's existing rule, rather than carried forward with an "independence uncertain" label that would understate the risk.

**Item 17's own resolution, following from this:** the disqualified meta-analysis is not replaced by a search for a different independent synthesis (none was found; the AASLD 2018 guideline was checked directly and ruled out on two grounds -- it doesn't address the entecavir-vs-tenofovir HCC comparison at all, and it predates the original paper). Instead, the item's current-evidence source is the same-author corrected replacement itself (Choi et al., *Scientific Reports* 2020) -- which is the structurally correct answer for a retract-and-replace item in the first place: the model is being tested on whether it cites the replacement, not on finding independent confirmation. `results/item17_raw_material.md` updated accordingly; the item is ready for the author to draft next.

**Consequences:** `docs/ANNOTATION.md` section 3.2/4 gains the four-stage pre-screen order and the explicit yield-cost warning. `paper/paper.md`'s limitations section should state the retract-and-replace independence-unverifiability point directly -- it is a real, identified constraint on this benchmark's construction method for this item class, worth a reviewer knowing about in advance rather than discovering as a gap. This near-miss, and ADR-058 catching it, belongs in the paper's methods as a worked example of why the independence-verification step exists at all.

---

## ADR-077: Corpus updated for RB-0005 through RB-0009; retrieval ceiling re-run across all 9 items -- a new pattern, notices ranking *above* their trap sources, appears for the first time and is plausibly a document-length artifact

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** RB-0005 through RB-0009's documents were not yet in the corpus. `add_documents()`/`content_sha256()` (ADR-054's mechanism) used again: 13 rows appended (5 benchmark sources, 4 notices -- RB-0005 has none, per ADR-071 -- and 4 resolvable evidence entries; RB-0007's evidence, the Nigeria DHS report, has no PMID and was not embedded, logged in `update_history` rather than silently dropped). Corpus: 400,551 -> 400,564 documents; hash `3f7561a9...` -> `30dbe86e...`. Retrieval ceiling then re-run for all 9 items.

**Result:**

| Item | Domain | trap_source_rank | notice_rank | rank_gap |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| RB-0001 | Pharmacology | 1 | 2 | 1 |
| RB-0002 | Obstetrics/Gynecology | 1 | 2 | 1 |
| RB-0003 | Obstetrics/Gynecology | 1 | 2 | 1 |
| RB-0004 | Pharmacology | 1 | 3 | 2 |
| RB-0005 | Obstetrics/Gynecology | 1 | null | null |
| RB-0006 | Pharmacology | 1 | 3 | 2 |
| RB-0007 | Obstetrics/Gynecology | 2 | 1 | -1 |
| RB-0008 | Pharmacology | 2 | 1 | -1 |
| RB-0009 | Rehabilitation/Therapy | 1 | 2 | 1 |

**RB-0005: `notice_rank=null`, not a bad rank -- there is no notice document to rank at all** (ADR-071, the source's `retraction_pubmed_id` equals its own `original_paper_pubmed_id`, no separate document exists to embed). This is a different, more severe category than "ranks below `practical_k`," and the per-domain `notice_invisible_in_practice_fraction` metric currently conflates the two -- Obstetrics/Gynecology's 0.25 figure (1 of 4) is driven entirely by this structural absence, not by a notice that exists but ranks poorly. Worth a metric refinement before this figure is used in the paper without the caveat attached here.

**RB-0007 and RB-0008: the notice ranks *above* the trap source -- the first time this has happened in nine items, in either direction.** Both share a property no earlier item had: their notices are confirmed `bare` with `notice_text=None` (nothing found beyond the record's own title), so the corpus document embedded for each notice is effectively **title-only**, far shorter than the trap source's full-abstract embedding. Plausible, not yet confirmed, explanation: a short, dense, question-adjacent title can score higher on cosine similarity than a longer document whose relevant content is diluted across a full abstract -- the four other `bare`-or-short-notice items in this benchmark (RB-0001/0002/0003/0009) still ranked their notice below the trap source, but none of those had a *fully empty* `notice_text` the way RB-0007/0008 do; RB-0004 and RB-0006, whose notices carry substantial real text, both show the largest same-direction gaps (2) in the other direction. The pattern is consistent with a document-length effect, not confirmed as one -- n=2 for the specific "fully empty notice" case.

**Per-domain summary:** Pharmacology (n=4): mean rank gap 1.0, but driven by two opposite-signed items (RB-0004/0006 at +2, RB-0008 at -1) rather than a consistent direction. Obstetrics/Gynecology (n=4): mean rank gap 0.33, similarly mixed (RB-0002/0003 at +1, RB-0007 at -1, RB-0005 undefined). Rehabilitation/Therapy (n=1, RB-0009 only, not a stratum per ADR-075): rank gap 1, consistent with the majority pattern.

**Consequences:** `results/retrieval_ceiling_report.json` committed with all 9 items. `corpus/retrieval_ceiling.py`'s `notice_invisible_in_practice_fraction` should be split into "ranks below practical_k" vs. "no notice document exists" before this metric is reported in the paper without qualification -- flagged, not fixed in this ADR. The notice-ranks-above-trap-source pattern, if it holds up as more bare/empty-notice items are annotated, is a genuinely new, reportable finding for the paper's retrieval-ceiling section: document length, not just notice existence, may materially affect whether a notice is retrievable in practice, in a direction that (for once) favors the model rather than working against it.

---

## ADR-078: Items 3 and 7 dropped from the annotation queue on time budget, logged as deferred, not rejected -- a new record type keeps the distinction out of the rejection-rate accounting

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Item 3 (record rw-5684, Boldt/Hempelmann cluster) has been blocked on paywalled guideline text since it was first opened (2026-08-06, `results/item3_raw_material.md`) and has already failed to resolve across two prior passes. Item 7 (record rw-6414, Iwamoto/Matsumoto/Takeda cluster) was originally prioritized under ADR-041's per-batch author-cluster cap while Rehabilitation/Therapy was still a candidate domain; that domain is now retired (ADR-075), so the reason item 7 was worth a slot ahead of other queued work no longer holds. With seven weeks left to the November deadline, a third pass at item 3 and a first real pass at item 7 are not worth the remaining time budget relative to other annotation work.

Neither candidate failed on eligibility, redundancy, author-group concentration, or any other quality judgment `RejectedCandidate` exists to record (ADR-074) -- the reason both are set aside is purely the project's remaining time, a fact about project state, not about the candidate.
