Logging them as `RejectedCandidate` with `reason=OTHER` and a note explaining the real reason was considered and rejected: the rejection rate is a reported finding about how often real search fails to find institutional evidence (ADR-019), and folding time-budget dropouts into that count would understate what fraction of genuine attempts actually fail on merit, biasing the rejection-rate finding downward from noise.

**Decision:** A new record type, `DeferredCandidate` (`doi`, `source_registry_id`, free-text `notes`, `deferred_date`), persisted separately to `data/benchmark/deferred_candidates.jsonl`, with matching `append_deferred`/`load_deferred` functions in `curate/benchmark_io.py`. Items 3 and 7 are logged there, not in `rejected_candidates.jsonl`. The rejection-rate accounting (ADR-074's four reasons) stays a clean measure of candidates that were actually evaluated and found wanting; deferrals are reported separately, as a fact about annotation throughput under a fixed deadline, not about the candidate pool's quality.

**Consequences:** The annotation queue now stands at one open candidate (item 17, ready to draft) plus whatever the author brings back next -- items 3 and 7 are closed out. `docs/STATUS.md` and `paper/paper.md` section 3.4 (institutional-source standard) note the distinction. If a future candidate is set aside for a reason that is itself about the candidate (e.g. "will revisit if the domain reopens") rather than pure time budget, that is closer to a rejection and should go through `RejectedCandidate`, not this path -- `DeferredCandidate` is specifically for schedule-driven deferral, not a general "not now" bucket.

---

## ADR-079: `evidence_direction` formalized as a schema field -- backfilled across all 9 items, an even 3/3/3 concordant/contradictory/absent split

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Several items' annotation notes already characterized how current evidence relates to the trap claim it supersedes -- RB-0006, RB-0008, and RB-0009 explicitly noted "Direction: CONTRADICTORY" or "contradictory" in free text; RB-0005's note said "Direction: concordant." This distinction was doing real analytical work (it was the deciding factor between item 11 and item 14, ADR-070) without a formal field, which put it at risk of being invisible in results the way every other dimension in this project gets a field once it recurs (`claim_polarity`, `no_answer_reason`, `replacement_exists`, `notice_specificity`, ...).

**Decision:** `EvidenceDirection` enum added (`concordant`, `contradictory`, `absent`), and `BenchmarkItem.evidence_direction` added as a required field, gated on `item_type` by the same pattern as `no_answer_reason` (ADR-063): `ABSENT` iff `item_type == SUPERSEDED_WITHOUT_CURRENT_ANSWER` (there is no current answer to compare a direction against), `CONCORDANT` or `CONTRADICTORY` otherwise. All 9 recorded items backfilled by reading the trap claim against the gold claim directly (not just re-reading each item's free-text note, since RB-0001 and RB-0002 never used the word "concordant" in notes but are concordant on inspection: RB-0001's ~4.1% current estimate sits in the same range as the retracted 4.9% figure, not a reversal; RB-0002's ~72% current live-birth rate is the same direction and a similar magnitude to the retracted 64-82% figures).

**Result:** An even split -- `concordant`: RB-0001, RB-0002, RB-0005. `contradictory`: RB-0006, RB-0008, RB-0009. `absent`: RB-0003, RB-0004, RB-0007 (all three `superseded_without_current_answer` items, exactly as the gating rule requires). Every `superseded_with_current_answer` item (6 of 9) splits evenly 3/3 between concordant and contradictory.

**On the 3/9 (one third) contradictory-direction rate specifically:** at the point item 14 (RB-0008) was being decided, only RB-0006 existed as a contradictory-direction item, and the reasoning at the time treated a second contradictory item as rare (roughly 2 of 9 expected). RB-0009, recorded afterward, also turned out contradictory on inspection, moving the observed rate to 3 of 9. **This is recorded here as a tracked quantity, not reported in the paper's Results section yet** -- n=9 is too small for a one-third rate to carry any real precision, and section 4 (Results) is deliberately unwritten until the benchmark is closer to final size (ADR-027). When results are written, `evidence_direction` is stratified in reporting, not pooled, matching the treatment already given to `claim_polarity` (ADR-057) and `evidence_level` (ADR-032) -- a model's plausible failure mode differs by direction (missing a *reversal* is arguably a more dangerous failure than missing a magnitude narrowing on a concordant claim), and pooling would hide that.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` section 3.6.5 (fields annotated per item) lists `evidence_direction`. Section 4.5's TODO stub notes the planned stratification. Every future item must have this field annotated at recording time, in the same annotation pass that produces `no_answer_reason` or `claim_polarity` -- not backfilled after the fact, now that this ADR has done the one necessary backfill for the 9 items that predate the field.

---

## ADR-080: The retrieval-ceiling ordering prediction (ADR-049), softened at n=1 (ADR-050), is restored and strengthened at n=9 -- the softening itself was a pre-registration discipline lapse, recorded as a methodological finding

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-049 pre-registered, before any item existed, that the supersession notice would rank below its own trap source "in the large majority of items" (the ordering prediction). ADR-050, written after RB-0001 was the only annotated item, found a 1-rank gap (notice rank 2 vs. trap rank 1) and used that single data point to soften the paper's framing: it introduced a second, weaker "practical invisibility" prediction, correctly separated as a distinct claim from ordering, but also shifted the project's working narrative toward "notices are retrievable, mildly subordinate" and explicitly flagged the stronger, more novel-sounding "infrastructure is structurally biased" framing as one the project should be suspicious of preferring. That shift in framing was made on n=1.

At n=9 (8 items with a notice document; RB-0005 has none, ADR-071, and is excluded rather than counted either way), the ordering prediction now reads: **6 of 8 items (75%) have `notice_rank > trap_source_rank`** -- RB-0001, RB-0002, RB-0003, RB-0004, RB-0006, RB-0009 are consistent; RB-0007 and RB-0008 run the other way (ADR-077). `paper/paper.md` section 3.11 currently states this as "consistent... in 7 of 9 items," which both uses the wrong denominator (9, when only 8 items have a notice to rank at all) and the wrong count (7, when the correct count against the 8-item denominator is 6) -- corrected in this ADR and in the paper. 75% is squarely "the large majority" ADR-049 predicted before any data existed. The practical-invisibility prediction remains unconfirmed at n=9 exactly as it was at n=1: 0 of 8 items have `notice_rank > PRACTICAL_K (10)`.

**The finding that matters more than the corrected arithmetic:** ADR-049's original prediction was made on zero data, which is what pre-registration is supposed to look like. ADR-050's softening was made on **one** data point -- also inadequate data, but treated as grounds to revise the framing rather than simply recorded and left for more evidence to confirm or overturn. At n=9, the evidence has moved back toward the original, unsoftened prediction, and moved further in that direction than a single 1-rank gap suggested it might: two items now show a 2-rank gap (RB-0004, RB-0006), and Obstetrics/Gynecology's notice recall@1 is only 0.25 across the domain. Both the original prediction and the subsequent revision were acted on before enough data existed to justify either -- the difference is that pre-registering ahead of any data is the correct discipline, while revising a pre-registered prediction's framing on a single subsequent item is exactly the practice pre-registration exists to prevent. This project did that once, and it was wrong to.

**Decision:** Recorded as a methodological finding for the paper, not filed away as a private lesson: `paper/paper.md` section 3.11 is rewritten to state the full sequence (ADR-049's zero-data prediction -> ADR-050's one-item softening -> ADR-077/this ADR's nine-item reversion) plainly, including that the softening was premature. A `Limitations` bullet records the general lesson: a pre-registered prediction should not be revised in response to a single early item; the correct response to an unexpected n=1 result is to record it and wait, which this project did not do the first time. No change to the two-prediction structure itself (ordering vs. practical invisibility) -- that separation, introduced in ADR-050, remains correct and is kept.

**Consequences:** Section 4.5 (Results, still `[TODO]`) will report both predictions' final outcomes at full benchmark size, per ADR-050's original instruction, unchanged by this ADR. This ADR's contribution is the corrected n=9 arithmetic and the explicit methodological narrative connecting ADR-049 -> ADR-050 -> now, which belongs in the paper as a demonstrated example of the discipline pre-registration is meant to enforce -- including a demonstrated failure of that discipline, corrected once more data arrived rather than left uncorrected.

---

## ADR-081: Notice length correlates with notice rank (Pearson r=0.80, Spearman rho=0.77, n=8) -- a post-hoc hypothesis prompted by the RB-0007/RB-0008 anomaly, explicitly not pre-registered, reported with that caveat attached

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-077 flagged RB-0007 and RB-0008 -- both with a confirmed-`bare`, fully empty (`notice_text=None`) notice -- as the first items where the notice ranks *above* its trap source, and hypothesized a document-length effect without testing it. Tested directly: for all 8 items with a notice document, character length of the indexed notice text (`f"Notice: {title}" + " " + (notice_text or "")`, matching the exact string `corpus/embed_index.py` embeds) plotted against `notice_rank`:

| Item | Notice length (chars) | `notice_specificity` | `notice_rank` | `trap_source_rank` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| RB-0008 | 121 | bare | 1 | 2 |
| RB-0002 | 127 | bare | 2 | 1 |
| RB-0007 | 159 | bare | 1 | 2 |
| RB-0001 | 463 | categorical | 2 | 1 |
| RB-0003 | 627 | unverifiable | 2 | 1 |
| RB-0004 | 747 | unverifiable | 3 | 1 |
| RB-0009 | 814 | specific | 2 | 1 |
| RB-0006 | 913 | categorical | 3 | 1 |

**Pearson r = 0.7985, Spearman rho = 0.7715, n=8.** The relationship is not a clean bare-vs.-not split -- RB-0002 is also `bare` (127 chars) but still ranks 2, worse than its trap source, contradicting a simple "any bare notice outranks its trap source" reading of ADR-077's hypothesis. The cleaner pattern is continuous: shorter notices tend toward better (lower-numbered) ranks across the full length range, not just at the bare/non-bare boundary.

**This was not pre-registered.** It is a hypothesis generated by looking at the two anomalous items and then tested against the same 8 items that motivated it -- a textbook post-hoc test, not an independent confirmation, and the correlation coefficient should be read accordingly: suggestive, not confirmatory, at n=8. **No strong claim is made from this ADR.** If the pattern holds as more items are annotated, the substantive interpretation is unusually sharp for this benchmark's purpose: `notice_specificity` (ADR-033) measures how much a notice explains about *why* a source was retracted, and length is a rough proxy for that explanatory content -- a notice that merely repeats the paper's own title shares maximal lexical overlap with a question about that paper's topic, while a notice that actually names the mechanism of the error introduces vocabulary the question is unlikely to contain, and may rank worse for exactly that reason. If confirmed, that would mean the more informative a correction record is, the less retrievable it becomes under dense-embedding cosine similarity -- a specific, mechanistic, and perverse property of the correction record itself, not of any model under test, and precisely the class of failure this benchmark exists to surface.

**Decision:** Recorded as a hypothesis with its current support, not adopted as a finding. `results/notice_length_correlation.md` holds the full computation and framing for reuse once more items exist. If the author wants to test this at scale, it should be formally pre-registered before the next batch of items is annotated (ADR-032/043/049 precedent) rather than left as a standing post-hoc observation re-tested informally each time.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` section 3.11 reports this alongside the ordering/invisibility predictions, explicitly labeled as exploratory and not pre-registered, with the exact r/rho/n stated so a reader can weigh it correctly. Section 4.5's TODO stub is updated to require this hypothesis be formally tested (not just narrated) once enough items exist for the correlation to carry real power -- n=8 is far too small to support the substantive interpretation above as anything but a hypothesis worth continuing to track.

---

## ADR-082: Item 17's "replacement" identification was checked against structured correction metadata, not just search summaries, and does not hold up -- corrected in the raw material, not resolved unilaterally

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** `results/item17_raw_material.md` (prior pass) identified the *Scientific Reports* 2020 paper (PMID 32782369) as the replacement JAMA Oncology's "Notice of Retraction and Replacement" refers to, on the strength of an exact author-list match and topical similarity, explicitly flagged as "well-supported... not yet a primary-source confirmation. Verify directly before drafting." That verification was run this pass against PubMed's and Europe PMC's own `CommentsCorrectionsList` structured metadata (not a search summary): the original paper (PMID 30267080) carries exactly one correction link, `RefType=RetractedandRepublishedIn`, to **PMID 31021386 -- the notice itself** (JAMA Oncol. 2019;5(6):913-914), which links back with the mirrored type. PMID 32782369 carries no structured correction link to either record, in either direction. The straightforward reading: JAMA's short (2-page) notice format is itself the replacement publication, per a documented JAMA convention for corrections that don't require a wholly new paper -- not a pointer to a separate, later study in a different journal.

Checked independently for whether this changes PMID 32782369's usability at all: its own full text (PMC7419516, open access) cites the retracted paper only as motivating background and runs its own independent, non-overlapping 404-patient cohort to test a specific confounder in the original's design -- it does not pool the retracted paper's data, so it is not disqualified by ADR-058's independence rule the way the first candidate source (Oh et al. 2022, ADR-076) was. It is, however, not "the replacement" in the structural sense the notice metadata implies.

**Decision:** The raw material is corrected in place (`results/item17_raw_material.md`), not silently replaced with a new confident resolution -- the file now states plainly that the prior identification does not hold up, what the metadata actually shows, and that PMID 32782369 remains usable as *independent adjacent evidence* if relabeled as such, distinct from "the replacement." The actual replacement content (the notice's own paywalled text, pp. 913-914, PMID 31021386) has still not been read directly in any pass. Per the project protocol (decisions with research consequences are the author's, not to be resolved unilaterally), this ADR does not choose between drafting item 17 now from the Scientific Reports paper (correctly relabeled) or holding until the notice text itself can be read -- both are recorded as live options for the author to choose between.

**Consequences:** Item 17 is **not** ready to draft as the prior session's raw material claimed. `docs/STATUS.md`'s "item 17 ready to draft" status is stale and must be corrected. This is the second time this specific item has had a source identification overturned by a deeper check after an initial pass judged it usable (the first was the independence failure on Oh et al. 2022, ADR-076) -- both times the error was caught before it reached a gold claim, by design (raw material is explicitly provisional, `BenchmarkItem` records are not written until the author drafts from confirmed material). Worth naming as a pattern for the paper's annotation-protocol discussion: author-list matching and topical similarity are not sufficient to identify a formal journal replacement, and Retraction Watch tags alone would not have caught this either -- only checking PubMed's/Europe PMC's own correction-link metadata directly did.

---

## ADR-083: Item 17 deferred rather than drafted from adjacent evidence -- the undiscoverable replacement is a fifth, distinct "record fails to deliver" shape, and it sharpens ADR-081's correlation into a reachability hypothesis

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-082 found that item 17's previously identified "replacement" (the *Scientific Reports* 2020 paper) is not what JAMA's structured correction metadata actually points to -- that metadata links the original exclusively back to the notice itself (PMID 31021386, paywalled), and no institutional access to read it has been available. The author's explicit decision: **hold, do not draft from the *Scientific Reports* paper relabeled as adjacent evidence.** The item's entire value is that it is a retract-and-replace case, where the correct gold claim is specifically "cite the replacement, not the original" (ADR-069) -- a structurally different, harder test than an ordinary void retraction. Drafting from adjacent evidence instead would produce a gold claim of the ordinary "no institutional source re-confirms this, cite current guidance" shape, silently discarding the one property that made this candidate worth the two research passes already spent on it.

**Decision:** Logged as `DeferredCandidate` (`rw-20361`, ADR-078's mechanism), reason: replacement not identifiable from structured metadata; official notice paywalled; revisit if institutional access appears. `results/item17_raw_material.md` marked deferred at the top, kept in full as valid research material for a future pass rather than discarded.

**This failure is itself a reportable finding, not just an annotation setback.** JAMA published a formal "Notice of Retraction and Replacement" -- the machine-readable correction infrastructure worked exactly as designed, correctly linking the retracted record to its correction. And a reader (or an automated system) following that link correctly arrives at a document they cannot read, with no further structured pointer to what should be cited instead. This is a fifth, distinct shape in the "the record fails to deliver what a downstream system would need" pattern this project has now measured four other instances of:

1. **Retraction status itself not machine-readable** -- PubMed's own `pubtype` field omits the `Retracted Publication` tag on a real, confirmed retraction (ADR-028/029, 7.4% forward gap).
2. **Notice uninformative** -- the notice is reachable and verifiable but states no mechanism (`notice_specificity = bare`, ADR-033).
3. **Original claim erased** -- the retracted paper's own abstract is overwritten by retraction boilerplate in the very record meant to preserve it (ADR-065, ~1 in 16 candidates).
4. **No notice document at all** -- Retraction Watch's `retraction_pubmed_id` equals the original paper's own PMID; there is nothing separate to retrieve (ADR-071, 5/296).
5. **Replacement exists but is not discoverable** (this ADR) -- the correction mechanism worked, the replacement exists, and it cannot be identified from any structured source available without the specific paywalled document itself.

Shapes 1, 2, and 4 are failures of the *pointer* (no tag, no content, no separate document). Shape 3 is destruction of the *original*. Shape 5 is new in kind: the pointer works, and what it points to is unreachable -- the infrastructure did its job and the content behind it is still inaccessible. Worth stating in the paper as the sharpest version of this project's upstream-infrastructure argument (ADR-029's framing): even a retraction handled by the book, end to end, can still leave no discoverable path to the correct citation.

**Connection to ADR-081, recorded explicitly per the author's instruction:** ADR-081 found that shorter, title-only notices retrieve *better* (rank above their trap source) than notices carrying substantive text, at n=8 and explicitly not pre-registered. Item 17's notice is a limiting case in the same direction from a different angle: its content isn't merely short, it is entirely inaccessible (paywalled), and the one item in this benchmark's queue where a notice's actual explanatory content was sought hardest is also the one item that had to be deferred rather than drafted. **Stated as a hypothesis, not a finding, exactly as ADR-081 was:** the notices that carry real explanatory content are disproportionately the ones that are paywalled or otherwise hard to reach, while the notices that retrieve well under dense-embedding similarity are disproportionately the ones that say nothing -- both point the same direction, that **the more a correction actually says, the harder it is for anyone, human or machine, to reach it.** This is stated explicitly here so a future pass with a larger sample (ideally including a measured paywall/open-access rate alongside notice length, not length alone) can test it properly; this project's own n is far too small to claim it.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` section 3.8 gains the five-way taxonomy, replacing/extending ADR-065's three-way framing rather than contradicting it (shapes 2 and 3 above are ADR-065's shapes 2 and 3 renamed for the broader list; ADR-065's shape 1, `notice_specificity=unverifiable`, is a sub-case of "notice uninformative" for this taxonomy's purposes and not double-counted as a sixth). `docs/STATUS.md` updated: item 17 closed out, no open candidates remain in the queue pending the next batch. The reachability hypothesis is noted for the next batch's pre-screening: worth recording open-access status per candidate going forward, not just `notice_specificity` and length, since this ADR's hypothesis needs that data to be testable later.

---

## ADR-084: Notice length measured across the full 296-candidate pool -- most notices are short (median 163 chars, 65% under 200), consistent with ADR-081's correlation but not a test of it; also surfaces an unreconciled gap against ADR-071's earlier count

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-081 found notice length correlates with notice rank at n=8, explicitly not pre-registered and explicitly not claimed as a finding. Before pursuing that correlation further, the author asked a prior, simpler question: how much of the full candidate pool even has short, title-dominant notices versus substantive ones -- if most notices are short, the correlation's implication (short notices retrieve well) applies to most of the pool by default, which changes how the eventual finding, if confirmed, should be framed.

**Method:** For all 296 unique candidates, Retraction Watch's own `RetractionPubMedID` field (`data/raw/retraction_watch_2026-08-05.csv`) identifies the notice PMID directly -- no need to re-derive it from PubMed's correction-link metadata the way ADR-082's item-17 check did. Candidates with no separate notice document (`RetractionPubMedID` blank/zero, or equal to the candidate's own `OriginalPaperPubMedID`, ADR-071's criterion) are excluded. For the remainder, each notice PMID's own title + abstract was fetched via batched PubMed EFetch and its character length recorded. Full methodology, the batching/caching mechanism, and the exact query: `results/pool_notice_length_distribution.{md,json}`, `data/interim/pool_notice_length_cache.json` (gitignored, regenerable).

**Result:** 33/296 (11.1%) have no separate notice document -- 26 blank/zero `RetractionPubMedID`, 7 same-PMID-as-original. Of the remaining 263 with a measurable notice, **median length is 163 characters, mean 336 (right-skewed), and 65.0% are under 200 characters** -- squarely the range RB-0007 (159) and RB-0008 (121) occupy. Only 9.9% exceed 1000 characters.

**This does not test ADR-081's correlation** -- no retrieval ceiling was run against the 296-candidate pool, only length was measured. What it establishes is the premise: if notice length does predict retrieval rank the way the n=8 result suggests, **most notices in this pool would be expected to retrieve well precisely because they say nothing**, since most of them are short by a wide margin. Combined with ADR-083's finding that item 17's notice -- one of the few genuinely sought out for its explanatory content -- turned out paywalled and unreadable rather than merely short, the same hypothesis is now visible from two independent angles: **the more a correction actually says, the harder it is for anyone, human or machine, to reach it.** Recorded explicitly as a hypothesis for a future pass with real statistical power (running the retrieval ceiling at pool scale, and adding Retraction Watch's own `Paywalled` field as a covariate, not used here), not claimed as a finding -- this project's own standing rule for anything discovered by looking at the data rather than predicted in advance.

**An unreconciled discrepancy, flagged rather than smoothed over:** this measurement's "no separate notice document" count (33/296, 11.1%) does not match ADR-071's previously reported "5 of 296." Breaking down this measurement's 33: the 7-candidate same-PMID-as-original bucket is close to but not identical to ADR-071's 5 (a small gap, possibly a raw-data snapshot difference or a stricter join, not investigated further here), and the 26-candidate blank/zero-`RetractionPubMedID` bucket **was not previously measured or reported at all** -- a materially larger, previously unstated share of the pool. Neither number is corrected in place; both are recorded as they stand, with the gap named as something a future pass should resolve by re-running ADR-071's original check against the same raw CSV row-for-row before either figure is treated as final in the paper.

**Consequences:** `results/pool_notice_length_distribution.{md,json}` committed; `data/interim/pool_notice_length_cache.json` gitignored (regenerable via the scratchpad script this ADR's measurement used). `paper/paper.md` section 3.11 (or a cross-reference from section 3.8's five-shape taxonomy) reports the pool-wide distribution alongside ADR-081's n=8 correlation, both labeled exploratory, with the reachability hypothesis stated explicitly and not claimed. The `RetractionPubMedID`-vs-ADR-071 reconciliation is flagged as an open item, not resolved.

---

## ADR-085: Next batch pre-screened -- 4 candidates ready to draft (2 Pharmacology, 2 Obstetrics/Gynecology), independence checked during pre-screening throughout per ADR-076, plus five informative near-misses worth recording

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The author authorized a next batch of 5 candidates, Pharmacology and Obstetrics/Gynecology only (ADR-075), balanced across the two, pre-screened with the independence check moved earlier into pre-screening rather than deferred to drafting (ADR-076). This is the first batch prepared entirely under ADR-076's 4-stage protocol from the start.

**Mechanizable pre-filter, run before any manual research:** of 296 unique candidates, excluding the 18 already used (9 annotated, 6 rejected, 3 deferred), restricted to the two active domains, requiring abstract-intact (`results/abstract_overwrite_measurement.json`, `original_retained`) and notice-reachable (`results/pool_notice_length_distribution.json`, ADR-084), and excluding any candidate whose author list overlaps >50% with any of the 9 recorded items' author sets (ADR-070) -- **177 candidates remained (93 Pharmacology, 84 Obstetrics/Gynecology)**, all four checks computed from data already gathered this session rather than re-derived. This is a reusable pre-filter for future batches, not a one-off.

**Manual research and independence-checking, ~12 candidates examined to reach 4 solid results:**

**Successes (raw material at `results/item{18,19,20,21}_raw_material.md`):**

1. **Item 18** (rw-20302, Pharmacology) -- Ghaderi et al.'s retracted NAC-schizophrenia meta-analysis. Evidence: Yolland et al. 2020 (PMID 31826654), independence confirmed directly (its 56-reference list does not cite the retracted paper; it pools 7 primary RCTs by their own PMIDs instead). `evidence_direction = concordant`, with two hedges from the current evidence (negative/total PANSS only, not positive; effect only at >=24 weeks) that the trap claim does not carry.
2. **Item 19** (rw-31612, Pharmacology) -- Wang et al.'s retracted vortioxetine RCT. Evidence: a 2023 real-world systematic review (PMID 37105713), independence confirmed on two grounds -- absent from its 65-reference list, *and* its inclusion criteria categorically exclude RCTs by design, so the retracted study could not have been eligible regardless. `evidence_direction = concordant`.
3. **Item 20** (rw-25419, Obstetrics/Gynecology) -- Apanga & Kumbeni's retracted Ghana breastfeeding-prevalence analysis, a "Retract and Replace" case. **The replacement was found and verified directly** -- not discoverable via PubMed's structured correction metadata (the same gap ADR-083 names as the fifth shape), but found by a direct author+title search: same authors, same title, published 2.5 months after the retraction notice, sample size corrected from 15,305 to 3,466 exactly as the notice described. This is the resolution item 17 could not reach. Prevalence is nearly unchanged (51.3%->52.3%, concordant), but the trap claim's largest specific association (antenatal care, aPOR 2.01) does not appear among the corrected predictors at all -- a specific, attributable loss worth carrying into the gold claim rather than a topline-only comparison.
4. **Item 21** (rw-20485, Obstetrics/Gynecology) -- Heath et al.'s retracted Colorado elevation/HDP null finding (`claim_polarity = negative`, the second negative-polarity candidate in this project's history after RB-0009). Evidence: a 2021 systematic review (PMID 34023533) finds a significant pooled HDP association (OR 1.31, P<.05) the retracted paper did not, with an internal gestational-hypertension/preeclampsia split showing opposite-direction sub-effects. `evidence_direction = contradictory` on the same pooled outcome category the trap claim used. **One open point, not resolved in this pass:** independence is argued by timing (the evidence source's search window postdates the retraction by 18 months) rather than confirmed via reference list -- Europe PMC's references API returned no data for this record, a data gap, not a confirmed-clean check. Flagged for direct full-text verification before drafting.

**Balance achieved: 2 Pharmacology, 2 Obstetrics/Gynecology**, not the requested 5, after ~12 candidates were examined and the remaining leads either failed independence or ran out of reasonable search effort. Consistent with -- if anything better than -- the ~10-15% no-institutional-source rejection rate measured in the original eligibility pilot (ADR-023/024/036), once the mechanizable pre-filter's own attrition is accounted for separately.

**Five informative near-misses, recorded because each is itself a small methodological data point, not merely a dead end:**

1. **rw-17838** (Menatetrenone/vitamin K2, Iwamoto/Matsumoto/Takeda cluster) -- excluded before any research spent, same author-group unit as item 7 (deferred) and rw-6531 (rejected for redundancy). A third appearance of this specific cluster in the pool.
2. **rw-26609167** (Vitamin D/depression, Sepehrmanesh et al.) -- the most natural evidence source, a 2024 *Psychological Medicine* dose-response meta-analysis, **directly confirmed via full text to pool the retracted trial's own data** in its subgroup analyses (a clean ADR-058 disqualification, same failure mode as item 17's first candidate). Vitamin-D/depression syntheses published before authors routinely exclude retracted primary studies appear structurally likely to have this problem -- worth a note for future candidates on this exact topic.
3. **rw-22856/rw-23410/rw-24742** (Maher & Sayyed cluster, three retracted Egyptian RCTs on sildenafil/cervical-mucus/nifedipine topics) -- one author-group unit under ADR-070 (>=66.7% pairwise overlap), capping this batch to at most one use; none used this round given the independence problems found on the sildenafil candidate specifically (below), leaving up to 2 uses of this cluster still available for a future batch.
4. **rw-28277352** (sildenafil for oligohydramnios, part of the Maher cluster above) -- the most topically rich candidate found this pass (its drug class carries a genuine, major, cross-indication safety signal: the Dutch STRIDER trial's data-safety-monitoring-board recommendation against antenatal sildenafil for fetal growth restriction, a *different* indication from this trap claim's oligohydramnios population). The most obvious evidence source, a 2019 PLOS One systematic review, **directly confirmed to cite the retracted trial in its reference list** -- disqualified. A post-2020 synthesis specific to oligohydramnios (not just FGR) would be needed to use this candidate; not found within this pass's search budget. Worth revisiting given how substantively interesting the underlying safety question is.
5. **rw-30688986** (antibiotic treatment for asthma exacerbation, Stefan et al., *JAMA Internal Medicine*) -- **a second real-world instance of ADR-083's fifth shape**, independently of item 17: another JAMA "Notice of Retraction and Replacement," structured metadata linking the retraction exclusively to a short (569-570), paywalled notice record, not to any independently discoverable replacement. Unlike item 20's successful resolution, no author+title search was attempted for this one within the batch's time budget (flagged as a candidate for a future pass, not concluded to have the same successful-replacement resolution item 20 found).
6. **rw-29121934** (rosuvastatin endothelial biomarkers) and a thoracic epidural/paravertebral block candidate (rw-27790554) -- no institutional-tier source could be found (rosuvastatin's PON-1/ADMA surrogate-marker claim is too narrow for guideline-level coverage), or the source found was itself contaminated (a 2021 thoracic-block meta-analysis pools the retracted trial; a 2025 alternative could not be independence-checked within budget, Europe PMC reference data unavailable for it) -- both dropped rather than forced.

**Consequences:** `results/item18_raw_material.md` through `item21_raw_material.md` are ready for the author to draft from (item 21 pending its one flagged verification). No `BenchmarkItem` records are written by this ADR -- per this project's standing convention, raw material is provisional until the author drafts from it (ADR-040), and corpus/retrieval-ceiling updates happen only once real items exist. `docs/STATUS.md` updated to reflect the new queue state.

---

## ADR-086: Retract-and-replace discoverability is now a tracked quantity -- 1 of 2 cases resolved (item 20), and the method that worked is a direct author+title search, not a structured-metadata lookup

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Two retract-and-replace candidates have now been fully worked: item 17 (record 20361, deferred, ADR-082/083) and item 20 (record 25419, resolved, ADR-085). Structured PubMed/Europe PMC correction metadata (`CommentsCorrectionsList`) failed to identify the replacement in **both** cases -- for item 17 it links the original exclusively to the paywalled notice itself; for item 20 it carries no "RetractedandRepublishedIn" entry at all, only a plain "Retraction of" link back to the original. Structured metadata is 0-for-2 as a discovery method across every retract-and-replace case this project has actually worked. Item 20's replacement was still found -- not through metadata, but by re-running the exact search a human annotator would run by hand.

**The reproducible method, stated precisely so anyone repeating this benchmark's construction can use it directly:** when a "Retract and Replace" notice states or implies a replacement was or will be published, and structured correction metadata does not name it, search PubMed (`ESearch`) for the retracted paper's **exact title text plus its author surnames**, not for its PMID or DOI. Item 20's replacement (PMID 33865418) shares the original's exact title and both authors' names, and was the only additional record such a search returned beyond the original and its notice -- confirmed, not assumed, by checking that the returned record's own abstract describes the specific correction the notice named (here, a changed sample size, 15,305 -> 3,466, matching the notice's stated reason "affected the overall sample size" exactly). **A title+author match alone is not sufficient confirmation** -- item 17's own raw material initially made exactly this mistake with the *Scientific Reports* paper (ADR-082), matching on authors and topic without checking the correction metadata or the paper's own claimed relationship to the retraction. The distinguishing step: the found candidate's abstract must independently corroborate the notice's own stated reason for the retraction, not merely share a topic or author list.

**Tracked quantity, recorded here for exactly this purpose -- to be updated as more retract-and-replace cases are worked, not treated as final at n=2:** **1 of 2 retract-and-replace cases resolved (50%).** This is not reported as a benchmark-wide rate in the paper (n=2 is far too small, the same discipline already applied to ADR-081's n=8 and ADR-084's pool-wide measurement) -- it is recorded so the number exists to update, rather than being reconstructed from memory each time a new case is worked.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` section 3.8 (the five-shape taxonomy) gains a note that shape 5 (replacement undiscoverable) is not universal across retract-and-replace cases -- it is what happened for item 17 specifically, not a property of the retract-and-replace mechanism itself; item 20 is the counterexample proving the mechanism can and does work. `docs/ANNOTATION.md` gains the title+author search method as a named step for retract-and-replace candidates, to run before concluding a replacement is undiscoverable (item 17 itself should be revisited with this exact method if institutional access to its paywalled notice does not appear first -- not yet attempted, flagged as a next step rather than assumed to fail).

---

## ADR-087: ADR-084's pool-wide notice-length measurement had a join bug -- ADR-071's original "5 of 296" was correct all along; corrected figures recorded, every downstream citation of the wrong numbers fixed

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-084 measured 33/296 candidates with no separate notice document (26 blank/zero `RetractionPubMedID`, 7 same-PMID-as-original) and flagged, without resolving, that this did not match ADR-071's earlier "5 of 296." Investigated directly, per the author's instruction to resolve rather than leave flagged.

**Root cause found:** ADR-084's script joined each candidate to a Retraction Watch row by `OriginalPaperPubMedID`, deduplicating "first row wins" when a PMID matched more than one raw row. **37 of the 296 candidate PMIDs have more than one row in Retraction Watch's raw data** -- overwhelmingly Expression-of-Concern-then-Retraction pairs (two correction *events* for the same paper, each logged as its own Retraction Watch record with its own `Record ID` and its own `RetractionPubMedID`), plus a small number of apparent data duplicates. `tier1_candidates_final3_verified.csv` already resolved this correctly at candidate-selection time -- each candidate carries a specific `record_id`, which is authoritative -- but ADR-084's script re-joined by PMID instead of by that `record_id`, and for candidates whose "first row" (by raw-file iteration order) was not the one the candidate pool actually selected, it silently pulled the wrong `RetractionPubMedID`. Two of these mismatches happened to land in the same-PMID-as-original bucket, inflating ADR-071's true 5 to a false 7; the remainder scattered across the other buckets, inflating 5/296 to 33/296 overall and corrupting 14 of the 263 notice-length values ADR-084 reported (9 wrong notice PMIDs measured, 5 candidates wrongly excluded as having no notice when they do).

**Which criterion is correct:** ADR-071's, unchanged -- `RetractionPubMedID` blank/zero, or equal to the candidate's own `OriginalPaperPubMedID`, joined **by `record_id`, not by PMID**. Re-run with the corrected join: **same-PMID-as-original = 5, exactly matching ADR-071.** ADR-071 was right; ADR-084's figures were wrong. No ambiguity in which criterion governs -- the bug was entirely in the join, not in a definitional disagreement.

**Corrected pool-wide figures (supersede ADR-084's in full):**

| | ADR-084 (wrong) | Corrected |
|---|---|---|
| No separate notice document, same-PMID-as-original | 7 | **5** (matches ADR-071) |
| No separate notice document, blank/zero | 26 | **23** |
| No separate notice document, total | 33 (11.1%) | **28 (9.5%)** |
| Notice length measured | 263 | **268** |
| Median length | 163 chars | **161 chars** |
| Mean length | 336.0 | **333.7** |
| Fraction under 200 chars | 65.0% | **66.4%** |
| Fraction over 1000 chars | 9.9% | **10.1%** |

The substantive conclusion (most notices in this pool are short) is unchanged and, if anything, marginally stronger under the corrected figures -- this was not a case where fixing the bug also overturned the finding it was attached to.

**Items 18-21 checked and confirmed unaffected:** none of their four original PMIDs (30446769, 29113521, 33143742, 30384782) is among the 37 with duplicate Retraction Watch rows, and the corrected measurement's notice PMIDs for all four (30972452, 34269645, 33541363, 31104530 respectively) match exactly what `results/item18-21_raw_material.md` already used. The author's in-progress drafting of items 18-20 rests on unaffected data.

**Every other reported figure checked for the same bug, not assumed clean by association:** the abstract-overwrite measurement (ADR-065/`results/abstract_overwrite_measurement.json`) fetches by PMID directly against PubMed (original papers, not Retraction Watch notice rows) and does not join through `RetractionPubMedID` at all -- not exposed to this bug. The author-cluster analysis (ADR-031/066) and the duplicate-record correction (ADR-066) join on `record_id` throughout, per their own methodology sections -- also not exposed. This bug is specific to ADR-084's script and does not generalize to other measurements in this project.

**Decision:** `results/pool_notice_length_distribution.{md,json}` overwritten in place with the corrected figures and the corrected methodology note (data files, not an ADR -- append-only applies to this decisions log, not to results artifacts, which are meant to reflect the current best measurement). `paper/paper.md`'s two citations of the wrong 33/296 and 65.0%/163-char figures (section 3.8's five-shape taxonomy, section 3.11's ADR-081 cross-reference) corrected to the values above. `docs/DECISIONS.md` itself is append-only -- ADR-084 is not edited in place; this ADR supersedes its specific figures and is the citation point for the correct ones going forward.

**Consequences:** Any future citation of "no separate notice document" pool-wide should cite ADR-087 (or ADR-071, whose figure this confirms), not ADR-084. The reusable mechanizable pre-filter noted in ADR-085 (used to select items 18-21's candidate pool) was run using the *original, buggy* `pool_notice_length_distribution.json` -- checked directly above and confirmed items 18-21 are unaffected, but a future batch drawing on a wider set of the 268-vs-263 candidates should use the now-corrected file, already overwritten in place.

---

## ADR-088: Item 21's independence confirmed directly (not by timing); items 18-20 recorded as RB-0010/0011/0012; retrieval ceiling re-run across 12 items -- notice-above-source pattern now n=3, ADR-081's correlation holds at n=11

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Per the author's explicit instruction ("do not accept a timing argument as independence... try once more to reach the review's reference list"), a second route to item 21's evidence source (PMID 34023533) was tried after Europe PMC's references API returned no data. The paper's DOI resolves via Unpaywall to an open-access accepted-manuscript copy at the Cambridge University repository. Fetched and read in full (187,494 characters, ending cleanly at the figure legends, not truncated) -- searched directly for the retracted paper's authors (Heath, Hyde, Norris) and title fragments ("Investigation of Elevation", "Elevation as a Risk Factor"): no match anywhere, including the complete 43-item reference list. **Independence is now directly confirmed, the same standard used for items 18-20, not argued by timing.** `results/item21_raw_material.md` updated accordingly; the item was no longer held on independence grounds by the time the author drafted it.

**Three items recorded** (author-written trap/gold claim text per ADR-040, exactly as supplied):

- **RB-0010** (item 18, Pharmacology, NAC-schizophrenia) -- `evidence_direction=concordant`, `evidence_tier_match=same_tier` (synthesis superseded by synthesis, ADR-089). A partial-outcome mismatch was flagged by the author (trap claim covers three PANSS subscales, current evidence explicitly reports only two) and deliberately **not** given a schema field -- recorded in the item's annotation note only, consistent with this project's "start minimal, add a field only once it recurs" pattern (`no_answer_reason`, `evidence_tier_match` were each added this way). If a second item shows the same shape, formalize it; not before.
- **RB-0011** (item 19, Pharmacology, vortioxetine) -- `evidence_direction=concordant`, **first use of `evidence_tier_match=lower_tier`** (ADR-089): current evidence is a synthesis of real-world observational studies that categorically excludes RCTs by design, superseding a placebo-controlled RCT -- a genuine step down the evidence hierarchy that `source_authority=systematic_review` alone cannot distinguish from RB-0005/RB-0006/RB-0009's systematic-reviews-of-RCTs (all `same_tier`).
- **RB-0012** (item 20, Obstetrics/Gynecology, Ghana breastfeeding, retract-and-replace) -- `evidence_direction=contradictory`, `replacement_exists=true`, `evidence_tier_match=same_tier`. The prevalence figure barely moves (51.3%->52.3%) while the trap claim's largest specific association (antenatal care, aPOR 2.01) disappears entirely from the corrected analysis. The author flagged this as arguing for scoring the item's two claims (prevalence, antenatal-care association) separately rather than as one answer -- noted for the eval module's future design (M1-M3 not yet built), not acted on now.

**Corpus updated and retrieval ceiling re-run across all 12 items** (`corpus/embed_index.add_documents()`, ADR-054/064/077's mechanism): 9 documents added (3 benchmark sources, 3 notices -- RB-0011's is bare, `notice_text=None`, embedded as title-only per the established convention -- and 3 evidence entries, all PMID-resolvable, none skipped). Corpus: 400,564 -> 400,573; hash `30dbe86e...` -> `fcd84598...`.

| Item | Domain | trap_rank | notice_rank | gap |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| RB-0010 | Pharmacology | 1 | 3 | 2 |
| RB-0011 | Pharmacology | 2 | 1 | -1 |
| RB-0012 | Obstetrics/Gynecology | 1 | 2 | 1 |

**RB-0011 joins RB-0007 and RB-0008 as a third case where the notice ranks *above* its trap source** -- all three share the same property: a confirmed-`bare`, effectively title-only notice. n=3 is still small, but three-for-three on the same shared property is a stronger pattern than the n=2 ADR-077 first reported.

**ADR-081's notice-length/notice-rank correlation re-run at n=11** (10 items with a notice document plus RB-0011, still excluding RB-0005 which has none): **Pearson r=0.7522, Spearman rho=0.7396** -- close to the original n=8 result (r=0.7985, rho=0.7715), holding up rather than collapsing as more points were added. Still explicitly exploratory and not pre-registered (ADR-081); `results/notice_length_correlation.{md,json}` updated with the n=11 data and both figures reported side by side, not overwriting the n=8 historical record.

**Consequences:** `docs/STATUS.md` updated: 12 annotated items, item 21's independence resolved (no longer an open point anywhere), items 18-21's batch fully closed out. `paper/paper.md`'s retrieval-ceiling section (3.11) and benchmark-composition figures need updating at the next paper-editing pass (not done in this ADR -- flagged, the author's instruction this turn was to record and report, not to edit the paper).

---

## ADR-089: `evidence_tier_match` added -- whether current evidence's study design is at least as rigorous as the trap claim's, or a step down (item 19's real-world-data-vs-RCT case)

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Item 19 (RB-0011)'s current evidence is a synthesis of real-world observational studies explicitly excluding RCTs, superseding a placebo-controlled RCT trap claim -- a design downgrade `source_authority` alone cannot express, since `source_authority=systematic_review` is shared with RB-0005/RB-0006/RB-0009's systematic-reviews-*of-RCTs*, which are not a downgrade. The author's instruction: add a field, `same_tier` or `lower_tier`, since a model citing observational data as though it settled a placebo-controlled question is a distinct calibration failure from citing an equally- or more-rigorous synthesis.

**Decision:** `EvidenceTierMatch` enum added (`same_tier`, `lower_tier` -- two values to start, per this project's standing pattern, ADR-063/079). `BenchmarkItem.evidence_tier_match` added, gated on `item_type` by the same pattern as `no_answer_reason`/`evidence_direction`: required (non-None) iff `item_type == SUPERSEDED_WITH_CURRENT_ANSWER`, must be `None` otherwise (there is no current evidence to compare tier against when there is no current answer). **This is a human-annotated judgment about the underlying study design, never inherited from `source_authority`** -- the field's own docstring states the RB-0011-vs-RB-0005 contrast explicitly as the reason `source_authority` cannot decide it mechanically.

**Backfilled across all 6 pre-existing `superseded_with_current_answer` items:** RB-0001, RB-0002, RB-0005, RB-0006, RB-0008, RB-0009 are all `same_tier` -- in every one of these, current evidence is a synthesis (systematic review, meta-analysis, or Cochrane review) at least as rigorous as what the trap claim rested on, several actually an upgrade (a single primary study or conference abstract superseded by a systematic review of RCTs), none a downgrade. **RB-0011 (item 19) is the first and only `lower_tier` item.** Plus RB-0010 and RB-0012 (both `same_tier`), recorded alongside RB-0011 in the same batch (ADR-088).

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` section 3.6.5 (fields annotated per item) should list `evidence_tier_match` alongside `evidence_direction`, stratified in results the same way once results exist. Currently 1/9 `with_current_answer` items (11%) are `lower_tier` -- far too few to report a rate with any precision; tracked, not claimed, the same discipline applied to `evidence_direction`'s 3/9 contradictory rate (ADR-079).

**RB-0010 is the named trigger case for a possible future `partial_outcome_match`-type field:** its partial-outcome mismatch (trap claim covers three PANSS subscales, current evidence explicitly reports improvement on only two) is recorded in the item's annotation note only (ADR-088), not given a schema field, on the same "start minimal, formalize once it recurs" basis as this field and `no_answer_reason`. If a second item shows the same shape -- current evidence answering only part of what the trap claim asserted, within an otherwise `superseded_with_current_answer` item -- formalize it then, citing RB-0010 as the first occurrence.

---

## ADR-090: The notice-above-trap-source mechanism, shown rather than only measured -- bare notices win on lexical overlap with the question, not on anything to do with supersession

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-088's notice-length/notice-rank correlation held at n=11 (r=0.7522, down only slightly from n=8's 0.7985). Per the author's instruction, a correlation coefficient does not explain a mechanism, and the three notice-above-trap-source cases (RB-0007, RB-0008, RB-0011) needed their actual embedded documents inspected directly, not just their lengths compared -- specifically to check whether a bare notice's high rank is explained by near-total lexical overlap with a question derived from the same paper, which would make the pattern a property of dense retrieval generically, not of supersession specifically.

**Investigated directly** (`results/notice_length_mechanism.md` has the full text of all six documents): all three notice-above-trap-source cases have `notice_text=None` -- their embedded document is *exactly* `f"Notice: {title}"` and nothing else. In every one, every content word in the item's `question` field appears in that title, because the question was written about the paper the title names. Three contrasting below-cases (RB-0004, RB-0006, RB-0009) also begin with the same high-overlap title, but each adds 550-800 more characters of retraction-notice boilerplate -- editor names, publisher policy references, journal-internal identifiers, procedural dates -- none of which shares vocabulary with the clinical question.

**Conclusion, stated plainly per the author's instruction not to let statistics stand in for a mechanism:** this is a straightforward lexical-overlap property of dense retrieval, not a finding about supersession. A bare notice ranks well because it is nearly identical, word for word, to a question written about the same paper; an informative notice ranks worse because any additional text uncorrelated with the question's vocabulary dilutes cosine similarity, regardless of what that text says or why it was added. The same effect would appear comparing a one-line document to a one-paragraph document on any topic, retraction or not -- there is nothing here specific to correction records as a class.

**What survives this explanation, and is not explained away by it:** the pool-wide finding that most notices in this pool *are* short (ADR-084, 66.4% under 200 characters) is a separate, real, measured fact about this corpus, and its practical implication stands independent of the mechanism: if bare notices systematically retrieve better than informative ones for a mechanistic reason that has nothing to do with correction quality, then a RAG system built on dense retrieval is, incidentally and for reasons unrelated to epistemics, more likely to surface the least explanatory version of a correction. The mechanism is mundane; the downstream consequence for what a retrieval-augmented system actually shows a user is not.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md`'s treatment of this pattern (section 3.11) must state the lexical-overlap mechanism explicitly and not imply that informative notices are somehow penalized by retrieval *because* they are informative -- the correct framing is that they are penalized because they are longer and less on-topic by a purely lexical measure, a distinction the paper must not blur. The correlation (ADR-088, n=11) and this qualitative mechanism account are both still exploratory and post-hoc; neither is upgraded to a finding by the other.

---

## ADR-091: Gold claims must contain clinical evidence only, never provenance about how this project validated it -- caught in item 21's own draft, three milder instances found on audit, none fixed without asking

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Drafting item 21/RB-0013, the author's own first-draft gold claim included a sentence stating that the evidence source "does not cite the retracted study, and independence was verified from the reference list" -- removed before recording, with the reasoning stated explicitly: the gold claim is the answer a knowledgeable current source would give, and no such source narrates this project's own verification procedure. `Evidence.independence_verified` and its `note` already exist to carry exactly that fact; the claim text is not the place for it.

**Decision:** New annotation rule (`docs/ANNOTATION.md`, adjacent to the existing gold-claim-scoping rule, ADR-034): gold claims are clinical evidence only. A gold claim narrating how this project checked a source's independence, verified a tag, or confirmed a reference list is describing the annotation process, not the medicine, and does not belong in claim text regardless of how factually accurate it is -- the same category of error ADR-034 already names for fabricated error-mechanisms, applied to a different leak direction (this project's own methodology leaking in, rather than an unstated inference leaking out).

**Audit of the 12 items recorded before this rule was written, per the author's explicit instruction not to fix anything found without asking first:** searched every existing `gold_claim.text` for verification-procedure language (`independent`, `verify`/`verified`/`verification`, `confirm`/`confirmed`, `checked`, `reference list`, `does/did not cite`). **No item contains a full sentence describing this project's verification procedure** -- item 21's original draft appears to have been the only instance of that specific, more severe pattern, and it was caught before recording. **Three items contain a milder, single-word version:** RB-0001 ("Two subsequent **independent** syntheses found..."), RB-0010 ("A 2020 **independent** meta-analysis..."), and RB-0011 ("A 2023 **independent** systematic review and meta-analysis..."). In each case "independent" describes the evidence source's relationship to the retracted paper -- a property this project verifies via `Evidence.independence_verified`, not necessarily something the source states about itself. This is a genuinely borderline case, not a clear-cut instance of the same failure: "independent replication" is also ordinary evidence-based-medicine vocabulary a clinician might use without reference to this project's specific checking method, and none of the three reads as describing a *procedure* the way item 21's removed sentence did. **Recorded as found, not fixed** -- the author's call whether the word crosses the line the new rule draws, per their explicit instruction.

**Consequences:** `docs/ANNOTATION.md` gains the rule with item 21's caught sentence as the worked example, and an explicit note about the single-word "independent" pattern to watch for on future items without treating it as automatically disqualifying. `data/benchmark/items.jsonl` is unchanged by this ADR -- RB-0001, RB-0010, and RB-0011's gold claims are exactly as recorded, pending the author's decision on the three flagged instances.

---

## ADR-092: Item 21 recorded as RB-0013 (13th item); corpus and retrieval ceiling updated; notice-length correlation now n=12 (r=0.71, still moderate, still exploratory)

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Item 21 (Colorado elevation/HDP, `results/item21_raw_material.md`) recorded as `RB-0013` -- author-written trap/gold claim text per ADR-040, `evidence_direction=contradictory`, `claim_polarity=negative` (second negative-polarity item after RB-0009, and the sharper of the two: the trap claim is a null finding, and reproducing it means denying a real risk rather than overstating a benefit), `evidence_tier_match=same_tier`, `independence_verified=true` per the direct full-text confirmation already completed in ADR-088. The author separately flagged, for the eval module's future design rather than action now: current evidence is not uniformly contradictory (gestational hypertension more common at altitude, preeclampsia less common) -- a model answering "yes" without that split is incomplete, not simply wrong, a distinct scoring consideration from a flat right/wrong judgment.

**Corpus updated** (`corpus/embed_index.add_documents()`, ADR-054/064/077/088's mechanism): 3 documents added (1 benchmark source, 1 notice -- bare, `notice_text=None` -- 1 evidence entry, PMID-resolvable). Corpus: 400,573 -> 400,576; hash `fcd84598...` -> `73688e6a...`.

**Retrieval ceiling re-run across all 13 items:** RB-0013 trap_rank=1, notice_rank=2, gap=1 -- consistent with the ordering prediction (ADR-049), not a fourth notice-above-trap-source case.

**Notice-length correlation (ADR-081/088/090) re-run at n=12:** **Pearson r=0.7142, Spearman rho=0.6828** (was r=0.7522/rho=0.7396 at n=11, r=0.7985/rho=0.7715 at n=8) -- a further gradual weakening but not a collapse, still a moderate-to-strong relationship. RB-0013 is itself `bare` (134 chars) but ranks *below* its trap source, joining RB-0002 as a second counterexample to reading "bare" as sufficient for ranking above the trap source: of 5 `bare`-notice items now recorded, 3 rank above (RB-0007, RB-0008, RB-0011) and 2 rank below (RB-0002, RB-0013) -- consistent with ADR-090's continuous lexical-overlap account, not a clean threshold at the bare/non-bare boundary. `results/notice_length_correlation.{md,json}` updated with the n=12 data; the historical n=8 and n=11 figures are kept, not overwritten. The file's "Interpretation" section was also found, in the course of this update, still carrying ADR-090's *pre-correction* framing ("the more informative a correction record is, the less retrievable it becomes... a property of the correction record itself") after the paper's own copy of that framing had already been corrected -- fixed here to match, a small consistency gap between two documents updated in the same session, caught on the next touch rather than left standing.

**Consequences:** `docs/STATUS.md` updated: 13 annotated items. `paper/paper.md` not touched by this ADR -- the author's instruction this turn was to record and report, not to edit the paper; the next paper pass should fold in RB-0013's item count, the n=12 correlation figures, and ADR-091's provenance-leak rule.

---

## ADR-093: Gold-claim provenance boundary sharpened to a testable question -- properties of the evidence are always in scope, this project's own verification acts never are

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-091 established that gold claims must not narrate this project's own verification procedure, and flagged three existing instances of the word "independent" (RB-0001, RB-0010, RB-0011) as a milder, ambiguous case without resolving whether they crossed the line, per the author's explicit instruction not to fix anything without asking first. The author's ruling: leave all three -- "independent meta-analysis" describes a property of the evidence (a separate team, a separate analysis), not this project's process for establishing that property, and is exactly the kind of language a clinician would use without reference to how this benchmark checks anything.

**Decision:** The rule in `docs/ANNOTATION.md` is rewritten as an operative test rather than a case-by-case judgment call, so future items do not each require raising the same question: **gold claims may describe properties of the evidence, including its independence from prior work, but must never describe how this project established those properties.** The test: would the sentence still make sense if this benchmark had never been built and someone else were simply describing the study? "An independent meta-analysis found X" passes. "We checked the reference list and confirmed independence" fails, because there is no "we" in a clinical answer. This resolves the three flagged instances explicitly (all pass, all left as recorded) rather than leaving them as an open item.

**Consequences:** `docs/ANNOTATION.md`'s gold-claim provenance section now states the boundary as an applicable test, with the resolved RB-0001/RB-0010/RB-0011 instances recorded as worked examples of the test passing, alongside item 21's removed sentence as the worked example of it failing. No change to `data/benchmark/items.jsonl` -- this ADR ratifies the existing text, it does not alter it.

---

## ADR-094: Term overlap, not length, is the sharper mechanistic test -- Jaccard overlap between question and notice predicts rank better than length does (|r|=0.83 vs 0.71, n=12), and a falsifiable n=30 prediction is recorded in advance

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-090 argued that bare notices rank well because they are lexically near-identical to a question written about the same paper -- but the test run there was against notice *length*, always described as a rough proxy for lexical overlap, not the causal variable itself. The author asked for the direct test: does term overlap between question and notice predict rank better than length, and does the correlation's declining trajectory (0.80 at n=8, 0.75 at n=11, 0.71 at n=12, ADR-081/088/092) look like a real, stabilizing effect or noise from an overestimating small first sample.

**Term-overlap test, run directly** (`results/notice_term_overlap.{md,json}`): question and notice text tokenized (stopwords removed), two overlap metrics computed against `notice_rank` across the same 12 items. **Recall** (fraction of question terms found in the notice) is a *worse* predictor than length: |r|=0.19. **Jaccard** (overlap normalized by the *union* of both term sets, so a notice with much vocabulary outside the question's is penalized even if it contains every question term) is a *substantially better* predictor than length: **|r|=0.83, rho=0.80** -- and this is not a stopword-list artifact: a naive tokenization with no stopword removal at all still gives |r|=0.79. **Recall's failure and Jaccard's success together identify the actual mechanism**: it is not that informative notices are missing the question's words, it is that they add enough additional vocabulary that the relevant overlap is diluted relative to the document's total content -- Jaccard measures this dilution directly; length only measures it by the imperfect assumption that longer notices dilute proportionally more. RB-0002 and RB-0013 (the two `bare`-but-ranks-below counterexamples) illustrate why length alone was never sufficient: both are short, but their titles simply share less vocabulary with their own questions than RB-0007/0008/0011's titles do with theirs.

**Correlation trajectory recorded explicitly, and a falsifiable prediction made in advance of n=30 data, per the author's explicit instruction that writing it down now is the only way to avoid rationalizing whichever number eventually appears:**

1. **If the mechanism is real**, both correlations should stabilize, not continue toward zero: length-vs-rank Pearson r predicted at **0.35-0.55** at n=30 (continued decline from 0.71, settling above noise); Jaccard-vs-rank Pearson r predicted at **0.45-0.65**, with **Jaccard's magnitude remaining larger than length's at every intermediate measurement**, the sharper comparative claim.
2. **If the pattern is small-sample noise**, both should continue declining past that range toward |r|<0.2 or a bootstrap CI including zero by n=20-30.
3. **A specific disconfirming pattern to watch for:** Jaccard's current advantage over length disappearing or reversing as more items are added -- that would indicate the current result is itself an artifact of this specific 12-item sample's vocabulary, not a stable mechanism, a distinct failure mode from simple regression to zero.

**Decision:** Recorded in `results/notice_term_overlap.md` as the citable pre-registration; not folded into `results/notice_length_correlation.md` (a separate, related-but-distinct test with its own denominator, worth keeping independently checkable). Both remain exploratory and post-hoc -- this test was itself generated by inspecting the same items that motivated ADR-090, so it inherits, not resolves, that status.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` section 3.11 should report the Jaccard-vs-length comparison alongside the length correlation once the paper pass runs, with the n=30 prediction stated as a pre-registered forecast so its eventual outcome is checkable against what was expected before the data existed -- the same discipline ADR-049/080 established for the ordering/invisibility predictions, now extended to this line of investigation.

---

## ADR-095: Next batch pre-screened -- 2 candidates ready to draft (1 Pharmacology, 1 Obstetrics/Gynecology), not the requested 5, after 6 candidates examined and 4 dropped for distinguishable reasons

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The author authorized a further batch of 5 candidates, Pharmacology and Obstetrics/Gynecology only, balanced, independence checked during pre-screening (ADR-076). The mechanizable pre-filter from ADR-085 was re-run against the *corrected* `pool_notice_length_distribution.json` (ADR-087, not the buggy file the first run of that filter used) and against all 13 recorded items' author sets: of 296 candidates, 22 already used/rejected/deferred, restricted to the two active domains, abstract-intact, notice-reachable, and outside every author-group cap -- **176 candidates remained (90 Pharmacology, 86 Obstetrics/Gynecology).**

**Successes (raw material at `results/item{22,23}_raw_material.md`):**

1. **Item 22** (rw-34512, Obstetrics/Gynecology) -- a retracted RCT on combination (Foley catheter + oral misoprostol) vs. single-method labour induction. Evidence: a September 2025 systematic review of 10 RCTs (n=8,683, PMID 41030509), independence confirmed via its 25-reference list. `evidence_direction = concordant`, `evidence_tier_match = same_tier`. The evidence source's own narrative-synthesis hedge ("due to clinical and methodological heterogeneity") is flagged for attribution in the gold claim.
2. **Item 23** (rw-35226, Pharmacology) -- a retracted meta-analysis (itself a synthesis, 16 studies) comparing DPP-4 inhibitors against sulfonylurea as metformin add-on therapy. Evidence: a 182-RCT meta-analysis (PMID 34364771), independence confirmed via its 44-reference list -- notably confirmed by direct reference-list absence rather than temporal impossibility, since the evidence source's epub date sits between the trap source's publication and its retraction. A genuine scope nuance is flagged: the evidence source pools DPP-4 inhibitor trials against placebo *or any* active comparator, not sulfonylurea specifically, and this must be carried into the gold claim's wording rather than presented as a sulfonylurea-specific replication.

**Balance achieved: 1 Pharmacology, 1 Obstetrics/Gynecology**, not the requested 5, after 6 candidates were examined. Four near-misses, each dropped for a distinguishable reason worth recording rather than silently discarding:

1. **rw-22847** (statin/cholesterol and primary open-angle glaucoma, Kang et al., *JAMA Ophthalmology*) -- **a third real-world instance of the undiscoverable-replacement shape** (ADR-083/086), independently of items 17 and rw-30688986. Another JAMA "Notice of Retraction and Replacement," `RefType="RetractionIn"` rather than the `RetractedandRepublishedIn` type items 17/20 carried, a bare paywalled notice, and no third record findable by the title-and-author search method that resolved item 20 -- checked directly, not assumed to have failed.
2. **rw-37530** (pre-conception thromboprophylaxis in antiphospholipid-syndrome-related recurrent pregnancy loss) -- two candidate evidence sources both **directly confirmed to pool the retracted trial's own data** (a 2020 network meta-analysis and a 2021 evidence review, both predating the 2022 retraction and both found, via reference-list check, to cite the retracted paper). A third, independent, post-retraction source was found (a 2026 systematic review) but was topically mismatched on inspection -- it explicitly excludes women with a history of recurrent miscarriage from its own scope, the population this candidate's trap claim is about.
3. **rw-21326** (besifloxacin for acute bacterial conjunctivitis, Wang et al.) -- the only apparently-independent later synthesis found (PMID 31850176, published ~3 months after the retraction) is **by the same four authors, under an almost identical title and abstract, in a different journal** -- a same-author-group republication, not independent verification, and not used.
4. **rw-38513** (amlodipine and preovulatory follicle blood flow in clomiphene-resistant PCOS) and **rw-34061** (inhaled tiotropium for postoperative cardiopulmonary complications in COPD patients undergoing lung cancer surgery) -- no institutional-tier source could be found for either within a reasonable search budget; both narrow, mechanism-specific claims without an obvious later synthesis.

**Consequences:** `results/item22_raw_material.md` and `item23_raw_material.md` are ready for the author to draft from, with no open verification points remaining in either (notices and evidence sources both read directly, independence confirmed for both). `docs/STATUS.md` updated to reflect the new queue state.

---

## ADR-096: ADR-094's falsifiable remedy tested directly on the real corpus -- enriching a notice with its own paper's title+abstract improves rank in exactly the case the mechanism predicts, and reveals a real failure mode in exactly the case it should not have been expected to help

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-094 found that Jaccard overlap between question and notice vocabulary predicts rank better than length, identifying dilution (added vocabulary uncorrelated with the question) as the mechanism. This implies a falsifiable, actionable prediction: restoring shared vocabulary to a diluted notice -- by indexing it jointly with its own paper's title and abstract -- should improve its rank. The author's instruction: test it directly, on the real corpus, not just reason about it.

**Method, run without touching the production corpus/index (ADR-046/048):** the existing 400,576-vector index was reconstructed in memory (no re-embedding of background documents); only the ~12 notice documents' vectors were recomputed with enriched text (`notice text + trap source title + trap source abstract`); a fresh in-memory index was built and searched with each item's real question via the same `measure_item` function the production ceiling uses. Full detail and per-item table: `results/notice_enrichment_intervention_test.{md,json}`.

**Result: mixed, not a uniform improvement -- 1 of 12 improved, 2 of 12 worsened, 9 unchanged.** RB-0004 (a diluted, `unverifiable`-specificity notice, low Jaccard overlap) moved from rank 3 to rank 1 -- the mechanism working exactly as predicted. RB-0007 and RB-0008 (the two `bare` notices already at maximal lexical overlap, entirely the paper's own title) moved from rank 1 to rank 2 -- enrichment does not help a notice that was already a concentrated pointer to the same content, and can cost it rank once its vector becomes similar enough to the trap source's to compete with it directly for the top position. **This is a mechanical consequence of rank being an ordering, not an absolute score:** the trap source's own rank shifted too (2->1, both cases) despite its vector never being touched, because making the notice more similar to it changes their *relative* order, not either document's individual retrievability.

**Decision:** Reported as a demonstrated intervention with an honest, mixed outcome, not a validated fix and not smoothed into a clean win. The paper's contribution is sharpened from "we found a failure" to "we found a failure, a candidate mechanism, and a candidate remedy that helps the diagnosed failure mode specifically and has a predictable limit" -- a real gain, reported at the same exploratory, n=12, post-hoc status as ADR-090's mechanism account and ADR-094's correlation, per the author's explicit instruction not to let a concrete result pull the language up.

**Explicitly not touched by this result:** ADR-094's pre-registered n=30 predictions (length r in 0.35-0.55, Jaccard r in 0.45-0.65) are unchanged. This ADR does not revise them, and no future ADR should on the basis of this one -- doing so would repeat the exact discipline lapse ADR-080 already corrected once.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` section 3.11 should report this intervention alongside the Jaccard result once the next paper pass runs, framed as a candidate remedy needing testing at scale, with the RB-0007/RB-0008 backfire stated as plainly as RB-0004's improvement -- not omitted because it complicates the story.

---

## ADR-097: Retract-and-replace discoverability downgraded from "a method" to "something that worked once" -- 1 of 3 cases resolved, JAMA 0-for-2 and the one success was a different publisher entirely

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-086 described the title-and-author search that resolved item 20 (RB-0012) as a discovery method. ADR-095 found a third real-world retract-and-replace case (rw-22847, statin/glaucoma, another JAMA "Notice of Retraction and Replacement") and the same search technique did not resolve it -- no third record findable by title or author. **Checked directly against each case's actual venue, not assumed:** item 17 is JAMA Oncology, rw-22847 is JAMA Ophthalmology -- both JAMA-family, both undiscoverable by every method tried. **Item 20's evidence source (rw-25419) is `International Breastfeeding Journal`, a BMC/Springer title -- not JAMA.** The search technique has therefore been tried exactly twice: once against a JAMA case (rw-22847, failed) and once against a non-JAMA case (item 20, succeeded) -- **0-for-1 against JAMA, 1-for-1 against the one non-JAMA case tried.** Item 17 never had the technique tried against it at all, only structured metadata (which failed). An earlier draft of this ADR stated "JAMA is 0-for-3," conflating item 20 (not JAMA) with the two genuine JAMA cases -- caught and corrected here before being carried into the paper.

**Decision:** The framing is downgraded, effective immediately, everywhere this is described: not "a method that resolves retract-and-replace cases," but **"a search technique that worked once, against one non-JAMA case, and failed once, against a JAMA case -- not shown to generalize in either direction."** Recorded as tracked counts, not rates, all too small to generalize from: 1/2 for the search technique across its two actual attempts; 2/2 for JAMA-family cases specifically remaining undiscoverable by any method tried (item 17, structured metadata only; rw-22847, both structured metadata and the search technique); 1/1 for the one non-JAMA case, resolved on the only attempt. **The publisher split (JAMA 0-for-2, one non-JAMA case 1-for-1) is worth stating precisely, not blurred into an aggregate "1 of 3 retract-and-replace cases resolved" that would obscure it.**

**Consequences:** `docs/STATUS.md`, `paper/paper.md` (sections 3.4 and 3.8), and any future ADR referencing this should use the downgraded framing above, not "the method" or "the resolution method."
