Future retract-and-replace cases should still try the title-and-author search (it is free and worked once), but the paper must not imply it is a validated technique on the strength of a single success.

---

## ADR-098: The notice-enrichment intervention is a conditional remedy, not a mixed result -- its predicted failures are evidence for the mechanism, not noise against it; and a worked example of why a wrong, dramatic claim is the one most likely to survive review

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-096 reported the notice-enrichment intervention as "1 of 12 improved, 2 of 12 worsened, 9 unchanged" -- an honest count, but one that reads as a mostly-null result unless the two worsened cases are shown to be *predicted*, not merely tolerated. The author's correction: a remedy whose failures are predicted by the same account that predicts its successes is stronger evidence for that account than uniform improvement would have been, because a uniform win is consistent with many mechanisms (more text simply retrieves better) while a *conditional* win matching a stated, falsifiable condition is not.

**The condition, made explicit and checked against the data:** enrichment should help a notice whose baseline Jaccard overlap with its question is low, and should not help (or should hurt) a notice already near-maximal, because there is no diluted vocabulary left to restore. Checked directly: RB-0004 (the one improvement) has baseline Jaccard 0.098, one of the lowest in the set. RB-0007 and RB-0008 (the two worsened cases) have the *two highest* Jaccard values in the entire set (0.412 and 0.267). Correlating baseline Jaccard against the rank change across all 12 items gives **Pearson r = -0.535** -- a real, moderate, correctly-signed relationship, computed once and not tuned. Two exceptions are reported alongside it, not smoothed over: RB-0010 (the single lowest Jaccard, 0.022) did not improve; RB-0011 (a high Jaccard, 0.333) did not worsen -- r=-0.535 is a real correlation at n=12, not a deterministic threshold, and these two items are why.

**Decision:** `results/notice_enrichment_intervention_test.md` and `paper/paper.md` section 3.11 are rewritten to lead with the condition and the correlation, not the raw improved/worsened/unchanged tally -- the tally is still reported, but framed as evidence *for* a conditional remedy rather than as a mixed result to be apologized for. The status stays exploratory, post-hoc, n=12 throughout, per standing practice; the sharper framing is not license to claim more than the data supports, only to state precisely what it does support.

**A worked example of a real hazard, recorded because the author asked for it explicitly, not because it needed further apology:** while writing up ADR-097's retract-and-replace downgrade, an earlier draft claimed "JAMA is 0-for-3," incorrectly attributing item 20's one success to JAMA (its actual publisher, checked afterward, is `International Breastfeeding Journal`, a BMC/Springer title). This was caught and corrected before the claim left this project's own working notes -- but it is worth naming precisely *why* it is worth recording as a case, not just as a fixed error: **the wrong version was directionally right and more dramatic than the true one, which is exactly the condition under which an uncorroborated claim is most likely to survive review.** A false "0-for-2" or a false "0-for-4" would have been easier to catch, because the shape of the claim would have looked less clean. "0-for-3" was wrong in a way that made the underlying point *more* total and more citable, not less -- the kind of error that a reviewer skimming for a strong number, rather than checking each count against its source, would be least likely to catch. The correction did not weaken the finding; checked precisely, the true picture (JAMA 0-for-2, the one success from an entirely different publisher on its only attempt) is sharper and more specific than the uncorrected version, not less.

**Consequences:** No change to ADR-097's substantive conclusion, which was already corrected before that ADR was written. This ADR exists to record the pattern -- dramatic, directionally-right, uncorroborated claims are the ones most worth double-checking, specifically because they are the least likely to be double-checked by anyone else -- as a standing note for this project's own future drafting, not only as history.

---

## ADR-099: Which planned comparisons stay headline vs. get demoted, decided now against a 40-60 item projection rather than discovered in October

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** As of 2026-08-12, 13 items are annotated after roughly seven weeks, projecting to 40-60 total items by the 2026-09-30 annotation cutoff (ADR-037) -- enough for the domain and direction comparisons, thin for anything stratified more finely. The author asked, before drafting items 22-23, to determine now which of the planned analyses (domain comparison, `evidence_direction`, `evidence_tier_match`, `item_type`/`no_answer_reason`, `claim_polarity`, notice-rank correlations) become underpowered at n=40 and n=50, so the answer is pre-registered rather than found retroactively in the results.

**Method:** Wilson 95% confidence intervals computed for each comparison's relevant stratum, projected to n=40/50/60 using current (n=13) observed rates as the working assumption. A comparison is flagged thin not just by CI width but by raw stratum count -- a low-rate stratum can show a deceptively reasonable CI width while its absolute count stays in single digits. Full table and reasoning: `results/power_analysis_projection.md` / `.json`.

**One constraint outranks the power analysis itself:** the project protocol's minimum of 50 hand-verified items is a non-negotiable floor, separate from statistical power. At n=40, that requirement is unmet regardless of any comparison's precision.

**Decision, by comparison:**
- **Keep as headline** (CIs stated explicitly, wide): domain comparison (Pharmacology vs. Obstetrics/Gynecology); `evidence_direction` pooled across domains (both the 3-way all-items split and the 2-way within-answer split); `item_type`'s overall with/without-current-answer split; the notice-length and Jaccard-overlap correlations pooled across domains (this one is genuinely well-powered -- its own n=30 target, ADR-094, is comfortably cleared by the projected 36-54 notice-bearing items at every benchmark size in range).
- **Demote to descriptive-only, effective now:** `evidence_tier_match`'s `lower_tier` rate (raw count 3-5 across the full projected range) and `claim_polarity`'s negative rate (raw count 6-9) -- both report as a point estimate with CI, neither as a comparison basis for further stratification.
- **Pre-registered exploratory only, effective now:** any domain-stratified version of `evidence_direction`, `evidence_tier_match`, or `claim_polarity` (cells of roughly 8-14 at best); domain-stratified notice-rank correlations (falls to ~18-27 per domain, below the correlation test's own n=30 target at every projected size, even though the pooled version clears it).
- **Drop as a reportable proportion, effective now:** `no_answer_reason`'s split within the without-current-answer stratum. That stratum itself never exceeds single digits in this projection (denominator ~5-7), and splitting it two ways guarantees cells of 2-3 -- exactly the n=4-territory result the author explicitly wants to avoid reporting. These items are to be described individually, by name, the way RB-0003/RB-0004/RB-0007 already are elsewhere in this project's notes, not summarized as a rate with a CI implying precision the count cannot support.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md`'s eventual Results section (still `[TODO]`) should report the headline comparisons as such, the descriptive-only ones as point estimates without confirmatory framing, and must not present the domain-stratified or `no_answer_reason` breakdowns as anything other than individually-described exploratory observations. This decision is made from a projection using n=13 rates, itself uncertain -- `results/power_analysis_projection.md` states this limitation directly and is a planning tool to check the eventual real numbers against, not a guarantee of them.

---

## ADR-100: RB-0014 and RB-0015 recorded (items 22-23) -- item_type classification on RB-0015 is a judgement call, recorded with its rejected alternative, and a fourth notice-above-trap-source case appears

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The two candidates pre-screened under the batch authorized 2026-08-12 (ADR-095) were drafted by the author by hand, per ADR-040. RB-0014 (item 22, Obstetrics/Gynecology): a 335-woman RCT found combining a Foley catheter with oral misoprostol reduced failure to achieve vaginal delivery within 24 hours vs. oral misoprostol alone (11.8% vs 28.7%, P=0.001); retracted 2021-12-01; a 2025 systematic review of 10 RCTs (n=8,683, PMID 41030509) found the same direction, combination methods outperforming single methods -- concordant, same_tier (arguably an upgrade), straightforward. RB-0015 (item 23, Pharmacology): a meta-analysis found no significant mortality difference between DPP-4 inhibitors and sulfonylureas as metformin add-on therapy (RR 1.14, 95% CI 0.98-1.33); retracted 2022-02-01; a 182-RCT meta-analysis (PMID 34364771) found DPP-4 inhibitors not associated with excess MACE/mortality/heart-failure risk -- concordant in direction, but against a different comparator.

**The judgement call, recorded with its rejected alternative rather than decided silently:** RB-0015's retracted paper compared DPP-4 inhibitors specifically against sulfonylurea. The current evidence (PMID 34364771) compares DPP-4 inhibitors against placebo or any active comparator, not sulfonylurea exclusively -- the gold claim states this scope gap explicitly, per standing practice (ADR-052/055/058) of attributing a source's own hedges rather than smoothing them away. Two classifications were both defensible:

1. **Chosen:** `item_type = superseded_with_current_answer`, `evidence_direction = concordant`, `evidence_tier_match = same_tier`. Reasoning: the comparison is adjacent, and the safety conclusion (no excess mortality/MACE/heart-failure risk from DPP-4 inhibitors as a class) is what a clinician would actually act on when weighing this drug class, even though it does not isolate the sulfonylurea comparator specifically.
2. **Rejected alternative:** `item_type = superseded_without_current_answer`, `no_answer_reason = adjacent_evidence_exists` -- the same shape as RB-0003, where authoritative adjacent evidence exists but does not directly answer the question as posed.

**This is the second time this exact boundary has done real work** (RB-0003, and now RB-0015's rejected alternative) **with no written test behind it.** The author's instruction, recorded here rather than left as an implicit precedent: if a third item with this shape appears, settle a rule -- e.g., a test along the lines of "does the current evidence's own population/comparator claim generalize to the trap claim's specific comparison, or must a reader supply that generalization themselves" -- rather than deciding case by case a third time. No rule is settled yet; two cases is not enough to generalize from safely, and a premature rule risks being shaped by these two examples rather than the actual distinction. This ADR is the flag, not the resolution.

**Third negative-polarity item.** RB-0015 joins RB-0009 and RB-0013. Its retracted claim is a null result (no significant mortality difference) -- a model reproducing it would report reassuring equivalence between two drug classes, resting on a comparison narrower than the one it would appear to generalize from once the scope gap above is accounted for.

**Corpus and retrieval ceiling, mechanically:** both items' documents (source, notice, evidence -- 6 rows) inserted via the standard incremental path (`corpus.embed_index.add_documents`, ADR-048's policy), full corpus/index untouched otherwise: 400,576 -> 400,582 records, hash `93ebca8a...`. Retrieval ceiling re-run across all 15 items.

**A new finding from that re-run, not assumed stable:** RB-0014 is a fourth notice-above-trap-source case (trap_source_rank=2, notice_rank=1), alongside RB-0007, RB-0008, and RB-0011 -- the pattern previously described as "stable at n=3" (ADR-092) is not stable; it is a pattern that keeps producing one more case every time the benchmark grows. This should not be described as "stable" again until a stretch of new items passes without adding one. RB-0014's own notice_text is empty (`bare`, verified, no content beyond "Retraction.") -- consistent with the dilution/Jaccard-overlap account (ADR-090/094): an empty or near-empty notice has nothing to dilute, so it stays close to the trap source's own vocabulary and can outrank it. This case has not yet been added to the term-overlap correlation dataset (`results/notice_term_overlap.md`) -- flagged, not done, since RB-0014 has no notice text to compute a Jaccard value from at all (unlike RB-0007/RB-0008/RB-0011, which have short but non-empty notice text). A zero-text notice is a different case from a diluted-text notice, not a smaller version of the same one; how (or whether) to fold it into that correlation is an open question, not resolved here.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` needs a follow-up pass for the n=15 figures and the RB-0014 pattern once the author confirms scope for that pass (not done in this ADR, per standing practice of not silently expanding a recording task into a paper edit). `docs/STATUS.md` updated to reflect 15 items and the fourth notice-above-trap case.

---

## ADR-101: n=15 checkpoint against the n=13 power projection -- no verdict changes, thin strata stayed exactly as thin as predicted

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-099 projected which planned comparisons would stay adequately powered at 40-60 items, using n=13 observed rates. RB-0014/RB-0015 (ADR-100) are the first two items recorded since that projection. The author asked to answer the statistical-power question again now that the data changed; per ADR-094's own precedent for the n=30 predictions (fixed once pre-registered, checked against later data rather than revised toward it), the right way to do that here is a checkpoint against the existing projection, not a fresh projection replacing it.

**What moved and what didn't, at n=15 (`results/power_analysis_projection.md`'s new top section, `.json`'s `n15_checkpoint` key):** every rate drifted a few points in the direction two items would be expected to move it -- `evidence_direction` contradictory share down from 38.5% to 33.3% (both new items are concordant), `claim_polarity` negative share up from 15.4% to 20.0% (RB-0015). **The two already-thin strata are unchanged in raw count, not just similar in rate:** `evidence_tier_match`'s `lower_tier` is still exactly 1 item (RB-0011), and `no_answer_reason`'s split is still exactly 2-vs-1 among 3 without-current-answer items, because neither new item landed in either category. This is not confirmation those verdicts were right -- two items is too small a step to confirm or disconfirm a stratum-size judgment -- but it is consistent with them, and is exactly the kind of check the projection was built to survive without needing to be redone.

**Decision:** No verdict from ADR-099 changes. The `no_answer_reason` drop-as-proportion decision in particular stands unmodified: a hypothetical 4th without-current-answer item would still leave that stratum at a single-digit denominator, the reason it was dropped in the first place. This checkpoint is recorded to make the "check, don't silently redo" discipline visible at the first opportunity to violate it, not because anything here demanded a change.

**Consequences:** Future checkpoints (at n=20, n=30, ...) should follow this same pattern -- add a dated row/section to the existing file, do not open a new projection file, and only revise a verdict if a stratum's raw count changes enough to change the conclusion, not merely because the rate moved.

---

## ADR-102: A minimum-viable-count threshold for "finding" language, applied to all six planned comparisons at the ~40-item September target -- two demoted to exploratory, two dropped as comparison dimensions

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-099 measured CI widths at n=40/50/60; the author's follow-up sharpens the question this ADR answers: not "how thin is each stratum" but "at what raw count does a stratum's rate stop being a describable observation and become defensible as a *finding*" -- and, applied to the sharper near-term target (roughly 40 items by 2026-09-30, not the wider 40-60 range), which comparisons cross that line and which do not.

**The threshold, stated explicitly so it can be checked rather than taken on faith:** the rarest cell in a comparison needs a raw count of **at least 10** before its rate is reported as a finding. At k=10, a single additional or missing item moves the point estimate by at most 10 percentage points -- the smallest count at which one item stops being able to single-handedly swing the qualitative conclusion. Below 10, and especially below 5 (the conventional "expected cell count" floor below which even a chi-square test is considered unreliable), a percentage implies a precision the count cannot support; the correct unit is a raw count or individual item description, not a rate. This is the same distinction ADR-099 already applied informally ("stays in single digits") -- here made a checkable number rather than a phrase.

**Projected smallest-cell count per comparison at n_total=40 (from n=15 current rates, same proportional-scaling method as ADR-099; full arithmetic in `results/power_analysis_projection.json`'s new `n40_viability` key):**

| Comparison | Rarest cell | Projected count at n=40 | Clears 10? |
|---|---|---|---|
| Domain (Pharmacology vs. Obstetrics/Gynecology) | either domain (currently 7/7 of 14 non-Rehab) | ~18.7 per domain | Yes, comfortably |
| `evidence_direction`, concordant vs. contradictory (within with-answer items) | contradictory (currently 5/12) | ~13.3 | Yes |
| `evidence_direction`, pooled 3-way (adds `absent`) | `absent` (currently 3/15) | ~8.0 | **No** |
| `claim_polarity`, positive vs. negative | negative (currently 3/15) | ~8.0 | **No** |
| `item_type`, with vs. without current answer, **used as a downstream comparison axis** | without-current-answer (currently 3/15) | ~8.0 | **No** |
| `evidence_tier_match`, same_tier vs. lower_tier | lower_tier (currently 1/12 with-answer items) | ~2.7 | **No, not close** |
| `no_answer_reason`, split within without-current-answer items | `no_adjacent_evidence` (currently 1/3) | ~2.7 | **No, not close** |

**One population, three names.** `evidence_direction=ABSENT`, `item_type=SUPERSEDED_WITHOUT_CURRENT_ANSWER`, and the denominator of `no_answer_reason` are the same set of items by construction (the schema's own validator enforces this). Counting all three as separate demotions would overstate how many independent findings are actually thin -- there is one thin population here, showing up in three field names, projected at ~8 items regardless of which field is used to describe it.

**Decision, by comparison:**

1. **Headline, unchanged:** domain comparison; `evidence_direction` restricted to concordant-vs-contradictory within with-current-answer items (the scientifically interesting axis -- does current evidence agree or disagree with the retracted claim). Both clear the threshold with room to spare.
2. **`item_type`'s own prevalence is unaffected and stays headline as a composition statistic** -- tracking the observed without-current-answer rate against the pre-registered 10-15% target (ADR-043) is a description of the benchmark itself, not an inferential comparison, and does not need this threshold. What changes here is narrower: **item_type used as a grouping variable to compare downstream results (e.g., M1-M3 broken out by with-vs-without-current-answer) is demoted to exploratory** -- the without-answer group, ~8 items projected, is too thin to trust a stratified comparison built on it. This is a sharpening of ADR-099, not a reversal.
3. **`claim_polarity` is demoted to exploratory** as a downstream stratification axis, for the same reason and the same projected count (~8). Its own prevalence (how many negative-polarity items exist, and what is distinctive about each) stays reportable as a descriptive count, unchanged from ADR-099.
4. **`evidence_direction`'s pooled 3-way version (including `absent`) is demoted to exploratory** -- not because concordant-vs-contradictory is weak (it isn't), but because adding the `absent` category pulls in the same ~8-item population as (2).
5. **`evidence_tier_match` is dropped as a comparison dimension entirely, per the author's explicit instruction.** At ~2.7 projected items in the `lower_tier` cell, it does not approach viability at any n this benchmark will plausibly reach by November. The field stays recorded on every item (it is not deleted or stopped being annotated) -- only the *comparison* built on it is dropped. RB-0011 remains describable by name as the one example, the correct unit for a count this small.
6. **`no_answer_reason` is dropped as a comparison dimension, reconfirming ADR-099's verdict with the same number** (~2.7 projected) rather than the earlier "single digits" heuristic. Also unchanged: the field stays recorded, items described individually.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md`'s eventual Results section should build any stratified comparison only on the "headline" list above; `claim_polarity` and `item_type`/`evidence_direction`-as-comparison get exploratory framing with the projected count stated plainly; `evidence_tier_match` and `no_answer_reason` appear only as recorded fields and individually-described items, never as a reported rate. `docs/STATUS.md` and `results/power_analysis_projection.{md,json}` updated with this table.

---

## ADR-103: RB-0014 folded into the Jaccard/length dataset (n=12 -> n=14, RB-0015 included too) -- the "zero vocabulary" premise corrected, the result still supports the mechanism, at the opposite extreme from what was expected

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The author asked for RB-0014 to be folded into the term-overlap dataset (ADR-094), framing it as "the limiting case of the dilution account, where non-question vocabulary is zero." **Checked directly before computing anything, this premise does not hold:** every notice document is indexed as `f"Notice: {source.title}"` plus its body text; RB-0014's body is empty, but its title is not -- the indexed text is 129 characters, the paper's own title, not a zero-length document. This makes RB-0014 a fourth member of the same `bare`, title-only cluster as RB-0007/RB-0008/RB-0011, not a new category. Flagged before computing rather than silently computed and reported as testing something it did not test.

**What actually happened, and why it is still a strong data point:** RB-0014's title happens to share unusually high vocabulary with its own question (`Foley catheter`, `misoprostol`, `induction`, `labor` appear in both, since the question closely paraphrases the trial's own title). Its Jaccard overlap is **0.5833 -- the highest value in the entire dataset** -- and its notice ranks 1, above its own trap source (rank 2). This is the dilution account's prediction at the opposite extreme from the one anticipated: not "zero diluting vocabulary in the limit" but "maximal share of a small, entirely on-topic vocabulary, because nothing was added to dilute it." Both are the same mechanism, tested at different ends of the scale; this ADR reports the one that actually occurred rather than the one hypothesized, and notes plainly that they are not the same test.

**RB-0015 folded in too, beyond exactly what was asked:** it also now has a notice document in the corpus (ADR-100), so excluding it while including RB-0014 would leave an unexplained gap -- every notice-bearing item is now in the dataset (14 of 15; RB-0005 remains the sole exclusion, ADR-071), not a partial update. RB-0015's Jaccard (0.2000, rank 2) is unremarkable and sits mid-range.

**Updated correlations, n=14 (full detail: `results/notice_term_overlap.{md,json}`):** length-vs-rank Pearson r = 0.7259 (was 0.7142 at n=12); recall-vs-rank r = -0.2557 (was -0.1882); **Jaccard-vs-rank r = -0.8018 (was -0.8312)** -- essentially unchanged, Jaccard remains the clear leader over both length and recall.

**Decision:** `results/notice_term_overlap.md` rewritten with an update block stating both the corrected premise and the n=14 result, per the file's own standing instruction to update in place and show original and updated numbers side by side. **The n=30 pre-registered prediction (ADR-094) is unchanged and stays unchanged** -- this is a checkpoint, the same discipline ADR-101 applied to the power projection.

**Consequences:** Future notice-length/Jaccard write-ups (paper or results file) should describe RB-0014 as "a fourth bare, title-only notice with unusually high title-question overlap," not as a zero-vocabulary limiting case -- the distinction matters because the latter claim is not one this data actually supports.

---

## ADR-104: Three instances of a claim outrunning its evidence, each caught before publication -- recorded as a pattern, with a discussion paragraph drafted

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Across this project's history, three claims were stated more strongly, or more specifically, than the evidence available at the time actually supported, and each was caught and corrected before reaching the paper:

1. **ADR-080:** the retrieval-ceiling ordering prediction (ADR-049, pre-registered on zero data) was *softened* after a single item (RB-0001, ADR-050) produced an unexpected 1-rank gap -- the softening itself, not the original prediction, was the discipline lapse. At n=9 the evidence moved back toward the original, unsoftened prediction and past it.
2. **ADR-097:** an early draft claimed "JAMA is 0-for-3" on discoverable retract-and-replace replacements, conflating item 20's one success (published by a BMC/Springer title, not JAMA) with the two genuine JAMA cases. Caught before the claim left the project's own working notes; the corrected count (JAMA 0-for-2, the one success from a different publisher) was sharper and more specific, not weaker.
3. **ADR-100:** the notice-above-trap-source pattern was described as "stable at n=3" (RB-0007, RB-0008, RB-0011). RB-0014, the very next item, was a fourth case -- the pattern had not been tested against new data at all; "stable" was a description of a sample that had not yet had the chance to change.

**The common shape, worth naming rather than treating as three unrelated corrections:** in each case, the claim was not fabricated -- it was an honest reading of the data available at the time, stated with more confidence than that data volume could support, and in each case the direction of the overclaim ran toward the more citable, more dramatic, or more narratively convenient version (a stronger discovery in (1)'s case for reverting to caution; a cleaner "0-for-3" in (2); an established, closed pattern in (3)). None were caught by an external reviewer; each was caught by checking the claim against its source before it left this project's own notes.

**Decision:** This pattern is worth a paragraph in the paper's eventual Discussion section, per the author's explicit instruction. **Discussion remains `[TODO]` in `paper/paper.md`** (out of scope to open now, per standing practice of Methods/Limitations-only edits), but the paragraph is drafted here, ready to place when Discussion is written:

> *Over the course of annotating this benchmark, three claims were stated more strongly than the evidence supporting them at the time actually warranted, and each was caught before publication rather than after. A retrieval-ordering prediction, pre-registered on no data, was weakened in response to a single unexpected item -- and the weakening, not the original prediction, was the error the later data corrected. A discoverability claim conflated one successful case with a different, unrelated set of failures, producing a rounder, more citable number than the evidence supported. A retrieval pattern was called stable after three items and falsified by the fourth. None of these were fabrications; each was an honest reading of too little data, stated with the confidence appropriate to more data than existed. What caught them was not a larger sample arriving on its own, but a standing practice of checking each claim against its underlying source before it left this project's working notes, and of recording the correction alongside the original rather than quietly revising it away. A benchmark's credibility rests as much on this kind of visible self-correction as on any single reported number -- reviewers cannot check what they cannot see, and a project that only ever showed its final, cleaned-up claims would be indistinguishable from one that had never needed correcting at all.*

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md`'s Discussion `[TODO]` stub gets a one-line pointer to this ADR so the paragraph is not lost when Discussion is eventually written. No other document changes -- this ADR is the paragraph's authoritative location until it is placed.

---

## ADR-105: Next batch pre-screened -- 5 candidates found (3 Obstetrics/Gynecology, 2 Pharmacology), 3 tried and rejected, one candidate flagged with a mixed evidence-direction the author must judge

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The author authorized a balanced batch of 5, independence checked during pre-screening. From the 240-candidate eligible pool (Pharmacology + Obstetrics/Gynecology, excluding every record already used, rejected, or deferred), 14 candidates were shortlisted by title relevance and researched for real, independently-verified post-retraction evidence -- every abstract, notice, and reference-list check fetched live via PubMed EFetch / Europe PMC, no invented data, per the project protocol. Two of this ADR's five successes (item24/rw-52582, item26/rw-47012) share an author ("Mohammed K Ali" / the Ali/Emam/Abdel-Aleem/Sobh and Abbas/Abd Ellah/Hosny/Abdellah author group) -- using both puts that group at exactly the 2-record cap (ADR-031/070); no third record from either author list should be added later.

**The five, with the scope nuance each carries (none are clean, unqualified replications -- every one has a stated gap between the trap claim's exact comparison and the surviving evidence's actual scope):**

- **item24 (rw-52582, Ob/Gyn):** repeat misoprostol vs. expectant management after a *failed initial* misoprostol course for incomplete miscarriage (69% vs. 16.7% complete miscarriage). Evidence: Cochrane network meta-analysis (78 RCTs, n=17,795, misoprostol RR 1.30 vs. expectant). Scope gap: the Cochrane arm is misoprostol for miscarriage generally, not the retracted trial's specific repeat-after-failure subgroup.
- **item25 (rw-70702, Ob/Gyn):** carbetocin vs. oxytocin for PPH prevention, hypertensive cesarean patients. Evidence: Lancet Global Health Bayesian network meta-analysis (167 RCTs, n=44,817, carbetocin RR 0.54 vs. oxytocin). Scope gap: general cesarean population, no hypertension-specific subgroup reported; the NMA also finds oxytocin-plus-tranexamic-acid beats carbetocin monotherapy, information the two-arm trap trial could not have addressed. **Notice reason unrecoverable** -- PubMed's XML carries no body text and the publisher page 403'd; recorded as unverifiable rather than guessed.
- **item26 (rw-47012, Ob/Gyn):** 2% lidocaine gel vs. placebo for pain during copper IUD insertion, women with prior cesarean only. Evidence: network meta-analysis (71 RCTs, n=10,870); top-ranked formulation is 4% gel, a different concentration, evaluated on a general IUD population. A false-lead retraction notice for an unrelated paper was caught and discarded mid-search by cross-checking the "original PMID retracted" field rather than assumed from a title match.
- **item27 (rw-45529, Pharmacology):** diacerein vs. **placebo** for knee osteoarthritis, with a claimed 2-month carryover effect. Evidence: 12-RCT/1,732-patient meta-analysis of diacerein vs. **NSAIDs** -- a materially different comparator, flagged as "the most significant nuance in this batch." A second, more directly comparable placebo-controlled meta-analysis (PMID 36401382) was found and correctly rejected: its own reference list cites the retracted paper directly, confirmed via Europe PMC, so it fails independence.
- **item28 (rw-42874, Pharmacology):** mycophenolate mofetil outperforming glucocorticoids head-to-head (91.3% vs. 67.9% response) for Graves' orbitopathy. Evidence: a 2026 systematic review (58 studies, semi-quantitative synthesis) that positions IV glucocorticoids as still first-line and MMF only as the best non-biologic alternative -- **not the same head-to-head comparison the trap claim rests on.** Flagged `evidence_direction = mixed`, not `concordant` -- `mixed` is not a schema value (`EvidenceDirection` has only `CONCORDANT`/`CONTRADICTORY`/`ABSENT`, ADR-079), and this ADR does not resolve which applies. **This is an open judgement call for the author, explicitly not decided here** -- calling it `concordant` would repeat exactly the pattern ADR-104 just named.

**Three tried and rejected, reasons recorded rather than silently dropped:** rw-55213 (progesterone/preterm birth) -- the trial pools three heterogeneous risk populations and current evidence diverges by subgroup, too heterogeneous for a clean claim; rw-21127 (calcium vs. OCP for PMS) -- both PubMed and Europe PMC have fully overwritten the original abstract with publisher withdrawal boilerplate, no verbatim original findings recoverable from any legitimate source; rw-43937 (postplacental IUD insertion) -- no synthesis exists published after the trial itself, only ones that predate it.

**Verification, spot-checked independently before accepting this ADR:** three of the batch's load-bearing claims (the Cochrane review's RR 1.30 figure, the rejected PMID 36401382's real existence and topic, and item25's retraction notice) were re-fetched directly and confirmed to match the raw-material files exactly.

**Decision:** `results/item24_raw_material.md` through `item28_raw_material.md` recorded, in the same format as `item22`/`item23`. None of these are `BenchmarkItem`s yet -- the trap/gold claim wording in each file is a draft candidate for the author's convenience, not a final annotation; the author writes the final wording by hand per ADR-040. Item28 in particular should not be promoted without the author's own resolution of its evidence-direction ambiguity.

**Consequences:** `docs/STATUS.md` updated to reflect the batch. No corpus, index, or `items.jsonl` changes -- this is pre-screening only, exactly as scoped.

---

## ADR-106: The item_type boundary rule, formalized -- RB-0015 reclassified (the author's own worked example was wrong on the rule's own terms), item28 confirmed, two more candidates found by re-checking everything, and a fourth instance of a claim caught before it propagated

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-100 flagged RB-0015's `item_type` classification as a judgement call and named the underlying boundary -- "adjacent evidence answers the question" vs. "adjacent evidence exists but does not answer it" -- as one this benchmark had leaned on twice (RB-0003, RB-0015) with no written test behind it, and asked that a third instance trigger a formal rule. Item 28 (mycophenolate mofetil vs. glucocorticoids for Graves' orbitopathy, ADR-105) was that third instance.

**The rule, as stated by the author:** could a clinician act on the current evidence to answer the question the retracted paper asked? Yes, even with caveats about comparator or population -> `superseded_with_current_answer`, with the mismatch stated explicitly in the gold claim. No -- the current evidence answers a different question, however adjacent -> `superseded_without_current_answer` / `adjacent_evidence_exists`.

**Applied to the three cases that motivated it:**
- **RB-0003** (adjuvant radiation practice-pattern change vs. NCCN guidance on what clinicians *should* do): confirmed, no clinician can answer a descriptive practice-pattern question from a prescriptive guideline. `superseded_without_current_answer`, unchanged.
- **RB-0015** (DPP-4 inhibitors vs. sulfonylurea, add-on to metformin): **the author used this item as their own worked example of "yes, with caveats" when first stating the rule, then caught on review that it fails the rule on its own terms** -- the retracted paper's comparison was DPP-4 inhibitors vs. sulfonylurea specifically; the surviving evidence (PMID 34364771) is DPP-4 inhibitors vs. placebo or any active comparator, not sulfonylurea isolated. A clinician choosing between those two specific drug classes cannot extract that comparison from the pooled estimate -- this is not a caveat on the same comparison, it is a different comparison. **Reclassified to `superseded_without_current_answer` / `adjacent_evidence_exists`.** `gold_claim.text` is unchanged -- it already stated the comparator scope gap in plain prose (recorded at annotation time, ADR-100), which is exactly what made the misclassification checkable once the rule existed. `trap_claim`, `supporting_sources`, and `post_supersession_evidence` are also unchanged; only `item_type`, `no_answer_reason` (now set), `evidence_direction` (now `absent`), and `evidence_tier_match` (now `None`) changed, validated against the schema's own gating rules before writing. A new `Annotation` entry documents the correction; the original annotation entry is left standing, not rewritten, per this project's append-only correction practice.
- **item28** (mycophenolate mofetil vs. glucocorticoids, Graves' orbitopathy): confirmed `superseded_with_current_answer`. The surviving 2026 review doesn't reproduce the trap claim's head-to-head effect size, but it does give a clinician something to act on -- favor glucocorticoids first-line, reach for mycophenolate as the best non-biologic alternative. That is a caveat on the same clinical choice, not a different question. `results/item28_raw_material.md` updated to record this as resolved rather than open.

**Re-checking every other item against the rule, as instructed, rather than stopping at three:** all 15 items' question/trap_claim/gold_claim were re-read against the rule. Nine of the eleven remaining `with_current_answer` items pass cleanly or with an already-stated, genuine caveat (RB-0001, RB-0002, RB-0006, RB-0008, RB-0009, RB-0010, RB-0012, RB-0013, RB-0014 -- each either a direct same-comparison replication or a caveat that narrows the same question rather than substituting a different one). **Two more items show the identical shape RB-0015 did, each with the mismatch already stated in its own `gold_claim.text` -- exactly the tell that made RB-0015's error visible:**

- **RB-0005** (HBV seroprevalence among women of reproductive age in *rural China*, specifically those *planning pregnancy within six months*): the gold claim's own text states the replacement evidence "concern[s] the general female population and pregnant women, respectively, and not specifically women in the preconception period." The trap claim's own regional data (1.1% in Shanxi to 13.0% in Tibet) shows the underlying rate is highly heterogeneous by region -- a global 4.1% estimate cannot answer what the rural-China question asked.
- **RB-0011** (vortioxetine *vs. placebo* for major depressive disorder): the gold claim's own text states the replacement evidence "come[s] from real-world observational evidence rather than a placebo-controlled randomized trial and therefore are not a like-for-like replacement." Real-world response/remission rates with no control arm cannot answer a vs.-placebo comparison at all -- there is no placebo arm to compare against. (This is also the sole `evidence_tier_match=lower_tier` item; the tier mismatch and the scope mismatch are two symptoms of the same underlying problem.)

**These two are not reclassified in this ADR.** Given that the rule's author got its own first worked example wrong, a second unilateral application without confirmation would repeat the error this ADR exists to correct. Flagged for the author's decision, not decided here.

**Two things recorded because the author asked them recorded carefully, not as a formality:**

1. **The rule caught a real error on its first real application, including one the rule's own author had just committed.** Three ad hoc judgements (RB-0003 correct, RB-0015 wrong, item28 pending) produced one misclassification; formalizing the criterion surfaced it immediately, on the same day the criterion was written. This is the sharper form of the argument this project has been making since ADR-080: it is not just that ad hoc judgement is *sometimes* wrong, it is that a written test finds the error faster and more reliably than continued ad hoc judgement would have -- extending ADR-104's discussion paragraph, worth stating in the paper alongside it.

2. **A fourth instance of a claim caught before it propagated -- the first where the flawed input was the author's, not a draft of the assistant's.** ADR-104 recorded three: ADR-080's n=1 softening, ADR-097's "JAMA 0-for-3" conflation, ADR-100's "stable at n=3" claim. This is the fourth: the author's message stating the rule cut off mid-sentence while applying it to RB-0015, and rather than completing the thought on the assistant's own judgement, the assistant's own reading was shown back and confirmation was requested before anything was reclassified. The distinction from the first three matters: those were all the project's own claims, checked against their own sources; this one was a claim in progress, not yet checked by the person making it, and the check happened by declining to finish it on their behalf. **ADR-104's discussion paragraph is superseded by the extended version below, not rewritten in place:**

> *Over the course of annotating this benchmark, four claims were stated more strongly, or completed more confidently, than the evidence or reasoning behind them at the time actually warranted, and each was caught before it did lasting damage. A retrieval-ordering prediction, pre-registered on no data, was weakened in response to a single unexpected item -- and the weakening, not the original prediction, was the error the later data corrected. A discoverability claim conflated one successful case with a different, unrelated set of failures, producing a rounder, more citable number than the evidence supported. A retrieval pattern was called stable after three items and falsified by the fourth. And a boundary rule, written specifically to replace case-by-case judgement with a checkable test, was wrong about its own first worked example -- caught not by further ad hoc reasoning but by applying the rule literally to the case that had prompted it. None of these were fabrications; each was an honest reading of too little data or too little checking, stated with more confidence than that data or that check could support. What caught the first three was a standing practice of verifying each claim against its underlying source before it left this project's working notes. What caught the fourth was different in kind: an incomplete instruction was left incomplete rather than finished on the author's behalf, and the resulting reasoning was shown back for confirmation before anything changed. A benchmark's credibility rests as much on this kind of visible self-correction, from every direction it can come from, as on any single reported number -- reviewers cannot check what they cannot see, and neither can collaborators.*

`paper/paper.md`'s Discussion `[TODO]` stub's pointer is updated to cite this ADR as the current version of the paragraph, with ADR-104 kept as the historical record of the first three instances.

**Decision:** the item_type boundary rule is adopted as written, recorded formally in `docs/ANNOTATION.md` (not just this ADR) so it is applied prospectively to every future item, not re-derived case by case a fourth time. RB-0015 reclassified. item28 confirmed. RB-0005 and RB-0011 flagged, pending the author's decision.

**Consequences:** `results/power_analysis_projection.{md,json}` corrected for the n=15 counts (`item_type` with-answer 11/without-answer 4, `evidence_direction` concordant 6/absent 4, `evidence_tier_match` same_tier 10/not-applicable 4) -- one ADR-102 verdict moves narrowly (the `item_type`/`evidence_direction`-`absent` population's n≈40 projection crosses the ≥10 threshold, ~10.7, but is recorded as borderline rather than promoted to headline on a single reclassification, per the same discipline this whole ADR is about). The Jaccard/length dataset (ADR-103) was checked directly, not assumed, and confirmed unaffected -- it keys on notice text and retrieval rank, neither of which changed. `docs/STATUS.md` updated throughout.

---

## ADR-107: RB-0005 and RB-0011 reclassified; the tell made a mandatory step; a methodological finding for the paper; standing authorization to apply the rule without further sign-off; the n≈40 projection's instability treated as a warning, not evidence

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-106 flagged RB-0005 and RB-0011 as failing the item_type boundary rule on inspection but did not reclassify them, pending the author's confirmation after getting the rule's own first worked example (RB-0015) wrong. The author confirmed both, for the reasons already stated in ADR-106: RB-0005's surviving evidence is general-population and pregnant-population HBV prevalence, not the rural, preconception-specific population the question asked about; RB-0011's surviving evidence is real-world response rates with no placebo arm, and the question was specifically about a placebo comparison.

**Reclassified, same procedure as RB-0015 (ADR-106):** both moved to `superseded_without_current_answer` / `adjacent_evidence_exists`, `evidence_direction` to `absent`, `evidence_tier_match` to `None`. `gold_claim.text`, `trap_claim`, `supporting_sources`, and `post_supersession_evidence` are byte-identical to before, validated programmatically rather than assumed. A new `Annotation` entry on each documents the correction; original entries left standing. RB-0011 was the benchmark's sole `evidence_tier_match = lower_tier` item -- that field's only recorded example is now gone; the tier mismatch and the scope mismatch this ADR resolves were two symptoms of the same underlying problem, not two separate findings.

**Verified before writing anything:** RB-0005 was never in the Jaccard/length dataset at all (no separate notice document, ADR-071 -- confirmed directly, not assumed from the general pattern). RB-0011's row in that dataset was checked directly and confirmed unaffected -- it keys on notice text and retrieval rank, neither of which the reclassification touches.

**The tell is now a mandatory step, not a note (`docs/ANNOTATION.md`).** All three misclassifications found so far (RB-0005, RB-0011, RB-0015) shared one property: the annotator had already, honestly, written the exact scope/population/comparator/tier mismatch into the gold claim's own prose, for reasons unrelated to item_type (the evidence-strength caveat and attribution rules, ADR-052/055) -- before any rule existed to check that prose against. **Before any item moves from draft to verified, a gold claim containing such a sentence must be explicitly checked against the boundary rule, with the outcome recorded in the item's own annotation note.** This is mechanical -- the sentence is already being written for another reason -- and costs one line; skipping it is exactly how the boundary was misjudged three times before this rule existed.

**A methodological finding recorded for the paper, not filed away as a private lesson:** honest disclosure of a limitation, required by this protocol for an unrelated reason, turned out to double as a detectable signal for a labeling error nothing else in the pipeline was checking for. Three items, three tells, three misclassifications -- worth stating in Methods (not Discussion; this is a property of the annotation pipeline's own design, not a results-dependent observation) as a general point: a pipeline that requires annotators to disclose evidentiary limitations in their own words creates a signal a later rule-based check can exploit, even though the disclosure was never designed with that check in mind. Added to `paper/paper.md` section 3.6.3.

**Standing authorization, replacing the caution in ADR-106:** the caution against reclassifying RB-0005/RB-0011 unilaterally was right for that specific moment -- the rule's own author had just gotten its first application wrong, and a second unchecked application risked compounding rather than correcting the error. It does not generalize to every future case. **The rule is written down now specifically so it can be applied without the author's involvement each time.** From this point forward: apply the item_type boundary rule directly to new and existing items as they are worked on, make the classification, record it, and report what changed. Escalate only items where the rule's application is genuinely ambiguous -- not every application, and not as a default caution.

**The n≈40 projection's instability, treated as a warning rather than a promotion, per the author's explicit instruction.** The without-answer population's projected count at n≈40 moved 8.0 (ADR-102) -> 10.7 (ADR-106) -> 16.0 (this ADR) across two reclassifications inside one session. A number that moves that far on individual, correct classification decisions is not measuring a stable population rate -- it is amplifying whatever the most recent call happened to be. **Decision: this stratum's reporting status (headline / exploratory / drop) will be decided once, at the real final item count, not from any interim projection.** Every projection computed for this specific population so far (ADR-102's ~8.0, ADR-106's ~10.7, this ADR's ~16.0) should be read as evidence of the instability itself, not as a number to act on. No other ADR-102 verdict is similarly affected -- domain and `evidence_direction`-within-with-answer have large enough denominators that two reclassifications don't meaningfully move them; `claim_polarity` is untouched by either correction.

**One composition fact stated plainly rather than left for a reader to notice:** the without-answer share of the recorded benchmark now stands at 6/15 (40%), well above the original 10-15% target (ADR-043). This was not produced by seeking without-answer items out -- ADR-043 explicitly instructs against that -- but by the boundary rule finding three items that had been sitting in the with-answer bucket incorrectly. Whether the true underlying rate in this domain pair is genuinely this high, or this particular first batch of 15 is unusually concentrated in this failure mode, is not yet answerable and is not guessed at here.

**Consequences:** `results/power_analysis_projection.{md,json}` carries both corrections in sequence, each dated, neither overwriting the other. `docs/STATUS.md` updated throughout. Items 24-28 (ADR-105), currently being drafted by the author, should have the mandatory tell-check applied as part of their own annotation before being recorded.

---

## ADR-108: Items 24-28 recorded (RB-0016 through RB-0020), all self-classified by the author against the item_type boundary rule -- item28 reversed on review, two more notice-above-trap-source cases, and a pre-registered failure mode partially arrives at n=19

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The author drafted all five items from the pre-screened batch (ADR-105) and applied the item_type boundary rule (ADR-106/107) to each themselves before submitting, including the mandatory tell-check (ADR-107). Three fail the rule: RB-0016/item24 (repeat misoprostol after failed initial treatment -- evidence on first-line misoprostol does not answer a question about a population defined by prior failure), RB-0019/item27 (diacerein vs. placebo with a 2-month carryover claim -- surviving evidence is diacerein vs. NSAIDs at 4 weeks, two independent mismatches, comparator and duration), and RB-0020/item28 (see below). Two pass: RB-0017/item25 (carbetocin vs. oxytocin, PPH prevention -- the head-to-head comparison exists, only the hypertensive subgroup is narrower) and RB-0018/item26 (lidocaine gel vs. placebo, IUD insertion pain -- intervention class and comparison both present, concentration and population differ but stay the same clinical choice). All five recorded with `gold_claim`/`trap_claim` exactly as the author wrote them; classification fields set to match.

**item28 is a reversal, and the responsibility for the error it corrects is shared, not solely the author's.** ADR-106 recorded item28 as `superseded_with_current_answer`, on the assistant's own application of the rule: "the surviving 2026 review doesn't reproduce the trap claim's head-to-head effect size, but it does give a clinician something to act on -- favor glucocorticoids first-line, reach for mycophenolate as the best non-biologic alternative. That is a caveat on the same clinical choice, not a different question." The author, drafting item28 properly, caught that this reasoning was wrong: knowing where two treatments sit in a sequence (first-line vs. fallback) is not the same as knowing whether one outperforms the other, which is what the retracted trial actually claimed (91.3% vs. 67.9% response, head-to-head). Positioning guidance answered a different, adjacent question -- exactly the shape RB-0015 failed on. **The ADR-106 confirmation was the assistant's own application of the rule, and it made the same directional error the rule exists to catch.** Reclassified to `superseded_without_current_answer` / `adjacent_evidence_exists`, recorded as a second `Annotation` entry, the first left standing rather than deleted.

**The directional bias, named precisely because the author asked it be named as the failure mode this rule most needs to guard against, including in the assistant's own application of it:** every misclassification found so far -- RB-0015, item28's first ruling, and by extension the shape RB-0005/RB-0011 also shared -- involved mistaking evidence that describes *where a treatment sits, how safe it is in general, or how common a condition is in a broad population* for evidence that answers a *specific, isolated comparison or subpopulation question*. Positioning is not magnitude; general safety is not a specific comparator; a broad prevalence is not a narrow one. The rule's own worked examples, misapplied twice now (once by the author on RB-0015, once by the assistant on item28), both ran in this same direction -- toward treating actionable-sounding adjacent information as though it closed the gap it does not close. This is recorded as the rule's known blind spot, not resolved by this ADR; anyone applying the rule, human or assistant, should check specifically for this shape of error, not just apply the test mechanically.

**Corpus and retrieval ceiling:** 15 new documents (5 sources, 5 notices, 5 evidence entries) inserted via the standard incremental path. Corpus: 400,582 -> 400,597 records, hash `5277c4e5...`. Retrieval ceiling re-run across all 20 items.

**Two more notice-above-trap-source cases, not one:** RB-0017 and RB-0018 (both `trap_source_rank=2`, `notice_rank=1`) join RB-0007/RB-0008/RB-0011/RB-0014. The count is now **6 of 20**, up from 4 of 15 -- this batch alone added two, not one, continuing exactly the growth pattern ADR-100 warned against describing as "stable."

**The Jaccard/length dataset folded in (all 5 new items have notice documents, 19 of 20 total; RB-0005 remains the sole exclusion) -- and the specific failure mode ADR-094 pre-registered as worth watching for has partially arrived, five items before the n=30 checkpoint.** ADR-094 stated in advance: if Jaccard's advantage over length disappears or reverses, that would suggest the original result was an artifact of the first small sample, "the one outcome that would undermine the dilution account without simply reducing to 'there is no effect.'" **At n=19, on Pearson specifically: |length| = 0.7235 vs. |Jaccard| = 0.6780 -- length's magnitude now nominally exceeds Jaccard's, for the first time.** This is reported prominently, not folded quietly into a table update. It is not unanimous: **Spearman's rho still favors Jaccard clearly (0.7635 vs. 0.6684)** -- Pearson is sensitive to a handful of high-leverage points at n=19 in a way Spearman is not, and which metric should be trusted more here is an open question this ADR does not resolve. Neither is picked as "the real number" because it reads better. **The n=30 pre-registered prediction (ADR-094) is unchanged and stays unchanged** -- this is a checkpoint five items short of the actual test, not a revision, and not grounds to declare the dilution account confirmed or refuted either way. If the Pearson crossover persists or widens by n=30, the honest conclusion the paper should state plainly is that the dilution account, while still directionally supported by both metrics, does not clearly outperform the simpler length-based proxy it was proposed to replace.

**Decision:** `results/notice_term_overlap.{md,json}` updated in place with the n=19 checkpoint, both metrics reported, disagreement stated rather than resolved. `docs/STATUS.md` updated throughout.

**Consequences:** The next real checkpoint on this correlation (whenever the benchmark reaches n=30 notice-bearing items) needs to state plainly whether the Pearson crossover held, widened, or reverted -- this is now the single most important open empirical question this project's retrieval-ceiling work has, more consequential than any other number currently in `results/`. `paper/paper.md`'s eventual Results/Discussion sections must not present the Jaccard result as settled ahead of that checkpoint.

---

## ADR-109: The Jaccard/length Pearson crossover is the pre-registration working, not the finding weakening -- investigated via leave-one-out, traced to one item, reported as unresolved

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-108 reported that at n=19, Pearson favors notice length over Jaccard for the first time (0.7235 vs. 0.6780), the specific outcome ADR-094 named in advance as the one result that would count against the dilution account. The author's correction to how this should be framed: a pre-registered falsification condition partially triggering is evidence the methodology is credible, not evidence the finding is collapsing -- the entire purpose of stating a condition in advance is so it can be checked honestly against real data, including checked and found to be occurring. This ADR removes the hedging language from ADR-108's report and replaces it with a direct investigation of what is actually producing the Pearson/Spearman disagreement.

**Investigation: does the divergence trace to specific points, or does it survive leave-one-out?** Pearson measures linear association and is sensitive to individual high-leverage points; Spearman measures monotonic rank association and is not. Standardized residuals from each metric's own fitted line were computed per item (19 items), then each item was removed in turn and both Pearson correlations recomputed on the remaining 18, checking whether the length-ahead-of-Jaccard ordering flips.

**Result: one item, RB-0008, is the pivot, and even its removal produces only a near-tie.** Full n=19: length_r=0.7235, |Jaccard|_r=0.6780 (length ahead by 0.0455). Removing RB-0008: length_r=0.7055, |Jaccard|_r=0.7081 (Jaccard ahead by 0.0026) -- the only one of 19 leave-one-out removals that flips the ordering at all, and it flips into a margin thin enough to call a tie. No other single removal comes close; every other leave-one-out result keeps length ahead by a comfortable 0.02-0.15. **RB-0008 has the single largest standardized Jaccard residual in the dataset (-1.59):** its notice is short (121 characters, in the same range as the other five bare-notice items that also rank above their trap source) and ranks 1st -- length's account fits it well -- but its Jaccard is only 0.2667, because its notice text happens to omit "antipsychotic," "monotherapy," and "treating" from its own question's vocabulary while retaining "alone," "electroconvulsive," "schizophrenia," and "therapy." A short notice with unexpectedly low overlap for its length is exactly the shape of point that weakens Jaccard's fit relative to length's.

**Decision, stated as precisely as the author asked:** the divergence does not survive leave-one-out as a fully structural property of the relationship -- it hinges heavily on one item. That item is not an anomaly to discard or a data-quality problem; RB-0008 is measured correctly and belongs in the dataset exactly as much as any other item. **The mechanism is unresolved at n=19.** The pre-registered condition that would undermine the dilution account has partially occurred, tracing mostly to one point's leverage at this sample size -- which is itself informative: at n=19, a single item can still move the comparative verdict, meaning neither "Jaccard clearly wins" nor "length has caught up" is a safe claim yet. **The n=30 prediction (ADR-094) is unchanged and is not revised here.**

**Consequences:** `results/notice_term_overlap.md` rewritten to lead with this framing -- pre-registration working as intended, investigated rather than left as ambiguity, result stated plainly regardless of which way it points. `paper/paper.md`'s eventual Results/Discussion must present this the same way: a falsification condition specified in advance, found to be partially met, traced to its source, not defended against.

---

## ADR-110: Item28's reversal folded into the five-instance paragraph -- and named as a structural property of the boundary rule, not a lapse by either reader

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-104 recorded three instances of a claim outrunning its evidence, each caught before publication, with a Discussion paragraph drafted. ADR-106 extended it to four, adding RB-0015's own misclassification by the rule's author. ADR-108 recorded a fifth: item28/RB-0020, originally ruled `superseded_with_current_answer` by the assistant's own application of the rule (ADR-106), reversed by the author on review for the identical directional error RB-0015 made -- mistaking adjacent positioning guidance for an answer to a comparative question.

**Reframed per the author's instruction: this is not "the author got it wrong twice."** RB-0015's error was the author's; item28's first ruling was the assistant's, applying the same written rule independently, with no visibility into how the other would apply it beyond the rule's own text. **Two readers, working from the same written test, converging on the same wrong answer on two different items is stronger evidence that the failure mode is a property of the rule's own blind spot than either single error would have been on its own.** A single person's mistake could be idiosyncratic -- a lapse in attention, a specific bias in how one person reads clinical evidence. Two independent applications making the identical category error is evidence about the *test itself*: the boundary between "adjacent evidence that is actionable" and "adjacent evidence that actually answers the question" is not obvious even to a careful reader applying a written rule in good faith, and the rule as currently worded does not fully close that gap. This is recorded as a property of the problem the rule is trying to solve, not as a scorecard of who has gotten it wrong.

**The paragraph, extended to five instances and rewritten now rather than left for October, when it would be harder to reconstruct accurately (supersedes ADR-106's four-instance version, which supersedes ADR-104's three-instance version; both kept as historical record, not rewritten):**

> *Over the course of annotating this benchmark, five claims were stated more strongly, or completed more confidently, than the evidence or reasoning behind them at the time actually warranted, and each was caught before it did lasting damage. A retrieval-ordering prediction, pre-registered on no data, was weakened in response to a single unexpected item -- and the weakening, not the original prediction, was the error the later data corrected. A discoverability claim conflated one successful case with a different, unrelated set of failures, producing a rounder, more citable number than the evidence supported. A retrieval pattern was called stable after three items and falsified by the fourth. A boundary rule, written specifically to replace case-by-case judgement with a checkable test, was wrong about its own first worked example -- caught by applying the rule literally to the case that had prompted it, not by further ad hoc reasoning. And the same rule, applied independently a second time by a different reader to a different item, produced the identical category of error: adjacent, actionable-sounding evidence mistaken for evidence that actually answers the question asked. That the same mistake occurred twice, in two independent hands, working from the same written text, is not two isolated lapses -- it is evidence about where the rule itself remains genuinely hard to apply, a property of the boundary rather than of either reader. None of these five were fabrications; each was an honest reading of too little data, too little checking, or too little scrutiny of one's own agreement with adjacent-sounding evidence, stated with more confidence than that reading could support. What caught the first three was a standing practice of verifying each claim against its underlying source before it left this project's working notes. What caught the fourth and fifth was different in kind: a rule specific enough to be checked against its own worked examples, applied to those examples rather than trusted because it had just been written. A benchmark's credibility rests as much on this kind of visible self-correction, from every direction it can come from and by every party capable of making the same mistake, as on any single reported number -- reviewers cannot check what they cannot see, and a rule that is never tested against the cases that motivated it is not yet a rule, only a stated intention.*

**Decision:** This is the paragraph's current authoritative version. `paper/paper.md`'s Discussion `[TODO]` stub pointer is updated to cite this ADR.

**Consequences:** If a sixth instance occurs, extend this paragraph again rather than starting a new one -- the pattern is now an established finding of this project's own process, not a one-off note. Any future application of the item_type boundary rule, by either party, should treat "does this adjacent evidence merely sound actionable, or does it actually answer the comparative question asked" as the specific place this rule is known to be fragile.

---

## ADR-111: Notice-above-trap-source given its own analysis line -- a verified-bare notice is necessary for the pattern in every case observed, but not sufficient

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The notice-above-trap-source pattern (a supersession notice outranking its own trap source in the retrieval ceiling) has been tracked as a running count since ADR-077 -- n=2, then "stable" at n=3 (ADR-092, later shown wrong), n=4 (ADR-100), and now n=6 of 20 items (RB-0007, RB-0008, RB-0011, RB-0014, RB-0017, RB-0018; ADR-108). The author's instruction: stop describing this as an anomaly riding on top of the Jaccard finding, and give it its own analysis -- what fraction of items show it, and what the notices that do share in common.

**Rate:** 6 of 20 items overall (30%); 6 of 19 notice-bearing items (RB-0005 excluded, no notice document at all, ADR-071) -- 31.6%.

**What the six have in common, checked directly against every other notice-bearing item, not assumed:** all six have a `SupersededSource.notice_specificity = bare` **and** `notice_text = None` -- a notice confirmed to carry no content beyond the paper's own title (the "verified-empty" case established in ADR-081/103, distinct from a merely short notice). This holds for 6 of 6 cases with no exception: **a verified-bare notice is necessary for this pattern in every instance observed so far.**

**It is not sufficient.** Nine of the nineteen notice-bearing items have this same verified-bare, empty-text property (the six above, plus RB-0002, RB-0013, RB-0020) -- and three of those nine (RB-0002, RB-0013, RB-0020) do **not** outrank their trap source. RB-0013 and RB-0008 have near-identical Jaccard values (0.2667 each) yet opposite outcomes; RB-0020's Jaccard (0.5385) is higher than four of the six flip cases yet it does not flip either. Jaccard alone does not cleanly separate the two groups within the bare-notice population -- something else, most plausibly the trap source document's own relative retrievability (how well *its* title and abstract match the question, a property of the source, not the notice, and not currently measured in this dataset), also has to be in play for the flip to occur, since the outcome is a *relative* comparison between two documents' ranks, not an absolute property of the notice alone. This is not resolved here -- flagged as the natural next measurement, not assumed.

**Decision:** Recorded as a second, partial finding, not folded into the Jaccard/length correlation as background noise around it. `bare` notice specificity (verified, zero added content) is the necessary condition this project has identified for a notice to outrank its own trap source; the sufficient condition remains open and likely involves the trap source's own retrievability, not the notice in isolation.

**Consequences:** A results file (`results/notice_above_trap_source.md`, new) records the full breakdown: rate, the necessary-not-sufficient finding, the nine-item bare population with per-item outcomes, and the open question about trap-source-side retrievability. `paper/paper.md`'s eventual Results section should report this rate and the bare-necessary finding as a discrete result, separate from (though mechanistically related to) the Jaccard-vs-length correlation.

---

## ADR-112: Next batch pre-screened -- 5 candidates found (2 Obstetrics/Gynecology, 3 Pharmacology), one with an unresolved independence gap stated plainly rather than assumed away

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The background pre-screening agent launched for this batch failed on an external session-limit error, not a research failure -- its partial progress was discarded rather than reused, and the batch was researched directly instead, from a freshly regenerated eligible pool (232 candidates after excluding all 20 recorded items, rejected/deferred candidates, and every author group already at its ADR-031/070 cap -- rw-50389 was excluded specifically because it shares "Mohammed K Ali" with items 24/26, already at the 2-record cap).

**The five, with the scope nuance each carries:**

- **item29 (rw-52159, Ob/Gyn):** sequential (day-3-and-day-5) vs. single-day embryo transfer for recurrent implantation failure. Evidence: systematic review (7 RCTs, n=909, OR 2.28 for ongoing pregnancy favoring sequential transfer). **The cleanest match in this batch** -- same intervention, same population, same direction, no material scope gap to flag.
- **item30 (rw-49782, Ob/Gyn):** moderate aerobic exercise for male-factor infertility (semen parameters, pregnancy rate). Evidence: systematic review (10 studies, n=1,511, concordant). Independence check found something worth naming rather than smoothing over: the review's reference list does not contain the retracted paper itself, but does contain a different, non-retracted study by the identical author pair (Hajizadeh Maleki & Tartibian) on a related high-intensity protocol -- checked directly and confirmed not itself retracted, but flagged plainly as an authorship overlap a fully independent check would not have.
- **item31 (rw-33597, Pharmacology):** single-dose ivermectin for asymptomatic SARS-CoV-2 (viral clearance, hospitalization). Evidence: a Cochrane review (11 RCTs, n=3,409, contradictory to the trap claim -- high-certainty evidence of no benefit). **Independence is not cleanly confirmed and is recorded as such (`independence_verified = false`), not guessed at:** the Cochrane review's own reference list contains both the retracted trial and its retraction notice, meaning the Samaha trial was known to the reviewers, but whether it was pooled into the 11-trial estimate or listed among excluded studies (the standard, expected handling) could not be confirmed -- the Cochrane Library's full text returned HTTP 403. This is the strongest candidate in the batch by topic relevance and the sharpest exception to this project's independence-verification standard (ADR-058) in one entry.
- **item32 (rw-34061, Pharmacology):** perioperative inhaled tiotropium to prevent postoperative cardiopulmonary complications in COPD patients undergoing lung cancer surgery. Evidence: a genuine RCT (not a synthesis; a design upgrade over the retracted retrospective study) in a *different* surgical population (esophagectomy, not lobectomy) with a *genuinely mixed* direction -- the primary, trap-claim-comparable outcome (pneumonia rate) shows no significant benefit, while secondary pulmonary-function outcomes favor tiotropium. **The most ambiguous candidate in this batch**, flagged explicitly for careful application of the item_type boundary rule rather than a quick call either way.
- **item33 (rw-21326, Pharmacology):** besifloxacin vs. placebo/other fluoroquinolones for acute bacterial conjunctivitis -- the retracted source is itself a synthesis (8-RCT meta-analysis), the same shape as RB-0015. Evidence: a Cochrane review of antibiotics as a class for bacterial conjunctivitis, drawing on primary besifloxacin trials directly (confirmed via Europe PMC, independent of the retracted secondary synthesis) but not isolating besifloxacin's own effect size in its headline figures -- a pooled-class-vs.-isolated-drug scope gap, the same shape RB-0015's own comparator-scope gap had.

**Decision:** `results/item29_raw_material.md` through `item33_raw_material.md` recorded, same format as prior batches. None are `BenchmarkItem`s yet -- draft candidate wording only, for the author's own hand-written final version per ADR-040. item31's independence gap should be resolved (or the gap itself stated in the gold claim) before that item is promoted; item32's mixed-direction/population-mismatch needs the item_type boundary rule applied carefully, not routinely.

**Consequences:** `docs/STATUS.md` updated to reflect the batch. No corpus, index, or `items.jsonl` changes -- pre-screening only.

---

## ADR-113: item31's independence resolved directly (the retracted trial was affirmatively excluded, not silently included); `outcome_hierarchy_mismatch` added as a new field, introduced by item32; item30's same-author overlap is not a concern

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-112 left item31 (ivermectin) with an unresolved independence question: the Cochrane review's reference list contained both the retracted trial and its own retraction notice, which is ambiguous in the worst way -- it could mean careful exclusion or uncorrected inclusion, and those are opposite facts. The author asked for extra effort specifically because ivermectin's COVID-19 evidence base is unusually politically loaded and very likely over-represented in model training data relative to its actual evidence quality, making this item unusually informative for testing whether groundedness tracks currency or merely tracks prior exposure.

**Resolved via the same route that worked for item 21:** Unpaywall resolved the Cochrane review's DOI to an open-access PMC copy (PMC9215332, green OA). Its full text states directly: **"One trial awaiting classification in the previous review version was retracted in the meantime (Samaha 2021); we excluded the trial in this review update."** Table 3 records the reason explicitly: "Trial did not pass research integrity check, due to published retraction notice." **This is the careful-exclusion case, confirmed affirmatively, not the uncorrected-inclusion case** -- Cochrane's own reviewers screened for retraction status and removed this specific trial for exactly that reason before pooling. `independence_verified = true`, with the mechanism recorded, not just the bare fact. `results/item31_raw_material.md` updated; no open points remain.

**`outcome_hierarchy_mismatch` added to `BenchmarkItem` (schemas.py), introduced by item32.** item32 (tiotropium for postoperative complications in COPD patients undergoing lung surgery) is not a case of ordinary ambiguity: the retracted trial's own stated primary outcome (postoperative cardiopulmonary complications) is exactly what the trap claim rests on -- no problem there. But the current evidence's own stated primary outcome (30-day pneumonia incidence) is **null** (28.6% vs. 27.8%, no significant difference); its positive findings (pulmonary function, exercise tolerance) are all **secondary** outcomes the trial was not designed to answer the trap claim's question with. **A model citing this evidence and reporting the secondary pulmonary-function improvements as though they answered the trap claim's complication-rate question is committing a specific, nameable error -- not merely citing weaker evidence (that is `evidence_tier_match`'s job), but citing the wrong tier *within* a single study's own outcome hierarchy.** The new field: `NONE` / `TRAP_CLAIM_SECONDARY` / `EVIDENCE_SECONDARY` / `BOTH_SECONDARY`, optional, not item_type-gated (a trap claim can rest on a secondary outcome regardless of whether current evidence exists at all). item32 is recorded as `EVIDENCE_SECONDARY` in its raw material. **This is the first case; no gating validator is added yet, and the field is not backfilled onto the 20 already-recorded items** -- they were not screened for this axis, and `None` on those items means "not yet assessed," not "confirmed absent." Backfilling is future work, not undertaken here, matching this project's standing practice of not silently expanding a task's scope.

**item32's raw material rewritten to state the outcome-hierarchy structure as the item's substance, not an open question:** the trial's own primary-outcome result (no significant complication reduction) is the fairer basis for comparing against the trap claim's own primary-outcome claim, making `contradictory` the more defensible `evidence_direction` reading -- stated as a candidate framing for the author's own final wording, not decided unilaterally. The population mismatch (esophagectomy vs. lung cancer lobectomy) is a second, separate flag that compounds rather than substitutes for the outcome-hierarchy issue.

**item30's same-author overlap (a different, non-retracted study by the identical author pair as the retracted trial) is not treated as a concern, per the author's explicit correction to how ADR-112 framed it.** The author-group cap (ADR-031/070) exists to prevent a benchmark dominated by one fraud case's author list, not to flag every subsequent appearance of a name that has ever had a retraction nearby.
