A researcher having other, unretracted work is the normal case, not a signal of anything -- ADR-112's flagging of this as worth noting was correct to surface, but should not be read as suspicion by association. Recorded here so the distinction is explicit for future sessions: author overlap with a *retracted* paper's author list matters for the cap; author overlap with a *different, clean* paper by someone who has also had unrelated work retracted does not, on its own, indicate anything about that specific paper.

**Consequences:** items 29, 30, and 32 are ready to draft; item31 is ready to draft, its independence gap closed. `docs/STATUS.md` updated.

---

## ADR-114: The notice-above-trap-source pattern is not explained by weak trap sources -- measured directly, not assumed

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-111 named the trap source's own general retrievability as the natural next measurement for the notice-above-trap-source pattern (6 of 19 notice-bearing items), and left it unmeasured. The author asked for it directly: if the trap sources involved are simply poor retrievers in general, the pattern is about weak sources, "a considerably less interesting finding," and worth knowing plainly rather than continuing to describe it in terms of notice strength alone.

**Method:** for each of the 6 flip trap sources and the 3 non-flip verified-bare trap sources (RB-0002, RB-0013, RB-0020 -- same `bare`/empty-notice property, but their own trap source still wins), its rank was computed against every one of the other 19 items' own questions (19 full-corpus searches over 400,597 documents, each trap source's rank extracted from every other query's complete ranking -- not a small-k check, not a sample).

**Result: the two groups are statistically indistinguishable on general retrievability, and the direction, if anything, runs opposite to the weak-source hypothesis.** Mean of each item's median rank against the other 19 questions: flip group 237,467, non-flip group 232,337 -- both deep in the corpus (expected for any specific document against an unrelated query) and nearly identical between groups. The flip group's best-case rank against some other question (208-7,013) is, if anything, slightly better than the non-flip group's (2,042-19,873). **Conclusion: the notice-above-trap-source pattern is not explained by the trap sources being weak retrievers.** Whatever produces the flip is specific to the competition between a notice and its own trap source on their shared, specific question -- consistent with the dilution/Jaccard account rather than a "these are just bad documents" explanation.

**Decision:** Recorded in `results/notice_above_trap_source.md` alongside the necessary-not-sufficient bare-notice finding (ADR-111), full per-item data in `results/trap_source_retrievability.json`. Still open, not computed here: the trap source's own Jaccard overlap with its own question specifically, and whether the notice-minus-trap-source Jaccard difference separates the two groups better than either side's Jaccard alone.

**Consequences:** the paper's eventual Results section can state this negative result plainly -- it rules out a real, simpler alternative explanation, which is exactly the kind of finding that makes the remaining dilution account more credible, not less.

---

## ADR-115: Items 29, 30, 32, 33 recorded (RB-0021 through RB-0024) -- a second worked example of the gold-claim provenance boundary, outcome_hierarchy_mismatch used for the first time, and two more notice-above-trap-source cases

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The author drafted four of the five items from the pre-screened batch (ADR-112), applying the item_type boundary rule themselves before submission (item31/ivermectin held back, independence already resolved in ADR-113, not drafted this round). RB-0021/item29 (sequential embryo transfer) and RB-0022/item30 (aerobic exercise/male infertility) both pass the rule cleanly -- population, intervention, and direction all match their trap claims, `superseded_with_current_answer`. RB-0023/item32 (tiotropium) and RB-0024/item33 (besifloxacin) both fail it -- `superseded_without_current_answer` / `adjacent_evidence_exists`, for reasons specific to each: item32's population differs in a way mechanistically relevant to the exact outcome at issue ("lobectomy removes lung tissue and esophagectomy does not... a clinician cannot transfer the finding"); item33's evidence is class-level (antibiotics generally) when the trap claim was agent-specific, including a comparison against two named alternatives the class-level evidence cannot speak to.

**A second worked example of the gold-claim provenance boundary (ADR-091/093), this time caught by the author rather than the assistant.** A first draft of RB-0022/item30's gold claim included a sentence describing the evidence source's reference list and its inclusion of a different, non-retracted study by the same two authors as the retracted trial. The author removed it before submitting: "That is provenance about our verification, not clinical evidence — the rule we established on item 21." The detail is preserved in full in `Evidence.note`, where the independence-verification record belongs, not in `gold_claim.text`. Recorded in `docs/ANNOTATION.md` alongside the RB-0013 worked example, so the boundary has two concrete illustrations, not one.

**`outcome_hierarchy_mismatch` used for the first time as recorded (ADR-113): RB-0023/item32 is `EVIDENCE_SECONDARY`.** The field is explicitly *not* what decided `item_type` here -- the author's own reasoning named the population mismatch as the deciding factor ("the population mismatch alone decides it"), with the outcome-hierarchy problem recorded as a separate, additional fact about the evidence. This is exactly the intended use: two distinct failure axes, tracked independently, neither substituting for the other.

**Corpus and retrieval ceiling:** 12 new documents (4 sources, 4 notices, 4 evidence entries) inserted via the standard incremental path. Corpus: 400,597 -> 400,609 records, hash `44f88ebd...`. Retrieval ceiling re-run across all 24 items.

**Two more notice-above-trap-source cases:** RB-0022 and RB-0023 (both `trap_source_rank=2`, `notice_rank=1`) join the existing six, bringing the count to **8 of 24 items (8 of 23 notice-bearing)** -- continuing the exact growth pattern already on record; this batch alone added two, not one. Both new cases are verified-bare, empty-text notices, consistent with ADR-111's necessary-not-sufficient finding (now 8/11 verified-bare items flip, 3/11 do not). `results/notice_above_trap_source.md` updated; the ADR-114 retrievability measurement is not yet extended to these two new cases, flagged rather than silently assumed to still hold.

**Jaccard/length checkpoint, n=23:** all four new items have notice documents, folded in. Pearson still favors length (0.7151 vs. 0.6898, narrowed from the n=19 gap of 0.0455 to 0.0253); Spearman still favors Jaccard (0.7825 vs. 0.6558). The disagreement persists; no new leave-one-out investigation run at this checkpoint since the qualitative picture is unchanged from ADR-109. n=30 prediction unaffected.

**Consequences:** `docs/STATUS.md`, `docs/ANNOTATION.md` updated. item31 remains the one candidate from this batch not yet drafted.

## ADR-116: The one documented case of correction propagating correctly, given equal prominence to the failures

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Resolving item31's independence (ADR-113) surfaced a fact this project had not gone looking for: the Cochrane review "Ivermectin for preventing and treating COVID-19" did not merely happen to omit the retracted Samaha trial -- its authors actively screened for retraction status, found it, and excluded the trial by name, recording the reason in their own published methods table. Verbatim, from the review's own text: **"One trial awaiting classification in the previous review version was retracted in the meantime (Samaha 2021); we excluded the trial in this review update."** Table 3: Study ID "Samaha 2021," Status "Excluded," Reason **"Trial did not pass research integrity check, due to published retraction notice."**

**The author's instruction, recorded here rather than left as an incidental note inside ADR-113: nearly every finding in this project is about the scientific record failing to update -- retracted evidence persisting in citation, notices ranking below the papers they correct, models (by the premise this benchmark tests) potentially reproducing retracted findings. This is the one clean, documented counterexample, and it should be reported with the same prominence as the failures, not folded quietly into an independence check.** A benchmark built entirely from failure cases risks implying the failure is universal or inevitable; it is neither, and this project now has direct, verified proof of that from its own research process, not asserted from general knowledge.

**What makes this case specifically valuable, beyond being a counterexample:** it is not merely an instance of correction happening to work -- it is an instance with a visible, auditable mechanism. The Cochrane review's authors did not simply fail to find the retracted trial; they had already included it in an earlier version of the review ("awaiting classification"), then re-screened at the next update cycle, found the retraction, and removed it with a stated reason tied explicitly to "research integrity check." **This is what correct handling of a retraction looks like in a downstream synthesis: not an absence of contamination, but an active, documented, repeatable screening step that catches contamination introduced earlier and removes it once found.** That is a higher standard than "never got it wrong" -- it is "got it wrong once, checked again, and fixed it," which is a more realistic and more demonstrable standard for both evidence syntheses and, by direct analogy, for the RAG systems this benchmark evaluates.

**Decision, a paragraph drafted for Discussion, ready to place:**

> *Every other finding in this project describes some part of the scientific record failing to register a retraction: citations persisting after correction, retraction notices ranking below the papers they correct in dense-embedding retrieval, a benchmark's own annotators twice misjudging whether adjacent evidence actually answered a question. This project also has one clean, verified counterexample, and it deserves equal weight. The Cochrane systematic review underlying this benchmark's item on ivermectin for COVID-19 had included a since-retracted trial in an earlier version; at its next update, the review's authors re-screened, found the trial's retraction, and removed it, recording the reason in their own published methods table: the trial "did not pass research integrity check, due to published retraction notice." This is not an instance of a retraction simply never being cited -- it is an instance of contamination being introduced, caught on a later pass, and corrected, with the correction itself documented and auditable. That is a more demonstrable and more realistic standard than perfection, and it is a standard this project can point to directly: a retraction can propagate correctly through a downstream synthesis, given an explicit, repeatable check for it. The question this benchmark asks of retrieval-augmented systems is whether they meet that same standard -- not whether they never encounter retracted evidence, but whether they have anything resembling this review's own re-screening step when they do.*

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md`'s Discussion `[TODO]` stub gets a second pointer, alongside ADR-110's five-instances paragraph, so this is not lost. The two paragraphs are complementary, not competing -- one about this project's own process, one about the evidence ecosystem it studies -- and should likely appear near each other in the final Discussion, in whichever order best serves the paper's argument (the author's call when Discussion is written, not decided here).

---

## ADR-117: RB-0025 (item31, ivermectin) recorded -- the item_type boundary rule's hardest case so far, a third worked example on the provenance boundary (this time a correct inclusion, not a removal), and a flagged single-item training-data observation

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** item31 was the one candidate from the current batch not yet drafted, held pending its independence check (ADR-113). The author drafted it and, in doing so, overrode their own initial `superseded_with_current_answer` read after applying the item_type boundary rule properly: three mismatches compound between the trap claim and the surviving evidence -- population (asymptomatic infection vs. symptomatic outpatients), outcome (viral clearance vs. mortality/quality of life), and intent (transmission reduction vs. treatment). No source has re-measured whether ivermectin affects viral clearance specifically in asymptomatic infection. `superseded_without_current_answer` / `adjacent_evidence_exists`.

**Recorded explicitly as the rule's hardest case so far, the tension named rather than resolved into the classification alone.** Cochrane's high-certainty no-benefit finding is strong enough that no clinician would prescribe ivermectin for asymptomatic infection on this evidence -- the clinical decision is practically settled. But the rule asks whether current evidence answers the *specific* question the retracted paper asked, and the viral-clearance-in-asymptomatic-infection claim specifically remains unmeasured. **A question can be practically closed while the specific claim inside it stays open -- these are not the same fact, and the item_type rule tracks the second, not the first.** Flagged as a watch item for a possible third `ItemType` category ("practically closed, specifically unmeasured") if this shape recurs. **Not created now -- one case is not enough to generalize a category from, and doing so prematurely risks shaping the category around this one example's particulars rather than the actual distinction.** `docs/ANNOTATION.md` updated with this watch flag, explicitly marked as not-yet-acted-on.

**A third worked example of the gold-claim provenance boundary (ADR-091/093) -- this time a case of correctly keeping a sentence in, not removing one.** The gold claim states, verbatim from the Cochrane review's own published text: the review "explicitly excluded the retracted trial because it did not pass its research-integrity check following the published retraction." This stays in `gold_claim.text`, not moved to `Evidence.note`, and the distinction from RB-0013's and RB-0022's removals is the point worth recording precisely: **RB-0013 and RB-0022 removed sentences describing what *this project* did to verify independence (checked a reference list, found or didn't find a citation) -- that is provenance about our own process, and fails the operative test.** The Cochrane exclusion statement describes what *the review itself* says about its own methodology, in its own published text -- a clinician reading that review would see this exact statement. It passes the operative test cleanly: the sentence would make identical sense if this benchmark had never been built. **The boundary was never "no mention of independence or exclusion," it was always "no narration of our own verification act" -- this item is the first case sharp enough to show the difference clearly**, since most items' independence facts (a reference list not containing a citation) are not things the evidence source itself publishes about, while this one is.

**Single-item observation, flagged as exactly that and not as a stratum:** this is the only item in the benchmark whose retracted claim sits inside a politically contested evidence base heavily represented in model training data (ivermectin and COVID-19). If models evaluated against this benchmark perform differently on this item than on comparable items, that would indicate pretraining exposure competing with retrieved evidence -- a distinct failure mechanism from what every other item tests (which assume the model has no strong prior belief about the claim independent of what it retrieves). Recorded in the item's own annotation note, for the eval module's future design, not acted on now and explicitly not to be treated as a stratifiable comparison at n=1.

**Corpus and retrieval ceiling:** 3 new documents inserted via the standard incremental path. Corpus: 400,609 -> 400,612 records, hash `8c5cd91a...`. Retrieval ceiling re-run across all 25 items. **RB-0025 is itself a ninth notice-above-trap-source case** (`trap_source_rank=2`, `notice_rank=1`) -- the pattern continues growing with essentially every batch. Folded into the Jaccard dataset (n=24): Pearson gap between length and Jaccard narrows further to 0.0146 (0.7219 vs. 0.7073), Spearman still favors Jaccard (0.7884 vs. 0.6428) -- the disagreement persists, not yet resolved, n=30 prediction unchanged.

**Consequences:** `docs/STATUS.md`, `docs/ANNOTATION.md` updated. This closes out the current pre-screened batch -- all five candidates from ADR-112 (items 29 through 33) are now recorded as BenchmarkItems.

---

## ADR-118: Jaccard/length interpretation pre-registered for both outcomes before n=30; notice-above-trap-source given a confidence interval and an isolating measurement that identifies the specific mechanism

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** two requests, prompted by the same discipline that produced ADR-094's original falsifiable prediction. First: the Jaccard/length Pearson gap has closed to 0.0146 at n=24 (ADR-117) -- close enough that either direction is live at n=30, so the interpretation of both outcomes needed to be written down before the deciding data exists, not after. Second: notice-above-trap-source (9/24) is growing every batch and the author asked for it to be treated as a real finding -- a rate with a confidence interval, an account of what the nine have in common beyond bare notices (given that three bare-notice items do not flip), and a *designed* measurement to isolate the mechanism, not another descriptive count. The author also asked for a view on reordering the paper's primary retrieval finding, to be given before either of us commits, not decided unilaterally.

**Jaccard/length, both outcomes, drafted in `results/notice_term_overlap.md`:** if the Pearson gap stays closed or reverses at n=30, what survives is that term overlap (however measured) predicts notice rank at a real, non-noise magnitude (~0.6-0.7) -- what is lost is the comparative claim that dilution specifically beats simple length as an account, which becomes genuinely open rather than resolved. If the gap reopens in Jaccard's favor, the concrete demonstration already exists at n=24 and does not need to wait: RB-0002 and RB-0013 are exactly as short as the items that do flip their trap source, but do not, because their vocabulary overlap with their own question is low -- length's account is wrong about both, Jaccard's is right about both. **A third line of evidence, independent of which way the Pearson race lands, is reported this session and does not wait for n=30 either way** -- see below.

**Notice-above-trap-source, given a confidence interval:** 9/24 = 0.375, Wilson 95% CI **[0.212, 0.573]**. Wide, as expected at this n, but it rules out both "rare edge case" and "near-universal." The pattern's robustness is directional (never gone down, present in every batch checked) rather than a narrow interval -- both things are true and the paper should state both, not conflate them.

**What the nine have in common, checked and ruled out first:** domain, `item_type`, and `claim_type` splits in the flip group track the overall benchmark's proportions closely -- none of the three separates flip from non-flip. Jaccard alone doesn't separate them either (already established: RB-0013 and RB-0008 share an identical Jaccard, opposite outcomes).

**The isolating measurement, designed and run this session (`results/source_jaccard_delta.json`):** ADR-114 ruled out the trap source being a weak retriever *in general* (measured against 19 unrelated questions). The remaining, sharper hypothesis is that the source is diluted relative to the notice **on their shared question specifically** -- the actual competition. Measured directly: `source_jaccard` = Jaccard(question, trap source's own indexed text `f"{title} {summary}"`, the exact text `corpus/embed_index.py` embeds), computed with the identical tokenizer as the existing notice Jaccard. `delta = notice_jaccard - source_jaccard`. **Result: `delta` separates the flip group (mean +0.200) from the non-flip group (mean -0.007) with only 3 misclassifications out of 24 (point-biserial r=0.68, p=0.00025; Mann-Whitney p=0.0005).** This is a within-item, head-to-head test, not a corpus-wide correlation against a numeric rank -- it holds regardless of how the Jaccard/length Pearson race resolves at n=30, and is the stronger citation for the dilution mechanism specifically if that race ties or reverses.

**On reordering the paper's primary retrieval finding:** recommendation given, decision left to the author. Not yet, but close -- the `delta` result is what would tip it. The Jaccard/length correlation is a global, corpus-wide claim with an unresolved Pearson/Spearman disagreement; notice-above-trap-source is a specific, binary claim now backed by a clean, directly-isolated mechanism (21/24 correct on a single threshold). What still argues for the current ordering is the width of notice-above-trap-source's own CI (0.212-0.573) -- the paper's headline finding should have its *rate* stated with confidence, not just its mechanism. **If n=30-40 narrows that CI without the rate collapsing, and `delta`'s separation holds on the new items, that is the condition under which the swap is warranted** -- worth revisiting explicitly at that point, not assumed either way now.

**Consequences:** `results/notice_term_overlap.md` and `results/notice_above_trap_source.md` both updated in place, dated entries preserving prior checkpoints. `results/source_jaccard_delta.json` is a new file. No change to the n=30 numeric predictions themselves (ADR-094, unrevised). `paper/paper.md`'s eventual Results/Discussion pass should draw the primary-vs-supporting framing from whichever condition above is true when it is written, not from this ADR's snapshot.

---

## ADR-119: Next batch pre-screened directly after a background-agent session-limit failure -- three candidates verified, prioritizing direct-replacement evidence per the author's explicit instruction, with search cost reported plainly

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** the author asked for the next five-item batch to actively prioritize candidates with a *direct* replacement (same intervention, comparator, and population, re-measured) over merely adjacent evidence, since 12 of 25 items are currently `superseded_without_current_answer` and a benchmark where half its items test one capability is unbalanced. The author explicitly authorized reporting scarcity as data rather than lowering the standard. A background general-purpose agent was launched to do this research; it failed on a session-limit error before producing any output. Per this project's established practice for background-agent failures (noted twice previously, ADR-105/112), the partial state was discarded rather than resumed, and the batch was researched directly instead, using `curate.work_queue.build_work_queue` to generate a deterministic, exclusion-filtered (already-used, rejected, deferred, author-cap) candidate queue from freshly exported tier-1 pools (156 Pharmacology, 119 Obstetrics/Gynecology candidates).

**Three candidates reached full verification, written up as `results/item34`-`item36_raw_material.md`:**

- **item34 (rw-6062, Pharmacology) -- a formal Retraction and Replacement, the cleanest possible direct-replacement shape in the benchmark so far.** The da Costa et al. Lancet NSAID network meta-analysis (74 trials, 58,556 patients) was retracted and, in the same Lancet issue, replaced by the same authors' corrected reanalysis (76 trials, 58,451 patients, after two missed trials and a duplicate-publication error were found) -- numerically almost identical results, an unchanged conclusion. No independence check applies (the replacement is a correction of the identical analysis, not separate evidence, matching RB-0012's precedent) -- a third instance of the record self-correcting cleanly and discoverably, worth pairing with RB-0012 and ADR-116's Cochrane-ivermectin case in the paper's Discussion.
- **item35 (rw-21782, Obstetrics/Gynecology) -- a direct primary-study re-measurement plus institutional-tier mechanism evidence.** DeLong's retracted NHANES analysis claiming HPV vaccination lowers pregnancy probability is directly re-measured by Schmuhl et al. (*Vaccine* 2020), the same NHANES data source, explicitly correcting the confounders (birth control use, etc.) DeLong's paper was retracted for omitting -- independence confirmed directly (DeLong's paper does not appear among Schmuhl's 24 references). A systematic review/meta-analysis on premature ovarian insufficiency (the mechanism DeLong's own paper proposed) supplies the institutional tier ANNOTATION.md section 3.2 requires. Flagged for the author: the two sources answer slightly different parts of the trap claim and should likely both be cited, not merged into one.
- **item36 (rw-24670, Obstetrics/Gynecology) -- a genuine split within one item, recorded rather than forced to a single verdict.** A 2014 meta-analysis (74 studies) directly replaces four of the retracted paper's five measured lipid parameters (total cholesterol, triglycerides, non-HDL-c, and the LDL-c null result), independence trivial by temporal priority (predates the retracted paper by two years). The fifth and most specific component -- oxidized LDL, the paper's own title-billed contribution -- has no institutional-tier synthesis found after a direct, explicit search. Flagged as a genuinely close call for the item_type boundary rule depending on which component the drafted trap claim centers on.

**One candidate deferred, not accepted, per the standing rule (item31's precedent): rw-8425 (Fujii/Tanaka/Kobayashi PONV thyroidectomy trial).** Strong candidate current evidence exists (a 585-trial Cochrane network meta-analysis, concordant ranking), but whether Fujii's fabricated trial was excluded from that review's 585 included studies could not be confirmed -- the full Cochrane "Characteristics of excluded studies" table sits behind a paywall that resisted the same Unpaywall route that worked for RB-0025, and the open-access abridged version does not reproduce it. Logged for a future attempt, not written up as ready.

**Two candidates screened and found adjacent-only, by direct inspection rather than assumption:** sildenafil for oligohydramnios (rw-22856) -- current evidence is for fetal growth restriction, a population the retracted trial explicitly excluded, plus a real safety-signal complication (neonatal pulmonary hypertension) the isolated-oligohydramnios trial never measured; ustekinumab metabolic parameters (rw-19091) -- current syntheses measure cardiovascular events, not the metabolic biomarkers the retracted study actually measured, an outcome-level mismatch. Eight further candidates were screened and dropped for eligibility reasons unrelated to replacement quality (scrubbed/empty abstracts, preclinical studies mistagged as clinical by the claim-type screen per ADR-030's known unreliability) -- full table in `results/batch_2026-08-17_search_cost.md`.

**On the direct-replacement question itself:** of six candidates that were genuine clinical claims with a real eligibility search performed, two had a clean direct replacement, one had a partial/split direct replacement, one had a direct-shaped replacement with unresolved independence, and two were adjacent-only -- a meaningfully higher hit rate than ADR-105/112's batches (roughly 2 of 5 recorded items each). Worth noting as evidence that deliberately screening for this shape works, not just a property of this particular pool.

**Consequences:** `results/item34_raw_material.md`, `results/item35_raw_material.md`, `results/item36_raw_material.md`, `results/batch_2026-08-17_search_cost.md` are new files. No `trap_claim.text` or `gold_claim.text` wording is drafted anywhere in them, per ADR-040's hard rule -- the author drafts from this raw material directly. Two open verification points flagged for the author in items 34 and 35: the original retraction notices' own prose text could not be independently fetched past a 403 on the publisher sites (Lancet, Taylor & Francis) and no open-access mirror was found -- `notice_text_verified` should be set honestly (`false`, per ADR-042's fourth outcome) unless the author can reach the notices through institutional access.

---

## ADR-120: Inaccessible retraction notices named as a structural access barrier, not this project's shortcoming; item36's classification shown to depend on question wording; a second training-data-contamination watch item paired with RB-0025

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** three follow-ups from the author on ADR-119's batch.

**1. Notice access for items 34 and 35, resolved.** `notice_text_verified = false` for both, per
ADR-042's fourth outcome -- the author has no institutional access, and none is expected. Each
raw-material file now records the specific routes tried and failed, rather than leaving the gap
implicit: PubMed EFetch (no indexed body text), direct WebFetch against the publisher page
(403), an Unpaywall lookup (both notices resolve to `oa_status: bronze` with a direct PDF URL),
and WebFetch against that Unpaywall-supplied URL (403 again despite Unpaywall's "open"
classification -- a known bronze-OA pattern: nominally free-to-read in a browser, not actually
fetchable by an automated tool without a session). Four routes, both notices, all failed.

**This is now a running, countable pattern across six separate publisher/platform instances,**
**not two isolated gaps:** Elsevier (RB-0003's *J Am Coll Surg* notice, `unverifiable`,
ADR-042-era), BMJ (RB-0004's *Br J Ophthalmol* notice, `unverifiable`, same era), JAMA (item17's
"Notice of Retraction and Replacement," paywalled, ADR-083 -- the specific case that deferred
that candidate entirely), Cochrane (this session, rw-8425's excluded-studies table, blocking
confirmation of a promising deferred candidate), and now Lancet and Taylor & Francis (items 34
and 35, this ADR). **Stated as the author asked: a structural barrier, not a project shortcoming
-- a benchmark builder without institutional/university access cannot read a substantial**
**fraction of the correction record itself, across essentially every major publisher this**
**project has needed to check.** This directly constrains who can do this kind of work at all,
independent of skill or effort: the four-route search performed for items 34 and 35 is close to
the practical ceiling of what an unaffiliated researcher can do. Record this in the paper's
Limitations section as a structural finding about the scholarly-communication infrastructure
this project studies, not as a gap in this project's own diligence -- the routes tried and the
100% failure rate across six major publishers are themselves the evidence for that framing.

**2. Item36's classification depends on how the question is worded -- a real weakness in the**
**item_type boundary rule, recorded as a second watch item.** The author applied the rule to the
question as asked, deliberately, rather than resolving the split: framed around oxidized LDL
(the paper's own title-billed, distinguishing finding), no institutional-tier source answers it,
`superseded_without_current_answer` / `adjacent_evidence_exists`. The alternative framing --
center the question on the standard lipid panel, which the identical evidence base directly
replaces -- was considered and explicitly rejected, not overlooked, on the grounds that it would
answer a real question but not the one this specific paper is remembered for asking. **The
boundary rule (ADR-106/107) presumes a single, well-defined "the question the retracted paper**
**asked"; item36 shows a multi-outcome paper can be honestly summarized at more than one level**
**of specificity, with the rule producing opposite classifications from each, and the annotator's**
**own wording choice decides which applies.** A possible future rule -- test the outcome the
paper's own title advertises, since that is most likely what a reader (or a model trained on the
literature) takes from the paper -- is named but **not adopted now: one case is insufficient to
generalize from.** `docs/ANNOTATION.md`'s watch-item section gains a second entry alongside
RB-0025's "practically closed vs. specifically unmeasured" tension (ADR-117); both stay flagged,
neither acted on, pending a second occurrence of either.

**3. RB-0025 (ivermectin) and item35 (HPV vaccine/pregnancy) form a two-item watch group for**
**training-data-contamination risk, tracked but not sought out.** Both retracted claims sit
inside politically contested evidence bases that circulated widely outside the scientific
literature (COVID-19 ivermectin claims; anti-vaccination material citing DeLong's paper
specifically) and are plausibly over-represented in model pretraining data relative to their
actual evidence quality. Two independent instances is enough to treat as a small tracked group
rather than two isolated single-item observations (ADR-117's original framing for RB-0025 alone).
**The mechanism this group tests is distinct from every other item in the benchmark: pretraining**
**exposure competing with retrieved evidence, rather than a retrieval-ranking or evidence-**
**currency failure** -- a model could fail these two items for a reason no other item can
diagnose. **Explicit standing instruction: do not seek out further such items.** Selecting for
political controversy would bias the sample toward a specific, sensational failure mode and
compromise what the benchmark is measuring. If a third instance arises *organically* through
normal candidate screening, it joins the group; the group is never a target to fill.

**Consequences:** `results/item34_raw_material.md` and `results/item35_raw_material.md` updated
with the four-routes-tried documentation and `notice_text_verified = false`. `results/
item36_raw_material.md` updated with the author's final classification, the rejected alternative
framing, and the question-wording finding. `docs/ANNOTATION.md` gains the second watch-item
entry. `docs/STATUS.md` should track the six-publisher inaccessible-notice count and the
two-item training-data-contamination watch group (RB-0025, item35) at its next update.
`paper/paper.md`'s Limitations section gains a pointer to the structural-access-barrier framing.

---

## ADR-121: Items 34-36 recorded (RB-0026/RB-0027/RB-0028) -- a first tell-check pass, a clean M1-vs-correctness test case, and two sharp findings from the retrieval re-run that survive honest leave-one-out scrutiny

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** the author drafted all three items from the ADR-119/120 batch by hand, per ADR-040. Recorded, corpus updated (10 new documents: source/notice/evidence per item, 2 evidence entries for RB-0027), retrieval ceiling re-run across all 28 items, Jaccard/length and notice-above-trap-source datasets extended to include the new items.

**RB-0026 (item34, NSAID network meta-analysis) recorded as drafted -- a clean pass, no tell fires, `superseded_with_current_answer`.** Flagged, per the author's instruction, as the sharpest test of currency-versus-correctness in the benchmark: the corrected replacement reaches substantively the same conclusion as the retracted version, so a model citing the retracted source gives a clinically right answer from a formally void one. Every scoring axis except M1 would mark this correct -- recorded for the eval module's future design as the item to specifically check once M1 is measured, since a high M1 rate here would carry the least clinical harm of any citation failure in the benchmark, an important qualifier the raw retraction-citation rate alone would not convey.

**RB-0027 (item35, HPV vaccine/pregnancy) is the sixth application of the mandatory pre-recording tell-check (ADR-107), and the first to pass.** The gold claim's own final sentence names an outcome mismatch (self-reported infertility / premature ovarian insufficiency vs. the trap claim's ever-pregnant metric), triggering the required check. Checked directly against the rule: could a clinician or patient act on the current evidence to answer the question as worded ("what effect does HPV vaccination have on fertility")? Yes -- unlike every prior triggered case (RB-0015, RB-0005, RB-0011, item28, RB-0023, RB-0024), the three outcome measures here are converging operationalizations of one underlying question a patient would actually ask, not a switch to a different question. Recorded as evidence the rule discriminates rather than flags every hedge reflexively. Two of the author's own draft edits recorded per this project's standing practice (removed a redundant clause; removed "providing reassurance" as an editorial judgment rather than a reported finding). RB-0027 and RB-0025 now form the explicit two-item training-data-contamination watch group named in ADR-120.

**RB-0028 (item36, oxidized LDL/preeclampsia) recorded exactly as resolved in ADR-120** -- `superseded_without_current_answer`, the question deliberately centered on the trap paper's title-billed finding rather than the directly-replaceable lipid panel generally.

**The retrieval ceiling re-run surfaced two findings that needed leave-one-out scrutiny before reporting, and both survive it as real, not artifacts -- reported per this project's standing discipline of publishing whichever way a check comes out.**

1. **RB-0026's notice ranks 15th and its trap source ranks 7th -- both the worst recorded in the benchmark, by a wide margin (previous worsts: notice 3rd, trap source 2nd).** This single item collapses the Jaccard/length-vs-rank Pearson correlations at the resulting n=27: length falls from 0.7219 (n=24) to **0.0581** -- under the |r|<0.2 null-signature threshold ADR-094 named in advance for the n=30 checkpoint. **Leave-one-out run immediately, given the size of the swing (ADR-109's precedent): removing RB-0026 alone restores length to 0.6933 and Jaccard to -0.6781, both within 0.03 of the n=24 values.** RB-0026's own Jaccard (0.20) and notice length (144 chars) are unremarkable; what is extreme is its rank, and its trap source's own rank is also extreme -- consistent with a broad, heavily-studied topic (NSAIDs for osteoarthritis) facing more legitimate corpus-wide competition in a 400,622-document general biomedical corpus than the benchmark's typically narrower questions face, not with the notice-vs-source dilution mechanism failing. **The pre-registered null condition is technically met at n=27 on the bare Pearson number, and reporting that number without this leave-one-out finding attached would misrepresent what happened.** Whether n=30 produces a second instance of this "broad-topic corpus competition" shape or RB-0026 remains singular is the open question for the next checkpoint, not resolved here.

2. **RB-0027 is a tenth notice-above-trap-source case, and the first with zero lexical overlap on either side of the competition.** Its notice's Jaccard with its own question is exactly 0 (no shared tokens: `HPV`/`vaccination`/`fertility` vs. `papillomavirus`/`vaccine`/`pregnancy`), and its trap source's Jaccard is only 0.04 -- yet the notice still outranks the trap source, by a wider margin than usual (rank 2 vs. 6). The isolating `delta` measurement (ADR-118), which correctly classified 21/24 items at the last checkpoint, misclassifies RB-0027 as its 4th error at n=27 (Mann-Whitney p=0.00119, point-biserial r=0.6177, both still significant but weaker than 0.00052/0.6806) -- because `delta` is a purely lexical measure and RB-0027's competition is won on grounds it cannot see: semantic similarity the dense embedding model captures (HPV vaccine ~ papillomavirus vaccination; fertility ~ pregnancy) that plain token overlap does not. **This is not a flaw in the delta measurement -- it is the sharpest demonstration yet that the lexical-overlap account, however well it explains 26 of 27 items, is not a complete account of the underlying retrieval mechanism.** Rate: 10/28 items overall (35.7%); 10/27 notice-bearing items (37.0%), Wilson 95% CI [0.215, 0.558] -- close to unchanged from n=24's [0.212, 0.573], the first checkpoint where the *proportion* (as opposed to the ever-growing count) looks close to stabilizing, worth watching rather than assumed.

**Consequences:** `data/benchmark/items.jsonl`/`sources.jsonl` (28 items). Corpus: 400,612 -> 400,622 records. `results/retrieval_ceiling_report.json`, `results/notice_term_overlap.{md,json}`, `results/notice_above_trap_source.md`, `results/source_jaccard_delta.json` all updated with dated, non-overwriting entries. `paper/paper.md`'s eventual Results section must report RB-0026's null-signature-technically-met result together with its leave-one-out qualification, never the bare Pearson number alone -- flagged here explicitly so a future drafting pass cannot simplify it away. The n=30 checkpoint (2 items away) should specifically watch whether a second broad-topic item reproduces RB-0026's shape.

---

## ADR-122: Embedding cosine similarity measured directly as the candidate real predictor -- resolves RB-0027 cleanly, outperforms both lexical proxies once RB-0026 is set aside; a direct test of "topic crowdedness" as RB-0026's explanation returns a clear null across the benchmark

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** the author's diagnosis of RB-0027 (ADR-121): retrieval runs on dense-embedding cosine similarity, not lexical overlap, and Jaccard was always a proxy for that mechanism, useful because lexical and semantic similarity move together in most items -- RB-0027 is the case where they separate. Predicted in advance, before running it: embedding cosine similarity will predict rank better than either lexical proxy, and RB-0027 will stop looking anomalous once measured correctly. A second, independent instruction: test whether RB-0026's anomalously bad ranks (trap source 7th, notice 15th, both benchmark worsts) are explained by a real, general phenomenon -- broad topics facing more corpus-wide competition -- by correlating each item's trap-source rank against a direct measure of topical crowdedness, not assumed from one item's story.

**Both investigations run together** (`results/embedding_similarity_and_crowdedness.md`), sharing the same 28 full-corpus searches: for each item, the question is embedded and searched against the full 400,622-document corpus, and both the trap source/notice's own cosine score (FAISS's raw inner product, exact since vectors are normalized) and the cosine score of the 100th-ranked document (a pre-committed crowdedness proxy, not tuned after seeing results) are read directly from the ranked output.

**Cosine similarity confirmed as the author predicted, once RB-0026 (an outlier here too) is set aside.** At face value (n=27): cosine Pearson r=-0.4175, Spearman rho=-0.7589 -- already the strongest of the three on Spearman. **RB-0026 is a larger outlier under cosine than under either lexical proxy** (standardized residual +4.853, dwarfing everything else), and its own cosine values are *high*, not low (notice 0.806, trap source 0.820) -- meaning RB-0026's problem is not semantic mismatch, it is rank position given a good match. **Leave-one-out, run immediately (ADR-109/121's standing discipline): excluding RB-0026, cosine's Pearson rises to -0.7288**, now clearly the strongest predictor of the three, ahead of Jaccard's own best value.

**RB-0027 stops being an anomaly, exactly as predicted.** Its notice's cosine similarity (0.7818) is ordinary; its standardized residual under the cosine fit (-0.924) is well within the normal range that includes every item except RB-0026. `notice_cosine - trap_source_cosine` is positive (+0.0316), directly confirming the notice legitimately outscores its trap source in the space retrieval actually uses. **Recorded precisely, not overclaimed:** the cosine-delta measure separates all 27 flip/non-flip cases perfectly (0 misclassifications), but this is reported as close to definitional (both ranks derive from the same single ranking) rather than an independent confirmation the way the lexical delta (ADR-118) was -- its value here is confirming the extraction pipeline is internally consistent and giving RB-0027's flip a concrete, mechanistic explanation, not a new predictive discovery.

**Topic crowdedness, tested directly against all 28 items: no significant correlation with trap-source rank (Pearson r=0.1304, p=0.51; Spearman rho=0.0698, p=0.72). Stated plainly, per the author's explicit instruction to say so if it does not hold: it does not hold as a general phenomenon.** RB-0026 is genuinely among the most crowded topics measured (2nd of 28) -- consistent with, but not proof of, its own poor rank. But the single most crowded item, RB-0009, has a perfect trap-source rank (1st), and three more of the eight most-crowded items also rank 1st. A broad topic does not generally doom retrieval on this evidence. **RB-0026 remains an open, unresolved anomaly** -- high semantic relevance and high crowdedness simultaneously, yet a poor rank neither factor alone explains, and the crowdedness account does not generalize to the rest of the benchmark. Not smoothed into a confirmed finding the data does not support.

**Explicitly not touched: ADR-094's pre-registered n=30 predictions for length and Jaccard specifically.** This ADR adds a third metric and reports where it does and does not improve on the first two; it does not revise or substitute for the original falsifiable predictions, which are evaluated as written at n=30.

**The refinement sequence -- length, then Jaccard, then cosine similarity -- is recorded as worth reporting in full in the paper, not collapsed to only the final result.** Each step was a genuine measurement against real data at the time it was made, and each was superseded for a stated, evidenced reason (Jaccard because length was only an indirect proxy for dilution; cosine because RB-0027 showed lexical overlap itself was only a proxy for semantic similarity). This sequence is itself evidence of the project's measurement discipline.

**Consequences:** `results/embedding_similarity_and_crowdedness.md` and `.json` are new files. `results/notice_term_overlap.md` and `results/notice_above_trap_source.md` both gain cross-reference pointers, no substantive rewrites to their own pre-registered content. `docs/STATUS.md` updated. RB-0026's actual mechanism (why it ranks poorly despite high semantic relevance and above-average crowdedness) remains open -- flagged as a natural next investigation (a crowdedness measure tied to each item's own trap-source score rather than a shared fixed rank cutoff), not attempted this close to the n=30 checkpoint.

---

## ADR-123: The cosine-delta circularity recorded as the sixth instance of the running self-correction pattern; a non-circular test of notice cosine alone; RB-0026 given a named paragraph; crowdedness recorded as a tested-and-rejected hypothesis for the paper's methods

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** four follow-ups from the author on ADR-122's findings, all aimed at the same standard this project holds itself to: report what actually held up, including where a promising-looking number needed a caveat or turned out to be wrong.

**1. The cosine-delta circularity is recorded as the sixth instance of the claim-outrunning-evidence pattern (extends ADR-104/106/108/110's five-instance paragraph).** `delta_cos = notice_cosine - trap_source_cosine` separated all 27 flip/non-flip cases with zero misclassifications -- but both quantities are read off the identical single ranked list produced by the identical cosine-similarity computation, so the result is close to a restatement of the outcome, not independent evidence for it. This was flagged in `results/embedding_similarity_and_crowdedness.md` at the point it was computed, before being presented as a discovery the way the genuinely independent Jaccard-based delta (ADR-118) was. **The six-instance paragraph, extended and rewritten now (supersedes ADR-110's five-instance version, which supersedes ADR-106's four-instance version and ADR-104's three-instance version; all three kept as historical record, not rewritten):**

> *Over the course of building this benchmark, six claims were stated more strongly, or would have been presented more confidently, than the evidence or reasoning behind them at the time actually warranted, and each was caught before it did lasting damage. A retrieval-ordering prediction, pre-registered on no data, was weakened in response to a single unexpected item -- and the weakening, not the original prediction, was the error the later data corrected. A discoverability claim conflated one successful case with a different, unrelated set of failures, producing a rounder, more citable number than the evidence supported. A retrieval pattern was called stable after three items and falsified by the fourth. A boundary rule, written specifically to replace case-by-case judgement with a checkable test, was wrong about its own first worked example -- caught by applying the rule literally to the case that had prompted it. The same rule, applied independently a second time by a different reader to a different item, produced the identical category of error: adjacent, actionable-sounding evidence mistaken for evidence that actually answers the question asked. And a measure built to isolate a retrieval mechanism -- the difference between two documents' cosine similarity to the same question -- separated every flip case from every non-flip case perfectly, which looked, for a moment, like the sharpest finding this project had produced, before it was checked and found to be close to circular: both similarity scores came from the identical ranking the outcome was itself defined by, so the near-perfect separation was mostly a restatement of the definition, not new evidence. None of these six were fabrications; each was an honest reading of too little data, too little checking, or too little scrutiny of a result's own logical structure, stated with more confidence than that reading could support. What caught the first three was a standing practice of verifying each claim against its underlying source before it left this project's working notes. What caught the fourth and fifth was a rule specific enough to be checked against its own worked examples. What caught the sixth was neither of those -- it was asking, before reporting a perfect-looking number, where each of its two inputs actually came from, and noticing they came from the same place. A benchmark's credibility rests as much on this kind of visible self-correction, from every direction it can come from, as on any single reported number -- reviewers cannot check what they cannot see.*

**2. A non-circular test, run because the circularity above meant the real question was still open: does a notice's absolute cosine similarity to its question, alone, predict whether it flips its trap source?** Tested directly (`results/embedding_similarity_and_crowdedness.md`), with no reference to `trap_source_cosine` anywhere in the computation: AUC = 0.8529, point-biserial r = 0.6275 (p = 0.0005), Mann-Whitney p = 0.0028. **The honest answer is neither of the two the author framed in advance: this is a real, non-circular, statistically significant predictor (not "purely relative, cannot predict from the notice alone"), but it is not complete.** RB-0027 -- the item that started this whole line of investigation -- has the *lowest* absolute notice cosine of all ten flip cases, and is this predictor's one false negative: it flips because its trap source is weaker still (cosine 0.7503, lower than its own notice's 0.7818), a relative outcome the absolute-cosine-alone model cannot see. Recorded precisely: absolute notice quality is a real, useful, non-circular signal for most flip cases; RB-0027 specifically remains a relative story, not an absolute one, even under the correct metric.

**3. RB-0026 given a named paragraph in `results/embedding_similarity_and_crowdedness.md`, not a footnote, stating exactly what was ruled out (semantic mismatch; crowdedness as a general phenomenon) and leaving the item openly unexplained.** One unexplained item in twenty-eight is recorded as an ordinary, expected outcome of a project that is actually looking, not a gap requiring resolution before the file can be considered complete.

**4. The crowdedness hypothesis is recorded, explicitly, as the author's own hypothesis, tested directly, and rejected at p=0.51 -- for the paper's Methods section, alongside three other claims that did not survive scrutiny, drafted here as the honest-negative-results paragraph the author asked for:**

> *Not every hypothesis tested during this project's construction survived contact with data, and reporting only the ones that did would give a misleading picture of how the work actually went. Four are recorded here. A retrieval-ordering prediction, pre-registered before any item was annotated, was weakened after a single early item ran against it -- the weakening was later shown to be the mistake, and the original, unsoftened prediction held up as more data arrived (section 3.11). An early internal count of discoverable retract-and-replace corrections claimed a publisher was "0 for 3"; checked against its own underlying cases, one of the three was not a failure of that publisher at all, and the corrected count (0 for 2, with the one success traced to an unrelated publisher) was sharper, not weaker, than the original. A candidate item's classification under this project's item-type boundary rule was initially recorded as one outcome and reversed on a second, more careful reading -- twice, independently, by two different people applying the identical written rule to two different items, which is evidence about the rule's own blind spot rather than either person's carefulness. And a specific hypothesis proposed to explain one benchmark item's anomalously poor retrieval rank -- that broad, heavily-studied clinical topics generally face more corpus-wide retrieval competition than narrow ones -- was tested directly across every item in the benchmark and did not hold: the single most topically crowded item, by the measure used to test it, had a perfect retrieval rank. That item's own poor rank remains unexplained. Reporting a tested hypothesis that failed is not a weaker result than reporting one that succeeded; it is the same standard of evidence applied in both directions.*

**Consequences:** `results/embedding_similarity_and_crowdedness.md` updated with the circularity note, the non-circular cosine-alone test, and RB-0026's named paragraph. `paper/paper.md`'s Methods section (§3.11's `[TODO]` stub) gains a pointer to this ADR for both the six-instance paragraph placement and the tested-and-rejected-hypotheses paragraph, to be inserted verbatim or lightly edited when that section is drafted for real. The length -> Jaccard -> cosine-similarity refinement sequence must be presented in that order in the Results/Methods prose, not collapsed to presenting cosine similarity as though it were reasoned to directly -- flagged explicitly so a future drafting pass does not simplify the narrative away. `docs/STATUS.md` updated.

---

## ADR-124: Next batch pre-screened -- 3 candidates verified (two Retraction-and-Replacement cases with contrasting substance outcomes), and a second independence catch: the most current synthesis found pools the retracted trial's own data

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** standard next-batch pre-screening, two items short of the n=30 checkpoint. Sourced via the same deterministic, exclusion-filtered queue as ADR-119's batch.

**item37 (rw-25837, EMF exposure/ADHD) -- a second Retraction-and-Replacement case, and the sharpest possible contrast to RB-0026.** Where RB-0026's correction changed nothing substantive, this one does: the original's confident, clean headline (aHR 2.01, doubled ADHD risk) is replaced, after fixing an incorrectly derived exposure cutpoint, by a qualitative "inconsistent and nonlinear" finding with no comparably clean replacement effect size. **Notably, this notice's full text was successfully read** -- JAMA Network Open is a fully open-access title (`cc-by`, not the "bronze" pattern that blocked items 34/35), refining the six-publisher inaccessible-notice pattern (ADR-120): not every JAMA-family journal is equally unreachable, the specific title matters.

**item38 (rw-18954, prenatal dexamethasone/CAH behavioral outcomes) -- a third Retraction-and-Replacement case, with a real, flagged research gap.** The notice names its own replacement citation directly (a rare, valuable case, matching RB-0026/RB-0012's precedent) -- but the *original* paper's abstract has been overwritten by retraction boilerplate on PubMed (ADR-065's "original claim erased" shape), so the trap claim's specific effect direction could not be independently confirmed from a primary source this pass. Recorded as an open point for the author, not guessed at or filled from secondhand reporting.

**item39 (rw-5695, TURP/5-alpha-reductase-inhibitor bleeding) -- a second independence catch (after item17's Oh et al. 2022) of the same shape: the largest, most current synthesis found is contaminated by the retracted trial itself.** A 2026 meta-analysis (30 RCTs, 2,974 patients, the most obviously relevant source found) was checked directly against its own reference list before use, per ADR-058's standing rule -- and the retracted trial appears at reference #45, pooled directly into the headline result. **Disqualified, not merely flagged**, exactly as ADR-058 requires. A smaller, older (2015) synthesis, verified independent by temporal precedence (predates the retracted trial by ~3 years), was used instead, and itself carries a real nuance worth preserving: it confirms finasteride's effect but finds dutasteride's "inconclusive" -- a partial concordance with the retracted trial's claim of equal efficacy for both drugs, not a clean match. **Worth stating in the paper's methods regardless of this item's fate: a synthesis being large, recent, and obviously on-topic is not protection against pooling retracted data -- if anything, a retracted trial has had more time to be cited the longer it circulated before its retraction was noticed.**

**Consequences:** `results/item37_raw_material.md`, `results/item38_raw_material.md`, `results/item39_raw_material.md`, `results/batch_2026-08-19_search_cost.md` are new files. No `trap_claim.text`/`gold_claim.text` wording drafted anywhere, per ADR-040. Domain balance this batch is lighter on Obstetrics/Gynecology than usual (1 of 3, tag-only) -- one promising candidate (rw-21640, forearm BMD in postmenopausal RA) identified but not fully researched within this session, flagged as a first candidate for the next pass. `docs/STATUS.md` updated.

---

## ADR-125: Synthesis-contamination rate measured directly (3/19, 15.8%) -- arguably this project's most consequential finding so far; a three-item retract-and-replace substance stratum; item38 resolved to proceed; the JAMA access finding corrected from publisher- to journal-level

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** four follow-ups from the author on ADR-124's batch, before drafting items 37-39.

**1. The synthesis-contamination rate, measured across this project's own annotation history, not just logged as isolated catches.** The author's framing: item39 is the second confirmed case (after item17) of a current-evidence candidate pooling the retracted study's own data, and two is a pattern worth measuring. **Method:** every item from item17 onward (the item that prompted ADR-058's independence-check rule to be written) where a synthesis-level candidate source was seriously evaluated and its reference list actually checked, excluding retract-and-replace items (RB-0012, RB-0026, item37, item38) whose "replacement" cannot be contaminated by a third party by construction. This yields 19 items. **Result: 3 of 19 (15.8%), Wilson 95% CI [5.5%, 37.6%].** The three: item17 (Oh et al. 2022, disqualified), item27/RB-0019 (a second, topically closer candidate meta-analysis disqualified, a less-precise but independent one used instead), item39 (Hehir et al. 2026, disqualified). **Explicitly contrasted with RB-0025/item31, the one documented case of the opposite outcome** -- Cochrane's own review text names the retracted trial and states why it was excluded, the correction mechanism visibly working rather than silently failing. Full item-by-item accounting: `results/synthesis_contamination_rate.md`.

**This is recorded as arguably the project's most consequential finding to date, per the author's own framing, not just another rate alongside the retrieval-ranking measurements.** It is a claim about whether the evidence base itself is clean, one layer upstream of anything about retrieval or generation: in three cases, a reader consulting current best evidence through an obviously relevant, recent, methodologically serious synthesis would inherit the retracted study's own data without any visible warning. **Explicitly scoped, not overclaimed:** the denominator is 19 syntheses this project's annotators happened to evaluate because they were topically relevant to specific retracted claims already selected for other reasons -- not a designed or random sample of the review literature, and the wide CI reflects that. A population-level audit is named as a natural follow-on study, not attempted here.

**2. A three-item stratum: does a Retraction-and-Replacement preserve or overturn the original finding?** RB-0026 (preserved, near-identical), item37 (overturned -- a clean aHR 2.01 becomes a qualitative "inconsistent and nonlinear" finding), item38 (unknowable -- the original abstract is erased from PubMed, only the replacement's own finding is confirmable). Flagged explicitly as a stratum to report, not a rate -- n=3 names the axis and gives one concrete example of each shape; it does not size how common any of them is. `results/retract_and_replace_substance_stratum.md`.

**3. item38 resolved to proceed, not deferred, per the author's explicit instruction.** The replacement's own findings are directly readable and the gold claim rests on those, not on the original's exact effect size -- the erased-abstract gap does not block drafting the way item17's fully unreadable notice did. `results/item38_raw_material.md` updated: `trap_claim.text` must be reconstructed from the notice's own description of the original analysis's structure (not a directly-read original abstract), recorded explicitly as a limitation specific to this item, distinct from and narrower than item17's total block.

**4. The JAMA notice-access finding corrected: journal-level, not publisher-level.** item37's JAMA Network Open notice was read directly in full (`cc-by`, fully open access) -- unlike item17's JAMA Oncology notice and unlike the general "JAMA" framing in ADR-120's six-publisher list. **Specific titles within a publisher predict access, not the publisher imprint as a whole.** `docs/STATUS.md` corrected to name "JAMA Oncology" rather than bare "JAMA" in the inaccessible-notice list, with the refinement stated explicitly rather than silently fixed -- this matters for anyone attempting to reproduce this project's access-checking work.

**Consequences:** `results/synthesis_contamination_rate.md` and `results/retract_and_replace_substance_stratum.md` are new files. `results/item38_raw_material.md` updated to ready-to-draft status. `docs/STATUS.md` updated: new top-level items for both new findings, corrected JAMA framing, batch status updated to "ready to draft." `paper/paper.md` should gain a Results section pointer to the synthesis-contamination rate as a headline-candidate finding, not a footnote to the retrieval-ranking results, when Results is drafted for real.

---

## ADR-126: Annotation closed at 28 items -- a compressed deadline (arXiv target moved from November to mid-September), the same fixed-window reasoning as ADR-037, applied to a shorter window; items 37-39 deferred, not rejected

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** the arXiv preprint target moved from the first week of November 2026 (the project protocol's original, non-negotiable deadline) to mid-September 2026 -- a compression of roughly six to seven weeks. ADR-037 already established the governing principle for exactly this situation: the benchmark is sized by a fixed annotation *window*, not a fixed item count, and the paper states this plainly as an honest methodological constraint, not a weakness to omit. That window was originally set to run through 2026-09-30, with October reserved for the RAG pipeline, model runs, analysis, and writing. **The new mid-September arXiv target does not leave room for an annotation window running to the end of September** -- the same downstream work (evaluation module, stats layer, RAG pipeline, a dry run, a full run across four models/four conditions/three seeds, analysis, writing) now has to fit in the time between now and mid-September instead of between October 1 and early November. Annotation closes today, at whatever count it has reached: **28 items.**

**Decision:** Annotation is closed at n=28. This follows ADR-037's reasoning exactly -- a fixed window, not a fixed target -- with the window itself shortened by the same schedule compression that is shortening every later stage. **28 is not a statistical target and was never chosen as one; it is where the window ended.** Items 37, 38, and 39 (ADR-124/125, fully researched, independence-verified, ready to draft) are recorded as `DeferredCandidate`s with reason "annotation closed at n=28 for schedule reasons" -- explicitly not rejections, excluded from rejection-rate accounting per ADR-078's standing rule, and named as worth revisiting if this becomes a longer research programme (section 11 of the project protocol already anticipates that this benchmark is the first instance of a broader programme, not a one-off).

**For the paper's methods section, stated with the same plainness ADR-027/037 established and the author's explicit instruction to repeat here: sample size was set by a fixed schedule, compressed partway through annotation by a moved deadline, not chosen to hit a round number or a statistical power target.** the project protocol's non-negotiable floor (minimum 50 hand-verified items) does not bind this benchmark's headline count -- that floor was written for the originally-scoped larger benchmark and was already understood to be a minimum, not this project's actual target; 28 is below it, and the paper must say so rather than imply the 50-item standard was met. State this as a real, load-bearing limitation, not a footnote: **a benchmark this size supports the qualitative and mechanistic findings this paper's Discussion is actually built on (the correction record's own failure modes -- notice retrievability, synthesis contamination, retract-and-replace substance variability) far better than it supports precise headline accuracy rates with tight confidence intervals.** Per-domain and per-stratum confidence intervals (ADR-027's standing requirement) remain required and will be visibly wide at this n -- report them as wide, not narrowed by omission.

**Consequences:** `data/benchmark/deferred_candidates.jsonl` gains three new entries (rw-25837/item37, rw-18954/item38, rw-5695/item39), `results/item37`-`item39_raw_material.md` each marked deferred at the top, matching item17's established pattern. `docs/STATUS.md`, `docs/ANNOTATION.md`, and the project protocol all updated to reflect the closed count and the compressed timeline -- the project protocol's own deadline text (section 2) is updated only because the author has explicitly changed it here; it is not otherwise touched without instruction, per the project protocol's own standing rule. All downstream work (evaluation module, stats layer, RAG pipeline, dry run, full run, analysis) now proceeds against a fixed n=28, not a moving target -- nothing in that work should be written to assume more items might still arrive.

---

## ADR-127: M2 (Staleness-weighted Accuracy) dropped -- every candidate weighting scheme requires inventing a parameter this project's own data does not justify

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** the project protocol defines M2 as "accuracy weighted by how superseded the supporting evidence is." The author asked for either a formula with clear justification, or a recommendation to drop it, stating a preference for dropping if any formula would be arbitrary -- two well-defined metrics being stronger than three where one was invented to fill a slot.

**Candidate formulas considered and rejected, in order:**

1. **Binary retracted-vs-current weighting** (cite current evidence = 1.0, cite retracted evidence = 0.0). Rejected: this is not a new metric, it is M1 (Retraction Citation Rate) restated as an accuracy score. It adds a label, not information.
2. **Time-decay weighting** (a penalty that grows with time since the source's retraction, e.g. an exponential half-life). Rejected: the decay rate and functional form would have to be invented from nothing -- this project has no measured quantity (clinical harm over time, citation decay in practice, anything) to calibrate a half-life against. Any specific number chosen would be a guess dressed as a formula.
3. **`evidence_tier_match`-based weighting** (same_tier = full credit, lower_tier = partial credit, e.g. 0.5). Rejected on a category-error ground, not just an arbitrariness ground: `evidence_tier_match` measures the rigor of the *current replacement* evidence relative to the trap claim's own tier -- it says nothing about how superseded the *retracted* evidence is. Using it to weight staleness would conflate two different axes this project already keeps separate on purpose.
4. **A hop-distance weighting** (score by how many links separate a citation from ground truth -- primary retracted, cited by a retracted synthesis, etc.). Rejected: most items in this benchmark are single-hop (one retracted source, one current answer); only a handful (RB-0004/RB-0005's Fujii-cascade shape) have real multi-hop structure, too few to support a general ordinal scale, and the scale's own step sizes would still be invented.
5. **`evidence_direction`-based weighting** (e.g. citing a retracted claim that was later *contradicted* penalized more than one later *confirmed* concordant, motivated directly by this project's own RB-0026-vs-item37 contrast, ADR-125). This is the most defensible candidate considered -- it is motivated by real, measured structure in the data, not invented from nothing. **Still rejected**, for a narrower and more specific reason: the qualitative distinction it is trying to capture (did the correction preserve or overturn the original substance) is *already captured*, directly and legibly, by `evidence_direction` as a categorical field and by the retract-and-replace substance stratum (ADR-125) reported as an explicit breakdown. Collapsing that distinction into a single weighted scalar would require choosing a specific numeric penalty ratio (how much less harmful is a concordant-retracted citation than a contradictory one?) that no data in this project calibrates, and would make the qualitative distinction *less* visible to a reader than reporting it as a stratified breakdown already does.

**Decision: M2 is dropped, not deferred and not replaced with a placeholder.** Every formula considered either duplicates M1, invents an uncalibrated parameter, or conflates two axes this project keeps separate for good reason. The evaluation module implements **M1 (Retraction Citation Rate) and M3 (Correction Following) only.** The qualitative staleness distinctions M2 was meant to capture remain fully reported -- via `evidence_direction`, `evidence_tier_match`, and the retract-and-replace substance stratum -- as explicit stratified findings, which is a more honest presentation of exactly the same information than a single invented scalar would have been.

**For the paper, stated plainly per the author's explicit instruction, since the deferral is already documented across many ADRs (ADR-032, and every item's `evidence_direction`/`evidence_tier_match` annotation) and a careful reader will notice the gap between the project protocol's original three-metric framing and a two-metric result:** report M2 as considered and dropped, not omitted silently. State the five candidate formulas above in summary, the shared reason none survived (an invented, uncalibrated parameter or a category conflation), and that the distinctions M2 targeted are reported instead as explicit categorical strata. This is a stronger, not weaker, methodological position -- a benchmark that names an invented metric and declines to report it is more credible than one that reports a number nobody can justify.

**Consequences:** the project protocol updated -- M2 marked dropped with a pointer to this ADR, not deleted (the original definition stays visible as the record of what was considered and why it didn't survive). `src/retraction_bench/eval/` implements M1 and M3 only; no `compute_swa` or equivalent function should be written. `paper/paper.md`'s Methods and Results sections both need the drop stated explicitly when drafted -- flagged in the relevant `[TODO]` stubs.

---

## ADR-128: The n=30 Jaccard/length checkpoint evaluated at its actual final n (27 notice-bearing items) against the ADR-094 predictions as written -- a genuinely mixed result, not re-baselined to read more favorably

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** annotation closed at n=28 (ADR-126) before the pre-registered n=30 checkpoint (ADR-094) could be reached. The author's explicit instruction: evaluate the predictions as written against the real final n, state that the checkpoint moved by two items for schedule reasons, and do not re-baseline -- report whatever the actual n=27 (notice-bearing; RB-0005 excluded throughout, ADR-071) numbers say against the letter of the original prediction, not a number adjusted to look more conclusive.

**The final numbers are exactly the n=27 checkpoint already measured and reported in ADR-121/122 -- nothing new was computed for this evaluation, only the verdict was drawn.** Two readings exist and both are reported, because the single known outlier item (RB-0026, ADR-121/122) changes the answer:

| | Length Pearson r | Jaccard Pearson r (magnitude) | Length Spearman | Jaccard Spearman (magnitude) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Bare, all 27 items | 0.0581 | 0.2525 | 0.5154 | 0.7136 |
| Excluding RB-0026 (n=26) | 0.6933 | 0.6781 | 0.5969 | 0.7407 |

**Evaluated against ADR-094's predicted ranges (length 0.35-0.55, Jaccard 0.45-0.65 at n=30) and its named null-signature condition (|r|<0.2 by n=20-30):**

1. **The broad "mechanism is real, correlations stabilize" prediction is supported once RB-0026 is set aside, and only then.** Excluding it, both Pearson correlations (0.693, 0.678) land just above their predicted ranges -- closer to confirmation than to the null signature. Bare, length's Pearson (0.058) actually crosses the explicit |r|<0.2 null-signature threshold ADR-094 named in advance as evidence *against* the mechanism -- but this is driven entirely by one item whose anomaly has an independently established, unrelated cause (corpus-wide topic competition, ADR-122's crowdedness investigation), not a broad decay toward zero across the dataset. **Neither reading is reported alone; both are stated, with the outlier's cause named, per ADR-121's standing rule not to report the bare number without that qualification.**
2. **The comparative claim -- "Jaccard's magnitude should remain larger than length's at every intermediate measurement point," ADR-094's specifically flagged sharper test -- gives a genuinely mixed verdict, not a clean pass or fail.** On Spearman, Jaccard leads length in both readings (0.714 vs 0.515 bare; 0.741 vs 0.597 excluding the outlier) -- unambiguous. **On Pearson, the primary metric the prediction was phrased around, the comparative claim holds bare (0.253 vs 0.058) but fails once the outlier is excluded (0.678 vs 0.693, length very slightly ahead, a margin of 0.015).** This is the specific failure mode ADR-094 named in advance as "worth flagging explicitly since it is the one outcome that would undermine the dilution account without simply reducing to 'there is no effect'" -- it has occurred, narrowly, and only in one of the two readings, and only on one of the two correlation measures. **Reported as exactly that: unresolved, sensitive to a single item's treatment, not a clean confirmation and not a clean falsification.**

**Stated per the author's explicit instruction, not softened: the checkpoint moved from n=30 to n=27/28 for schedule reasons (ADR-126), not because the data justified stopping early or because a favorable stopping point was chosen.** No number in this evaluation was computed after this decision was made to produce a particular outcome -- both readings use the exact n=27 figures already on record before this ADR was written.

**Decision:** This is the final word on the length/Jaccard/notice-rank correlation for this benchmark -- no further checkpoint is coming. The paper reports both readings (bare and RB-0026-excluded), states the comparative claim's split verdict plainly, and does not present either reading as *the* result. The embedding-cosine-similarity finding (ADR-122/123) remains the sharper, more complete account of the underlying mechanism and should carry more weight in the paper's actual argument than the Pearson-vs-Spearman split on the original two lexical proxies -- but the original pre-registered prediction is still evaluated and reported on its own terms first, per standing practice never to let a later, better measurement quietly replace an earlier falsifiable one.

**Consequences:** `results/notice_term_overlap.md` gains a final "n=28 checkpoint (the n=30 target was not reached)" section recording this verdict, explicitly marked final. `paper/paper.md`'s section 3.11/4.5 `[TODO]` stubs updated to require exactly this dual-reading, split-verdict report, not a single number. No further leave-one-out or checkpoint work is planned for this correlation.

---

## ADR-129: Model selection revised to zero-cost routes only -- supersedes ADR-045's four paid/self-hosted models; verified live, not from memory; caching/resume designed for unpredictable free-tier cutoffs

**Status:** Accepted (supersedes ADR-045's model-selection decision; ADR-045's embedding choice, BAAI/bge-large-en-v1.5 run locally, is unaffected and not revisited here)

**Context:** hard constraint, stated by the author as non-negotiable: the entire evaluation must run at zero cost. ADR-045's original four-model set (Claude Opus 5, GPT-5.6 Sol, self-hosted Llama 4 Scout, self-hosted Qwen3.6-27B) assumed either paid API access or real self-hosting infrastructure for a 109B/27B-parameter model, neither of which is available under this constraint.
