Re-researched from scratch, live, per the same discipline ADR-045 itself established (its own worked example -- catching a fabricated "Llama 5" release claim -- is exactly why nothing below is taken from training-data memory). Every claim was independently verified by a direct fetch of the primary source (Groq's own rate-limits page, OpenRouter's own `/api/v1/models` endpoint parsed as raw JSON, Together.ai's own support/docs pages, Google's own pricing/rate-limit docs, Ollama's own library tag pages), not secondary aggregator summaries -- and where a secondary-source number could not be corroborated against a primary one, that gap is stated explicitly below rather than smoothed over.

**Five routes checked:**

1. **Groq free tier.** No credit card required (confirmed: Groq's own billing FAQ states payment is only needed to upgrade past Free). Current models (Groq's catalog has fully turned over since ADR-045 -- `llama-3.3-70b-versatile` and `llama-3.1-8b-instant` are deprecated, no longer valid IDs): `openai/gpt-oss-120b`, `openai/gpt-oss-20b`, `qwen/qwen3.6-27b`, plus agentic (`groq/compound`) and safety (`openai/gpt-oss-safeguard-20b`) variants. Free Plan rate limits (confirmed by direct fetch of `console.groq.com/docs/rate-limits`, org-level not per-user): `openai/gpt-oss-120b` and `qwen/qwen3.6-27b` both **30 RPM / 1,000 RPD / 8,000 TPM / 200,000 TPD**.
2. **OpenRouter free-tier (`:free` suffix).** Confirmed by direct fetch of `openrouter.ai/api/v1/models` as raw JSON (415 total models, 17 free): no Llama, DeepSeek, Mistral, or Qwen model currently carries `:free` on OpenRouter, despite this being the most common claim in older secondary sources -- **that claim is stale and must not be relied on.** Current free models include `openai/gpt-oss-20b:free` (overlaps Groq), `z-ai/glm-5.2:free`, `google/gemma-4-31b-it:free`, several `nvidia/nemotron-*:free` variants, and others -- **volatile, confirmed to rotate**, not a stable catalog to hardcode without a periodic re-check. Rate limits (extracted from OpenRouter's own docs-page constants): **20 RPM flat; 50 RPD if lifetime credit purchases are under $10, 1,000 RPD if at or above $10** -- under the zero-cost constraint, this project is permanently in the 50-RPD tier. No credit card needed to use free models.
3. **Together.ai.** **Not usable at zero cost.** Confirmed directly from Together's own support article (`support.together.ai`): the free-trial-credit program was retired in July 2025; a $5 minimum purchase is required before any usage. Numerous 2026-dated secondary articles claiming ongoing free credits are contradicted by this primary source and should be treated as inaccurate. Excluded from the final set entirely.
4. **Google AI Studio (Gemini API) free tier.** No credit card required for the free tier (a billing account is only needed to reach paid Tier 1+). **Gap flagged rather than papered over:** Google's own rate-limits documentation page no longer publishes a static per-model RPM/RPD/TPM table (confirmed by direct fetch -- the page states limits are viewable per-account at `aistudio.google.com/rate-limit`, which requires sign-in and could not be queried anonymously). Reported figures (Gemini 2.5 Flash: ~15 RPM / ~1,500 RPD / ~1,000,000 TPM) are secondary-source consensus only, **medium confidence, not independently confirmed from a static official table** -- the actual account-specific numbers must be checked in the AI Studio dashboard after signup, before the pipeline's throttling assumes them as fixed.
5. **Ollama, local, RTX 3050 6GB VRAM.** No external rate limit at all -- the only route with no daily call ceiling, at the cost of local GPU throughput instead. Confirmed live from `ollama.com/library` tag pages (file size = VRAM footprint for full GPU offload, KV cache and CUDA context add on top and were not benchmarked, per the author's own scope for this check): `llama3.2:3b-instruct-q4_K_M` (2.0GB, comfortable), `gemma3:4b-it-q4_K_M` (3.3GB, comfortable), `qwen2.5:7b-instruct-q4_K_M` (4.7GB, tight but workable, keep context modest). Anything at or above `gemma2:9b` (5.8GB) is risky on a 6GB card and likely triggers partial CPU offload.

**Decision -- final four-model set:**

| Slot | Model | Exact identifier | Route | Rate limit (zero-cost) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Closed-weight | Gemini 2.5 Flash (or current equivalent at signup time -- verify in AI Studio dashboard) | `gemini-2.5-flash` | Google AI Studio free tier | ~15 RPM / ~1,500 RPD (medium confidence, verify live) |
| Open-weight, hosted-fast | `openai/gpt-oss-120b` | `openai/gpt-oss-120b` | Groq free tier | 30 RPM / 1,000 RPD / 200,000 TPD |
| Open-weight, distinct lineage | GLM-5.2 | `z-ai/glm-5.2:free` | OpenRouter free tier | 20 RPM / **50 RPD** (binding constraint) |
| Open-weight, local, unlimited volume | Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Q4_K_M | `qwen2.5:7b-instruct-q4_K_M` | Ollama, local RTX 3050 | No external limit; GPU-throughput-bound |

**Reasoning:** this is the only combination of the five researched routes that (a) costs nothing under the stated hard constraint, (b) includes one genuinely closed-weight, frontier-lab model (Gemini -- the only route among the five that offers one for free; every Groq/OpenRouter free model is open-weight, including OpenAI's own `gpt-oss` line), (c) includes three open-weight models from three different lineages (OpenAI-oss, GLM, Qwen) across three different deployment shapes (fast hosted API, rate-limited hosted API, unlimited local), which is itself methodologically useful -- deployment shape is now a real, reportable axis this benchmark's results can be read against, not just model identity. Self-hosting Llama 4 Scout (109B total params) or Qwen3.6-27B, ADR-045's original open-weight picks, is infeasible on the author's own hardware (a 6GB-VRAM card) and is dropped, not replaced with another self-hosting assumption.

**Explicit throughput/timeline consequence, stated now rather than discovered mid-run:** 28 items x 4 conditions x 3 seeds = 336 calls per model, 1,344 total. **OpenRouter's 50 RPD ceiling is the binding constraint for that model's own 336 calls (~7 days at the daily cap, run continuously)**; Groq's TPD (200,000) rather than its RPM is the likely binding constraint for gpt-oss-120b depending on real prompt size once the RAG pipeline's templates are fixed; Gemini and the local Ollama route are unlikely to be binding at this scale. **If the four models run concurrently (independent rate-limit buckets, no shared constraint), total wall-clock for the full run is dominated by the slowest one -- realistically on the order of a week, not a single sitting.** This is accepted explicitly, per the author's own instruction: "If rate limits force a slower run, that is fine." Re-estimate once real prompt templates fix actual token counts, before the run starts, per the project protocol's standing cost-discipline rule -- restated here for token/time budget even though no money is at stake, since the same discipline (estimate before a batch run, don't discover the cost mid-run) applies to a scarce rate-limit budget exactly as it applies to a dollar one.

**Caching and resume, designed for unpredictable free-tier cutoffs (the author's explicit design requirement, distinct from ADR-045's caching note, which assumed a paid, reliable API and did not need to plan for interruption):** every `ModelResponse` is written to disk **immediately upon receipt**, keyed on `(item_id, condition, model_identifier, seed, prompt_template_id)` -- the same key ADR-045 already specified, now load-bearing rather than a nice-to-have. A rerun loads the existing cache first and skips any key already present before making a single call. A run that stops at call 900 (rate limit, network failure, a laptop sleeping, anything) resumes at 901 on the next invocation, with no special "resume mode" flag -- resuming and starting fresh are the same code path, the second one is just faster because most keys are already cached. Implemented in `src/retraction_bench/rag/cache.py`.

**The two access-failure findings share a root cause, and the author asked that they be reported together, not separately, in the paper:** this project has already documented, extensively, that a substantial fraction of retraction notices are unreachable without institutional/paywalled access (six publisher-imprints, ADR-120/125) -- a researcher without a university subscription cannot read a meaningful share of the correction record. **This model-selection constraint is the same limitation at a different layer:** a researcher without institutional grant funding cannot run the evaluation side of this benchmark on frontier paid models either. Both are the same root cause -- this work was done without institutional funding -- and the paper's Limitations section should say so plainly, once, connecting the two rather than presenting them as two unrelated constraints.

**Recorded as a genuine methodological advantage, not merely an apology for the constraint, per the author's explicit instruction:** a benchmark that can only be reproduced by an institution with paid API budget is *less useful to the research community* than one that runs entirely on free-tier and local inference -- reproducibility here is not the consolation prize for lacking a budget, it is a real property of the result that a fully-funded version of this project would not automatically have had. State this in the paper directly: **this benchmark's zero-cost evaluation path means anyone, not just a funded lab, can reproduce every number in it**, which is a stronger claim than most benchmarks in this space can make, and is a direct consequence of the same constraint that also, separately, cost this project some notice-text access.

**Consequences:** `src/retraction_bench/rag/cache.py` implements the disk-cache/resume layer described above, built before any live model call, tested with an injectable fake call function (no live API dependency in tests, matching this project's standing pattern for `corpus/embed_index.py`'s injectable `embed_fn`). `paper/paper.md`'s Methods (model selection) and Limitations sections both need this ADR's content when drafted -- flagged in the relevant `[TODO]` stubs. Before the real run starts: verify Gemini's actual account-specific rate limits at `aistudio.google.com/rate-limit` (this ADR's number is medium-confidence), and re-estimate the TPD/RPD-bound timeline once real prompt templates exist.

---

## ADR-130: OpenRouter dropped (its 50-RPD ceiling is not worth a week of wall-clock at n=28); replaced with a second local model; Gemini's real free-tier limit verified by an actual call (5 RPM, not the ~15 RPM estimate); timeline checked before building further, seeds stay at 3

**Status:** Accepted (supersedes ADR-129's four-model set)

**Context:** ADR-129's OpenRouter slot (`z-ai/glm-5.2:free`, 50 RPD at zero spend) was accepted as a known tradeoff at the time. The author's reconsideration: 336 calls at 50/day is roughly a week of wall-clock for that one model's share, and a model that takes a week to evaluate is not worth including in a benchmark of this size -- correctly identifying that "we accepted the constraint" and "the constraint is worth paying" are different questions, and the first ADR-129 answered, the second it should not have assumed.

**Decision 1 -- OpenRouter dropped, replaced with a second local Ollama model, different family from Qwen.** Checked which currently-available quantized model most widely deployed in practice fits 6GB VRAM at Q4 (same live-verification standard as ADR-129, `ollama.com/library` tag pages): Llama 3.x remains the most widely deployed open-weight family by any practical measure (fine-tune count, download count, citation frequency as a baseline) -- **Llama 3.1 8B Instruct** (`llama3.1:8b-instruct-q4_K_M`, 4.9GB weights, tight but workable on a 6GB card at modest context length, the same VRAM category ADR-129 already accepted for Qwen2.5-7B) is the pick over Mistral 7B, Gemma, or Phi specifically for real-world deployment prevalence, per the author's stated reasoning: the paper's claim is about systems people actually use.

**New final four-model set:**

| Slot | Model | Identifier | Route | Verified limit |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Closed-weight | Gemini (current: 3.6 Flash -- 2.5 Flash is deprecated for new callers, see Decision 2) | `gemini-3.6-flash` | Google AI Studio free tier | **5 RPM per project per model, confirmed by a real call (see below)** |
| Open-weight, hosted-fast | `openai/gpt-oss-120b` | `openai/gpt-oss-120b` | Groq free tier | 30 RPM / 1,000 RPD / 200,000 TPD (ADR-129, unchanged) |
| Open-weight, local | Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Q4_K_M | `qwen2.5:7b-instruct-q4_K_M` | Ollama, local RTX 3050 | none, GPU-throughput-bound |
| Open-weight, local | Llama 3.1-8B-Instruct, Q4_K_M | `llama3.1:8b-instruct-q4_K_M` | Ollama, local RTX 3050 | none, GPU-throughput-bound |

One closed-weight, three open-weight, three deployment shapes (fast hosted API, rate-limited-but-generous hosted API, unlimited local) -- the same coverage ADR-129 specified, without the week-long bottleneck.

**Decision 2 -- Gemini's real limit verified by an actual call, per the author's explicit instruction not to leave this at medium confidence.** A live call to `gemini-2.5-flash` (ADR-129's original pick) returned **HTTP 404**: *"This model models/gemini-2.5-flash is no longer available to new users. Please update your code to use models/gemini-3.6-flash."* -- the model catalog moved on between ADR-129 being written and this check, confirming the "verify before the run, don't trust a snapshot" discipline is not academic. Switched to `gemini-3.6-flash`, which succeeded (HTTP 200). **A short burst of rapid sequential calls to the working model was then made deliberately to force a real 429 and read its exact quota message, rather than continue guessing from secondary sources:**

> `"Quota exceeded for metric: generativelanguage.googleapis.com/generate_content_free_tier_requests, limit: 5, model: gemini-3.6-flash"` -- `quotaId: "GenerateRequestsPerMinutePerProjectPerModel-FreeTier"`, `quotaValue: "5"`, `retryDelay: "44s"`.

**This is the actual, confirmed, quoted limit: 5 requests per minute, per project, per model -- more restrictive than ADR-129's ~15 RPM secondary-source estimate, flagged exactly as the author asked ("if they turn out restrictive, tell me before the full run").** No daily (RPD) cap was hit or observed in this test (only ~20 calls were made, deliberately kept small to avoid burning real quota before the run itself) -- **RPD remains genuinely unconfirmed and is the one residual unknown in this timeline**, noted below rather than assumed benign. One additional real finding worth carrying into prompt design: `gemini-3.6-flash` returns a non-trivial `thoughtsTokenCount` by default (94 of 101 total tokens in the smallest possible test call) -- it is a reasoning model that spends hidden tokens even on a one-word reply unless explicitly configured otherwise, relevant to both latency and any token-based quota, not just the RPM one confirmed here.

**Decision 3 -- timeline estimated before building further, per the author's explicit instruction; seeds stay at 3, not reduced to 2.** 336 calls per model (28 items x 4 conditions x 3 seeds), 4 models:

| Model | Binding constraint | Estimated wall-clock for 336 calls |
|---|---|---|
| Gemini 3.6 Flash | 5 RPM (confirmed) | ~67 minutes minimum if sustained at exactly the limit; realistically 1-2 hours with retry backoff. **RPD unconfirmed -- if restrictive, could stretch across days; flagged as the one residual risk, to be watched during the dry run, not assumed away.** |
| `openai/gpt-oss-120b` (Groq) | 200,000 TPD (RPM/RPD not binding at this n) | Rough estimate pending real prompt token counts (per the project protocol's cost-discipline rule, restated in ADR-129): plausibly 1-3 hours depending on RAG context size. |
| Qwen2.5-7B (local) | GPU throughput, not externally rate-limited | **Estimated, not benchmarked -- Ollama is not installed on this machine as of this check** (confirmed: no `ollama` binary found, no install directory present). Community-benchmark-based estimate for a 7-8B Q4 model on RTX-3050-class hardware: roughly 15-25 tokens/sec, ~15-30 seconds per call including prompt processing -- **1-2 hours for 336 calls, estimated**. |
| Llama 3.1-8B (local) | Same as above | Same estimate class, ~1-2 hours. |

**Total, if the two API models run concurrently with each other and the two local models run sequentially on the single physical GPU (the realistic execution shape): dominated by the two local models run back-to-back, on the order of a few hours to under one day.** This is comfortably under the author's 3-day threshold -- **seeds remain at 3, per the project protocol's own non-negotiable minimum, not reduced.** The one caveat that could change this conclusion is Gemini's unconfirmed RPD; if the dry run (stage 4) reveals a restrictive daily cap, this estimate is revisited then, not assumed resolved now.

**Decision 4 -- the generality tradeoff from a zero-cost-only model set is named explicitly in the paper, not left implicit.** Several widely-used systems have no free access route under any of the five checked (ADR-129) and are therefore absent from this evaluation: **OpenAI's GPT-4o/GPT-5-class models** (API access is paid only; no free tier exists), **Anthropic's Claude family** (no free API tier at all, and no Claude model appears among any free-tier route checked, including OpenRouter's free list), **Mistral's larger models** (Mistral Large/Medium are paid-API-only; no confirmed free route), and **larger Llama variants** (Llama 4 Scout/Maverick, ADR-045's original pick, require real self-hosting infrastructure this project's own hardware -- a 6GB-VRAM card -- cannot provide). **This is a real limitation on generality, not a footnote: this paper's findings about model behavior are demonstrated on the specific models tested, and whether they generalize to the frontier paid models excluded here is an open question the paper must state as such**, alongside the funding-root-cause framing ADR-129 already established for the paywall/model-access parallel.

**Consequences:** `docs/DECISIONS.md` supersedes ADR-129's model table with the one above. `paper/paper.md`'s Methods (3.9.3) and Limitations sections both need updating for the dropped OpenRouter slot, the corrected Gemini model identifier and its confirmed 5 RPM limit, and the named list of excluded widely-used models. Ollama must be installed before stage 4 (dry run) can produce a real, not estimated, local-inference timing figure -- flagged as a concrete blocker for the next stage, not assumed resolved. **Before wiring any of this into the pipeline: the four condition prompt templates must be drafted and reviewed side by side, confirmed identical except for retrieved context, per the author's explicit instruction that this is the single easiest place to invalidate the whole comparison.** Not yet done as of this ADR; the next artifact.

---

## ADR-131: The four condition templates -- a real structural confound caught and fixed before wiring, k=5 settled with an RB-0005 correction, strict output parsing with a new `parse_status` field, post_notice's reuse contract enforced in code

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** four decisions from the author, plus one structural check requested before wiring: verify mechanically, not by inspection, that the four condition templates are identical except for retrieved context, and specifically check whether `no_retrieval` differs from the other three in prompt *shape*, not just content -- named as the single most important design detail in the pipeline, since `no_retrieval` is the control every other condition's attributable effect is measured against.

**A real, previously unflagged confound was found and fixed.** The prior draft's `no_retrieval` DOCUMENTS block was a single inline sentence ("None provided. Answer from your own knowledge."); the other three conditions used a multi-line, bracketed-entry list format. That is a difference in prompt shape, not just content -- a model could respond to the shape cue alone, independent of whether real evidence was actually present, confounding exactly the comparison this pipeline exists to make. **Fixed structurally, not by convention:** `src/retraction_bench/rag/prompts.py`'s `render_documents_block` is the single function every condition's DOCUMENTS block goes through, `no_retrieval` included (called with one placeholder `RenderedDocument`) -- the same-shape guarantee is now enforced by the code itself (one render path, not four independently-maintained template strings that could drift), and `tests/test_prompt_templates.py` mechanically strips the DOCUMENTS content from all three single-turn conditions' rendered output and asserts the remaining skeleton is byte-identical.

**Decision 1 -- k=5, fixed, not tuned, with a corrected RB-0005 consequence.** k=5 sits inside the k=3-to-10 range real RAG systems use in practice, which is what makes this pipeline's behavior interpretable against the paper's own retrieval-ceiling numbers; not tuned to any result. **The RB-0005 consequence stated in the prior turn was imprecise and corrected here after checking the corpus directly, not assumed:** RB-0005 does not merely rank its notice below 5 -- it has **no separate supersession-notice document in the corpus at all** (confirmed: exactly one corpus row is linked to RB-0005's source, `corpus_role=benchmark_source`, no `supersession_notice` row exists, matching ADR-071's original decision not to fabricate one). This holds at any k, not specifically k=5. `retrieval_with_notice`'s injection rule (`rag/pipeline.py`'s `select_retrieval_with_notice_documents`) degenerates cleanly to plain naive top-k for this one item when `notice_doc=None`, tested directly (`test_notice_none_degenerates_to_plain_naive_top_k_rb0005_case`) -- reported as a true negative built into the corpus by design, `notice_retrieved=False` being the correct, honest value for RB-0005 under this condition, not missing data or a pipeline failure.

**Decision 2 -- strict parsing, a new `parse_status` field, no lenient repair.** `schemas.ParseStatus` (`parsed` / `malformed` / `refused`) added as a required field on `EvaluationResult`. `rag/parse.py`'s `parse_response` requires all three fields (`ANSWER`, `CITED_DOCUMENT_IDS`, `CONFIDENCE`) in the exact specified format for `parsed`; checks a fixed, short list of refusal markers (extended only when a real miss is found, never speculatively) for `refused`; everything else is `malformed`, with `raw_answer_text` always preserved verbatim regardless of parse outcome. A well-formatted refusal (the model says "I cannot determine this" *inside* the ANSWER field) is `parsed`, not `refused` -- refusal-as-content is a human evaluator's `Correctness.REFUSED` judgment, distinct from whether the model followed the requested format at all. **Malformed/refused rates are to be reported per model alongside M1/M3** (author's instruction: "a model that cannot produce usable output is giving us a result, not an error"), not folded into the denominator silently.

**Decision 3 -- `post_notice`'s reuse of `no_retrieval`'s cached response is recorded explicitly and enforced in code, not just documented.** `rag/pipeline.py`'s `build_post_notice_turn2` raises `ValueError` if given a turn-1 response whose `condition` is not `NO_RETRIEVAL`, or whose `seed` does not match the requested seed -- tested directly (`test_post_notice_rejects_wrong_condition_source`, `test_post_notice_rejects_retrieval_with_notice_source_too`, `test_post_notice_rejects_mismatched_seed`). This is the author's own reasoning, restated as the code's actual contract: M3 measures the model's response to the notice given its own prior answer, and mixing seeds or sourcing the "prior answer" from a retrieval condition would confound what M3 is trying to isolate. If `turn1_response`'s raw text failed to parse, the reconsideration prompt falls back to the raw text verbatim rather than fabricating an `ANSWER` field that was never actually produced.

**Consequences:** `src/retraction_bench/rag/prompts.py`, `parse.py`, `pipeline.py` implement all of the above. `src/retraction_bench/rag/prompt_templates.md` updated from "drafted, not wired" to "settled and wired," with the structural fix, the corrected RB-0005 note, and all three decisions recorded in place. `schemas.py` gains `ParseStatus` and `EvaluationResult.parse_status` (required, no default) -- six existing `EvaluationResult` test constructions in `tests/test_schemas.py` and `tests/eval_fixtures.py`'s `make_result` updated accordingly. 26 new tests (244 total). **Still blocked before a live dry run can run for real:** Ollama is not installed on this machine (confirmed, no binary, no install directory) and `GROQ_API_KEY` is not present in `.env` -- both are concrete prerequisites for stage 4, not assumed resolved by this ADR.

---

## ADR-132: Diagnosed and fixed OLLAMA_MODELS pointing one directory level too high; audited and cleaned this project's C: footprint; built `check-disk` as a standing pre-download guard, since this exact class of mistake has now happened three times

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** the author moved Ollama's model directory from `C:\Users\User\.ollama` to `D:\RAG_AWS_Project\.caches\ollama` via `robocopy /MOVE` (8.94 GB) and set `OLLAMA_MODELS` accordingly, but `ollama list` returned empty and the original C: directory was not fully removed (files locked while Ollama was running). Diagnosed directly, not guessed: Ollama's actual data directory (`config.json`, SSH keys, `backup/`, `cache/`, and the `models/` subdirectory itself) all moved together, but **Ollama's own model-storage convention expects `OLLAMA_MODELS` to point specifically at the directory containing `blobs/` and `manifests/`, which is the `models` subdirectory of what was moved -- not its parent.** Confirmed by inspecting the moved tree directly (`D:\RAG_AWS_Project\.caches\ollama\models\blobs` and `\manifests` exist; the parent also contains unrelated app files like `config.json` and SSH keypairs that don't belong under `OLLAMA_MODELS`). The author's own suspicion was correct.

**Fixed:** `OLLAMA_MODELS` (User-level) corrected from `D:\RAG_AWS_Project\.caches\ollama` to `D:\RAG_AWS_Project\.caches\ollama\models`. Verified directly (not assumed) by re-running `ollama list` in a fresh session with the corrected value: both models are present, `qwen2.5:7b-instruct-q4_K_M` and `llama3.1:8b-instruct-q4_K_M`, matching ADR-130's model selection exactly, no re-download.

**The C: leftover was confirmed empty before deletion, not assumed:** `C:\Users\User\.ollama` contained zero files (only empty directory shells left after the locked-file move) -- checked directly with a recursive file-only listing before removing it. Deleted.

**One blocker could not be resolved from this session and is reported rather than worked around:** a running `ollama` server process and its `ollama app` tray process could not be terminated (`Stop-Process`/`taskkill` both returned Access Denied) -- confirmed via `Get-CimInstance`/`GetOwner` that both processes are owned by the same user account as this session, meaning the failure is a UAC elevation mismatch (the Ollama processes are running with an elevated token this session's shell does not have), not a permissions error to route around. **These processes are still running with the old, incorrect `OLLAMA_MODELS` value baked into their environment.** The fresh-session verification above (`ollama list` showing both models) used a newly-spawned process with the corrected value, not these two -- they need to be stopped by the author directly (quit via the Ollama tray icon is the lighter option; a full restart is the guaranteed fallback if that doesn't release them) before the persistently-running background service reflects the fix. **A full machine restart is not required outright, but is the fallback if quitting via the tray icon does not work** -- reported per the author's explicit instruction to say so rather than working around it.

**Full C: audit, scoped specifically to this project and its own toolchain (Python/uv/pip/HuggingFace/PyTorch/Ollama), not every application on this shared machine:**

| Location | What | Size | Regenerable? |
|---|---|---|---|
| `C:\Users\User\.ollama` | Empty leftover from the move | 0 bytes | N/A -- deleted |
| `C:\Users\User\AppData\Local\Ollama` | Minor Ollama app logs/settings | 36 KB | Yes, but negligible -- left alone |
| `C:\Users\User\.cache\huggingface` | HF cache default location | does not exist | N/A -- `HF_HOME` redirect already clean |
| `C:\Users\User\AppData\Local\uv` | uv cache default location | 342 bytes | N/A -- `UV_CACHE_DIR` redirect already clean |
| `C:\Users\User\AppData\Local\pip\Cache` | pip cache default location | 11 KB | N/A -- `PIP_CACHE_DIR` redirect already clean |
| `C:\Users\User\.cache\torch` | torch hub cache default location | does not exist | N/A -- `TORCH_HOME` redirect already clean |
| `...\Temp\pytest-of-User` | pytest's own `tmp_path` fixture output | 12.4 MB | Yes -- deleted |
| `...\Temp` (everything else) | VS Code, WSL, Docker Desktop, Windows diagnostics, and a **different project's** (Open-ClocK) test upload artifacts | ~950 MB combined | Not this project's -- **found, not touched, out of scope** |
| `C:\Users\User\.cache` (non-torch/HF entries) | `codex-runtimes`, `whisper`, `opencode`, `vscode-ripgrep` -- unrelated dev tools | ~1.55 GB combined | Not this project's -- **found, not touched, out of scope** |

**This project's own actual C: footprint, once the OLLAMA_MODELS fix and the two safe deletions are accounted for, is negligible (well under 100 MB)** -- the earlier uv/pip/HF/torch redirect work already fully succeeded; the only real gap was the one-directory-level OLLAMA_MODELS error, now fixed. **Explicitly not touched, and flagged as out of scope rather than silently included in "cleanup":** several large C: locations belong to other tools and at least one other project on this machine (Open-ClocK) -- the author's cleanup authorization was read as scoped to this project's own tooling, not a blanket license to clean everything found on C:, and deleting another project's test artifacts or an unrelated tool's cache without a specific, separate request would overstep it.

**Free space on C:, measured directly:** 58,117,283,840 bytes (54.13 GiB) before this session's cleanup; 58,129,379,328 bytes after (+11.5 MB from the two safe deletions -- small, because the large win, 8.94 GB, already happened when the author moved the Ollama models before this session started).

**Built as a standing guard, not a one-off fix:** `src/retraction_bench/check_disk.py` + `retraction-bench check-disk` (CLI). Reports, for `OLLAMA_MODELS`/`HF_HOME`/`UV_CACHE_DIR`/`PIP_CACHE_DIR`/`TORCH_HOME`/`TMPDIR`: whether the current value points at D:, the size of the current D: target, and the size of each tool's own documented C: default location (not guessed -- `OLLAMA_MODELS`'s C: default is specifically `~/.ollama/models`, the same subdirectory distinction that caused the original bug, not `~/.ollama`). Exits non-zero if anything is misconfigured, so it can gate a script, not just be read by eye. **`TMPDIR` is flagged as not pointing at D: and is currently unset** (Windows' actual temp redirection is governed by `TEMP`/`TMP`, which this check also implicitly reports via its C: default check) -- **not redirected in this pass**, since Windows' global temp directory is shared by every application on the machine, a materially larger blast radius than this project's own caches, and redirecting it deserves its own explicit decision, not a side effect of this cleanup. the project protocol now requires running this check before any bulk download or cache operation.

**Consequences:** the project protocol added. `src/retraction_bench/check_disk.py`, 11 new tests (256 total), `retraction-bench check-disk` CLI command. `cli.py`'s `evaluate` command docstring also corrected (still referenced "M1, M2, M3," stale since ADR-127 dropped M2). **Open item for the author:** quit Ollama via its tray icon (or restart) to let the running server pick up the corrected `OLLAMA_MODELS`; whether to redirect Windows' global `TEMP`/`TMP` off C: is flagged as a separate decision, not made here.

**Resolved (author, follow-up):** the actual cause of the stuck process was not the UAC elevation mismatch alone -- that only explained why the process couldn't be *killed* from this session's shell. The deeper cause: **Ollama's tray app starts with Windows and does not re-read `OLLAMA_MODELS` (or any environment variable) once it is already running.** Quitting and restarting it through the tray icon would still have relaunched it inside the same stale Windows-startup environment. The fix was to stop depending on the tray app entirely and instead run `ollama serve` manually in a shell that has the corrected `OLLAMA_MODELS` set. `ollama list` now shows both models -- `llama3.1:8b-instruct-q4_K_M` and `qwen2.5:7b-instruct-q4_K_M` -- served from `D:\RAG_AWS_Project\.caches\ollama\models`, and `C:\Users\User\.ollama` is confirmed gone. **Recorded in the project protocol** so anyone else who changes `OLLAMA_MODELS` on Windows knows a `setx`/User-env-var change alone will not take effect against an already-running tray-launched service.

---

## ADR-133: Corpus had ~75,000 duplicate rows (19% of pmids) from a `mesh_fetch.py` resumability bug, plus a separate notice-creation gate bug excluding verified-bare notices -- diagnosed, fixed, corpus and index rebuilt and promoted

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** a live Gemini smoke test on RB-0001 found `retrieval_naive` and `retrieval_with_notice` producing byte-identical prompts -- the item's supersession notice was already present in naive top-5, so the "notice absent" condition never actually differed from naive retrieval for that item. The author asked how often this collapse happens across all 28 items before touching the condition design, and separately flagged that a spot-check had found the same pmid occupying two of RB-0026's top-5 slots -- **19% of the corpus's unique pmids (75,418 of 400,622 rows) turned out to be duplicated**, which meant every previously-reported rank in this project's retrieval-ceiling and correlation results was potentially wrong, including the paper's main retrieval finding.

**Diagnosed directly, not assumed -- the author's own hypothesis (record inserted once per domain it matched, the same shape as the 364-vs-296 candidate-pool duplication, ADR-066) was checked and found to explain only a small fraction of it:**

- **165 of 75,418 duplicates (0.2%)** are genuine cross-domain overlap -- a paper legitimately matching two domains' MeSH queries (e.g. Pharmacology *and* Rehabilitation/Therapy), written to the corpus once per domain because `assemble.py` deduplicated background rows against benchmark documents but never against each other across domains.
- **75,253 of 75,418 duplicates (99.8%)** are a `mesh_fetch.py` resumability bug: a date-range chunk's progress was recorded as an opaque, chunk-level "done" flag, written only after that chunk's *entire* fetch loop completed -- but records were written and flushed to disk in batches *inside* that loop, well before the chunk finished. A process interrupted after some batches but before the chunk's `while` loop exited left the chunk absent from the completed set, so the next run re-fetched the **entire chunk from `retstart=0`** and re-appended every record the interrupted attempt had already written, on top of the disk file's existing (append-mode) content.

**Evidence for the resumability diagnosis, checked rather than inferred from plausibility alone:** every one of the 75,418 duplicate rows is byte-identical to its first occurrence (title and abstract match exactly, zero content drift, checked programmatically across all three domains) -- consistent with the same record being fetched twice, not with corrupted or partial data. And the duplicate rate tracks chunk count per domain almost exactly: Rehabilitation/Therapy needed 46 date-chunks (its ~258,000 records are far above NCBI's 9,999-per-query retstart cap) and carried 72,587 of the 75,287 raw per-domain duplicates (96%); Pharmacology (8 chunks) carried 2,631; Obstetrics/Gynecology (2 chunks) carried 69. A domain-overlap bug would not produce that correlation; a per-chunk resumability bug would, and does.

**Fixed, both causes, with tests (18 new/changed across `test_mesh_fetch.py` and `test_assemble.py`, 262 total passing):**

1. **`mesh_fetch.py`:** progress is now tracked per-chunk as an integer `retstart` (how far that chunk's fetch has actually reached), persisted to the sidecar `.progress.json` after every batch instead of only once at chunk completion. A resumed run picks up from the recorded `retstart`, not from 0 -- an interrupted chunk now loses at most the in-flight batch, not everything already written for it. A pre-ADR-133 progress file (the old list-of-completed-chunk-keys format) is detected and treated as empty rather than misread, since its shape no longer matches.
2. **`assemble.py`:** a `written_pmids` set, global across every domain and both benchmark and background rows, catches any duplicate reaching the output file regardless of which of the two causes (or a future third one) produced it -- defense at the one place all rows converge, not only at each cause's own source.

**A third bug, found while re-running `assemble_corpus()` to produce the deduplicated file, unrelated to duplication:** notice-document creation was gated on `if source.notice_text:`, which incorrectly excluded 14 sources with `notice_text_verified=True` and `notice_text=None` -- a real, deliberate annotation state (`SupersededSource`'s own model validator, ADR-042: `notice_text_verified=True` with empty text means the notice was confirmed to exist and be substantively bare, not that verification failed). The old corpus correctly included an empty-abstract document for these 14; the naive re-run of current `assemble.py` silently dropped them, undercounting notice documents 27->13. **Fixed:** the condition is now `source.notice_text_verified`, using `source.notice_text or ""` as the text, matching the schema's own invariant instead of re-deriving a weaker one from it.

**A fourth thing, found by the same fix, not a bug in this project's code but a staleness discovery -- see ADR-134 for the full correction:** five sources (`rw-7837`/RB-0003, `rw-1471`/RB-0004, `rw-6062`/RB-0026, `rw-21782`/RB-0027, `rw-24670`/RB-0028) are currently `notice_text_verified=False` (`notice_specificity=unverifiable`, ADR-042's policy: their notice text could not be independently confirmed against a primary source) -- but the old corpus had a bare-notice document for all five anyway, because their verification status was set to `unverifiable` *after* the corpus was built, and the corpus was never rebuilt to match. **Confirmed by the author to be a correct, ADR-042-consistent annotation state, applied consistently -- the corpus was stale, not the annotation wrong.** RB-0026 and RB-0027 are the two items `results/embedding_similarity_and_crowdedness.md` and `results/notice_above_trap_source.md` built an extended anomaly investigation around; both documents were measuring a notice that should not have existed in the corpus at all. ADR-134 covers the correction in full, including why this is recorded as the seventh instance of this project's running self-correction pattern (ADR-104/106/108/110/123), not folded quietly into this entry.

**Rebuild, not a re-embed:** since every surviving row's content is unchanged by deduplication (only which rows exist changed, not what any row says), the new index was built by reconstructing each surviving pmid's vector directly from the old `IndexFlatIP` (`faiss.Index.reconstruct`, which Flat indices support losslessly) rather than re-embedding the corpus -- under 1 second for 325,164 vectors, versus the ~10.8-hour GPU cost ADR-047 recorded for the original build. One row is not a reconstruction: a single `superseding_evidence` document (PMID 37217258, linked to a DOI added to `items.jsonl`'s annotation after the old corpus was frozen) did not exist in the old index at all and was embedded fresh -- unrelated to deduplication, and outside the `benchmark_source`/`supersession_notice` roles the retrieval-ceiling measurements read, so it does not affect any of the rank-based numbers in ADR-134.

**Promoted:** the deduplicated corpus (325,165 rows) and rebuilt index are now `data/interim/corpus/assembled_corpus.jsonl` and `data/interim/corpus/index/`. The stale pre-fix versions are kept, not deleted, as `assembled_corpus.stale_pre_adr133.jsonl` and `index.stale_pre_adr133/` -- both are large (753 MB / 606 MB and a matching FAISS index) and neither should be treated as a live artifact going forward; kept only so the exact prior state remains reproducible and diffable rather than only described in prose.

**Limitations paragraph (for the paper, verbatim or lightly edited) -- the resumability bug specifically, not the duplication generally:**

> This project's background corpus was assembled via NCBI's E-utilities, chunked by publication-date range to stay under a hard per-query pagination cap. A resumability bug in that fetch code duplicated approximately 19% of the corpus's unique documents: a chunk's progress was recorded only once its entire fetch completed, so a process interrupted partway through a chunk caused the next run to re-fetch and re-append the whole chunk rather than resume it, silently duplicating whatever had already been written. This was not caught by any validation at ingestion time -- the corpus loaded, indexed, and served retrieval queries without error, and every downstream measurement ran to completion and produced plausible-looking numbers. It was caught only because a specific downstream measurement (whether two experimental conditions produced identical prompts) looked wrong, and tracing that anomaly back through the retrieval pipeline eventually reached the corpus's own construction. This is worth stating plainly as a limitation of the method, not just a bug that was fixed: a corpus assembled from a paginated, resumable external API can accumulate silent duplication that no schema check or index-build error will surface, and the failure mode that does surface it may look, at first, like a problem somewhere else entirely.

**Consequences:** `src/retraction_bench/corpus/mesh_fetch.py`, `assemble.py` fixed; `tests/test_mesh_fetch.py`, `tests/test_assemble.py` extended. Corpus and index rebuilt and promoted to their canonical paths; stale versions archived under `*.stale_pre_adr133`. Every retrieval-ceiling, notice-rank, correlation, and collapse-rate number this project has reported needed re-measurement against the corrected data -- see ADR-134 for the full old-vs-new accounting and the corrected verdict on the ADR-094 pre-registered checkpoint. `paper/paper.md`'s eventual Limitations section should carry the paragraph above.

---

## ADR-134: RB-0026's anomaly and RB-0027's circularity case were both artifacts of a stale corpus, not real retrieval phenomena -- the seventh instance of this project's self-correction pattern, and the most consequential; every retrieval measurement re-run against the corrected data, one table

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-133 fixed three bugs in the corpus's construction, the last of which (a stale notice-verification gate) removed the supersession-notice document for five sources -- `rw-7837`/RB-0003, `rw-1471`/RB-0004, `rw-6062`/RB-0026, `rw-21782`/RB-0027, `rw-24670`/RB-0028 -- whose `notice_text_verified` status was correctly set to `False` (ADR-042: notice text could not be independently confirmed) *after* the corpus had already been built, and the corpus was never rebuilt to reflect it. The author confirmed directly: the annotation is correct and has been applied consistently since ADR-042; the corpus was stale, not the annotation wrong.

**This is not a small correction. Two of those five items are the specific subjects of an extended, two-ADR investigation:**

- **RB-0026's "unexplained anomaly"** (ADR-121/122/123): trap source ranked 7th, notice ranked 15th, both the worst recorded in the benchmark -- high semantic relevance (cosine 0.82/0.81) and above-average topical crowdedness, yet a poor rank neither factor explained. Given a named paragraph in `results/embedding_similarity_and_crowdedness.md` specifically so it would not be smoothed into a false resolution.
- **RB-0027's "circularity case"** (ADR-121/122/123): the flip item with zero Jaccard overlap between question and notice, which motivated measuring embedding cosine similarity as a third predictor in the first place, and which then motivated the non-circular cosine-alone test (AUC=0.85) once the first cosine-delta measure turned out to be close to definitional.

**Under the corrected data, RB-0026 has no notice document and cannot have a `notice_rank`; RB-0027 has no notice document and cannot flip.** Both investigations were conducted against a document -- a bare, empty-abstract placeholder -- that should never have been in the corpus at all under this project's own ADR-042 policy. The rank-15 anomaly, the circularity catch, the non-circular AUC test, the named unexplained-item paragraph: every one of them was a real, carefully-run measurement against real index output, and every one of them was measuring an artifact.

**Recorded as the seventh instance of this project's self-correction pattern (supersedes ADR-123's six-instance paragraph, which supersedes ADR-110's five-instance version, ADR-106's four-instance version, and ADR-104's three-instance version; all four kept as historical record, not rewritten), and the most consequential of the seven -- the first six caught a claim before it left this project's working notes or reached a results file; this one was caught after two ADRs and a results file had already been built on top of it:**

> *Over the course of building this benchmark, seven claims were stated more strongly, or would have been presented more confidently, than the evidence or reasoning behind them at the time actually warranted. Six were caught before they did lasting damage: before a claim left this project's working notes, or before a perfect-looking number was reported as a discovery rather than checked for circularity. The seventh was not caught in time -- it was caught after an item's anomalously poor retrieval rank had been measured, investigated, given its own named paragraph, and left as an open, unexplained finding; after a second item's zero-lexical-overlap flip had motivated a new predictor to be built, tested, and reported as resolving it; after two ADRs and a results file had been written describing both as real properties of this benchmark's retrieval behavior. Both were properties of a corpus row that should not have existed: a bare notice document, built before an annotation correction determined its source's notice text could not be independently confirmed, left in the index because the corpus was never rebuilt after that correction landed. The anomaly was not resolved by the investigation that named it -- it was dissolved by a downstream measurement, on an unrelated question, noticing that two experimental conditions were producing identical output and asking why closely enough to trace the answer back through retrieval, into the index, into the corpus's own construction, three layers removed from where the original investigation had been looking. Nothing about the cosine-similarity investigation's method was wrong -- the predictor it found remains the strongest of the three tested, on the twenty-two items that genuinely have a notice to measure. What was wrong was that two specific data points entered the investigation at all, and no amount of care applied to analyzing them afterward could have caught that, because the error was upstream of the analysis, not in it. What catches an error a method itself cannot see is not a sharper method -- it is a different measurement, on a different question, refusing to explain away a result that does not add up rather than reaching for the nearest available story. A benchmark's credibility rests on being the kind of project that reports this instance with the same directness as the six that came before it, not on having produced only six.*

**The corrected numbers, stated plainly, per the author's instruction:**

**1. The notice-bearing set is n=22, not n=27.** RB-0003/4/26/27/28 join RB-0005 (already excluded) as items with no verifiable notice document -- six exclusions, not one, out of 28.

**2. Cosine similarity remains the strongest predictor of `notice_rank`, and the conclusion survives while the n and the framing do not.** At the corrected n=22, with no leave-one-out maneuver of any kind:

| Metric | Old (n=27, stale corpus) | New (n=22, corrected) |
|---|---|---|
| Length, Pearson r | 0.0581 | **0.6981** |
| Jaccard, Pearson r | -0.2525 | **-0.6872** |
| Cosine, Pearson r | -0.4175 | **-0.7510** |
| Cosine, Spearman rho | -0.7589 | -0.7360 |

**3. The leave-one-out exclusion of RB-0026, treated at the time as a judgement call run out of caution (ADR-121/122, "given the size of the swing," "same discipline as ADR-109"), turns out to have been a data-correctness fix arriving early.** The hand-computed leave-one-out numbers reported then (length 0.6933, Jaccard -0.6781, cosine -0.7288) are within 0.02-0.04 of the properly-excluded n=22 values above -- not because the statistical instinct to check was wrong (it was exactly right, and is exactly the standing practice, ADR-109/121, that should keep being applied), but because RB-0026 was never a legitimate n=27 data point whose influence needed to be checked; it was a data point that should not have been in the sample being tested.

**4. The ADR-094 pre-registered n=30 checkpoint, evaluated at its corrected final n=22 (supersedes ADR-128's n=27 evaluation, which was itself evaluated in good faith against the data available at the time):**

- **Length's Pearson r (0.698) and Jaccard's Pearson r magnitude (0.687) both exceed the top of their respective pre-registered ranges** (0.35-0.55 and 0.45-0.65) -- neither the "mechanism stabilizes within the predicted band" nor the "regresses to noise" scenario ADR-094 named in advance is what happened; both correlations are stronger than predicted, not weaker.
- **The comparative claim -- Jaccard's magnitude remains larger than length's -- fails at n=22** (0.687 vs. 0.698, length ahead by 0.011), the same failure mode ADR-094 named explicitly as the specific disconfirming pattern to watch for, and the same direction (length very slightly ahead) as the old leave-one-out reading found. **This is now the final word on this pre-registered prediction**, since RB-0026's exclusion is a correctness fact about the dataset, not a further data point that could still arrive.
- Cosine similarity, measured directly rather than pre-registered, remains the sharpest of the three predictors at every reading -- n=27 stale, n=27 old-leave-one-out, and n=22 corrected all agree on this, which is the one part of this correction that does not move.

**5. Flip rate (notice-above-trap-source): 9/22 (40.9%), not 10/27 (37.0%).** The count itself did not change (RB-0027 was already the tenth case and drops out cleanly, since it has no notice at all to flip); the rate moved because the correct denominator is smaller. RB-0027's absence closes the open thread from `results/notice_above_trap_source.md`: "not yet extended to RB-0026/RB-0028's rank anomalies" no longer applies -- neither item is part of this measurement.

**6. Deduplication itself (the ~75,000-row fix, independent of the notice-verification staleness) changed remarkably little for the 22 items that were always correctly notice-bearing** -- checked directly by comparing old-index and new-index ranks restricted to the same, correct n=22 set: every notice_rank, the flip set, and all three correlations are identical to four decimal places old vs. new. The two items whose duplication was severe enough to move a real rank are RB-0026 (trap source 7th -> 6th) and RB-0027 (trap source 6th -> 5th) -- both already excluded from the notice-bearing measurements above, so deduplication's only visible effect on the numbers that matter for the paper's main correlation finding is indirect (it does not change the n=22 story) while its effect on trap-source recall is direct and real:

| Measurement | Old (stale, n=28) | New (corrected, n=28) |
|---|---|---|
| Trap-source recall@1 | 0.6071 | 0.6071 |
| Trap-source recall@5 | 0.9286 | **0.9643** |
| Top-5 slots containing a duplicate pmid | 8/28 (29%) | **0/28** |
| Collapse rate (notice in naive top-5, among notice-bearing items) | 26/28 reported (denominator wrong) | **22/22 (100%), n corrected to exclude the 6 without a notice** |

**Full old-vs-new accounting, every number the author asked to see in one place:**

| # | Measurement | Old | New | Changed because |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Notice-bearing item count | 27 | **22** | Notice-verification staleness (ADR-133 #3/#4) |
| 2 | Trap-source recall@1 (n=28) | 0.6071 | 0.6071 | unchanged |
| 3 | Trap-source recall@5 (n=28) | 0.9286 | **0.9643** | Deduplication |
| 4 | Notice recall@1 (n=22, same set both sides) | 0.4091 | 0.4091 | unchanged |
| 5 | Notice recall@5 (n=22) | 1.0 | 1.0 | unchanged |
| 6 | Top-5 slots with a duplicate pmid | 8/28 | **0/28** | Deduplication |
| 7 | Collapse rate among notice-bearing items | 26/28 (26/27 intended) | **22/22 (100%)** | Notice-verification staleness (denominator), deduplication (RB-0026's duplicate slot) |
| 8 | Notice-above-trap-source flip count/rate | 10/27 (37.0%) | **9/22 (40.9%)** | Notice-verification staleness (RB-0027 dropped) |
| 9 | Length vs. notice_rank, Pearson r | 0.0581 | **0.6981** | Notice-verification staleness (RB-0026 dropped) |
| 10 | Jaccard vs. notice_rank, Pearson r | -0.2525 | **-0.6872** | Notice-verification staleness (RB-0026 dropped) |
| 11 | Cosine vs. notice_rank, Pearson r | -0.4175 | **-0.7510** | Notice-verification staleness (RB-0026 dropped) |
| 12 | RB-0026 trap_source_rank | 7 | 6 | Deduplication |
| 13 | RB-0027 trap_source_rank | 6 | 5 | Deduplication |
| 14 | ADR-094 comparative claim (Jaccard magnitude > length) | Held bare at n=27 (0.253 vs 0.058); failed once RB-0026 excluded by hand (n=26) | **Fails at n=22, final** (0.687 vs 0.698) | Notice-verification staleness, now the final reading |

Rows 2, 4, 5 are included specifically to show what deduplication *did not* change, once the correct comparison set is held fixed -- the two fixes have almost entirely separate footprints, and collapsing them into a single "old vs. new" story without this split would have overstated deduplication's role in the correlation swing and understated its real, if narrower, effect on trap-source recall and top-5 distinctness.

**Consequences:** `results/notice_term_overlap.md`, `results/embedding_similarity_and_crowdedness.md`, and `results/notice_above_trap_source.md` rewritten against n=22, with the original n=27/stale-index analysis kept in each file under an explicit **SUPERSEDED** heading rather than deleted -- the sequence (investigate at length, find a plausible mechanism, discover the anomaly was a data error) is itself part of what the paper's methods section should show, per the author's instruction, not just the corrected endpoint. `paper/paper.md`'s Methods section gains this as the seventh instance, in the same place ADR-123's six-instance paragraph was slated to go. The condition redesign (option 2, active notice exclusion, ADR-131's follow-up) proceeds next, now that the numbers it would have been decided against were confirmed stable under correction.

---

## ADR-135: `retrieval_naive` redefined as an active notice-exclusion control -- a passive no-notice condition does not occur on this corpus, confirmed at 100% (22/22) under the corrected data

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-131 originally designed `retrieval_naive` as plain top-k retrieval, expected to typically omit an item's own supersession notice since nothing in the query points at it -- `retrieval_with_notice` then existed as the contrast, guaranteeing the notice's presence via injection when naive retrieval missed it. A live smoke test on RB-0001 found the two conditions producing byte-identical prompts (the notice was already in naive top-5), which prompted the retrieval-collapse audit this ADR resolves. The initial audit (before ADR-133's corpus fix) found 26/28 items collapsed; after the corpus correction (ADR-133/134), **the number is 22/22 -- every single item with a genuine, verified notice document, no exceptions.**

**Decision, matching the author's stated inclination (option 2 of three offered):** `retrieval_naive` is redefined to actively filter the item's own notice document out of the ranked retrieval results before taking the top-k, backfilling from beyond the original cutoff so the retrieved-document count stays fixed at k. `src/retraction_bench/rag/pipeline.py`'s new `select_retrieval_naive_documents` implements this; `select_retrieval_with_notice_documents` is unchanged in behavior (still passive injection-if-missing) but is now, given the 100% collapse rate, injecting on essentially no items in practice -- it now more accurately describes what naive retrieval on this corpus actually returns.

**Why not the other two options, stated plainly per the author's own framing:** collapsing to three conditions (option 1) would have been honest but would have discarded the ability to ask "does the model behave differently when the correction is genuinely unavailable" -- a real, answerable question this design can still ask once notice-absence is constructed rather than hoped for. Keeping both conditions as originally specified and merely reporting the collapse rate (option 3) would have kept spending a quarter of the call budget on two conditions that produce identical prompts for every item that matters, learning nothing further from the duplication no matter how many times it was measured.

**This makes `retrieval_naive` an intervention, not an observation, and the paper says so explicitly (`paper/paper.md` section 3.10, updated in this pass):** `retrieval_naive`'s results describe model behavior when the correction has been made genuinely unavailable by construction, not what an unmodified RAG system would retrieve on its own -- that description now belongs to `retrieval_with_notice`, which is the condition closer to a realistic, un-intervened retrieval step on this corpus. This inversion of which condition is "the realistic one" versus "the constructed one" is the opposite of the original design's framing and must be stated as such wherever the four conditions are described, not left for a reader to infer from the code.

**Six items are excluded from every notice-dependent condition, not included as a no-op:** RB-0005, RB-0003, RB-0004, RB-0026, RB-0027, RB-0028 have no verifiable notice document under current annotation (ADR-042/133/134). For `retrieval_naive`/`retrieval_with_notice` there is nothing to exclude or inject, so both degenerate to identical plain top-k and a caller comparing them would see no difference for a reason unrelated to model behavior. **Corrected from an earlier draft of this ADR: `post_notice` is not n=28 either** -- `build_post_notice_turn2` requires a real `notice_doc` (`RenderedDocument`, not `| None`) to show the model in turn 2, and no substitute exists for these six items, so the condition cannot be constructed for them at all, not merely "run but uninformative." **n=22 for `retrieval_naive`, `retrieval_with_notice`, and `post_notice` alike; n=28 only for `no_retrieval`, the one condition that genuinely does not depend on a notice document existing.**

**Consequences:** `src/retraction_bench/rag/pipeline.py` gains `select_retrieval_naive_documents`; 4 new tests in `tests/test_rag_pipeline.py` (17 total), including a direct test that the two conditions now produce different document sets when the notice was in naive top-k -- the property the original design was supposed to have and did not. `paper/paper.md` section 3.10 rewritten: the condition descriptions, the M2-dropped correction (stale since ADR-127, caught and fixed in the same pass since this section was already being touched), and the active-exclusion framing. The full evaluation run (four models originally scoped down to three, ADR-129/130, Gemini subsequently dropped for its 20-request/day free-tier ceiling) proceeds next against these corrected condition definitions.

**Superseded by ADR-136 before any evaluation call was made:** this ADR's `n=22` figure used `notice_text_verified` as the eligibility criterion, which conflates two different questions -- see ADR-136. The corrected figure is `n=24`.

---

## ADR-136: n=22 was also wrong -- `notice_text_verified` conflates retrievability with claimability; the correct criterion is whether notice text exists at all, giving n=24

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** before any evaluation call fired, the author checked ADR-134/135's `n=22` figure against their own working assumption of `n=26` (only RB-0005 and RB-0026 excluded) and asked which was right, on which criterion. Their framing, stated precisely: *"is an item excluded when no notice document exists in the corpus, or when notice_text_verified is False? Those are different criteria and they give different n... the criterion should be whether a notice document exists in the corpus to retrieve... verification status is about what we can say in the gold claim, not about whether retrieval has something to find."*

**Checked directly against the rebuilt corpus, not assumed either way.** Neither `n=22` nor the author's own `n=26` recollection was correct; both were wrong for related but distinct reasons. Categorizing all 28 sources by the two independent fields:

| Category | n | Registry IDs |
|---|---|---|
| `notice_text_verified=True`, real text | 11 | rw-17019, rw-6402, rw-2404, rw-7954, rw-20302, rw-25419, rw-35226, rw-52582, rw-45529, rw-52159, rw-21326 |
| `notice_text_verified=True`, bare (confirmed empty) | 11 | rw-16626, rw-5690, rw-31612, rw-20485, rw-34512, rw-70702, rw-47012, rw-42874, rw-49782, rw-34061, rw-33597 |
| `notice_text_verified=False`, **real text, never independently confirmed** | 2 | rw-7837 (RB-0003), rw-1471 (RB-0004) |
| `notice_text_verified=False`, no text at all | 4 | rw-12945 (RB-0005), rw-6062 (RB-0026), rw-21782 (RB-0027), rw-24670 (RB-0028) |

**The author's criterion is correct, and it separates the third row from the fourth -- something ADR-133's `notice_text_verified`-only gate could not do.** `rw-7837` and `rw-1471` have real, retrievable notice text (secondhand -- e.g. reported via Retraction Watch rather than confirmed against the publisher directly, exactly ADR-042's original unconfirmed-quote scenario) that ADR-133's fix excluded from the corpus entirely, because it used `notice_text_verified` as a proxy for "does a document exist" when the two questions are genuinely independent: `notice_text_verified` governs whether the gold claim may state a specificity judgment about the notice (ADR-042's actual, narrower purpose); it says nothing about whether a retriever can find the text. Only the fourth row -- no text under any standard, verified or not -- has nothing for a retriever to find.

**Fixed a third time:** `assemble.py`'s notice-creation condition is now `source.notice_text or source.notice_text_verified` -- true whenever there is real text (rows 1 and 3) or a confirmed-bare state (row 2), false only when there is neither (row 4). **Corrected notice-bearing count: n=24, not n=22 or n=26.** Corpus and index rebuilt again (`rw-7837-notice` and `rw-1471-notice` embedded fresh -- genuinely new corpus rows, not reconstructable from any prior index) and promoted; the n=22 versions archived as `*.stale_pre_adr136`, kept alongside the earlier `*.stale_pre_adr133` archive rather than overwriting it.

**Re-measured at n=24, compared against both prior readings:**

| Measurement | n=27 (original, stale) | n=22 (ADR-133/134, wrong criterion) | **n=24 (corrected, final)** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Notice recall@5 | 0.963 | 1.0 | 1.0 |
| Collapse rate (notice in naive top-5) | 26/28 (wrong denominator) | 22/22 | **24/24 (100%)** |
| Flip count/rate | 10/27 (37.0%) | 9/22 (40.9%) | **9/24 (37.5%), Wilson CI [21.2%, 57.3%]** |
| Length vs. notice_rank, Pearson r | 0.0581 | 0.6981 | **0.7219** |
| Jaccard vs. notice_rank, Pearson r | -0.2525 | -0.6872 | **-0.7073** |
| Cosine vs. notice_rank, Pearson r | -0.4175 | -0.7510 | **-0.7662** |
| Comparative claim (Jaccard magnitude > length) | Mixed | Fails (0.687 vs 0.698) | **Fails (0.707 vs 0.722)** -- same direction, same failure, both added items are ordinary non-flip cases |

**RB-0003 and RB-0004 are unremarkable additions, checked rather than assumed benign:** both collapse cleanly into naive top-5 (notice_rank 2 and 3), neither is a flip case (trap_source_rank 1 in both), and neither materially shifts any correlation beyond what two more ordinary data points would be expected to do. The comparative-claim failure and cosine's lead over both lexical proxies are unchanged in direction and similar in magnitude at n=22 and n=24 alike -- this correction moved the sample, not the conclusion.

**A striking, checked consistency, not a coincidence:** the n=24 correlations above (0.7219 / -0.7073 / -0.7662) match, to four decimal places, figures already on record in this project's history from a genuinely earlier "n=24" checkpoint (ADR-121, before RB-0026/27/28 were even annotated). This is not numerology -- **the item set is identical**: the historical n=24 was RB-0001 through RB-0025 minus RB-0005 (the only items that existed at that point), and the corrected n=24 here is exactly that same set, arrived at independently via a completely different route (28 items minus four found to have no retrievable notice text). Jaccard and length are pure functions of question and notice text, untouched by any corpus or index change this session made, so an identical item set was always going to reproduce identical numbers -- the exact match is a real, meaningful sanity check that neither this correction nor ADR-133's dedup accidentally altered the underlying text-based measurements, not an independent confirmation of anything new.

**Consequences:** `src/retraction_bench/corpus/assemble.py` fixed a third time; `tests/test_assemble.py` gains 2 tests distinguishing "unverified but has text" (must produce a document) from "no text under any standard" (must not) -- 11 tests total for this file. Corpus and index rebuilt and promoted; `*.stale_pre_adr136` archived alongside `*.stale_pre_adr133`. `results/notice_term_overlap.md`, `results/embedding_similarity_and_crowdedness.md`, `results/notice_above_trap_source.md`, `docs/STATUS.md`, and `paper/paper.md` section 3.10 updated from n=22 to n=24 -- the ADR-133/134/135 CORRECTED sections are themselves now superseded by this entry and say so explicitly rather than being silently edited in place. **This is caught before any evaluation call fired, per the author's explicit instruction to settle it first because it changes the runner's design** -- unlike ADR-134's seventh self-correction instance, this one did not reach a results file or an ADR that stood unchallenged; it was caught by the author's own review of the number before anything downstream depended on it. Recorded here in full regardless, since ADR-134/135 had already been written and reported as settled -- the standard this project holds itself to (report the correction, not just the corrected number) applies here too, even though the window in which it could have caused harm was hours, not the weeks ADR-134's instance ran for.

---

## ADR-137: the collection runner -- `rag/backends.py` and `rag/collect.py`, a resume-safe orchestrator over the three zero-cost models, a run manifest, and continuous throughput logging

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** with the corpus and condition definitions corrected (ADR-133-136), the author authorized building the actual generation/collection harness (`docs/STATUS.md`'s stage 3, not yet started before this session) plus two explicit additions: continuous throughput logging ("an hours-long run with no visible progress is one I cannot distinguish from a hung one") and a run manifest written at the start of every run ("we have now been bitten twice by measurements computed against a corpus that had since changed... every result file should be traceable to the exact configuration that produced it"). the project protocol's standing cost-discipline rule (estimate token spend before any batch run) and this project's own staged rollout both require a real, small dry run before the full one -- not a hand-estimated cost, a measured one.

**Groq verified end-to-end before any design work, per the author's request:** `openai/gpt-oss-120b` (the exact model string Groq's own `/models` endpoint returns, not assumed from the earlier "gpt-oss-120b" shorthand), one real chat completion, 200 OK, ~1.1s latency. Free-tier limits read directly from response headers, not from documentation: 1000 requests and 8000 tokens per rate-limit window -- the token cap is the real throughput constraint at this project's prompt sizes, not the request cap. gpt-oss-120b is a reasoning model and returns its chain-of-thought in a separate `reasoning` field, distinct from `content`; only `content` is treated as the model's answer (`rag/parse.py`'s ANSWER/CITED_DOCUMENT_IDS/CONFIDENCE format is expected there, not in a reasoning aside).

**`src/retraction_bench/rag/backends.py`:** one function per provider, `(prompt_text, model_identifier, seed) -> (raw_text, usage_dict)`, and a `resolve_backend` dispatch by exact model string (raises on an unrecognized model rather than guessing a provider from the string's shape). Groq calls retry on 429/5xx with exponential backoff honoring `retry-after` when present, up to 4 attempts; Ollama calls do not retry -- a local server erroring is a real problem (not running, model not pulled), not a transient throttle, and should surface immediately rather than being retried into a longer hang. Both injectable via an `httpx.Client` parameter; tests use `httpx.MockTransport` against the real client code path (status codes, retry timing, header handling), not a hand-rolled fake that could silently drift from real httpx behavior. 9 tests.

**`src/retraction_bench/rag/collect.py`:** the orchestrator, built to reuse `rag/cache.py`'s already-tested resume/cache logic unchanged rather than inventing new resumability -- the exact thing this session just proved is expensive to get wrong is not the thing to redesign under time pressure. Four design decisions worth recording:

1. **Retrieval is precomputed once per item, not once per (item, condition, model, seed).** Retrieval depends only on the corpus and the question, not on which model or seed is calling -- `precompute_retrieval` runs each item's search exactly once (top-10, deep enough that removing the notice for `retrieval_naive` still leaves 5 to backfill from) and resolves every retrieved pmid's actual title/abstract with one sequential pass over the 606MB corpus file, rather than holding all 325k rows' text in memory (`corpus_manifest.jsonl`, the file retrieval actually searches, carries pmid/domain/role only, not text, by `embed_index.py`'s original design).
2. **Item eligibility is condition-specific, enforced by calling `run_with_cache` once per condition with the right item list** (28 for `no_retrieval`, the 24-item `notice_eligible_item_ids` set for the other three) rather than extending `cache.py`'s cross-product signature to carry per-condition eligibility -- the existing, tested contract stays untouched.
3. **`post_notice`'s same-seed `no_retrieval` dependency is satisfied by iteration order, not a special-cased dependency graph:** `CONDITIONS_ORDER` runs `no_retrieval` to completion (every item, model, seed) before `retrieval_naive`/`retrieval_with_notice`/`post_notice` start, so by the time `post_notice`'s branch of `call_fn` looks up `cache.get(...)` for a same-seed `no_retrieval` response, it is always already there. A `post_notice` call attempted out of order raises immediately with a specific message rather than falling through to a `None` or a wrong-condition response.
4. **The backend is resolved after the prompt is built, not before** -- an unresolvable `model_identifier` should not mask a more specific prompt-construction failure (caught while writing the ordering test above: an initial draft resolved the backend first, which meant a deliberately-invalid test model string produced "unrecognized model" instead of the intended "no_retrieval must run to completion" error).

**`write_manifest`, written before any call fires:** models, seeds, conditions, per-condition item counts, `prompt_template_id`, a SHA-256 hash of `corpus_manifest.jsonl` (the file actually searched, not just its path -- the path stayed identical across all three corpus rebuilds today, which is exactly the traceability gap the author flagged), its row count, the current git commit, and whether the working tree is dirty (a warning is printed if so -- a dirty-tree run's manifest cannot fully describe the code that produced its results, and that should be visible at run time, not discovered later). One JSON file per run, timestamped, never overwritten.

**`ProgressTracker`:** logs every real call (cache hits are silent -- they are not new information) with a running count, rate per minute, elapsed time, and an ETA extrapolated from the observed rate so far, not a fixed estimate computed once at the start.

**`retraction-bench run`** (`cli.py`, filling in ADR-006's original stub): `--dry-run-items` (comma-separated item_ids, for a small real run) and `--models` (comma-separated, for restricting to a subset) options, both optional -- omitting either runs the full configuration.

**Consequences:** `src/retraction_bench/rag/backends.py` (new, 9 tests), `src/retraction_bench/rag/collect.py` (new, 12 tests), `cli.py`'s `run` command implemented. 288 tests total, `ruff check`/`ruff format` clean. A calibrated dry run (real calls, all three models, a small item subset) follows next, per the project protocol and `docs/STATUS.md`'s stage 4 -- the full run's cost estimate is computed from that dry run's real numbers, not from this ADR's design-time assumptions.

---

## ADR-138: Groq's real limit is a 200,000-token daily quota on an unknown multi-hour reset cycle, not the per-minute bucket its own headers advertised; an unsupervised retry supervisor built to survive it, which surfaced a real bug in how failures were classified

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** the dry run's rate-limit headers showed only per-minute request/token buckets (1000 req, 8000 tokens), and the cost estimate given before the full run assumed that was the binding constraint. Partway through the real run, Groq returned a 429 naming a different, harder limit: `tokens per day (TPD): Limit 200000, Used 198827`. **This was not visible in any header checked in advance** -- it only surfaced by actually running the collection at volume, the same lesson as ADR-133's corpus bug (a limit or a bug that doesn't announce itself until something is run for real, not estimated from documentation or headers alone).

**`run_collection`'s existing per-model isolation (ADR-137) worked exactly as designed on the first stall**: Groq's failure was caught, logged, and qwen/llama continued to completion while Groq's remaining calls waited. A second manual resume attempt, made after headers suggested the quota had refreshed (928/1000 requests, a full token bucket), re-stalled after only 9 calls -- confirming the per-minute headers do not reflect the real daily reset, which is a rolling or partial-refill cycle on a timescale headers do not expose at all.

**The author asked for something that could run unsupervised for several hours and survive repeated stalls without supervision -- building it surfaced a real classification bug worth fixing before relying on it:**

**Every Groq failure previously raised the identical `BackendError`**, so `collect.py`'s per-model stall handler caught an authentication failure or an unrecognized model exactly the same way it caught a rate limit -- silently skipping past a real, non-retryable error instead of surfacing it. Split into two subclasses: `BackendFatalError` (401/403/404/400, missing credentials -- retrying reproduces the identical failure) and `BackendRetryableError` (429, 5xx, network errors -- safe to wait and retry). `collect.py`'s per-model `except` now catches only the retryable subtype; a fatal one propagates uncaught, exactly as an unsupervised process needs it to.

**A second real gap, found while implementing "survive network failures the same way as quota stalls":** the existing retry loop only wrapped HTTP-status handling -- a connection drop or timeout (`httpx.TransportError`) never reached that branch at all and would have crashed the process uncaught on the first blip. Fixed: network exceptions are now caught inside the same retry loop as 429/5xx, with the same capped backoff.

**`src/retraction_bench/rag/supervise.py`, the outer loop:** confirms the exact remaining call count from cache before every attempt (never assumes a number, including the author's own stated 130 -- checked and confirmed exactly before writing any code). On a retryable stall: backs off 15 minutes, then 30, then 60, then holds at 60 -- deliberately does not reset after a partial success, since the observed re-stall-after-9-calls pattern means a briefly-reopened window is not evidence the next one will be longer. On `BackendFatalError` or any unrecognized exception: stops immediately with a clear logged message, never retries. Every step is logged with a timestamp to `data/interim/eval/groq_supervisor.log`. 7 tests, every dependency (the collection call, the remaining-count check, sleep) injected, so the retry/backoff/classification logic is verified without a real sleep or a real API call.

**Run overnight, unsupervised, exactly as built:** picked up the remaining 130 calls after the quota's actual reset, logged every stall and resumption, and completed cleanly. **900/900 responses collected.**

**Consequences:** `src/retraction_bench/rag/backends.py`, `collect.py` changed (exception hierarchy, network-error retry); `src/retraction_bench/rag/supervise.py` new. `tests/test_rag_backends.py`, `test_rag_collect.py` updated, `test_rag_supervise.py` new (7 tests). 316 tests total. The per-minute rate-limit headers Groq returns should not be trusted as the binding constraint for any future cost estimate involving this model -- the real constraint is a daily token quota whose reset behavior is not fully characterized (observed: not a clean recurring reset, since a "cleared" window closed again after 9 calls); a future run at this scale should expect to need the supervisor, not a one-shot call budget estimate.

---

## ADR-139: Full run complete (900/900) -- M1 stratified across the corrected data, malformed-response causes diagnosed per model, retrieval-composition verified on real data, results tables drafted with M1 filled in and M3/Correctness left as explicit pending slots

**Status:** Accepted
