**Context:** ADR-138's supervisor completed the collection run overnight. Before any human label exists for M3 or Correctness, the author asked for everything computable without them: M1 fully stratified now that `openai/gpt-oss-120b`'s cells are no longer thin, malformed/refusal rates on the complete 900, retrieval-rank composition within the actual retrieved sets, confirmation that the ADR-135 redesign produced genuinely different document sets in practice (not just in the unit tests), the post_notice cache-guard re-verified on the real full data, and the results tables drafted with M1's numbers in and M3/Correctness's slots shaped but empty.

**M1, full data, the finding sharper than the partial-data version suggested:** `retrieval_naive` 0.920 (n=213), `retrieval_with_notice` 0.794 (n=209) overall. By model under `retrieval_with_notice`: llama 0.909 (n=66, CI [0.833, 0.970]), gpt-oss 0.859 (n=71, CI [0.775, 0.930]), qwen 0.625 (n=72, CI [0.514, 0.736]). **This is not "llama differs from qwen" as the partial data (gpt-oss at n=6) suggested might be the story -- it is that two of three models are largely unmoved by the notice's presence (llama's and gpt-oss's CIs overlap each other substantially and neither overlaps qwen's at all), and qwen is the outlier that responds.** llama citing at ~91% even with the correction directly in front of it, while also being a model to watch closely once M3 labels exist (does high citation coexist with high acknowledgment in the same model, or do they trade off) is flagged explicitly in the paper draft as the comparison M3 will test, not assumed either way.

**Malformed responses, full 900, two models with mechanistically distinct causes -- not the same failure counted twice:** llama 9/300 (3.0%), gpt-oss 5/300 (1.7%), qwen 0/300. llama's cause (established on partial data, unchanged): a repeatable single-line formatting habit on 3 specific items, identical across all 3 seeds each. **gpt-oss's cause, newly visible only at full n** (the partial run's 161 gpt-oss responses happened to contain none of these): all 5 hit `finish_reason: length` at exactly the shared `MAX_TOKENS=512` cap, with 408-441 of those tokens consumed by the model's own hidden reasoning before the visible answer could complete -- a token-budget design property (`rag/backends.py` shares one cap across reasoning and answer tokens), not a formatting choice. Zero refusals across all 900, any model, any condition -- unchanged from the partial-data finding, now confirmed at full scale.

**Retrieval composition, checked directly on the real 900 rather than assumed from the design:**

- `retrieval_naive`: trap source at rank 1 within the shown documents for 24/24 eligible items; notice present in 0/24 -- the active exclusion has zero leakage.
- `retrieval_with_notice`: trap source rank 1 for 15/24, rank 2 for 9/24 (displaced by the notice); notice rank 1 for 9/24, rank 2 for 10/24, rank 3 for 5/24. **The 9 items where the notice outranks the trap source match exactly the 9-item flip count from the corpus-wide retrieval-ceiling measurement (ADR-134/136)** -- an independent cross-check (real per-response retrieved-document data vs. a full-corpus FAISS search) landing on the identical count and the identical items, not just the same number by coincidence.
- **`retrieval_naive` and `retrieval_with_notice` produced different document sets for 24/24 eligible items, 0 exceptions** -- the ADR-135 redesign's stated goal, confirmed on the actual collected data, not only in the unit tests that shipped with it.
- Retrieval was confirmed precomputed exactly once per item (identical document sets across every model and seed within a condition, for every item) -- no per-call drift.

**post_notice cache-guard, re-verified on all 216 real responses (not just the 18 from the original dry run):** 0 problems -- every one found its same-seed `no_retrieval` response in cache, correctly ordered, correct seed, correct model. The guard holds at full scale exactly as it held on the smoke-test sample.

**Results tables drafted (`paper/paper.md` section 4.2-4.4):** section 4.2 (M1) filled in completely with the numbers above. **Section 4.3, the slot originally scoped for the dropped M2 (ADR-127), repurposed for Correctness** -- a distinct, later-introduced axis (does an answer's substantive claim match the gold answer, independent of citation) rather than a reintroduction of M2 or an undocumented third metric; a note added to the Reproducibility appendix (section 8) so a reader who remembers "two metrics, not three" is not confused by a third section appearing. Both section 4.3 (Correctness) and section 4.4 (M3) have their full table shapes drafted -- every row and column the eventual numbers will fill -- with `[PENDING: human labels in progress]` in place of any figure, so the labeling artifact's export drops in without restructuring anything.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` sections 4.2, 4.3, 4.4 updated; appendix note added. No code changed this pass -- purely computation and drafting against the completed 900-response cache. `docs/STATUS.md` should be updated to reflect the full run's completion before the next session, not done in this pass since it was not asked for.

## ADR-140: M3 and Correctness hand-labeling complete (150/150, 100/100) -- results computed and drafted into the paper, three labels required a second re-check before the author converged

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** ADR-139 left M3 and Correctness as explicit `[PENDING: human labels]` slots pending the author's blind, randomized hand-labeling of 150 `post_notice` responses and 100 correctness responses. Labeling was done via two interchangeable, identical-content channels sharing the same `card_id`s: the published web artifact and a generated Word document (`data/interim/eval/response_labeling.docx`, built from the same `m3_labeling_key.json`/`correctness_labeling_key.json` so either source, or a mix, resolves to the same underlying responses). Labels were submitted to the assistant in batches over chat rather than via either channel's own export, and recorded live into `data/human/m3_hand_labels.csv` and `data/human/correctness_hand_labels.csv` (per the project protocol's data/human/ convention for irreplaceable hand labels) as each batch arrived.

**A real labeling-reliability issue, not swept under an average:** R035, R037, and R051 received three different labels across three separate submission attempts (defends -> silently_revises -> acknowledges/defends/defends on the final pass), with no majority across the first three tries. Three other items (R036, R041, R056) also varied across attempts but resolved by 2-of-3 majority. Rather than silently pick a value for the unresolved three, the author was asked to re-examine only those three items one more time, told in advance that whatever came back would be treated as final. Final values (acknowledges, defends, defends) are recorded as authoritative with a `note` field in the CSV flagging them as a confirmed final re-check, not silently indistinguishable from a first-pass label. This instability is disclosed here rather than only in the CSV's own note column, in case it becomes worth a one-line mention in the paper's limitations as a fact about how difficult a small number of these classifications are, not evidence of carelessness in the average case (144/150 labels were stable on first submission).

**A second real catch, at the schema level rather than a computation level:** a batch of 30 correctness labels (C031-C060) was initially submitted using M3's label vocabulary (`acknowledges`/`silently_revises`/`defends`) instead of Correctness's (`correct`/`incorrect`/`refused`). This was not written to disk -- the assistant recognized the vocabulary mismatch before persisting anything, flagged it, and the author re-sent the same 30 items with the correct vocabulary. No corrupted labels ever reached `correctness_hand_labels.csv`.

**Results, computed with the project's own `stats/bootstrap.py` (seed 42, 10,000 resamples, percentile CI -- identical methodology to M1, not a separate ad hoc computation):**

- **M3, overall (n=150):** acknowledges 0.560 [0.480, 0.640], defends 0.253 [0.187, 0.327], silently_revises 0.187 [0.127, 0.253], unclear 0/150. By model: llama 0.611/0.181/0.208 (n=72), qwen 0.486/0.347/0.167 (n=72), gpt-oss-120b 0.833/0.000/0.167 (n=6 -- capped at Groq's real availability in the labeling sample, not padded to match the other two models; reported per `stats/strata.py`'s n>=5 floor but flagged as suggestive, not load-bearing). **Answers the question ADR-139 flagged as the comparison to watch: llama's high trap-source citation under `retrieval_with_notice` (~91%, ADR-139) coexists with the highest acknowledgment rate of the two well-sampled models (0.611 vs. qwen's 0.486) -- high citation and high correction-following are not mutually exclusive in the same model.**
- **Correctness, overall (n=100):** correct 0.400 [0.300, 0.500], incorrect 0.470 [0.370, 0.570], refused 0.130 [0.070, 0.200]. By condition, the standout: `post_notice` refused jumps to 0.320 (vs. 0.040-0.080 elsewhere) and correct (0.320) is not higher than plain `no_retrieval` (0.560) -- showing the model the retraction notice directly converts many wrong answers into refusals, not into correct ones, and does not net improve correctness once refusal is counted as its own outcome rather than folded into either correct or incorrect.

**Consequences:** `data/human/m3_hand_labels.csv` (150 rows) and `data/human/correctness_hand_labels.csv` (100 rows) are new, tracked, irreplaceable inputs. `paper/paper.md` sections 4.3 and 4.4 updated with real tables and findings in place of the `[PENDING]` markers; the LLM-judge agreement sub-tables in both sections remain genuinely pending on `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` being set (unchanged blocker from before this ADR). `docs/STATUS.md` should be updated to reflect this before the next session, not done in this pass since it was not asked for.

## ADR-141: Results sections 4.1, 4.5-4.10 drafted from existing annotation-phase data; the retrieval-ceiling measurement caught as stale (pre-ADR-136) and re-run against the corrected corpus, surfacing a cleaner version of this project's central argument

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** With M1/M3/Correctness complete (ADR-139/140), the author asked for a project-wide status audit followed directly by work on what remained: paper sections still `[TODO]`. `results/*.md` already held most of the annotation-phase content needed for sections 4.1 (benchmark composition) and 4.6-4.7 (synthesis contamination, retract-and-replace substance stratum) as straight transcription. Sections 4.8-4.9 needed new computation against existing hand-labels and cached responses. Section 4.5 (retrievability) needed the retrieval-ceiling instrument re-run.

**A real catch: `results/retrieval_ceiling_report.json` was dated 2026-08-19, one day before ADR-133/134/136's corpus corrections, and still carried notice_rank values for RB-0026/RB-0027/RB-0028 -- exactly the three items ADR-136 established do not have a genuine, verifiable notice document at all.** Using this file's numbers directly in section 4.5 would have silently reintroduced a bug this project already spent three ADRs catching and fixing elsewhere. Caught by checking the file's own mtime against the correction date before trusting it, not by assuming a `results/` file is current by default. `corpus/retrieval_ceiling.py`'s `measure_all` was re-run directly against the current, corrected index (`data/interim/corpus/index`) rather than patching the stale JSON by hand. **Sanity check: the recomputed flip count (9/24, notice outranks trap source) matches ADR-139's independently-derived count from real per-response retrieved-document data exactly** -- two different measurement routes landing on the identical number, not a coincidence worth ignoring. The stale file is preserved as `results/retrieval_ceiling_report.json.stale_pre_adr136` (project convention, ADR-133/136), not deleted; the corrected version replaces it in place.

**The recomputation surfaces a sharper version of this project's argument than either prediction alone would have:** of the 24 items with a genuine notice, the notice ranks worse than its own trap source for 15/24 (62.5%, Wilson CI [42.7%, 78.8%]) -- prediction 1 (ordering) holds, but not overwhelmingly. **Prediction 2 (practical invisibility, notice_rank > k=10) fails completely: 0/24.** The full notice-rank distribution across all 24 items is minimum 1, maximum 3 -- every supersession notice in this benchmark ranks in the top 3 of a 400,538-document corpus, no exception. **Holding prediction 1 without prediction 2 locates the failure this benchmark measures in generation, not retrieval:** a document that is never worse than rank 3 is not hard to surface (`retrieval_naive` has to actively exclude it, `retrieval_with_notice` finds it present unforced almost every time); section 4.2's finding that two of three models cite the trap source at similar rates whether or not this trivially-findable notice sits in the same context window is therefore not a retrieval story at all.

**Section 4.9 (epistemic honesty on `superseded_without_current_answer` items) required a new, disclosed mechanical check, not a re-transcription:** a fixed 20-phrase keyword scan (same discipline as `rag/parse.py`'s own refusal-marker check, not an LLM judgment, not offered as a substitute for this project's human-labeled ground truth elsewhere) over all 117 `no_retrieval` responses on the 13 no-current-answer items. Result: 3/117 (2.6%), all from one model on one item, and even those are a soft hedge rather than a direct "no current answer" statement. Zero hits from the other two models across all 39 responses each. The pre-registered ADR-043 prediction (models will almost never say there is no current answer) holds decisively, not just directionally.

**Section 4.8 (synthesis vs. primary-study retraction) tested the ADR-032 pre-registered prediction against the completed Correctness hand-labels, stratified by the supporting source's `evidence_level`:** correct rate 43.8% (primary_study, n=80) vs. 25.0% (synthesis, n=20); refused 8.7% vs. 30.0%. Direction matches the prediction; the synthesis stratum's n=20 CI [0.100, 0.450] overlaps primary_study's [0.325, 0.550] substantially, so this is reported as directionally consistent, not a statistically decisive confirmation.

**Section 4.10 (domain-stratified comparison) computed M1, M3, and Correctness by domain for the first time at the evaluation-phase level (M1 was previously reported only pooled and by model):** the sharpest split found anywhere in this paper is Correctness by domain -- 56.6% correct on Obstetrics/Gynecology items vs. 18.2% on Pharmacology items, non-overlapping 95% CIs. Framed explicitly in the paper as a fact about which specific claims this fixed, non-representative 28-item benchmark happened to select in each domain, not a general claim about domain knowledge, and flagged again for Limitations.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` sections 4.1, 4.5-4.10 all moved from `[TODO]` to drafted content with real numbers (only 4.11's cross-reference-only note and the appendix-level background-corpus retracted-tag rate, `paper/paper.md` section 3.9.1, remain genuinely open -- the latter requires a new PubMed verification pass over the full 400k-document background pool, a heavier data-collection task not attempted in this pass). `results/retrieval_ceiling_report.json` corrected in place, stale version preserved with the standard suffix. `data/interim/eval/retrieval_ceiling_report_corrected.json` is a working copy of the same corrected data, safe to remove once `results/`'s copy is confirmed sufficient. Still open: Abstract, Related Work (section 2), Discussion (section 5), Ethics statement (section 8), Appendix A, and the LLM-judge secondary-agreement tables (blocked on `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`).

## ADR-142: Appendix A (Reproducibility) drafted; `CORPUS_FREEZE.json`'s top-level fields caught as stale relative to the actual corpus used for every reported result

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Continuing the same drafting pass as ADR-141, Appendix A needed the final model/embedding versions, seeds, and corpus freeze hash. The model table was straightforward (ADR-129/130 superseded ADR-045, and ADR-139's own text already confirms Gemini was dropped before the real run). The corpus hash was not: `data/interim/corpus/CORPUS_FREEZE.json`'s top-level `record_count` (400,622) and `content_sha256` reflect only the incremental per-item insertions tracked in its own `update_history` array (ADR-054's mechanism, used correctly through RB-0026/27/28) -- **but the two later, larger corpus corrections, ADR-133's ~19% duplicate-row removal and ADR-136's notice-creation-gate fix, were full structural rebuilds of `assembled_corpus.jsonl`, not incremental insertions, and were never run back through this manifest.**

**Checked directly, not assumed:** `assembled_corpus.jsonl` was reloaded and hashed with `corpus/freeze.py`'s own `content_sha256` function. **325,167 rows**, hash `bbc8b01a600ab78b1b96b2d5310f81f1de1357bd23d2d883b8de2b81e182b174` -- nothing close to the manifest's stale 400,622. The drop is consistent with ADR-133's ~19% dedup figure (400,622 * 0.81 ≈ 324,504, within rounding of the real 325,167). **The corpus actually driving every retrieval-dependent number in this paper (the live FAISS index at `data/interim/corpus/index`) is not in question here** -- ADR-141's retrieval-ceiling recomputation against that same index reproduced ADR-139's per-response flip count (9/24) exactly, an independent cross-check that the *index* is correct. What is stale is specifically this one JSON manifest's bookkeeping, which nothing in the actual pipeline reads at run time (it exists only as a human-readable freeze record).

**Decision:** Report the freshly-computed, verified hash and count in Appendix A, not the stale manifest values, and state plainly that `CORPUS_FREEZE.json` itself needs a fresh top-level write before `make reproduce`'s own claim to authoritatively fingerprint the corpus is true. Not fixed in this pass (writing the manifest is a small, separate, mechanical follow-up, not a paper-content question) -- flagged as an open item rather than silently left inconsistent or silently patched without a record of why.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` Appendix A now states the corpus hash/count directly from a fresh computation, with the manifest gap disclosed in the same paragraph. A follow-up task (not done here): regenerate `CORPUS_FREEZE.json`'s top-level fields from the current `assembled_corpus.jsonl`, or extend `update_history` with the two rebuild events the same way per-item insertions are already recorded, so the file's own claim to be authoritative becomes true again.

## ADR-143: Discussion (section 5) drafted -- the author chose "the failure sits in generation, not retrieval" as the central argument, from four candidate framings offered directly

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** With Results (sections 4.1-4.11) and Appendix A complete (ADR-141/142), Discussion was the largest remaining interpretive gap -- the section that states what this paper's actual argument is, not just what was measured. Per the project protocol ("when a decision has research consequences... stop and ask, these are the author's calls, not mine"), four candidate central arguments were put to the author directly rather than one chosen unilaterally: (1) the problem sits in generation, not retrieval -- notices are never buried yet two of three models cite the trap source regardless; (2) the evidence base itself is contaminated upstream (synthesis contamination, 15.8%); (3) models rarely say "I don't know" (epistemic honesty, 2.6%); (4) a broad synthesis folding all findings into one story. **The author chose (1).**

**Discussion drafted around that choice, with the other three findings folded in as compounding, subordinate evidence rather than competing headlines:** opens with the retrieval-ceiling result (section 4.5, notices never rank worse than 3rd) stated as directly as the data allows; sharpens with the by-model M1 split (two of three models flat, qwen the exception) and the M3 cross-reference (llama acknowledges most under direct post-notice questioning despite citing most during retrieval-based generation -- comprehension is present, default weighting is not); places the pre-drafted Cochrane counterexample (ADR-116, verbatim) as the auditable standard of correct handling the tested models are shown not to meet by default; folds in synthesis contamination (section 4.6) as an independent, upstream-layer compounding failure; folds in the epistemic-honesty result (section 4.9) as a third compounding axis; closes by scoping the claim explicitly ("a specific, narrow claim... not a general indictment of RAG") and cross-referencing section 7's research-programme framing. **The five/six-instance self-correction paragraph (ADR-110/123) was not repeated verbatim a second time** -- it already lives in section 3.11 as of ADR-141; Discussion instead adds one short methodological-parallel paragraph pointing back to it, rather than duplicating a long paragraph the reader has already seen once in the same paper.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` section 5 moved from `[TODO]` to a full draft. Remaining: Abstract (written last, now unblocked since Discussion exists), Related Work (a literature-search task, not a computation), Ethics statement (minor), and the LLM-judge tables (blocked on `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`). Limitations (section 6) should be revisited once Discussion's actual argument is final, to check its existing content doesn't understate or contradict the framing chosen here.

## ADR-145: Related Work (section 2) drafted with 8 verified real citations; LaTeX preprint skeleton set up and confirmed to compile

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Two remaining items from the author: (1) write Related Work, the last major content gap (§2's own TODO named four adjacent areas: RAG faithfulness evaluation, retraction-citation bibliometrics, medical hallucination benchmarks, temporal-validity/knowledge-conflict work); (2) the paper will ultimately be typeset in LaTeX for the arXiv preprint -- author chose a plain, unbound arXiv-preprint style over a conference template (no ACL/NeurIPS submission planned).

**Related Work: every citation was verified against a real source (arXiv abstract page or publisher page fetched directly) before being written -- none guessed from memory or from a search snippet's summary text alone**, per the project protocol's standing rule against fabricated claims, applied here to citations specifically. Eight sources across the four required areas: Es et al. 2023 (RAGAS) and Yu et al. 2024 (RAG evaluation survey) for faithfulness/groundedness metrics; Hsiao & Schneider 2022 and Woo & Walsh 2024 for the bibliometric literature on continued citation of retracted papers (the human-citation analogue of this paper's model-side finding); Pandit et al. 2025 (MedHallu) and Singhal et al. 2023 (Med-PaLM/clinical knowledge) for medical hallucination/factuality benchmarks; Yin et al. 2023/2024 (temporal knowledge editing) and Wang et al. 2025 (RAG with conflicting evidence) for temporal validity and knowledge-conflict work. Each subsection states explicitly how this paper's contribution differs from the cited work, not just that it is adjacent -- per the TODO's own instruction to distinguish "does the model know X" benchmarks from "does the model know X is no longer true" benchmarks. A References section added to `paper/paper.md` (a new addition -- none existed before).

**LaTeX skeleton (`paper/latex/main.tex`, `refs.bib`):** a plain, single-column arXiv-preprint style (`article` class, `times`, `geometry`, `natbib`, `hyperref`, `titlesec` for clean section headers) -- no conference-specific `.sty` file, so nothing binds this paper to a venue it has not been submitted to. **Content migration is deliberately deferred, not done in this pass:** the skeleton has section headers and TODO comments pointing back to `paper/paper.md`, per the author's own reasoning (agreed with directly) that converting ~2050 lines of still-partially-open Markdown content to LaTeX now would mean maintaining two copies during the remaining edits (Related Work was still open when this was raised) -- full migration is planned as a single later pass once `paper/paper.md`'s content is final. **The skeleton and toolchain were verified end-to-end, not just written and assumed to work:** `pdflatex` hung on its first real run (MiKTeX's package-auto-install was not enabled, so it silently waited on a prompt that never arrives in a non-interactive shell) -- diagnosed by checking the process was still alive and consuming memory rather than assuming a crash, fixed via `initexmf --set-config-value=[MPM]AutoInstall=1`, then a full three-pass compile (pdflatex, bibtex, pdflatex, pdflatex) completed cleanly, including confirming bibtex correctly reads `refs.bib` and `plainnat.bst` (the "no \citation commands" message is expected and correct for a skeleton with no `\citep` calls yet, not an error to fix).

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` section 2 and its new References section are the only paper-content change. `paper/latex/main.tex`, `refs.bib`, `.gitignore` are new. `paper/paper.md`'s remaining genuine gaps: the deferred background-corpus retracted-tag rate (section 3.9.1, a heavy new measurement, still not attempted) is now the only substantive open item; the LaTeX migration itself is the next major piece of work, scheduled for after this Markdown draft is considered final.

## ADR-146: Background corpus's own retracted-tag rate measured (0.103%, n=325,086) -- the last deferred paper TODO resolved; a real robustness gap found and worked around in `ingest/pubmed.py`'s verification pipeline

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** Section 3.9.1's last remaining deferred TODO asked for the background (distractor) pool's own PubMed-retraction-tag rate, reusing the section 3.8 verification pipeline (`ingest/pubmed.py`) against the full pool rather than just the 364-candidate screening set. Estimated cost before running: 325,086 unique background PMIDs at ESummary's 150-per-batch limit and the no-API-key 3 req/sec politeness rate is ~2,168 batches, roughly 25-40 minutes, free (a public NCBI API, no paid service) -- run directly since the author had already authorized "do what's left."

**A real fragility in the shipped pipeline, found by running it at this scale for the first time:** `verify_pmids` has no retry on transient HTTP errors, and `verify_pmids_cached` only calls `save_cache` once, after `verify_pmids` returns in full -- so a single mid-run 5xx from NCBI (which happened twice across this run: one 500, one 502, both transient) kills the entire batch with zero progress saved, not just the one failed request. This had never been exercised before at a scale where hitting a transient server error across ~2,168 requests was likely. **Worked around at the driver-script level rather than patching the shipped pipeline in this pass:** the verification was split into 3,000-PMID chunks (20 batches each), each chunk wrapped in its own retry loop (up to 5 attempts, backoff up to 120s) before moving to the next chunk -- since `verify_pmids_cached` does persist its cache after each chunk it completes, a chunk-level failure now costs at most 3,000 PMIDs of rework, not the whole run. Both real transient errors encountered were recovered by this retry loop without manual intervention.

**Result: 335 of 325,086 background documents carry PubMed's `Retracted Publication` tag (0.103%, Wilson 95% CI [0.093%, 0.115%]), 6 lookup errors excluded from the denominator.** Read alongside section 3.8's 7.4% forward-verification gap (known retractions PubMed's own tag misses): the two figures answer different questions and are not in tension -- retraction is rare in the literature generally (~1 in 1,000 documents here), but among documents that are genuinely retracted, the tag itself is unreliable roughly 1 in 14 times. The background corpus's low base rate does not make section 3.8's tagging gap harmless, since that gap is specifically about the documents where the tag matters.

**Also caught and corrected while touching this paragraph:** the background pool size quoted in the surrounding prose (400,538) was the pre-ADR-133 stale figure; updated to the corrected, current 325,086 (with the corrected per-domain split), consistent with Appendix A's already-disclosed corpus-count correction (ADR-142) -- the ~21-fold domain-size imbalance persists after deduplication, so this is not an artifact of the earlier duplicate-row bug.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` section 3.9.1 updated with the real finding and the corrected pool size. `data/interim/eval/background_pubmed_verification_cache.csv` is a new, regenerable interim artifact (gitignored, per repo convention -- not tracked). **`ingest/pubmed.py`'s retry-on-transient-error and incremental-checkpointing gap is not fixed in `src/` this pass** -- worked around at the driver-script level only, since the measurement itself was the deliverable, not a pipeline rewrite. Flagged here as a known, real gap: a future `make reproduce` run of this specific measurement would hit the identical fragility at this scale and should either reuse the chunked-retry pattern from this ADR or have the fix applied to `verify_pmids`/`verify_pmids_cached` directly first. This is the last item from the paper-writing session's own tracked TODO list; remaining work is proofreading and the LaTeX content migration (ADR-145).

---

## ADR-147: Full content migration from `paper.md` to LaTeX -- a custom, reusable converter (`paper/latex/build.py`) rather than hand-typing ~2,050 lines, verified by a real end-to-end compile

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** With `paper.md`'s content complete (ADR-140-146), the author asked to migrate it into the LaTeX skeleton set up earlier (ADR-145) so the arXiv submission itself could proceed. Hand-converting ~2,050 lines of Markdown -- heavy inline formatting, ~17 tables, two long blockquotes, several numbered/bulleted lists, snake\_case identifiers throughout needing underscore escaping -- risked exactly the kind of silent transcription error this project's own standards exist to catch. No `pandoc` installation was available on this machine (checked directly, not assumed).

**Built a tailored Markdown-to-LaTeX converter (`paper/latex/build.py`), not a generic one, matching this specific document's own conventions:** bold/italic/inline-code spans, snake\_case and special-character escaping (a token-placeholder scheme so nested spans -- code inside bold, etc. -- convert correctly regardless of processing order), `--`-as-em-dash normalization (this document's own house style, alongside literal unicode em-dashes used in the earlier-drafted Introduction/Abstract), Markdown tables to `tabular`/`booktabs`, the two long blockquote paragraphs to `quotation` environments, numbered/bulleted lists to `enumerate`/`itemize` with indentation-based continuation detection, and an explicit `CITE_MAP` translating this paper's own "(Author, Year)" / "Author (Year)" in-text citation prose to `\citep{}`/`\citet{}` against the `refs.bib` keys already set up (ADR-145) -- chosen per-citation to match whether the original prose already supplied the author names (`\citet`, textual) or expected them supplied by the citation itself (`\citep`, parenthetical), since treating all eight uniformly produced doubled author names or doubled parentheses on the first attempt, caught by direct inspection of the generated `.tex` before compiling, not assumed correct.

**Two real bugs found and fixed by actually compiling, not by treating a clean script run as sufficient:** (1) an em-dash-normalization step applied only to the outer paragraph text, never to text captured inside bold/italic spans (which are stored as opaque placeholder tokens before the outer normalization runs) -- unicode em-dashes inside `\textbf{}`/`\textit{}` content survived unconverted into the `.tex` output until this was traced and the normalization moved inside the span-building functions too. (2) Wide tables (long prose cells, e.g. the model-selection table, the synthesis-contamination table) initially used plain `l` columns and overflowed the page margin by 500-1200pt on first real compile -- fixed with a length-based heuristic (`max_cell_len > 50` triggers wrapped `p{}` columns sized as even fractions of `\textwidth`, narrow numeric-result tables keep plain `l` columns since they already fit). A single very long unbroken token (the SHA-256 corpus hash in Appendix A) still overflowed after that fix since `\texttt` doesn't hyphenate -- generalized into the converter itself (`SEQSPLIT_THRESHOLD`): any code span over 40 characters with no internal spaces is wrapped in `\seqsplit{}` automatically, not hand-patched as a one-off.

**Verified end-to-end, not just "no Python traceback":** full four-pass compile (`pdflatex`, `bibtex`, `pdflatex` x2) completed cleanly -- 41 pages, zero undefined references or citations, only 10 trivial (6-72pt) cosmetic overfull-box warnings remaining from individual long model identifiers inside already-wrapped table cells, down from the hundreds-of-points overflows before the table fix. `microtype` and `\sloppy` (with a 2em emergency stretch) added to the preamble to absorb ordinary paragraph-level justification slack, reducing what would otherwise have been many more minor warnings across 41 pages of migrated prose.

**Consequences:** `paper/latex/main.tex` now holds the complete, real paper content (not a TODO-stub skeleton) and compiles to a 41-page PDF. `paper/latex/build.py` is committed as a reusable, documented tool -- rerunning it after any future edit to `paper/paper.md` regenerates `main.tex` from scratch rather than requiring hand-sync of two copies, closing the exact double-maintenance risk the author and I agreed to defer migration to avoid until content was final (ADR-145). `paper.md` remains the canonical source; `main.tex` is a generated artifact of it, stated as such in `build.py`'s own docstring so a future session does not mistake one for the other. Remaining before arXiv submission itself: a full proofread pass (not yet done), and the actual submission action (the author's own, per the standing rule that public, hard-to-reverse actions are not taken unilaterally).

---

## ADR-148: Author self-critique acted on -- five real figures added, ~150 internal-only references (ADR/the project protocol/ANNOTATION.md) stripped from the main text, self-narrating process prose compressed, and two real LaTeX converter bugs caught by actually reading the compiled PDF rather than trusting a clean build log

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The author read the migrated LaTeX draft and gave a direct, harsh self-critique: zero figures in a 41-page paper, internal decision-log IDs cited throughout the main text as if they were real citations, a self-narrating research-log tone in Methods/Discussion, no empirical demonstration of the paper's central claim, thin Related Work, and Methods disproportionately long relative to Results/Discussion. Asked to work through the list in priority order.

**Figures (five, all real, all data-driven or schematic, none illustrative filler):** `paper/latex/figures/make_figures.py` generates three results figures directly from the exact numbers already in the corresponding paper.md tables (M1 by model x condition, M3 by model, Correctness by domain, each with 95% CI error bars) using matplotlib; `make_schematics.py` generates two structural figures (the four experimental conditions as a box-and-arrow diagram, the annotation funnel from 71,540 raw Retraction Watch rows to the final 28-item benchmark). `build.py` extended with Markdown image-syntax support (`![caption](path "label")` -> `\includegraphics`/`\caption`/`\label`) so figures live in `paper.md` like everything else, not hand-inserted into `main.tex` only. A sixth addition, not a figure: a real worked example (RB-0001, `llama3.1:8b`, `retrieval_with_notice`) quoting the actual notice text and the actual model answer verbatim from the response cache, addressing the "never shows a real example" gap directly.

**Internal-reference stripping:** every `(ADR-XXX)`-style citation (225 instances), every the project protocol section N` reference (16), and every `ANNOTATION.md`/`docs/DECISIONS.md`/`docs/STATUS.md` pointer (6) removed from the main text -- these are this project's own engineering log and governance document, meaningless and unprofessional-looking to an external reviewer, a different category from the `results/*.md` and rubric-file pointers that were kept (those function as genuine supplementary-material references, analogous to what would ship alongside the code release). Done in three automated regex passes (safe trailing/leading pure-ADR parenthetical removal, mixed-content parenthetical surgery, possessive-form rewriting) followed by manual review and fix of every remaining instance individually, since the mixed cases could not be handled safely by regex alone. **Caught two real grammar breaks left by the automated passes while doing that manual review** (an orphaned "?." from a mid-sentence parenthetical removal; a dropped connector before "every weighting formula" in the M2-dropped sentence) -- both fixed, and the manual-review requirement this discovery implies (never trust a bulk regex edit across a full paper without a full re-read afterward) is the reason the remaining ~15 mixed cases were done one at a time rather than trusting a second, cleverer regex.

**Self-narration compression:** two "Status: drafted for real..." working notes (Introduction, section 7) deleted outright -- pure drafting metadata, never paper content. The six-instance self-correction narrative and the four-item honest-negative-results paragraph in section 3.11 (previously ~600 words across two long blockquotes) compressed to one ~180-word paragraph retaining every substantive correction but dropping the "this project's working notes" framing device. The Discussion's parallel methodological-note paragraph updated to match. A handful of "the author flagged"/"per the author's instruction" process-narration phrases rephrased to state the finding directly.

**Two real bugs in `build.py` caught only by rendering the actual compiled PDF and looking at it page by page, not by trusting "compiles with 0 errors":** (1) list-item continuation lines in `paper.md` use single-space indentation, but the converter's continuation check required 2+ spaces (`line.startswith("  ")`), silently misparsing every multi-line list item in the document into scrambled, interleaved content -- fixed to accept any leading whitespace (`re.match(r"^[ \t]+\S", line)`). This had been present since the very first successful compile (ADR-147) and had gone unnoticed because no one had actually looked closely at the rendered list pages until this pass. (2) Multi-line blockquotes (consecutive `> ` lines, as used by the new worked-example callout) were each wrapped in their own separate `quotation` environment instead of being joined into one paragraph, producing fragmented, oddly-wrapped output -- fixed to consume all consecutive `>`-prefixed lines as a single block before closing the environment. Also fixed: wide tables were using uniform equal-width columns regardless of actual content, causing narrow columns (e.g. a `Slot` column next to a long `Identifier` column) to overflow into their neighbors -- switched to proportional column widths computed from each column's own longest cell; and table cells specifically now force `\seqsplit` on any code span over 10 characters (vs. 40 in body prose), since a column narrow enough to need wrapping at all needs a much lower bar for "this token might not fit."

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` and `paper/latex/build.py` both updated; `paper/latex/main.tex` regenerated (43 pages, clean 4-pass compile, 5 trivial cosmetic overfull-box warnings remaining, zero undefined references or citations). `paper/latex/figures/` is new. Verified by rendering multiple pages to PNG and visually inspecting them directly, not just checking the LaTeX log for errors -- the two converter bugs above would not have been caught by log-checking alone, only by looking at the actual output. Still open from the original self-critique list: an empirical faithfulness-metric demonstration of the paper's central claim (a real RAGAS-style check run against this project's own cached responses), and a further Related Work expansion. Both larger undertakings than the fixes in this ADR, not started in this pass.

---

## ADR-149: The paper's central claim tested empirically, not just argued -- a direct NLI-based faithfulness check against this project's own model outputs (section 4.12), finding 97.2% of trap-source citations would pass; two more real LaTeX converter bugs (nested emphasis) caught by re-reading the compiled PDF

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The author confirmed the target is eventually a journal submission, not only an arXiv preprint, and asked the manuscript be prepared to that standard -- the remaining items from ADR-148's self-critique list (an empirical demonstration of the paper's central conceptual claim; deeper Related Work) were explicitly still open. This ADR addresses the empirical-demonstration item.

**The claim being tested:** sections 1 and 2 argue conceptually that a faithfulness/groundedness check, as currently practiced, has no mechanism for detecting supersession -- a citation of a retracted source, faithfully summarized, should pass such a check. This had never been demonstrated against this project's own data, only argued.

**Method (`src/retraction_bench/eval/faithfulness_check.py`, new module):** for every parsed `retrieval_naive`/`retrieval_with_notice` response that cites the trap source (M1's own `cites_superseded` condition), score NLI entailment between the trap source's title+abstract (premise -- exactly the text the model was shown, matching `rag/prompts.py`'s own document-rendering format byte for byte) and the model's parsed answer (hypothesis), using `roberta-large-mnli` (Liu et al. 2019's RoBERTa fine-tuned on Williams et al. 2018's MNLI), in the methodological lineage of SummaC (Laban et al. 2022)'s NLI-based factual-consistency checking. Explicitly not subject to the project protocol's no-LLM-ground-truth rule the way M1/M3/Correctness are -- this applies a real, citable, automated *metric* to the model's own output and reports what that metric says, exactly as a real faithfulness pipeline would, never as a substitute for any ground-truth label elsewhere in the project.

**A real methodological finding surfaced immediately: strict NLI entailment is too demanding a bar even for genuine paraphrase.** The section 4.2 worked example (RB-0001) -- which restates its trap source's claim closely enough that no reader would call it unfaithful -- scores `NEUTRAL` (81.1%), not `ENTAILMENT`. This matches Laban et al.'s own finding that raw document-level NLI (premise longer than the sentence-level pairs NLI models are trained on) underperforms a more careful sentence-segmented approach (SummaC's actual contribution) -- disclosed explicitly as a limitation of this simpler check, not smoothed over. **The label distribution across all n=362 qualifying responses: 60.5% ENTAILMENT, 36.7% NEUTRAL, 2.8% CONTRADICTION.** Given entailment's demonstrated over-strictness even on a known-faithful case, the paper reports the practically operative pass criterion real inconsistency-detection pipelines actually use -- not-contradiction, not strict entailment -- as the headline number.

**Result: 352/362 (97.2%, Wilson-style bootstrap CI [95.3%, 98.9%]) of trap-source citations would pass an NLI-based faithfulness check. Under `retrieval_naive` specifically, the rate is 196/196 -- not most, all.** Under `retrieval_with_notice`, where the correction sits directly alongside the trap source in the same context the model was shown, the pass rate drops only to 94.0%, nowhere near zero. This is the direct empirical demonstration the paper's Introduction and Related Work previously only argued conceptually, and is now cited from both (plus the Abstract and the relevant Discussion paragraph) rather than left as a single isolated Results subsection.

**Three new, verified citations** (checked against their actual arXiv/ACL pages before being written, per this project's standing rule against fabricated references): Laban et al. (2022, SummaC, TACL, arXiv:2111.09525), Liu et al. (2019, RoBERTa, arXiv:1907.11692), Williams et al. (2018, MultiNLI, NAACL). Added to `paper/paper.md`'s References, `paper/latex/refs.bib`, and `build.py`'s `CITE_MAP`.

**Two more real converter bugs found by re-reading the recompiled PDF, not by trusting a clean log (continuing the pattern from ADR-148):** nested `*italic*` markup inside a `**bold**` span (two instances in the document, e.g. "a story about *when* they do") was silently dropped by the bold-conversion path, since it escaped the inner text without first converting nested single-asterisk spans -- the literal asterisks survived into the PDF as plain characters instead of rendering as italics. Fixed by running the italic-conversion regex on a bold span's inner content before escaping it, using the same token-placeholder mechanism already used elsewhere in the converter. Both instances confirmed rendering correctly in the recompiled PDF.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` section 4.12 is new; Abstract, Related Work, and Discussion updated with pointers to it, so the empirical result is visible at every level a reader might read the paper (abstract-only, related-work skim, or full read), not buried in one subsection. `src/retraction_bench/eval/faithfulness_check.py` is a new, documented module (not part of the headline M1/M3/Correctness pipeline, no test suite written for it -- an intentional scope call for a secondary demonstration script, matching this project's existing treatment of `eval/llm_judge.py`'s role). `data/interim/eval/faithfulness_check_nli.json` is a new, regenerable, gitignored interim artifact. `paper/latex/{build.py,refs.bib,main.tex}` updated; 44-page PDF recompiles cleanly, 0 undefined references. Still open from the original self-critique: a deeper Related Work pass (more citations, a comparison table against prior benchmarks) -- the one remaining item, not started in this pass.

---

## ADR-150: Related Work deepened for journal-submission standard -- three more verified citations, a direct benchmark comparison table; two more real table-rendering bugs found and fixed by visual inspection

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The author clarified the target: an arXiv preprint now, a journal submission soon after -- prepare the manuscript to that standard. This is the last item from ADR-148's self-critique list (Related Work depth) not yet addressed.

**Three more citations, each verified against its real arXiv page before being written (same discipline as ADR-145/149, no exceptions):** RAGTruth (Niu et al., 2024, arXiv:2401.00396, span-level hallucination annotations for RAG), RAGBench (Friel et al., 2024, arXiv:2407.11005, 100k-example RAG evaluation benchmark with a four-facet TRACe framework), RealTime QA (Kasai et al., 2022, arXiv:2207.13332, weekly-refreshed temporal QA). A new Related Work paragraph places RAGTruth/RAGBench directly against this paper's own central claim: both treat the retrieved context as fixed ground truth and ask only whether the output is supported by it, never whether the retrieved document itself has been superseded -- the same blind spot argued earlier in the section, now named against two large, recent, purpose-built RAG benchmarks specifically rather than left at the level of faithfulness metrics in the abstract. RealTime QA folded into the existing temporal-validity paragraph as a third adjacent literature (gradual real-world drift, distinct from retraction's discrete formal event).

**A direct comparison table added** (RAGTruth, RAGBench, MedHallu, RealTime QA vs. this paper), on the axes that actually matter for the comparison: what failure mode is tested, whether formal supersession of the retrieved evidence is a first-class condition, domain, and how ground truth is established. This is exactly the artifact a journal reviewer expects and a preprint reader does not strictly require -- added now per the author's explicit journal-standard instruction, not before.

**Two more real bugs found by rendering and reading the recompiled PDF (continuing the exact pattern of ADR-148/149 -- every table-rendering bug caught in this project so far has been found this way, never by a clean compile log alone):** (1) a long slash-separated run with no spaces ("Utilization/relevance/adherence/completeness", RAGBench's own description) had no break point and overflowed its column -- fixed generally in `escape_plain` by permitting (not forcing) a line break after every literal `/` in plain text, a safe global change since `\allowbreak` never alters layout unless a break is actually needed there. (2) A single unbroken bolded proper noun ("RetractionBench") in the table's own row-label column still overflowed even in a proportionally-sized column, since English hyphenation patterns offer LaTeX no break point inside it -- fixed at the content level (the row is labeled "This paper," the standard convention for a comparison table's own method row, rather than repeating the paper's name) rather than by further automating around an inherently narrow, low-value case. The residual risk (a single long unbreakable plain-text word in a narrow column) is now documented directly in `build.py` for future maintainers rather than silently left for someone to rediscover.

**Consequences:** `paper/paper.md` section 2 substantially expanded (two new paragraphs, one new table); `paper/latex/{build.py,refs.bib,main.tex}` updated. 45-page PDF recompiles cleanly, 0 undefined references, every table and citation visually verified by rendering pages to PNG and reading them directly. This closes the last item from ADR-148's original self-critique list -- Related Work depth, the empirical faithfulness demonstration (ADR-149), real figures, internal-reference stripping, and self-narration compression (ADR-148) are all now addressed. Remaining before submission: a full read-through proofread pass (not yet done), and the arXiv/journal submission actions themselves, which remain the author's own to take.

---

## ADR-151: External reviewer critique acted on -- bootstrap pseudoreplication fixed (item-clustered CIs replace per-response CIs throughout), a proper paired/permutation test replaces CI-overlap eyeballing for the paper's central per-model comparison, and several overclaimed or stale statements corrected

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The author, preparing this manuscript to journal-submission standard, ran a detailed external-reviewer-style critique against the full draft (57 numbered points, a severity triage, a scoring table) and asked the actionable items be worked through. Several points required new data collection this project cannot manufacture in this pass (a second independent annotator, matched non-retracted control items, expanding the benchmark from 28 to 75-100 items, a formal mixed-effects regression as the primary statistical model) and are recorded here as genuine follow-on work, not attempted. This ADR covers what was fixable now: a real statistical bug, and a set of overclaimed or stale prose statements the corrected statistics and a fresh read surfaced.

**The central finding, and the most serious point in the critique: every bootstrap CI in this paper (M1, M3, Correctness) resampled individual responses as if independent, when up to 3 seeds of the same benchmark item are correlated through the item, not 3 independent draws (pseudoreplication).** Fixed by adding `bootstrap_proportion_ci_clustered` to `stats/bootstrap.py` (resamples whole item-clusters with replacement, keeping every response of a resampled item together; point estimate unchanged, only the CI's resampling unit changes), with 6 new tests including a regression test proving clustered CIs are wider under within-cluster-correlated data. `eval/m1.py`'s `compute_m1` and `eval/m3.py`'s `compute_m3` now call the clustered version; both docstrings extended to explain why. All 322 tests pass. On this project's own real data, item-clustered CI widths are roughly double the (wrong) per-response widths for the headline M1 numbers -- confirmed directly, not assumed, via scratch re-analysis scripts before touching any paper content. `paper.md`'s M1 (4.2), Correctness (4.3), M3 (4.4), and Domain-stratified comparison (4.10) tables were all recomputed and replaced with the item-clustered values (Correctness has no dedicated `eval/` module -- computed via the same clustered bootstrap function directly against `data/human/correctness_hand_labels.csv`, matching M1/M3's method exactly, still not folded into a new module in this pass).

**A second, related fix: the paper's central per-model comparison ("two of three models are unmoved by the notice, one is not") was previously asserted from eyeballing CI overlap, which the critique correctly identified as not a real hypothesis test.** Replaced with a proper paired bootstrap + sign-flip permutation test (10,000 permutations, seed 42) on the per-item rate difference (`retrieval_with_notice` minus `retrieval_naive`) for each model, over the 21-24 items each model has in both conditions. Result, verified by hand (all 21 of llama's paired items printed and checked individually, not just trusted from the aggregate): `llama3.1:8b` shows an exact null (0/21 items changed at all, permutation p=1.0000) -- the cleanest result in the paper, not a hedge; `qwen2.5:7b` shows a real, significant decrease (mean diff -0.306, 95% CI [-0.500,-0.111], p=0.0067); `openai/gpt-oss-120b` is genuinely inconclusive (mean diff -0.097, 95% CI [-0.236,0.028], p=0.1887) -- this directly contradicts the paper's previous characterization of gpt-oss as "effectively flat" alongside llama; its point estimate moves the same direction as qwen's decrease, but the evidence does not support calling it either flat or changed. Section 4.2, the Discussion's opening paragraph, and the relevant Limitations bullet were all rewritten to state this three-way split instead of the previous two-versus-one framing.

**Overclaiming and staleness fixes, each a direct response to a specific critique point:**
- The Discussion's central claim ("the failure sits in generation, not retrieval") softened to the critique's own suggested formulation: a substantial generation-stage failure alongside retrieval and record-quality limitations, not a retrieval failure ruled out. Propagated into the Abstract, Introduction-adjacent Discussion opening, section 4.5, and the relevant Limitations bullet.
- The `superseded_without_current_answer` item-type (46.4% of the benchmark) reframed everywhere it is restated as "no source identified under our predefined, time-boxed search protocol," not "no answer exists" -- the schema field name itself (`src/retraction_bench/schemas.py`) was left unchanged (a code/data/test-touching rename judged out of scope for a prose-accuracy fix), but every headline restatement of the 46.4% figure (Abstract, section 4.9, Discussion) now carries the protocol-relative qualifier.
- Section 4.9 renamed from "epistemic honesty" to "explicit uncertainty expression rate," with a stated rationale (a disclosed 20-phrase keyword scan cannot support a claim about honesty or calibration) -- matches a limitations bullet that already made this exact point but the section title had not been updated to match.
- A stale "four models are under test" statement in section 3.9.3, and a stale "findings are demonstrated on the four models actually tested" statement near the end of that subsection, both corrected to three -- Gemini was part of the initially-selected four-model set but was dropped after the dry run (already correctly stated in Appendix A, which section 3.9.3's prose had not been kept in sync with). The model table in 3.9.3 gained an explicit "In final evaluation?" column so this is unambiguous at the table level, not just in prose.
- A corpus-size inconsistency (400,538/400,542, the stale pre-correction frozen-manifest count, still appearing in the Abstract, Discussion, and section 4.5 well after Appendix A had already disclosed the manifest is stale and the live, actually-used corpus is 325,167 rows) corrected to "~325,000" at all three headline locations, consistent with Appendix A's disclosed live count.
- An orphaned Limitations bullet about an "optional medical-specialized model (MedGemma 1.5)" being "an order of magnitude smaller than the other four models under test" was removed outright -- MedGemma appears nowhere else in the paper and was never actually run; the bullet described a comparison that does not exist in any result this paper reports.
- The Abstract decluttered from roughly 11 numbers to roughly 6 headline ones (dropped the 15.8%-of-19 synthesis-contamination figure and the "100% under retrieval_naive" parenthetical from the faithfulness-check sentence) and rewritten around the corrected three-way per-model split rather than a two-versus-one framing.
- Figure captions for `fig_m1_by_model.pdf` and the Correctness-by-domain figure rewritten to match the corrected statistics (the M1 caption previously asserted "two of three models show no meaningful drop," which the paired test now contradicts). `paper/latex/figures/make_figures.py`'s CI arrays for all three data-driven figures (M1 by model, M3 by model, Correctness by domain) updated to the item-clustered values and regenerated.

**Verified, not assumed:** all 322 tests pass (`python -m pytest tests/`); `paper/latex/main.tex` regenerated via `build.py` and recompiled 4-pass (pdflatex, bibtex, pdflatex x2) -- 46 pages, 0 undefined references, 0 errors, 7 trivial cosmetic overfull-hbox warnings (same class as before, not new). Every changed table and both regenerated figures were rendered to PNG and read directly (continuing the standing discipline from ADR-147-150), not trusted from a clean compile log alone -- this specifically caught the stale M1 figure caption above, which a log check would have missed entirely.

**Consequences:** `src/retraction_bench/stats/bootstrap.py`, `eval/m1.py`, `eval/m3.py`, `tests/test_bootstrap.py` changed (new clustered-bootstrap function, wired into the two eval modules it applies to). `paper/paper.md` substantially revised across the Abstract, sections 3.9.3, 4.1-4.5, 4.9, 4.10, 4.12-adjacent Discussion, and Limitations. `paper/latex/figures/make_figures.py` and its three regenerated PDF/PNG figure pairs, plus `paper/latex/main.tex`, all updated to match. Correctness still has no dedicated `eval/correctness.py` module (computed via ad hoc script against the item-clustered bootstrap function directly) -- formalizing it into `src/` remains open, lower-priority follow-on work, not blocking. Explicitly out of scope for this pass and not attempted: a second independent annotator, matched non-retracted control items, expanding the benchmark past 28 items, and a mixed-effects logistic regression as the paper's primary statistical model -- each requires new data collection or a substantially larger statistical undertaking the author was told plainly requires their own further time, not a text or code edit.

---

## ADR-152: Second round of external review acted on -- multiplicity correction, a genuinely fixed (not just disclosed) corpus manifest, a within-item seed-stability check, prespecified hypotheses stated explicitly, and a real table-overflow bug in the LaTeX converter found and fixed by rendering the new content

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The author submitted a second, more detailed external-reviewer-style critique against the ADR-151 revision (again scored on a 9-dimension rubric, "Major Revision" verdict), confirming the statistical fixes from ADR-151 landed correctly while surfacing a further, more subtle layer of issues. As with ADR-151, several of the highest-priority items require new experiments or new human labor this pass cannot manufacture -- a placebo retrieval condition that swaps the lowest-ranked distractor rather than adding the notice outright, a placebo `post_notice` condition to isolate the confrontation effect from the notice effect itself, clinician-paraphrased question variants to test whether notice retrievability generalizes beyond this benchmark's paper-derived query formulation, a second independent annotator with reported kappa, and labeling the remaining ~800 cached responses beyond the current 100-response Correctness sample -- and are recorded here as genuine follow-on work, not attempted. This ADR covers everything fixable now without new data collection.

**Multiple-comparisons correction added to the paper's central per-model test.** The three model-specific paired tests introduced in ADR-151 (llama/qwen/gpt-oss) are a single prespecified family, and reporting only raw p-values without correction was a fair critique. Holm-adjusted p-values now accompany the raw ones everywhere the paired test is reported (section 4.2's table, the Discussion, Limitations): qwen's result (raw p=0.0067) survives correction at Holm-adjusted p=0.0201, still comfortably below 0.05; llama's and gpt-oss's non-significant results remain non-significant after correction (Holm-adjusted p=1.0000 and 0.3774 respectively). Computed and verified against a hand check of the Holm procedure before writing anything into the paper.

**"Exact null" language softened everywhere it appeared (6 locations: abstract, section 4.2's table and prose, the M1 figure caption, section 4.5, Discussion, Limitations).** A non-significant paired test with a point estimate that happens to land exactly on zero is evidence of "no observed item-level change," not proof the true effect is exactly zero -- conflating the two is a real inferential overreach a formal equivalence test would be needed to justify, and this project does not run one (no prespecified smallest-effect-size-of-interest). Every instance now uses "no observed item-level change" as the precise claim, with an explicit one-sentence caveat attached at each first substantial occurrence.

**A new, directly computable finding added to strengthen (not just soften) the llama result: within-item seed agreement, checked and reported rather than assumed.** For every (model, condition, item) group with at least 2 of 3 seeds parsed, we checked how often all seeds agreed on the M1 outcome. `llama3.1:8b` agrees 3/3 on every single one of its 97 such groups -- 100%, zero exceptions -- which is the direct mechanical explanation for why its paired test lands on an exact-zero point estimate: this model's citation behavior is close to deterministic given (model, condition, item), not noisy behavior that happened to average out. `qwen2.5:7b` (99/100 groups at 3/3) and `openai/gpt-oss-120b` (94/99 at 3/3) are similarly stable. Reported in section 4.2, directly addressing the critique's point that 3 seeds' worth of characterization was being asserted without ever being checked.

**Retrieval-ceiling and generation-stage claims scoped explicitly to this benchmark's own paper-derived query formulation, not stated as general retrievability or generation-failure claims.** The critique correctly noted that this benchmark's questions are written directly against each retracted paper's own claim, which mechanically inflates lexical overlap with that paper's (and its notice's) title -- the retrieval-ceiling result ("every notice ranks in the top 3") is real but specific to this query formulation, not a claim about notice discoverability under a clinician's own phrasing of the same question. Sections 4.5, 5 (Discussion), and the closing paragraph of section 4 now state this scope explicitly wherever the top-3 or "not the bottleneck" claims appear, and note that testing clinician-paraphrased variants is exactly the kind of follow-on work this design cannot itself answer. The Discussion's central causal claim was similarly hardened from an implied general result to "consistent with a generation-stage weighting failure... not proof of the underlying mechanism."

**M1 redefined precisely as citation, not "citation or reliance."** The original definition ("cite or rely on a retracted source") overstated what `cites_superseded` actually measures (an explicit document-ID citation). Fixed in `paper.md` section 3.10's metric definitions and in `eval/m1.py`'s own docstring -- a model could in principle be influenced by a retracted source without citing its ID, a case M1 does not detect and does not claim to.

**Denominator-imbalance caveat added wherever the pooled 56% M3 acknowledgment figure is reported (abstract, section 4.4, introduction).** `openai/gpt-oss-120b` contributes only 6 of the 150 hand-labeled responses behind that figure; restricted to the two adequately-sampled models (`qwen2.5:7b` and `llama3.1:8b`, n=144), the rate is 54.9%, computed directly from `data/human/m3_hand_labels.csv` rather than approximated. Both figures are now reported together everywhere the 56% number appears as a headline claim.

**Figure 4 (M3 by model) now visually, not just textually, marks `openai/gpt-oss-120b`'s n=6 as non-comparable.** Its three bars are hatched and rendered at reduced opacity (`make_figures.py`), and its x-axis label now reads "(n=6, descriptive only)" -- a reader scanning the chart, not just reading the caption, now sees the difference. This directly addresses the critique's point that a caption alone does not stop a casual reader from treating three visually-identical bars as comparable estimates.

**The corpus-manifest staleness ADR-151 could only disclose is now actually fixed, not merely disclosed.** `CORPUS_FREEZE.json`'s top-level fields (record_count, content_sha256, domain/role counts, pub-year distribution) had fallen out of sync with two later structural corpus rebuilds, and the paper's own Appendix A previously stated this as a known, open gap -- exactly the kind of thing the critique called out as unacceptable for a reproducibility-focused submission ("fix it, don't hide it"). `corpus/freeze.py` gained a new `write_manifest` function (4 new tests, `tests/test_freeze.py`, all passing) that recomputes the manifest against a live corpus and appends one `update_history` entry rather than discarding the prior ones -- a committed, tested, reusable fix, not another one-off script. Run against the live `assembled_corpus.jsonl`, it reproduces the exact same record count (325,167) and content hash (`bbc8b01a600ab78b1b96b2d5310f81f1de1357bd23d2d883b8de2b81e182b174`) Appendix A had already independently computed by other means -- the two matching exactly is itself a confirmation the fix is correct. Appendix A's prose rewritten from "here is a known, disclosed gap" to "here is what was wrong and here is how it was fixed."

**Three hypotheses (H1, H2, H3) stated explicitly and up front in section 4**, addressing the critique's point that the paper implicitly tested several hypotheses without ever stating a primary confirmatory set. H1 (notice presence reduces citation), H2 (direct confrontation increases correction recognition), and H3 (supersession-aware correctness is lower than raw citation behavior would suggest) are now named before Results begins, with every other comparison in section 4 (domain splits, notice-length correlations, synthesis contamination) explicitly labeled exploratory rather than granted the same evidentiary weight. A one-paragraph justification is included for not fitting a single pooled mixed-effects model across all three hypotheses (limited power at this benchmark's size; reporting each hypothesis's own appropriate test judged more transparent than one model with many interacting terms), with that pooled-model approach named as open follow-on work rather than silently skipped.

**Title changed** from "Evaluating Evidence Currency in Medical Retrieval-Augmented Generation" to "Evaluating Superseded-Evidence Handling in Medical Retrieval-Augmented Generation" -- "evidence currency" implied a broader scope (new RCTs, guideline revisions, evolving safety signals) than this benchmark, which tests only formal retraction/supersession, actually covers. Also fixed: "This paper's contribution is not the benchmark" reframed as "one of this paper's contributions, but not the only one" (the original framing read as an unnecessary and inaccurate philosophical disclaimer); "three widely-deployed model families" softened to "three contemporary language models representing distinct open-weight model families and deployment configurations" (an unnecessary and unoperationalizable claim); the PubMed 7.4% gap and the synthesis-contamination 15.8% figure both reframed at their headline occurrences (abstract, introduction, section 4.6) as candidate-pool-specific findings, not population estimates, with their exact denominators (27/364, 3/19) stated alongside the percentage rather than left implicit; the zero-variation `evidence_tier_match` dimension (15/15 same-tier, 0/15 lower-tier) now states explicitly that no same-tier-vs-lower-tier comparison is analyzable from this data, rather than only reporting the raw counts and leaving a reviewer to notice the implication themselves; the "no current authoritative replacement at all" sentence in section 4.1 (which had drifted back to the un-hedged framing ADR-151 fixed everywhere else) corrected to match the protocol-relative framing used consistently elsewhere.

**A real LaTeX table-overflow bug found and fixed, continuing this project's standing discipline of rendering and reading the compiled PDF rather than trusting a clean compile log.** The new 6-column paired-test table (Model / mean difference / CI / raw p / Holm-adjusted p / Verdict) overflowed the page margin under `build.py`'s existing proportional-width heuristic, which only activated when a single cell exceeded 50 characters -- this table's cells were all individually under 50 characters, but six of them together still overflowed `\textwidth` at plain-`l` column sizing. Root cause: the heuristic checked only the single longest cell, never the combined width across all columns. Fixed by also triggering proportional-width columns whenever the summed column-max character count exceeds 90, independent of any single cell's length -- verified by rendering the specific page both before (visibly overflowing past the right margin) and after (fully contained, all six columns legible) the fix, not merely by checking the compile log stayed clean, since the log's "overfull hbox" warning for this table exists in both the broken and fixed versions (a `\seqsplit`-related false-positive already documented in this project's own build.py, not a sign of remaining breakage) and would not by itself have distinguished them.

**Verified, not assumed:** all 326 tests pass (`python -m pytest tests/`, up from 322 -- 4 new tests for `write_manifest`). `paper/latex/main.tex` regenerated via `build.py` and recompiled 4-pass -- 48 pages, 0 undefined references, 0 errors, 8 cosmetic overfull-hbox warnings (one more than ADR-151's 7, from the wider document; each individually confirmed benign by rendering its page). Every changed table and the re-hatched M3 figure were rendered to PNG and read directly before being reported as fixed.

**Consequences:** `src/retraction_bench/corpus/freeze.py` and `tests/test_freeze.py` changed (new `write_manifest` function and its tests); `src/retraction_bench/eval/m1.py`'s docstring updated for the citation-not-reliance definition; `paper/paper.md` revised across the Abstract, Introduction, sections 3.9.3, 4 (new hypothesis statement), 4.1, 4.2, 4.5, 4.9, Discussion, Limitations, and Appendix A; `paper/latex/build.py` (table-width heuristic fix, title string), `paper/latex/figures/make_figures.py` (M3 figure hatching), and `paper/latex/main.tex` all updated and recompiled. `data/interim/corpus/CORPUS_FREEZE.json` rewritten on disk (gitignored, regenerable, not a tracked change). Explicitly out of scope for this pass and not attempted, same pattern as ADR-151: a placebo retrieval condition isolating the notice effect from context-composition changes, a placebo `post_notice` condition isolating confrontation from the notice itself, clinician-paraphrased question variants, a second independent annotator, and labeling the remaining ~800 responses beyond the 100-response Correctness sample -- each requires new model calls, new human labor, or both, and remains the author's own to resource next.

---

## ADR-153: Full repository audit and manuscript restructuring -- the reproducibility gap actually closed with committed code, the paper cut from 48 to 33 pages across three condensation passes, six new figures and four tables added

**Status:** Accepted

**Context:** The author requested a comprehensive audit-and-rewrite pass against a detailed 30-part specification (shorten the manuscript to Q1 length, add publication-quality figures and tables, split process detail into a Supplementary Information document, and produce standing audit/review deliverables), followed immediately by two rounds of explicit author pushback that the resulting page count was still too long for a typical Q1 journal. This ADR covers the combined result of the audit and all three condensation passes; code referenced elsewhere in this project as "ADR-153" (in `eval/citation.py`, `eval/correctness.py`, `stats/paired_test.py`, `retraction_bench/reproduce.py`) refers to the reproducibility-gap-closing work summarized here, which was implemented before this entry was written -- a real gap in the project's own append-only-decision-log discipline, caught and closed only when a later report referenced this ADR and it did not exist. Recorded now, not backdated to hide the gap.

**Audit (`AUDIT_REPORT.md`).** Before any rewrite, every number in the manuscript was checked against its source file (corpus manifest, cached model responses, hand-label CSVs) and matched exactly -- no fabricated or drifted number was found. One critical, submission-blocking gap was found instead: `make reproduce` was a literal no-op stub (`echo "Not yet implemented."`), and the paper's single most important statistical result -- the item-level paired bootstrap + permutation test + Holm correction behind the per-model M1 comparison -- along with every Correctness statistic, had no committed source code at all. Both had been computed, for the entire project, by uncommitted scratch scripts during authoring sessions. Appendix A's own claim that `make reproduce` "regenerates every number in this paper... without any live API call" was therefore false as written.

**Reproducibility gap closed with new, tested modules, not a patched claim.** `src/retraction_bench/eval/citation.py` (the trap-source-citation check, previously only in scratch scripts, 7 tests), `eval/correctness.py` (Correctness had no `eval/` module at all, unlike M1/M3, 8 tests), `stats/paired_test.py` (paired bootstrap + sign-flip permutation test + Holm-Bonferroni correction, 11 tests), and `retraction_bench/reproduce.py` (ties all of the above to the real cached data, callable via `retraction-bench evaluate` or `make reproduce`, writes `results/reproduced_paper_numbers.{json,md}`). `eval/m1.py`/`eval/m3.py` were refactored, not rewritten, to expose `compute_m1_from_outcomes`/`compute_m3_from_labels` -- the same tested statistic, callable without a fully-populated `EvaluationResult` per response, which is what blocked this from being wired up before: `EvaluationResult.correctness` is a required schema field, but only 100 of 900 responses were ever hand-scored for it, so a real `EvaluationResult` cannot be honestly constructed for the other 800 without fabricating a label -- a real, disclosed schema limitation, not worked around by weakening the schema. `tests/test_reproduce_paper_numbers.py` runs the full pipeline against the real 900-response cache and asserts the output matches every number published in `paper.md`, exactly -- verified, and now a permanent regression test against future drift. 358 tests pass (up from 322).

**Six figures and four tables added, every one generated from real, verified repository data, not invented values.** A conceptual-framework figure (the evidence lifecycle, and the retrieved != used != correct distinction the Discussion is built around), a single-item walkthrough (RB-0001, using text already verbatim elsewhere in the paper, not newly derived), a forest plot of the item-level paired M1 test (read directly from `results/reproduced_paper_numbers.json`, not hand-transcribed, so it is mechanically tied to the same committed pipeline that produces the paper's own numbers), a failure-cascade summary across six independently-denominated measurements (explicitly captioned as not one shrinking population), and a two-panel notice-retrievability figure. Building the retrievability figure caught a real data-currency problem: a candidate source file (`embedding_similarity_and_crowdedness.json`) still showed a pre-correction notice rank of 15 for RB-0026, contradicting the paper's own "every notice ranks top 3" claim -- checked against the live, corrected `results/retrieval_ceiling_report.json` before use, and the stale file was rejected rather than plotted. A real LaTeX bug was caught by rendering and reading the compiled PDF: tables were floating away from their own "Table N." caption text onto the next page under `build.py`'s `[ht]` placement; fixed by adding the `float` package and switching to `[H]` (exactly-here) placement for every generated table.

**Manuscript restructuring, across three condensation passes, not one.** Pass 1 (48 -> 42 pages): construction-process narrative from Methods (screen validation failures, domain-selection deliberation, the retrieval-ceiling mechanism-hunting narrative, benchmark-size planning history) moved to a new `paper/supplementary.md`, replaced in the main text with tight summaries pointing there -- nothing deleted, only relocated. The author correctly pushed back that 42 pages was still too long. Pass 2 (42 -> 35 pages): Results sections 4.6-4.10's case-level detail (the synthesis-contamination case table, the retract-and-replace substance table, the full synthesis-vs-primary breakdown, the uncertainty-expression worked example, domain comparison's extended interpretation) moved to Supplementary; Discussion cut roughly in half by removing numbers already shown in Table 3/Figure 4; Related Work and Appendix A tightened; the correctness-by-domain figure moved to Supplementary since that section is explicitly secondary/exploratory; a duplicate section (4.11, which had become a pure restatement of the newly-added Table 4) removed and subsequent sections renumbered. The author pushed back a second time, on an explicit "keep going" instruction. Pass 3 (35 -> 33 pages): the remaining Methods sections 3.1-3.6 (annotation protocol, record-quality findings, corpus/model-selection deliberation, experimental-condition rationale) tightened further, and the Introduction's five-finding list condensed without dropping any of the five findings, since they are cited back to from the Abstract, Discussion, and Limitations.
